# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 16

## Page 8010

Volume Sixteen
Book of Quranic Commentary
Part Three
From Sūrah al-Zumar to Sūrah al-Ikhlās

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

All praise belongs to Allah alone, and may blessings and peace be upon the one after whom there is no prophet.

Sūrah al-Zumar

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section

Allah Most High says:

“Those who listen to the Word and follow the best of it”

“The Word” here means the Quran, as explained by the early generations of this community and its leading scholars. Likewise, Allah Most High says:

“Have they not pondered the Word, or has there come to them something that did not come to their forefathers?”

The definite article identifies the particular discourse already known, for the entire surah is devoted to commending the Quran and listening to it. We have discussed this at length elsewhere and demonstrated the error of those who cite this verse as evidence for listening to singing and the like, construing it as general in scope. We have also shown that extending it to every kind of speech is invalid by the consensus of the Muslims.

A well-known question arises here concerning His words:

“They listen to the Word and follow

---

## Page 8011

the best of it.” He has thus divided the Word into what is good and what is better, although the Quran is to be followed in its entirety. This is their argument.

The response is as follows:

The answer has three aspects, comprising a counterargument and a resolution.

The first:

This is like His words:

“Follow the best of what has been sent down to you from your Lord.”

And His words:

“We wrote for him on the Tablets an admonition concerning all things and a detailed explanation of all things. So hold firmly to them and command your people to follow the best of them.”

He commanded the believers to follow the best of what was sent down to them from their Lord, and commanded the Children of Israel to uphold the best of the Torah. This is stronger than the verse in question, which merely praises following what is best.

There is no doubt that the Quran contains accounts and commands concerning what is good and what is better. Following a discourse means acting in accordance with what it entails. What it entails includes both what is good and what is better; it is not all of the latter kind, even though the Quran itself is the best of all discourse. A distinction must therefore be drawn between the excellence of a discourse compared with other discourse, and its excellence in relation to what it entails—the things it commands and describes.

The second aspect:

It may be said that He says:

“So give good tidings to My servants, who listen to the Word and follow the best of it. Those are the ones Allah has guided, and those are the people of understanding.”

The Quran contains accounts and commands. Its accounts concern the righteous and those brought near to Allah, as well as the disbelievers and the wicked. There is no doubt that following the example of the first two classes is good

---

## Page 8012

and following the example of those brought near to Allah is better. Its commands include both obligatory and recommended acts. There is no doubt that confining oneself to obligatory acts is good, while performing recommended acts as well is better. Whoever follows what is best, taking those brought near to Allah as examples and drawing near to Allah through voluntary acts after fulfilling obligatory duties, is more deserving of glad tidings.

Accordingly, His words:

“Follow the best of what has been sent down to you from your Lord,”

and:

“Command your people to follow the best of them,”

likewise command this. However, commands encompass both obligation and recommendation. Thus, whatever is obligatory in these passages is commanded as an obligation, and whatever is recommended is commanded as a recommendation. The same applies to His words:

“Indeed, Allah commands justice, good conduct, and giving to relatives,”

and His words:

“He commands them to do what is right.”

What is right includes both categories. Likewise, His words:

“Do good, so that you may succeed,”

encompass both categories, as do His words:

“Bow and prostrate,”

and other such passages.

---

## Page 8013

He also said, may Allah have mercy on him:

Section: On Listening

The foundation of the listening that Allah has commanded is listening to what the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, brought, with understanding and acceptance.

In this respect, people fall into four categories: those who turn away and refuse to listen; those who hear the sound but do not understand the meaning; those who understand it but do not accept it; and, fourth, those who listen with understanding and acceptance.

The “first category” are like those of whom He said:

“Those who disbelieve say, ‘Do not listen to this Quran, but make noise during its recitation, so that you may prevail.’”

The “second category” are those who hear its sound but do not understand its meaning. Allah Most High said:

“The example of those who disbelieve is like that of one who shouts at what hears nothing but calls and cries. Deaf, dumb and blind, they do not understand.”

Allah Most High also said:

“And among them are those who listen

---

## Page 8014

to you, but We have placed coverings over their hearts so that they do not understand it, and deafness in their ears. Even if they see every sign, they will not believe in it. When they come to you, disputing with you, those who disbelieve say, ‘This is nothing but tales of the ancients.’”

Allah Most High also said:

“And among them are those who listen to you. But can you make the deaf hear, even though they do not understand?”

“And among them are those who look at you. But can you guide the blind, even though they do not see?”

“Indeed, Allah does not wrong people at all, but it is people who wrong themselves.”

Allah Most High also said:

“When you recite the Quran, We place a hidden barrier between you and those who do not believe in the Hereafter.”

“And We place coverings over their hearts so that they do not understand it, and deafness in their ears. When you mention your Lord alone in the Quran, they turn their backs in aversion.”

“We know best how they listen when they listen to you, and when they converse in secret, as the wrongdoers say, ‘You follow none but a bewitched man.’”

Allah Most High also said:

“Who does greater wrong than one who is reminded of the signs of his Lord, then turns away from them and forgets what his own hands have sent forth? Indeed, We have placed coverings over their hearts so that they do not understand it, and deafness in their ears. Even if you call them to guidance, they will never be guided.”

His words, “so that they do not understand it,” encompass both those who do not understand the meaning of its wording as it can be understood through knowledge of Arabic alone, and those who understand that meaning but do not recognize the actual realities to which it refers: the “entities,” “actions,” and “attributes” intended by its commands and statements. They may see these realities without knowing that they are what the discourse denotes. For example, someone may know of a blameworthy quality and himself possess it, or something of the same kind, yet not realize that he falls within its scope.

Allah Most High also said:

---

## Page 8015

“Indeed, the worst of living creatures in Allah’s sight are the deaf and mute who do not reason.”

“Had Allah known any good in them, He would have made them hear. But even if He had made them hear, they would have turned away in aversion.”

He said this after His words:

“O you who believe, obey Allah and His Messenger, and do not turn away from him while you hear.”

“And do not be like those who say, ‘We hear,’ yet do not hear.”

His words, “Had Allah known any good in them, He would have made them hear,” do not mean merely making them hear the sound, for two reasons.

First, hearing in this sense is indispensable: the proof cannot be established against those being called to faith unless they hear it. As He said:

“If any of the polytheists seeks your protection, grant him protection so that he may hear the words of Allah; then convey him to his place of safety.”

He also said:

“That I may warn you through it, and whoever it reaches.”

And He said:

“We never punish until We have sent a messenger.”

Second, such hearing alone brings no benefit. All the disbelievers who listened to the Quran and rejected it experienced this, as noted above. This differs from being made to hear with understanding, which is what Allah grants to those in whom there is good. This is comparable to the Prophet’s statement, reported in the two Sahīh collections, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“When Allah intends good for someone, He grants him understanding of the religion.”

This verse and hadith indicate that if someone has not attained the hearing through which he understands what is said, Allah has not known any good in him and has not intended good for him. They also indicate that anyone in whom Allah knows good, or for whom He intends good, must be made to hear and granted understanding.

For the hadith makes clear that Allah grants understanding to everyone for whom He intends good. The former necessarily entails the latter, and the wording is general. Thus, anyone whom He has not granted understanding does not fall within that general statement, so Allah cannot have

---

## Page 8016

intended good for him. Since the necessary consequence is absent in his case, that which entails it is also absent.

Likewise, His words:

“Had Allah known any good in them, He would have made them hear.”

He explains that the former is a condition for the latter—a condition in the grammatical sense, which entails the latter and is its cause. This means that everyone in whom Allah knows good is made to hear in this way. Thus, anyone whom He has not made to hear in this way is someone in whom He has not known any good. Reflect, then, on how this hearing and this understanding necessarily follow. Such is the state of the believers, unlike those who speak of hearing without understanding, or understanding without hearing—by which I mean this particular kind of hearing.

As for His words:

“But even if He had made them hear, they would have turned away in aversion.”

Many people may find this difficult to understand. They suppose that the hearing posited in this conditional clause is the same hearing negated in the first sentence, which would have occurred had He known any good in them. Yet nothing in the verse requires this interpretation; rather, both its apparent and underlying meanings contradict it.

For the pronoun in His words:

“Even if He had made them hear”

refers to the same people as the two pronouns in His words:

“Had Allah known any good in them, He would have made them hear.”

The statement indicates that Allah did not know any good in these people and therefore did not make them hear, since law (“if”) always indicates that the condition is not fulfilled. Since Allah did not know any good in them, even if He had made them hear, they would have turned away in aversion. They are like the Jews who said, “We hear and disobey.” These constitute “the third category.” The verse also indicates that not everyone who hears and understands has good in him;

---

## Page 8017

rather, a person may understand yet fail to act upon his knowledge, and thus derive no benefit from it and have no good in him. The verse also indicates that making someone hear so that he understands is to be sought only for someone in whom there is good, for he is the one who benefits from it. As for someone who does not benefit from it, bringing him to understand is not to be sought.

The “third category” consists of those who hear the words and understand them, but neither accept them nor obey their command, like the Jews of whom Allah said:

“Among the Jews are those who distort words from their proper places and say, ‘We hear and disobey,’ and ‘Listen, may you never hear,’ and ‘Attend to us,’ twisting their tongues and disparaging the religion. Had they said, ‘We hear and obey,’ and ‘Listen,’ and ‘Look after us,’ it would have been better for them and more upright. But Allah has cursed them for their disbelief, so they believe but little.”

And He, Exalted is He, said:

“Do you then hope that they will believe you, when a group of them used to hear the words of Allah and then knowingly distort them after understanding them?”

Continuing to His words:

“And among them are unlettered people who know nothing of the Scripture except recitation.”

That is, merely reciting it. These belong to the “first category”: those who hear and read but neither comprehend nor understand. The passage continues to His words:

“And when We took the covenant of the Children of Israel: ‘Worship none but Allah, and be good to parents.’”

Continuing to His words:

“We certainly gave Moses the Scripture and sent messengers after him in succession. We gave Jesus, son of Mary, clear proofs and supported him with the Holy Spirit. Is it that whenever a messenger came to you with something your souls did not desire, you grew arrogant, rejecting some and killing others?”

“And they said, ‘Our hearts are covered.’ Rather, Allah has cursed them for their disbelief, so little do they believe.”

Just as

---

## Page 8018

He said in that verse:

“But Allah has cursed them for their disbelief, so they believe but little.”

And He said in Sūrat al-Nisā’:

“For their breaking of their covenant, their disbelief in Allah’s signs, their killing of the prophets without right, and their saying, ‘Our hearts are covered’—rather, Allah has sealed them because of their disbelief, so they believe but little—”

“And for their disbelief and their uttering against Mary a grievous slander.”

The account continues to its conclusion. He thus described the sins for which they deserved what befell them. These included their saying:

“Our hearts are covered.”

It is therefore clear that they were lying in making this claim, intending thereby to evade their obligation. Hence He said:

“Rather, Allah has cursed them,”

and:

“Allah has sealed them because of their disbelief.”

Thus, although their hearts hear and understand the message, they neither accept it nor believe in it, neither affirming its truth nor obeying it, even though they recognize it. As He said:

“Those to whom We have given the Scripture recognize him as they recognize their own sons.”

Ghulf is the plural of aghlaf (covered). As for the form with a vowel on the middle consonant, it is the plural of ghilāf (covering). A covered heart is analogous to an uncircumcised person. They made this claim, but they were lying. A curse means exclusion from mercy. Had they acted upon the message, they would have received mercy; but they did not act upon it, so they incurred wrath and were cursed. This is the recompense of those who recognize the truth but do not follow it, and who understand the words of the messengers but do not conform to them by acknowledging their truth and acting upon them.

The “fourth category” consists of those who hear with understanding and acceptance. This is the hearing that is commanded, as He, Exalted is He, said:

“When they hear what has been revealed to the Messenger, you see their eyes overflowing with tears because of the truth they recognize.”

And He, Exalted is He, said:

“Say:

---

## Page 8019

It has been revealed to me that a group of jinn listened and said, ‘We have heard a wondrous Quran, which guides to right conduct, so we have believed in it, and we will never associate anyone with our Lord.’”

And He, Exalted is He, said:

“And when We directed a group of jinn towards you to listen to the Quran, upon hearing it they said, ‘Listen attentively.’ When it was concluded, they returned to their people as warners. They said, ‘O our people, we have heard a Book revealed after Moses, confirming what came before it, guiding to the truth and to a straight path. O our people, respond to Allah’s caller and believe in him.’”

And the verses continue.

And He, Exalted is He, said:

“Indeed, those who were given knowledge before its revelation, when it is recited to them, fall upon their faces in prostration, saying, ‘Glory be to our Lord! Our Lord’s promise is surely fulfilled.’”

And the verse continues. And He, Exalted is He, said:

“The believers are only those whose hearts tremble when Allah is mentioned, and whose faith increases when His verses are recited to them.”

And He, Exalted is He, said:

“Whenever a surah is revealed, some of them say, ‘Which of you has this increased in faith?’ As for those who believe, it increases them in faith, and they rejoice. But as for those in whose hearts is disease, it adds impurity to their impurity, and they die as disbelievers.”

And He, Exalted is He, said:

“We send down of the Quran that which is a healing and a mercy for the believers, but it only increases the wrongdoers in loss.”

Likewise, He said:

“Say: It is guidance and healing for those who believe. But those who do not believe have deafness in their ears, and it is blindness for them.”

Similar to this is His saying:

“This is a clarification for mankind, and guidance and admonition for the God-fearing.”

The clarification extends to everyone who understands it, while the guidance and admonition are for the God-fearing.

And His saying:

“This is insight for mankind, and guidance and mercy

---

## Page 8020

for people who are certain.” And His saying:

“Alif Lām Mīm.
This is the Book in which there is no doubt, a guidance for the God-fearing.”

There is a subtle point here that resolves a possible difficulty: this God-fearing believer need not have been among the God-fearing believers before hearing the Quran. First, that is impossible, since someone who has heard nothing of the Quran cannot be a God-fearing believer. Second, a condition need only accompany that which depends upon it; it need not precede it in time, as with facing the prescribed direction during prayer.

Third, the aim is to clarify two points:

The first is that although the Quran brings about benefit through guidance, admonition, and mercy, there must be a receptive recipient as well as an effective agent. Speech has no effect on someone unreceptive to it, even when it is capable of guiding, admonishing, and bringing mercy. This is true of all speech.

The second is to explain that those guided by it are the God-fearing believers, and that failure to be guided by it indicates an absence of faith and fear of Allah. Thus, one may say, “Those who study the book of Hippocrates are physicians,” even though they were not physicians before studying it, but became physicians through studying it. Likewise, one may say, “The book of Sībawayh is of great benefit to grammarians,” even though they became grammarians only by studying it. Similarly, one may say, “This is a suitable place for archers and riders.”

---

## Page 8021

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

Allah Most High said:

“Have you not seen how Allah sends down water from the sky and channels it into springs in the earth, then brings forth crops of different colours with it? Then they wither, and you see them turn yellow; then He makes them dry fragments. Indeed, in that is a reminder for people of understanding.”

He, glorified be He, tells us that He channels the water descending from the sky into springs. Yanābīʿ (springs) is the plural of yanbūʿ, meaning a source of water, such as a spring or a well. Thus, the Quran indicates that water descending from the sky wells up from the earth. Observation bears this out: when rainfall is abundant, springs become abundant; when it is scarce, they become scarce. Rainwater descends from clouds, which Allah forms from the air in the atmosphere and from rising vapours.

The Quran does not state that all water that wells up comes from rainwater, nor is this established by observation, for water may spring from within mountains,

---

## Page 8022

and there may be vapours within them from which He creates water. Vapours and other forms of air can undergo transformation. For example, if ice is placed in a vessel, water forms around it: this is air that has turned into water, not water from the sky. This shows that there may be water in the earth that has not come from the sky. Thus, one cannot assert with certainty that all water comes from the sky, even if most of it does. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 8023

Shaykh al-Islām Taqī al-Dīn Abū al-ʿAbbās Ahmad ibn ʿAbd al-Halīm ibn ʿAbd al-Salām ibn Taymiyyah al-Harrānī—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section

On the words of Allah Most High:

“Say, ‘O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of Allah’s mercy. Indeed, Allah forgives all sins. He is indeed the All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.’”

“And turn to your Lord in repentance and submit to Him.”

We have mentioned elsewhere that this verse concerns those who repent. As for the two verses in Surah al-Nisā in which He says:

“Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating partners with Him, but He forgives anything less than that for whom He wills,”

they cannot concern those who repent, as some of the Muʿtazilah claim. For whoever repents of associating partners with Allah is forgiven even that sin, according to the explicit texts of the Quran and the consensus of the Muslims.

The verse from al-Nisā contains a restriction and a qualification, whereas the other verse is general and unqualified. In the former, He singles out associating partners with Him as something He does not forgive, while He does not state categorically that He forgives other sins. Rather, He makes their forgiveness contingent upon His will, saying:

“But He forgives anything less than that for whom He wills.”

---

## Page 8024

We have noted elsewhere that, just as this verse refutes the Waʿīdiyyah—those among the Kharijites and Muʿtazilah who maintain that divine threats must be fulfilled—it also refutes those Murjiʾah who suspend judgment, saying: “He may punish every evildoer and forgive none, or He may forgive them all.” For He has said:

“But He forgives anything less than that for whom He wills.”

He thus affirms that sins less than that are forgiven, but only for whom He wills. If He were to forgive no one, this would contradict His words:

“But He forgives anything less than that.”

And if He were to forgive everyone, this would contradict His words:

“For whom He wills.”

By affirming both that He forgives anything less than that and that forgiveness is for whom He wills, He indicates that forgiveness encompassing all sins short of associating partners with Him does occur, but only for some people.

Accordingly, whoever is forgiven will not be punished, and whoever is not forgiven will be punished. This is the position of the Companions, the early generations, and the leading scholars: it is certain that some sinners within the Muslim community will enter the Fire, while others will be forgiven.

But does this occur according to the weighing of deeds and wisdom, or is the weighing of deeds given no consideration? Those who affiliate themselves with the Sunnah, both scholars of our school and others, hold two views on this. These stem from the underlying question concerning divine acts: whether they are governed by wisdom and justice. Moreover, numerous scriptural texts concerning recompense indicate that deeds are weighed, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

The point here is that His words:

“O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of Allah’s mercy. Indeed, Allah forgives all sins,”

forbid despairing of Allah Most High’s mercy, however grave and numerous one’s sins may be. Thus, no one may despair of

---

## Page 8025

Allah’s mercy, however grave his sins may be, nor may he cause others to despair of Allah’s mercy. One of the early generations said: “The truly learned scholar is one who neither makes people despair of Allah’s mercy nor emboldens them to disobey Allah.”

Despair arises when a person believes that Allah will not forgive him: either he believes that, even if he repents, Allah will not accept his repentance and forgive his sins; or he says that his own soul will not yield to repentance. Rather, it has overpowered him, and Satan has gained mastery over him. He therefore despairs of ever repenting, even though he knows that Allah would forgive him if he repented. This afflicts many people.

Despair may arise in either of these two ways. The first is exemplified by “the monk who told the man who had killed ninety-nine people that Allah would not forgive him. So he killed the monk, bringing his total to one hundred. He was then directed to a scholar, whom he approached and questioned. The scholar told him that Allah would accept his repentance.” The hadith is recorded in the two Sahīh collections. The second is exemplified by someone who considers repentance to have many conditions, or is told that it has many conditions which he is unable to fulfil, and so despairs of repenting.

People have disputed whether a person can reach a state in which repentance is impossible for him even if he wishes to repent. The correct view, held by Ahl al-Sunnah and the majority of scholars, is that repentance is possible from every sin, and that Allah may forgive it. In discussing this, they have proposed hypothetical cases: someone who finds himself in the middle of unlawfully seized land, and someone surrounded by wounded people, such that any movement he makes will kill some of them. It has been said that such a person has no way to repent. The correct view, however, is that if he repents, Allah will accept his repentance.

---

## Page 8026

As for someone who finds himself in the middle of unlawfully seized land, his departure with the intention of vacating the place and restoring it to its rightful owner is neither prohibited nor unlawful. Indeed, the jurists agree that if someone seizes a house and leaves his belongings and wealth there, then is ordered to restore it to its rightful owner, he must leave and remove his family and possessions. Although this involves a form of use of the property, its purpose is to vacate it. Likewise, a polytheist who enters the Sacred Precinct is ordered to leave, even though doing so involves passing through it.

A similar example is the hadith of the Bedouin, whose authenticity is agreed upon. When he urinated in the mosque, the people rose to stop him, but the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not interrupt him”—that is, do not interrupt his urination—and instructed them to pour a bucket of water over his urine.

Once he had begun urinating, it was better for him to finish than for them to interrupt him and thereby cause him to soil his clothes and body. Likewise, if a man were to have unlawful intercourse with a woman and then repent, he would withdraw and would not be sinning by withdrawing. But is withdrawal itself considered intercourse? There are two views on this, both transmitted from Ahmad.

Suppose, then, that a man swears not to have intercourse with his wife, making a threefold divorce conditional upon doing so. Those who hold that a threefold divorce takes effect if he has intercourse with her disagree over whether he may lawfully do so. There are two views, both transmitted from Ahmad.

The first is that it is permissible, as al-Shāfiʿī holds. The second is that it is not permissible, as Mālik holds. He argues: “If you permit intercourse, it follows that he will have intimate contact with her while withdrawing, when she is forbidden to him. This is permitted only out of necessity; it does not make initiating intercourse permissible.” The proponent of the other view maintains that withdrawal is not forbidden.

---

## Page 8027

Likewise, those who hold that a man counts as having had intercourse if dawn breaks while he is still penetrating have two views concerning withdrawal, found in the school of Ahmad and elsewhere. According to the position we have defended, however, there is no need to address any of these questions: if someone breaks such an oath, he offers expiation for it, and the threefold divorce does not take effect. Nor is there any blame for eating or intercourse while ascertaining whether dawn has broken, because Allah says, “until”.

The point is that no one may despair of Allah’s mercy or cause anyone else to despair of it. Allah has forbidden this and has declared that He forgives all sins.

If it is objected: His words,

“Indeed, Allah forgives all sins,”

are a universal statement of fact, indicating that Allah forgives every sin. Yet it is known that He did not mean that He forgives everyone who sins, whether a disbeliever or anyone else, without punishing them either in this world or in the Hereafter. That would contradict what is established by self-evident knowledge, mass-transmitted reports, the Quran, and scholarly consensus. Allah has destroyed many nations because of their sins, and some members of this community have been punished for their sins in this world before the Hereafter, whether through His decree or through the punishments prescribed by His law.

Allah Most High has said:

“Whoever does evil will be recompensed for it.”

And He has said:

“So whoever does an atom’s weight of good will see it,”

“and whoever does an atom’s weight of evil will see it.”

This would entail that the verse is not to be understood according to its apparent meaning. Rather, what is meant is that Allah may forgive all sins—that is, this is something He may do—or that He forgives them for everyone who repents. But then one may ask: Why does He use categorical, unrestricted wording where possibility and qualification are intended?

The reply is: Rather,

---

## Page 8028

the verse means exactly what it says: Allah has stated that He forgives all sins, but He has not said that He forgives every sinner. Rather, He has stated in several places that He does not forgive those who die as disbelievers. He says:

“Indeed, those who disbelieve and turn others away from the path of Allah, then die as disbelievers—Allah will never forgive them.”

And He says concerning the hypocrites:

“It is all the same for them whether you ask forgiveness for them or do not ask forgiveness for them; Allah will never forgive them.”

Yet this wording, though universal with respect to sins, is unqualified with respect to sinners. It neither affirms nor denies forgiveness for the sinner: he may be forgiven, or he may not be forgiven. If he does what warrants forgiveness, Allah forgives him; if he persists in what is incompatible with it, He does not forgive him.

As for any given kind of sin, Allah does forgive it in some cases: disbelief, associating partners with Him, and all other sins—He forgives them for those who repent of them. No sin exists that the Lord Most High does not forgive; rather, Allah Most High forgives every sin in some cases.

This is a momentous, comprehensive verse, among the most beneficial of all verses. It refutes several groups, including those who say that the repentance of someone who calls others to religious innovation is not accepted. They cite as evidence an Israelite report which states:

“That advocate was told: ‘But what of those you led astray?’”

This view is held by some who affiliate themselves with the Sunnah and hadith but are not learned in them, such as Abū ‘Alī al-Ahwāzī and others like him who do not distinguish between

---

## Page 8029

authentic and fabricated hadiths, or between what may and may not be cited as evidence. Rather, they narrate everything related to the subject and cite it as evidence. Some have reported this as an opinion within Ahmad’s school or as a position transmitted from him. Yet the apparent position of his school, in agreement with the schools of all the other imams of the Muslims, is that such a person’s repentance is accepted, just as the repentance of someone who calls others to disbelief or turns people away from their religion is accepted.

The leaders of the Confederates—such as Abū Sufyān ibn Harb, al-Hārith ibn Hishām, Suhayl ibn ‘Amr, Safwān ibn Umayyah, ‘Ikrimah ibn Abī Jahl, and others—repented after their calls had led others to be killed while still disbelievers. They became among the finest Muslims, and Allah forgave them. Allah Most High says:

“Tell those who disbelieve that if they desist, their past will be forgiven.”

‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās had been among the foremost in calling others to disbelief and harming the Muslims. Yet when he embraced Islam, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to him:

“O ‘Amr, did you not know that Islam wipes away what came before it?”

In Sahīh al-Bukhārī, Ibn Mas‘ūd said concerning Allah’s words:

“Those whom they invoke themselves seek a means of drawing near to their Lord, each striving to be closest.”

“Some humans used to worship certain jinn. Those jinn embraced Islam, while the humans continued to worship them.”

This shows that those who embraced Islam were not harmed by others continuing to worship them after their conversion, even though they themselves had initially led those people astray.

---

## Page 8030

Moreover, although someone who calls others to disbelief and religious innovation has led others astray, those others are punished for their own sins, because they accepted his call and followed him. He bears his own burden of sin and the burden of those who follow him until the Day of Resurrection, while their burdens remain upon them. If he repents of his sin, neither his own burden nor the burden he incurred by leading them astray remains upon him. As for them, their situation is unchanged whether he repents or not. His repentance, however, first requires him to reverse his former course by calling others to guidance, just as many disbelievers and adherents of religious innovation have repented and become callers to Islam and the Sunnah. Pharaoh’s magicians were leaders in disbelief; then they embraced Islam, and Allah granted them a good end.

Another instance is the repentance of someone who has committed murder. The majority hold that it is accepted, whereas Ibn ‘Abbās said that it is not. Two positions are reported from Ahmad.

The hadith in the Sahīhs of al-Bukhārī and Muslim about the man who killed ninety-nine people is evidence that a murderer’s repentance is accepted. This verse also indicates that. The verse in Sūrat al-Nisā’ merely contains a Quranic threat of punishment, like His words:

“Indeed, those who unjustly consume the property of orphans consume nothing but fire into their bellies, and they will burn in a blazing Fire.”

Yet this applies only if the person does not repent. By unanimous agreement, every threat of punishment in the Quran is conditional upon the absence of repentance. On what grounds, then, would the threat against a murderer still apply to him even if he repents? This is an exceedingly weak position.

It may, however, be said that his repentance is not accepted in the sense that it does not extinguish the right of the person wrongfully killed. Rather, repentance extinguishes Allah’s claim, while the victim will still demand his right from the murderer. This holds true for all rights owed to other people, even debts, for in the Sahīhs of al-Bukhārī and Muslim it is reported from the Prophet

---

## Page 8031

, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that he said:

“A martyr is forgiven everything except debt.”

The human claimant, however, receives what he is owed from the murderer’s good deeds. Part of completing his repentance is therefore to accumulate good deeds until he has enough to satisfy the victim’s claim. Perhaps Ibn ‘Abbās considered murder the gravest sin after disbelief, so that the murderer would not have enough good deeds to satisfy the victim’s claim, and would inevitably retain sins for which he would be punished. What he said may apply to some people. The question thus remains concerning someone who repents sincerely but cannot accumulate enough good deeds to satisfy the wronged person’s claim: will some of the victim’s sins be transferred to him, for which he will be punished? This is a subtle issue, and the report from Ibn ‘Abbās should be understood as referring to a case of this kind.

None of this, however, contradicts what the verse entails: that Allah Most High forgives every kind of sin—associating partners with Him, murder, unlawful sexual intercourse, and others—as a general principle. The verse is thus general as to acts, but unqualified as to individuals.

A similar example is His saying:

“Kill the polytheists wherever you find them.”

It is general as to individuals and unqualified as to the circumstances of . . . (1) the feet, since they may be covered by leather socks, and the wording does not address their circumstances. Likewise, His saying, Most High:

“Allah instructs you concerning your children.”

It is general as to children, and general as to their circumstances? A child may share the parent’s religion or differ from it, and may be free or enslaved. The wording does not address these circumstances.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) There is an omission in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 137):
> The passage, with the omitted text restored, appears to read:
> “‘Kill the polytheists wherever you find them’ is general as to individuals and unqualified as to the circumstances of [the polytheists. Likewise, His saying, Most High, ‘And wipe your heads and your feet up to the ankles,’ is general as to feet and unqualified as to the circumstances of] the feet, since they may be covered by leather socks…” (1).
> 
> Note:
> One line later, he writes: “‘Allah instructs you concerning your children’ is general as to children and general as to circumstances.” This is—Allah knows best—a copyist’s error. The correct wording is: “general as to children and unqualified as to circumstances.”
> 
> (1) See al-Minhāj, 4:179.

---

## Page 8032

Likewise, His saying:

“He forgives sins”

is general as to sins, but unqualified as to their circumstances. A person may have repented of a sin or may persist in it; the wording does not address this. Rather, the passage makes clear that a sin is forgiven in some circumstances but not others. Allah commands the deeds through which sins are forgiven and forbids those that lead to punishment on the Day of Resurrection without forgiveness. He says:

“Turn to your Lord in repentance and submit to Him before the punishment comes upon you; then you will not be helped.

“And follow the best of what has been sent down to you from your Lord before the punishment comes upon you suddenly, while you are unaware,

“lest a soul should say, ‘Alas for my neglect of my duty towards Allah! I was indeed among those who mocked,’

“or say, ‘Had Allah guided me, I would have been among the righteous,’

“or say, when it sees the punishment, ‘If only I could have another chance, I would be among those who do good.’

“Yes indeed! My signs came to you, but you denied them, acted arrogantly, and were among the disbelievers.”

This states that on the Day of Resurrection He will punish souls whom He has not forgiven, such as those who denied His signs, acted arrogantly, and were among the disbelievers. Yet Allah has forgiven others for sins like these because they repented of them.

If it is asked: But Allah Most High has said:

“Indeed, those who disbelieve after having believed, then increase in disbelief—their repentance will never be accepted. It is they who have gone astray.”

And He Most High has said:

“Indeed, those who believe, then disbelieve, then believe, then disbelieve, then increase in disbelief—Allah will neither forgive them nor guide them to a way.”

The answer is: The Quran has explained in several places that a disbeliever may repent, even one who has apostatized and then returned to Islam, as in His saying, Most High:

“How can Allah guide a people who disbelieved after having believed and testified that the Messenger is true

---

## Page 8033

and clear proofs had come to them? Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people. Their recompense is that upon them is the curse of Allah, the angels, and all mankind.”

“They will remain under it forever. Their punishment will not be lightened, nor will they be reprieved.”

“Except those who repent thereafter and mend their ways; surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

His words, “How can Allah guide,” mean that He does not guide them while they remain wrongdoing apostates. Hence He says:

“Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people.”

Whoever abandons the religion of Islam is necessarily astray and does not attain guidance, whatever religion he turns to.

“The point” is that Allah neither guides nor forgives these people unless they repent. Likewise, in connection with His words:

“Whoever disbelieves in Allah after having believed, except one who is compelled”

—and whoever disbelieves in Allah after having believed, without being compelled, is an apostate—He says:

“Then, surely your Lord, towards those who emigrated after being persecuted, then strove and remained steadfast—surely your Lord, after that, is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

In Āl ʿImrān, He, glorified be He, mentions the apostates, then those among them who repent, then those whose repentance will not be accepted and those who die as disbelievers. He says:

“Indeed, those who disbelieve after having believed, then increase in disbelief—their repentance will never be accepted. It is they who have gone astray.”

“Indeed, those who disbelieve and die as disbelievers—even an earth full of gold would not be accepted from any of them, were they to offer it as ransom. They will suffer a painful punishment, and they will have no helpers.”

Various explanations have been offered concerning those whose repentance will not be accepted. It has been said that this is because of their hypocrisy, and it has been said

---

## Page 8034

that they repented of sins short of associating partners with Allah, but did not repent of that. It has also been said that their repentance will not be accepted after death. The majority, including al-Hasan, Qatāda, ‘Atā’ al-Khurāsānī, and al-Suddī, said that their repentance will not be accepted when death is upon them. This would thus be like His words:

“Repentance is not accepted from those who continue to do evil until, when death comes to one of them, he says, ‘Now I repent,’ nor from those who die as disbelievers.”

Likewise, His words:

“Indeed, those who believe, then disbelieve, then believe, then disbelieve, then increase in disbelief—Allah will neither forgive them nor guide them to a way.”

Mujāhid and other commentators explained “increase in disbelief” as remaining steadfast in it until they died.

I say: This is because the repentant person turns back from disbelief, whereas one who does not repent persists, increasing in disbelief upon disbelief. Thus, His words, “then increase,” are equivalent to saying that they then persisted in disbelief, continued in disbelief, and remained in disbelief. They disbelieved after embracing Islam, and their disbelief then increased rather than diminished. These are the ones whose repentance will not be accepted—meaning repentance when death is upon them. For whoever repents before death is upon him has repented in good time and turned back from his disbelief, so his disbelief has not increased but diminished. This is unlike someone who persists until death is before his eyes: no time remains for his disbelief to diminish, let alone be eradicated.

In the other verse, He says:

“Allah will not forgive them.”

And He mentions that they

---

## Page 8035

believed, then disbelieved, then believed, then disbelieved, then increased in disbelief. It has been said that this is because, when an apostate repents, his disbelief is forgiven; but if he subsequently disbelieves and dies a disbeliever, his faith comes to nothing, and he is punished for both his first and his second disbelief. Thus, in the two Sahīh collections, Ibn Mas‘ūd reported:

“The Messenger of Allah was asked, ‘Will we be held accountable for what we did in the time of ignorance?’ He replied, ‘Whoever does well in Islam will not be held accountable for what he did in the time of ignorance, but whoever does evil in Islam will be held accountable for both his earlier and his later deeds.’”

Had He said, “Those who believe, then disbelieve, then increase in disbelief—Allah will not forgive them,” these would have been the same people He mentioned in Āl ‘Imrān, saying:

“Indeed, those who disbelieve after having believed, then increase in disbelief—their repentance will never be accepted.”

Rather, He mentioned that they believed, then disbelieved, then believed again. This describes the apostate who repents. If such a person then disbelieves and increases in disbelief, his previous disbelief will not be forgiven either. But if they believed, then disbelieved, then believed, then disbelieved, then believed again, they would not have increased in disbelief and would therefore not fall within the scope of the verse.

When jurists disagree over accepting the repentance of someone who has repeatedly apostatized, or accepting the repentance of a zindīq (heretic), their disagreement concerns only the outward legal ruling, because his repentance cannot be trusted. If, however, he has inwardly repented sincerely to Allah, then he falls within His words:

“O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of Allah’s mercy. Indeed, Allah forgives all sins. He is indeed the All-Forgiving, the Most Merciful.”

What we actually maintain is that the repentant person is not punished, either in this world or in the Hereafter

---

## Page 8036

—neither under the revealed law nor by divine decree. As for prescribed or discretionary punishments, the offence warranting them may be established by evidence—for example, evidence that someone has committed unlawful sexual intercourse, stolen, or drunk intoxicants. If such a person professes repentance, his claim cannot be trusted. Were the prescribed punishment waived merely on that basis, no prescribed punishment would ever be carried out, since everyone against whom evidence was established would say, “I have repented.” If he is inwardly repentant, however, the prescribed punishment serves as an expiation, and he is rewarded for his patience.

But if he comes forward of his own accord, confesses, and comes repentant, then carrying out the prescribed punishment upon him is not obligatory according to the apparent position of Ahmad’s school. Ahmad expressly stated this in several places. This is among the issues addressed in al-Taʿlīq, and al-Qādī supported it with several hadiths. The hadith concerning the man who said:

“‘I have incurred a prescribed punishment, so inflict it on me.’ Then the prayer commenced,”

also falls within this category, because he came repentant. If he testifies against himself, as Māʿiz and the Ghāmidī woman did, and chooses to undergo the prescribed punishment, it is carried out upon him; otherwise, it is not. Thus, in the hadith of Māʿiz, the Prophet said:

“Why did you not leave him alone?”

He also sent the Ghāmidī woman away time after time. Neither the ruler nor the people are obliged to carry out the prescribed punishment upon such a person. But if he requests it, it is carried out upon him. This is like someone who sins in secret: no one is obliged to carry out a prescribed punishment upon him, but if he chooses to confess and undergo the prescribed punishment, it is carried out, even if he is not repentant.

This is like the death of someone who plunges into the enemy ranks: it is a means by which Allah raises his rank. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“She has repented with such repentance that, had a collector of unjust taxes repented in the same way, he would have been forgiven. Have you found anything better than her giving her life for Allah?”

It has also been said that Māʿiz retracted his confession. This is one of the two opinions

---

## Page 8037

on this matter within Ahmad’s school and others; this view is weak, and the first is sounder.

Those who hold this view say that the prescribed punishment was waived because he retracted his confession, and that his retraction is admissible. This is weak. Rather, there is a distinction between someone who confesses repentantly and someone who confesses without repentance. Waiving punishment on account of repentance—as the scriptural texts indicate—is more justified than waiving it on account of retracting a confession. A confession is a person’s testimony against himself. If retraction were accepted, no prescribed punishment would ever be carried out on the basis of a confession. If repentance is not accepted after confession, even though he may be sincere, then a retraction in which he is lying is all the more deserving of rejection.

Here it ends. All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. May Allah bless our master Muhammad and his family, and grant them abundant peace until the Day of Judgment.

---

## Page 8038

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about the saying of Allah Most High:

“The Trumpet will be blown, and all who are in the heavens and all who are on earth will be struck down, except those whom Allah wills.”

The commentators said: “All who are in the heavens and all who are on earth, except those whom He wills” (*) will die from terror and the intensity of the sound.

Abū al-Fath Muhammad ibn ʿAlī al-Kūfī al-Sūfī informed us, saying: Abū al-Hasan ʿAlī ibn al-Hasan al-Tamīmī informed us, saying: Muhammad ibn Ishāq al-Ramlī narrated to us, saying: Hishām ibn ʿAmmār narrated to us, saying: Ismāʿīl ibn ʿAyyāsh narrated to us, from ʿUmar ibn Muhammad, from Zayd ibn Aslam, from his father, from Abū Hurayrah—may Allah be pleased with him—who reported:

“The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—asked Gabriel about this verse: ‘The Trumpet will be blown, and all who are in the heavens and all who are on earth will be struck down, except those whom Allah wills.’ He asked: ‘Who are those whom Allah does not will to strike down?’ Gabriel replied: ‘They are the martyrs, with their swords hanging from their necks, around the Throne.’”

This is also the view of Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, ʿAtāʾ and Ibn ʿAbbās. Muqātil, al-Suddī and al-Kalbī said: Those excepted are Gabriel, Michael, Isrāfīl and the Angel of Death.

“Then it will be blown again, and behold, they will be standing”—meaning that all creation will be standing on their feet—“waiting” to hear what they will be told and what they will be commanded to do.

These are al-Wāhidī’s words in his book al-Wasīt. Explain to us

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd said (p. 138):
> There are three points here:
> First: This fatwa is repeated; it previously appeared in 4/259–261. However, the question was abridged there and presented in detail here.
> Second: Some text has been omitted here, as becomes clear by comparing this fatwa with its earlier occurrence. The omissions occur in two places:
> 1. On p. 34, line 7: “Aristotle’s followers who claimed that the angels are the intellects.” An omission occurred here through the copyist’s eye skipping ahead. The complete wording, as found in 4/259, is:
> “Aristotle’s followers and their like, [and those who joined them from among those professing Islam, or the Jews and Christians, such as the authors of Rasā’il Ikhwān al-Safā’ (Epistles of the Brethren of Purity)], and their like who claimed that the angels are the intellects.”
> 2. On p. 35, line 7: After “when fear is removed from their hearts,” its explanation—“that is, He removes fear from their hearts”—has been omitted; it appears in 4/260.
> Third: At the end of the fatwa, the following occurs: “We could not be certain of that, and this would be like knowing the nearness of the Hour, the identities of the prophets, and similar matters about which we have not been informed.” The phrase “the nearness of the Hour” is a textual corruption here. The correct wording is “the time of the Hour,” as in 4/261, because the nearness of the Hour is known; what is unknown is its time.

---

## Page 8039

the true meaning of being struck down: does it denote death for those mentioned? And what is the true nature of the exception?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The view held by most people is that all created beings will die, even the angels and even Azrael, the Angel of Death. A hadith traced back to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—has been reported concerning this. Muslims, Jews and Christians agree that this is possible and that Allah has the power to bring it about. The only dissenters are certain groups of philosophers who follow Aristotle, and others like them, who claim that the angels are the intellects and souls, and that they cannot possibly die under any circumstances. Rather, in their view, they are the gods and lords of this world.

The Quran and the other revealed Books declare that the angels are servants subject to His governance, as He, glorified be He, says:

“The Messiah will never disdain to be a servant of Allah, nor will the angels brought near to Him. Whoever disdains to worship Him and is arrogant—He will gather them all to Himself.”

And He, Most High, says:

“They say, ‘The Most Merciful has taken a child.’ Glory be to Him! Rather, they are honored servants. They do not speak before He speaks, and they act at His command. He knows what is before them and what is behind them, and they intercede only for those He approves.”

And He, Most High, says:

“How many angels there are in the heavens whose intercession avails nothing except after Allah gives permission for whomever He wills and approves.”

Allah, glorified and exalted be He, is able to cause them to die and then bring them back to life, just as He is able

---

## Page 8040

to cause human beings and jinn to die and then bring them back to life. He, glorified be He, has said:

“He is the One who originates creation and then repeats it, which is even easier for Him.”

It is established in authentic hadiths transmitted through several chains from more than one of his Companions that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“When Allah speaks words of revelation, the angels are overcome by a swoon.”

In another version:

“When the angels hear His speech, they fall unconscious.”

And in another version:

“The angels hear a sound like a chain being dragged over smooth stone, and they fall unconscious. Then, when fear is lifted from their hearts, they ask, ‘What has your Lord said?’ They reply, ‘The truth,’ and call out, ‘The truth! The truth!’”

These authentic hadiths thus tell us that the angels are struck down unconscious. If they can be struck down unconscious, they can also be struck down in death. Yet these philosophizers allow neither possibility. Being struck down unconscious is like what happened to Moses, peace be upon him. Allah, Most High, says:

“When his Lord revealed Himself to the mountain, He leveled it to dust, and Moses fell unconscious.”

The Quran speaks of three blasts of the Trumpet: the blast of terror, mentioned in Sūrat al-Naml in His words:

“On the Day the Trumpet is blown, all those in the heavens and on earth will be terrified, except those whom Allah wills.”

And the blast that strikes them down and the blast of rising, both mentioned in His words:

“And the Trumpet will be blown, striking down those

---

## Page 8041

in the heavens and on earth, except those whom Allah wills. Then it will be blown again, and at once they will be standing, looking on.”

The exception includes the wide-eyed maidens in Paradise, for there is no death in Paradise, and it includes others as well. We cannot identify with certainty everyone whom Allah has excepted, for Allah has left this unspecified in His Book.

It is authentically reported that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“People will fall unconscious on the Day of Resurrection, and I will be the first to regain consciousness. I will find Moses holding onto one of the legs of the Throne, and I will not know whether he regained consciousness before me or was among those whom Allah excepted.”

It has been said that this striking down is a fourth occurrence, and it has also been said that it is one of those mentioned in the Quran. In either case, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—withheld judgment as to whether Moses was included among those whom Allah excepted. If the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—did not identify with certainty everyone whom Allah excepted, then neither can we. This is therefore like knowing how near the Hour is, the identities of the prophets, and similar matters about which we have not been informed. Such knowledge can be attained only through revelation. And Allah knows best.

May Allah bless Muhammad, his family, and his Companions, and grant them abundant peace.

---

## Page 8042

Surah al-Shūrā (Consultation)

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

I have already written something concerning the words of the Most High:

“What is with Allah is better and more lasting for those who believe and put their trust in their Lord,”

through to His words:

“But whoever is patient and forgives—indeed, that is among the matters requiring firm resolve.”

Thus, He praises them in one instance for defending themselves and in another for patient endurance.

“The point here” is that Allah’s praise of them for these qualities—faith, trust in Him, avoidance of major sins, responding to their Lord, establishing prayer, consulting one another in their affairs, defending themselves when subjected to oppression, forgiveness, patient endurance, and the like—demonstrates that the opposites of these qualities are not praiseworthy but blameworthy. These qualities necessarily entail the absence of their opposites. If their opposites were praiseworthy, then the absence of something praiseworthy would itself be praiseworthy. Yet the absence of something praiseworthy can only be praiseworthy if something else praiseworthy takes its place.

Furthermore, praising and commending these qualities amounts to calling for them and commanding them, even if only by way of recommendation. Commanding something entails forbidding its opposite, whether directly or by necessary implication. The opposite of defending oneself is helplessness, and the opposite of patient endurance is impatient distress. There is therefore no good in either helplessness or impatient distress, as we observe in the condition of many people, including some religious people, when they commit wrongdoing or

---

## Page 8043

intend to do something reprehensible: they neither defend themselves nor endure patiently, but instead become helpless and distressed.

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd, it is related from ‘Awf ibn Mālik that two men brought their dispute before the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The man against whom judgment was given said, “Allah is sufficient for me, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.” The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Allah blames helplessness. Rather, you must be resourceful; then, if something overcomes you, say, ‘Allah is sufficient for me, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.’”

In Sahīh Muslim, Abū Hurayrah relates that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“The strong believer is better and more beloved to Allah than the weak believer, though there is good in both. Strive for what benefits you, seek Allah’s help, and do not succumb to helplessness. If something overcomes you, do not say, ‘If only I had done this, such and such would have happened.’ Rather, say, ‘Allah has decreed, and He has done as He willed,’ for ‘if only’ opens the door to Satan’s work.”

Do not succumb to helplessness in carrying out what you are commanded to do, nor give way to distress over what has been decreed. Some people combine both evils. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—commanded striving for what is beneficial and seeking Allah’s help. A command entails obligation—or, if not, recommendation. He forbade helplessness and said:

“Allah blames helplessness.”

The helpless person is the opposite of those who defend themselves. The command to endure patiently and the prohibition against giving way to distress are well established in many passages.

This is because a person faces two kinds of matters: something he has been commanded to do, which he must do

---

## Page 8044

and pursue it diligently, seek Allah’s help, and not succumb to helplessness; and something that befalls him through no act of his own, which he must endure patiently without giving way to distress.

For this reason, one of the wise—Ibn al-Muqaffaʿ or someone else—said: “Matters are of two kinds: those you can do something about, so do not be helpless in dealing with them; and those you can do nothing about, so do not give way to distress over them.”

This applies to all matters. For the believer, however, what he can do something about is what Allah has commanded him to do and loves for him to do. Allah has commanded him only to do what lies within his ability, for He does not burden any soul beyond its capacity. He has commanded him to do every good that lies within his ability. What he can do nothing about is what befalls him through no act of his own.

The terms “good things” and “bad things” encompass both categories. As applied to actions, examples include the words of Allah Most High:

“Whoever comes with a good deed will have ten times its like, and whoever comes with an evil deed will be recompensed only with its like.”

And His words:

“If you do good, you do good for yourselves; and if you do evil, it is against yourselves.”

And His words:

“The recompense for an evil deed is an evil deed like it.”

And His words:

“Yes, whoever earns evil and is encompassed by his sin.”

As for what befalls a person by divine decree, whether good or bad, an example is His saying:

“We tested them with good things and bad things, so that they might return.”

There are many other verses of this kind. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 8045

Surah al-Zukhruf

He also said:

Section

His words:

“And when one of them is given news of what he has ascribed to the Most Merciful as a likeness, his face darkens, and he is filled with suppressed grief,”

resemble His words:

“And when the son of Mary is put forward as an example, your people immediately cry out in derision. They say, ‘Are our gods better, or he?’ They put him forward to you only for the sake of argument. Rather, they are a contentious people.”

It therefore seems—and Allah knows best—that putting forward a likeness refers to their making the Messiah His son and the angels His daughters. A child resembles its father, so they made him a likeness and counterpart to Allah. Alternatively, the meaning concerning the Messiah may be that he is an example comparable to their gods, since he was worshipped besides Allah.

On the first interpretation, those who put him forward as a likeness are comparable to those who ascribe a likeness to the Most Merciful: the Christians and the polytheists. On the second, the one who put him forward as an example was the one who invoked him to challenge His words:

“Indeed, you and what you worship besides Allah are fuel for Hell.”

Thus, when Ibn al-Zibaʿrā said, “I will surely defeat Muhammad in argument,” and challenged him with the case of the Messiah, using it to dispute his statement, he had put him forward as an example, drawing an analogy between their gods and him. This interpretation is supported by His words:

“They put him forward to you only for the sake of argument.”

It is therefore clear that they are the ones who

---

## Page 8046

put him forward as an example, not the Christians. For an “example” may refer both to the original case and to the case inferred from it. It may also refer to an individual case or to the complete argument constituting an inference. As I have already explained, setting forth an example is inferential reasoning. In analogical inference, the example is the individual case. As for syllogistic inference, calling it “setting forth an example” is like calling it “inference,” as I have explained elsewhere. This is because mental concepts correspond to external particulars and resemble them, and because a particular individual corresponds both to the universal concept encompassing its instances and to the other instances.

For a universal concept takes shape in the mind that resembles the particular individual, while each individual resembles the other. Thus, this meaning resembles that one, and each of them resembles the universal meaning encompassing both. This, Allah knows best, is why it is called “setting forth an example” and “inference.” For darb (setting forth) means bringing together; this bringing together occurs in the heart and on the tongue, and consists in generalization and inclusion. Thus, bringing together, setting forth, generalization, and inclusion occur in the mind, both in meaning and in wording. When an example is set forth, either a corresponding generalization or a resembling individual case has been formulated. Reflect upon this, for it is a valuable insight, Allah willing.

You may also say: Every report conveying a likeness that the speaker has formed in the mind is a setting forth of

---

## Page 8047

an example; for the speaker brings together a likeness in his own mind and in the listener’s through a report corresponding to what is reported. The example is thus the report itself—that is, the description—as in His words:

“The description of Paradise which the righteous have been promised,”

and His words:

“An example is presented, so listen to it.”

I have discussed this expression in detail elsewhere, along with the excellent judgments and proofs it encompasses.

---

## Page 8048

Surah al-Ahqāf

Another man asked about the words of Allah Most High:

“And before it was the Book of Moses, as a guide and a mercy.”

He said: “From the explicit texts of the Quran and hadith, we have heard only that the scripture preceding ours was the Gospel.” Another replied: “Jesus was a follower of Moses, and the Gospel merely relaxes the rulings, making them easier than those in the Torah.” A man objected to this, saying: “Jesus had a law different from that of Moses.” He cited as evidence His words:

“To each of you We have prescribed a law and a way.”

The other asked: “What, then, is to be made of His words:

‘And when Jesus, son of Mary, said, “O Children of Israel, I am Allah’s Messenger to you, confirming the Torah that came before me”’?”

He replied: “This is no proof.”

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—answered:

Allah has told us in the Quran that Jesus said to them:

“And to make lawful for you some of what was forbidden to you.”

Thus, we know that he made some of it lawful, not all of it. Allah also tells us that He taught the Messiah the Torah and the Gospel, saying:

“And He will teach him the Book and wisdom, the Torah and the Gospel.”

It is well known that, had he not followed some of what was in the Torah, his learning it would not have been

---

## Page 8049

a blessing for him. Do you not see that we have not been commanded to preserve the Torah and the Gospel, even though many of the laws in these two scriptures agree with the law of the Quran? This and other considerations clarify what Muslim scholars have stated: the Gospel contains only a few rulings of its own, while in most rulings it follows the Torah. These differences distinguish the two laws. For this reason, Christians have agreed upon preserving and reciting the Torah, just as they preserve the Gospel.

Thus, when the Negus heard the Quran, he said:

“Surely this and what Moses brought shine from the same lamp-niche.”

Likewise, Waraqah ibn Nawfal said to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—when the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—described to him what was coming to him:

“This is the angel of revelation who used to come to Moses.”

Likewise, the jinn said:

“We have heard a Book sent down after Moses.”

Allah Most High also said:

“But when the truth came to them from Us, they said, ‘Why was he not given the like of what Moses was given?’ Had they not previously disbelieved in what Moses was given?”

They said, “Two sorcerers supporting one another”—meaning Moses and Muhammad. In the other Quranic reading:

“Two works of magic supporting one another”—meaning the Torah and the Quran.

He also said:

“They did not give Allah His due when they said, ‘Allah has revealed nothing to any human being.’ Say, ‘Who sent down the Book brought by

---

## Page 8050

Moses as a light and guidance for mankind?’”—up to His words:

“And this is a blessed Book which We have sent down, confirming what came before it.”

This and similar passages, in which the Torah is mentioned alongside the Quran and singled out for mention, clarify what the scholars have stated: the Torah is the foundation, while the Gospel follows it in many rulings, although it differs from it in some.

For this reason, He mentions the Gospel together with the Torah and the Quran in passages such as:

“He has sent down to you the Book in truth, confirming what came before it; and He sent down the Torah and the Gospel previously, as guidance for mankind, and He sent down the Criterion.”

He also says:

“A true promise binding upon Him in the Torah, the Gospel, and the Quran.”

Thus, He sometimes mentions all three, and sometimes mentions the Quran together with the Torah alone. There is an underlying reason for this: the Gospel is an independent foundation in one respect and follows the Torah in another. The Quran’s relationship to the Torah is different: it is an independent foundation in every respect. Indeed, it stands as a guardian over the scriptures that preceded it, even though it agrees with the Torah in the foundations of religion and its books of law. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 8051

Surah Qāf

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about Allah’s words:

“On the Day We will say to Hell, ‘Are you full?’ and it will say, ‘Is there any more?’”

What is meant by “more”?

He replied:

It has been said that its words, “Is there any more?” mean: “There is no room in me for any more.”

The correct view, however, is that it says, “Is there any more?” as a request, meaning: “Is there anything more to be added to me?” What is meant by “more” is the jinn and humans whom Allah adds to it. As related in the two Sahīh collections, Abū Hurayrah reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“People will continue to be thrown into Hell while it says, ‘Is there any more?’ until the Lord of Might places His Foot in it—or, according to another narration, upon it—whereupon its parts draw together, and it says, ‘Enough, enough!’”

When it says, “I have enough! I have enough!” it will have been satisfied with what has been cast into it and will no longer say, “Is there any more?” Rather, it becomes full with what it contains because its parts draw together. Allah will constrict it around those within it because of its vastness, for He has promised it that He will fill it

---

## Page 8052

with jinn and humans alike. It is vast, so it will not become full until He constricts it around those within it.

He said:

“As for Paradise, Allah will bring people into being for it and admit them to Paradise.”

He thus made it clear that Allah, glorified be He, will not constrict Paradise. Rather, He will bring people into being for it and admit them to Paradise, for Allah admits to Paradise those who have done no good deeds, since this is an act of benevolence. Punishment in the Fire, however, is only for those who have disobeyed; He does not punish anyone who has committed no sin. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 8053

Surah al-Mujādalah (The Pleading Woman)

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

Concerning the words of the Most High:

“Allah will raise those of you who believe and those who have been given knowledge in rank.”

He, glorified be He, singled out those endowed with knowledge and faith for elevation in standing and rank. They are those whose testimony He invoked in His words:

“Allah bears witness that there is no deity except Him, as do the angels and those endowed with knowledge; He upholds justice.”

He also declared that they are the ones who recognize what was revealed to the Messenger as the truth, saying:

“Those who have been given knowledge see that what has been revealed to you from your Lord is the truth.”

This shows that learning the proof and upholding it raises the ranks of those who uphold it, as the Most High said:

“We raise in rank whom We will.”

Zayd ibn Aslam said: “Through knowledge.”

Elevation in rank and standing is thus proportionate to the hearts’ engagement with knowledge and faith. Many a person completes the Quran once or twice a day, another stays awake all night, and another fasts every day, yet others perform fewer acts of worship

---

## Page 8054

than they do and are held in higher esteem by the Muslim community. Kurz ibn Wabara, Kahmas and Ibn Tāriq complete the Quran ninety times a month, yet Ibn al-Musayyib, Ibn Sīrīn, al-Hasan and others hold a higher place in people’s hearts.

Likewise, you see many who wear wool, forsake worldly pleasures and lead austere lives, while others endowed with knowledge and faith, who come nowhere near them in these practices, are more highly esteemed and more dearly loved. This is solely because of the strength and purity of their inward devotion, its freedom from selfish desires and human impurities, and its purity from the hearts that cloud the devotion of those others. They attained this only through their firm certainty in what the Messenger brought, their hearts’ complete faith in him, their affection and love for him, and their dedicating all religious devotion to Allah alone. For the highest rank of hearts is their complete delight, elation and joy in what the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—brought, as the Most High said:

“Those to whom We have given the Scripture rejoice in what has been revealed to you.”

And the Most High said:

“Say, ‘In the bounty of Allah and in His mercy—in that let them rejoice.’”

And so the verse continues. Allah’s bounty and mercy are the Quran and faith. Whoever rejoices in this rejoices in the greatest possible cause for joy. Whoever rejoices in something else has wronged himself and misplaced his joy. When knowledge of Allah’s sufficiency for His servant, His mercy towards him, His forbearance with him, and His constant kindness and goodness to him becomes firmly established in the heart, it brings him joy and happiness greater than any lover’s delight in any beloved other than Him. Thus he continues to ascend

---

## Page 8055

to ever higher ranks in proportion to his progress in this knowledge. This concerns “knowledge of Allah’s names and attributes.”

As for “understanding the Quran,” he constantly reflects on its meanings and ponders its wording, finding in its meanings and judgments all he needs, without recourse to human discourse. Whenever he hears anything of people’s statements or learning, he measures it against the Quran. If the Quran attests to its soundness, he accepts it; otherwise, he rejects it. If it attests neither to its acceptance nor to its rejection, he suspends judgment. His attention remains devoted to what his Lord intends by His words.

He does not direct his efforts towards those fields of learning that have veiled most people from the realities of the Quran: obsessive concern with the articulation of its letters, their light or emphatic pronunciation, vowel inclination, and long, short, or intermediate vowel prolongations, and other such matters. This places a barrier before hearts, cutting them off from understanding what the Lord intends by His words.

The same applies to preoccupation with pronouncing a-andhartahum (“whether you warn them”), giving the letter mīm in ‘alayhim (“upon them”) a u-vowel and connecting it to a wāw, and giving its hā’ an i-vowel or a u-vowel, and similar matters. The same applies to attending to melody and beautifying the voice, and to pursuing the various possibilities of grammatical analysis and devising strained interpretations that resemble riddles and puzzles more than clear exposition.

---

## Page 8056

The same applies to directing one’s mind towards recounting people’s statements and the conclusions of their thinking. It also applies to interpreting the Quran according to the views of an authority whom one blindly follows in religion or doctrine: one resorts to every strained interpretation to make the Quran subordinate to one’s school of thought and supportive of one’s imam’s position. What all these people hold veils them from understanding Allah’s intent in His words in many or most of these matters.

Likewise, those who fail to appreciate the Quran as it deserves suppose that it is insufficient for knowing Allah’s oneness, His names and attributes, what must be affirmed of Him, and what He must be declared free of. Rather, they consider the intellects of the perplexed and bewildered sufficient for this, although every one of them has plainly contradicted the Quran’s explicit statements. These people are the most severely veiled from understanding the Book of Allah Most High. And Allah, glorified and exalted be He, knows best.

## Page 8057

Sūrat al-Talāq (Divorce)

He also said:

Section

As for His words:

“Whoever fears Allah, He will make a way out for him”

“and provide for him from where he does not expect.”

Here Allah explains that He wards off harm from the God-fearing person by making a way out for him, and brings him benefit by facilitating his provision. Provision is a term for everything that nourishes a human being; this encompasses provision in this world and provision in the Hereafter.

Someone once said, “No God-fearing person has ever fallen into poverty.” They asked, “Why?” He replied, “Because Allah says:

‘Whoever fears Allah, He will make a way out for him’

‘and provide for him from where he does not expect.’”

Someone may object: “We see people who fear Allah yet are deprived, and others who are the opposite yet receive provision.”

The answer is that the verse establishes that the God-fearing person receives provision from where he does not expect; it does not indicate that those who are not God-fearing receive no provision. Rather, every created being must receive provision. Allah Most High says:

“There is no creature on earth but that its provision rests with Allah.”

So much so that

---

## Page 8058

even what a servant consumes unlawfully is included in this provision. Disbelievers may receive provision through unlawful means, and they may receive good provision; sometimes they obtain provision only through strenuous effort. As for those who fear Allah, He provides for them from where they do not expect. Their provision does not come through unlawful means, nor is it impure.
