The God-fearing person is not deprived of the provision he needs; rather, he is shielded from worldly excess out of mercy and kindness toward him. Indeed, abundant provision may harm its recipient, whereas restricting it is a mercy to him.

Allah Most High says:

“As for man, when his Lord tests him by honoring him and bestowing blessings upon him, he says, ‘My Lord has honored me.’

But when He tests him by restricting his provision, he says, ‘My Lord has humiliated me.’

No!”

That is, the matter is not as he supposes. Not everyone whose provision is made abundant is thereby honored, nor is everyone whose provision is restricted thereby humiliated. Rather, a person may be given abundant provision as a reprieve, drawing him gradually toward his downfall; and his provision may be restricted to protect and safeguard him. A religious person's provision may also be restricted because of his sins and misdeeds, as one of the early generations said:

“A servant may indeed be deprived of provision because of a sin he commits.”

In a hadith, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever frequently seeks forgiveness, Allah will grant him relief from every worry, a way out of every hardship, and provide for him from where he does not expect.”

Allah Most High has stated that good deeds erase bad deeds, that seeking forgiveness is a means of obtaining provision and blessings, and that sins are a cause of calamities and hardship. He, Most High, says:

---

## Page 8059

“Alif Lām Rā. A Book whose verses have been perfected, then set out in detail, from One who is All-Wise, All-Aware.”

Up to His words:

“And grant everyone of merit their due reward.”

Allah Most High says:

“Seek your Lord’s forgiveness, for He is ever All-Forgiving.”

Up to His words:

“And provide you with gardens and provide you with rivers.”

Allah Most High says:

“Had they remained steadfast on the right path, We would have given them abundant water, to test them thereby.”

Allah Most High says:

“Had the people of the towns believed and feared Allah, We would have opened for them blessings from heaven and earth. But they denied the truth, so We seized them for what they used to earn.”

Allah Most High says:

“Had they upheld the Torah, the Gospel, and what was sent down to them from their Lord, they would have received sustenance from above them and from beneath their feet.”

Allah Most High says:

“Whatever calamity befalls you is because of what your own hands have earned, yet He pardons much.”

Allah Most High says:

“If We let man taste mercy from Us, then withdraw it from him, he becomes utterly despairing and ungrateful.”

Allah Most High says:

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself.”

Allah Most High says:

“So We afflicted them with poverty and hardship, that they might humble themselves in supplication. Why, then, did they not humble themselves when Our punishment came upon them? Instead, their hearts hardened, and Satan made their deeds seem fair to them.”

Allah Most High has stated in His Book that He tests His servants with good and evil: the good consists of blessings, and the evil consists of calamities, so that the servant may be steadfast in patience and abundant in gratitude.

It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“By Him in whose hand is my soul, Allah decrees nothing for the believer except that it is good for him. This is true of no one but the believer: if prosperity comes his way, he is grateful, and that is good for him; and if adversity befalls him, he is patient, and that is good for him.”

---

## Page 8060

And he also said:

Section

Allah Most High says:

“Whoever fears Allah, He will make a way out for him,

and provide for him from where he does not expect. Whoever puts his trust in Allah, He will suffice him. Allah will surely accomplish His purpose. Allah has appointed a measure for everything.”

It has been reported from Abū Dharr that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “If all people were to act upon this verse, it would suffice them.”

Some of the early generations explained His words “a way out” as meaning a way out of everything that causes people distress. This verse corresponds to His words:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help,”

which encompass the knowledge contained in all the divinely revealed scriptures.

This is because fearing Allah is the worship that He has commanded. Fearing Allah, worshipping Him, and obeying Him are closely related, equivalent, and inseparable expressions. Putting one’s trust in Him means seeking His help. Thus, “Whoever fears Allah” corresponds to “You alone we worship,” and “Whoever puts his trust in Allah” corresponds to “You alone we ask for help.” As He says:

“So worship Him and put your trust in Him.”

And He says:

“In You we have put our trust, and to You we turn.”

And He says:

“In Him I have put my trust, and to Him I turn.”

He then made fearing Him yield two benefits: that He would make a way out for a person and provide for him from

---

## Page 8061

where he does not expect.

A “way out” means both the place through which one leaves and the act of leaving itself. One seeks a way out of confinement and hardship: this is relief, victory, and provision. Thus, He makes clear that fearing Him brings victory and provision, as He says:

“Who has fed them against hunger and made them secure against fear.”

For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Are you granted victory and provision except through the weak among you—through their supplications, their acts of kindness, and their prayers for forgiveness?”

One brings benefit, while the other wards off harm.

As for putting one’s trust in Allah, He makes clear that Allah will suffice the person who does so. This explains reliance upon Allah in terms of His sufficing those who trust in Him, as He says:

“Is Allah not sufficient for His servant?”

This is contrary to those who say that reliance consists solely of entrusting one’s affairs to Him and being content. Moreover, Allah will surely accomplish His purpose; He is not like one who is powerless.

“Allah has appointed a measure for everything.”

The verse has also been explained as referring to a way out of the distress caused by doubts, through sound evidence, clear knowledge, and direct experience. As they have said: “He teaches him without human instruction and grants him insight without learning through experience.” Abū Tālib al-Makkī mentioned this. Similarly, concerning His words:

“If you fear Allah, He will grant you a criterion,”

they have said that it is a light by which one distinguishes truth from falsehood. They have also explained it as “insight.”

The verse encompasses a way out of both outward and inward distress. Allah Most High says:

“Whoever Allah wills to guide, He opens his heart to Islam; and whoever He wills to lead astray, He makes his heart tight and constricted, as though he were climbing into the sky.”

It also encompasses what bodies experience and what hearts experience of knowledge and faith. A similar explanation has been given of His words:

“And spend out of what We have provided for them.”

And likewise His words:

“He sends down water from the sky,”

meaning the Quran and faith.

---

## Page 8062

Surah al-Tahrīm (The Prohibition)

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about Allah Most High’s words:

“O you who believe, turn to Allah in sincere repentance.”

“Is Nasūh the name of a man who lived during the time of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace? And what does His word ‘nasūhan’ (sincere) mean?”

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah.

Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, and other Companions and Successors, may Allah be pleased with them, said: “Sincere repentance means repenting of a sin and then never returning to it.”

The word nasūh (sincere) is an adjective describing repentance. It is derived from nush and nasīhah, denoting sincerity and sincere goodwill. The underlying meaning is purity. A person is said to have sincere goodwill towards another when he wishes him well with a pure intention, free of deceit. He is said to deceive him when he inwardly wishes him harm while outwardly professing to wish him well—like a debased coin.

An example of this usage is Allah Most High’s saying:

“There is no blame upon the weak, nor upon the sick, nor upon those who find nothing to spend, provided they are sincere towards Allah and His Messenger.”

That is, they are sincere in their intentions and love towards Allah and His Messenger. Another example is the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the authentic hadith:

---

## Page 8063

“Religion is sincerity”—he said this three times. They asked, “Towards whom, O Messenger of Allah?” He replied, “Towards Allah, His Book, His Messenger, the leaders of the Muslims, and the Muslims at large.”

The foundation of religion is good intention and sincerity of purpose. Hence the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There are three things towards which a Muslim’s heart harbours no resentment: dedicating one’s deeds sincerely to Allah, offering sincere counsel to those in authority, and remaining with the Muslim community, for their supplication encompasses them from behind.”

That is, a Muslim’s heart bears no resentment towards these three qualities; rather, it loves and approves of them.

Sincere repentance, then, is repentance free of all deceit. When repentance is truly of this kind, a servant does not return to sin: he returns only because something of it remains within his soul. Whoever has rid his heart of doubt and sinful desire will not return to the sin. This is sincere repentance, and it is obligatory because Allah Most High has commanded it.

Even if a servant repents and then returns to the sin, Allah accepts his first repentance. When he returns to it, however, he deserves punishment; if he repents, Allah again accepts his repentance. A Muslim who repents and then returns to sin must not persist in it. Rather, he must repent, even if he returns to it a hundred times a day. Imam Ahmad narrated in his Musnad, from Alī, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Indeed, Allah loves the servant who is tried by temptation and repeatedly repents.”

Another hadith states:

“No sin is minor when persisted in, and no sin is major when forgiveness is sought.”

Another hadith states:

“Whoever seeks forgiveness has not persisted in sin, even if he returns to it a hundred times a day.”

---

## Page 8064

As for any ignorant person who claims that “Nasūh” was the name of a man who lived in the time of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and that people were commanded to repent as he did, such a person is a fabricator and a liar, ignorant of hadith and Quranic commentary, ignorant of the Arabic language and the meanings of the Quran.

For this is a man whom Allah Most High never created. No one among the people of earlier times was named Nasūh, nor has any scholar related this story. Had it been as this ignorant person claims, the wording would have been, “Repent to Allah with the repentance of Nasūh.” Instead, He said:

“with sincere repentance.”

A nasūh is someone who repents.

Whoever claims that this verse refers to a man or woman named Nasūh—even someone who lived in the time of Jesus or anyone else—is a liar who must repent of this claim. If he does not repent, he must be punished, according to the consensus of the Muslims. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 8065

Surah al-Mulk (Dominion)

He also said—may Allah Most High have mercy on him:

Allah Most High’s words,

“Does He who created not know, when He is the Most Subtle, the All-Aware?”

demonstrate His knowledge of all things in several ways, encompassing the proofs advanced by those who engage in rational inquiry:

First: He is their Creator. Creation is origination according to a determined measure; it therefore entails their determination in His knowledge before they are brought into being.

Second: Creation necessarily entails will and volition, which in turn require a conception of what is willed. This is the familiar line of argument among most speculative theologians.

Third: All things proceed from Him, and He is their complete cause. Knowledge of the source entails knowledge of what derives from it; thus, His knowledge of Himself necessarily entails knowledge of everything that proceeds from Him.

Fourth: He is the Most Subtle, perceiving the finest details, and the All-Aware, perceiving what is hidden. This is what necessitates knowledge of all things. Thus, the necessitating factor must exist, since the complete cause exists.

---

## Page 8066

Surah al-Qalam (The Pen)

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

Surah (Nūn) is the surah of “character,” which encompasses the religion with which Allah sent Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Allah Most High says in it:

“And indeed, you are of a great moral character.”

Ibn ʿAbbās explained: “You follow a great religion.” Ibn ʿUyaynah said the same, and Ahmad took this explanation from him.

Religion, habit, and character are closely related terms in what they denote, although they differ in the qualities they express. Thus, the word “religion” is used in the sense of habitual practice in the verse:

“This is ever his way, and mine.”

One of the heretics brought the two together in his words:

“It is all but a single order,
Containing neither praise nor blame;
Only custom, nature, and the Lawgiver
Have determined the judgments that apply.”

---

## Page 8067

(Nūn)

He, glorified be He, swore by the pen and what they write. For the pen is the means by which speech is recorded in writing—speech encompassing commands, prohibitions, will, and knowledge embracing all things. The oath is thus by the Pen of Decree and what it records. It encompasses two momentous matters corresponding to what the oath affirms.

First: comprehensive knowledge of events before they occur. One who knows a thing before it occurs surpasses one who knows it after it occurs; hence His account of it is more authoritative and truthful.

Second: a thing’s being written and decreed entails its being spoken, uttered, and known, but not conversely. Thus, His swearing by the last of the stages of knowledge entails the first, but not conversely. Knowledge reaches its fullest extent and becomes firmly established when it is written down. Not everything known is spoken, nor is everything spoken written. This shows you the wisdom of describing the prior decree in terms of writing, rather than speech alone or knowledge alone.

What the oath affirms consists of three statements:

“By your Lord’s grace, you are not mad.”

“And indeed, yours will be an unending reward.”

“And indeed, you are of a great moral character.”

He denied that the Prophet had the defect that would discredit him, and affirmed for him the perfection sought in this world and the Hereafter. For what he brought must be either true or false. If it were false, it would have to be accompanied either by sanity or by its absence. These, then, are the possible alternatives in cases of this kind.

---

## Page 8068

First: that it is false and he lacks sanity. Such a person is mad: he incurs no blame, but he is not to be followed.

Second: that it is false and he is sane. Such a person deserves blame and punishment.

Third: that it is true and he is sane. Allah first denied that the Prophet was mad, then affirmed for him an everlasting reward—the opposite of punishment—and then declared that he possessed a great moral character. This demonstrates the greatness of the truth he follows, after falsehood has been ruled out in his case.

Furthermore, people fall into two categories: those who are punished and those spared punishment. Those spared punishment fall into three categories: those not subject to religious accountability; those who are accountable and have done righteous deeds, following a moderate course; and those foremost in good deeds. He thus arranged the oath according to these three conditions to show that the Prophet belongs to the highest category of the blessed. This is the utmost perfection attained by those foremost in good deeds. It is a remarkable composition, supremely precise.

Then He said:

“So do not obey the deniers,”

and the verses that follow. This encompasses two principles.

One is that He forbade him to obey these two groups. This carries several benefits, including the following: forbidding obedience to someone implies, all the more, forbidding imitation of him. Thus, one must not

---

## Page 8069

obey the denier or the habitual swearer, nor imitate their conduct. This is like His saying:

“And do not obey the disbelievers and the hypocrites,”

and similar passages. For forbidding someone to heed a person who urges defective conduct is a stronger deterrent than forbidding him to adopt that conduct himself.

Another benefit is that this conveys greater honor and respect. Saying, “Do not lie, swear oaths, insult others, or disparage them,” is not like saying, “Do not obey someone characterized by these traits.” The latter honors the person addressed and affirms his freedom from such traits.

Another benefit is that character traits are acquired through companionship. This therefore warns him against acquiring any of their traits through association with them. He must be on his guard, for he needs to associate with them in order to call them to Allah Most High.

Another benefit is that they present apparent benefits in what they urge others to do. Do not, then, obey anyone of this kind, even if he presents such benefits. What drives them to issue their commands is the ignorance and wrongdoing within their souls. When the underlying motive for a command is corrupt, it must not be accepted from the person issuing it. For issuing commands depends upon knowing what is beneficial and intending it. If a person is ignorant, he does not know what is beneficial; and if his character is corrupt, he does not desire it. This is a profound point.

---

## Page 8070

The Second Principle

He mentions two categories: those who deny the truth and those of corrupt character. This is for several reasons.

First, what is commanded is faith and righteous deeds; their opposites are denial and corrupt deeds.

Second, believers are commanded to exhort one another to truth and patience. Just as we are commanded to accept this counsel and offer it to others, we are forbidden to accept its opposite: denying the truth and abandoning patience. These corrupt traits arise only from a lack of patience, while patience governs the character traits we are commanded to cultivate. He therefore concluded the surah with it, and said:

“But none is granted it except those who are patient.”

Thus, Surah al-ʿAsr contains what is explained here. He forbade him to obey someone who is in loss, whose condition is the opposite of that of believers who enjoin truth and patience. The one in loss is the contemptible liar, who abandons truth and patience.

The Third Principle (1): Human rectitude lies in beneficial knowledge and righteous deeds. This knowledge is the good word that ascends to Allah, while righteous deeds encompass all justice. Everything Allah has forbidden people falls under wrongdoing, as has been established elsewhere.

Allah Most High said:

“But man undertook it. Indeed, he was unjust and ignorant.”

Denial of the truth stems either from ignorance or from wrongdoing; in the latter case, the person is a denier

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original, although he stated that the verses contain two principles.

---

## Page 8071

who obstinately resists it. A person of corrupt character falls into such conduct for one of two reasons: either ignorance of what it entails and what its opposite entails—in which case he is ignorant—or inclination and transgression, which constitute wrongdoing. Thus, no one commits evil deeds unless he is ignorant of them or needs them and takes pleasure in them—in which case he is a wrongdoer. He therefore forbade him to obey the ignorant and the wrongdoers.

Concerning His words:

“They wish that you would compromise,”

and the rest of the verse, He states that they want him to compromise so that they may compromise in turn. Their directions are not offered out of sincere concern. Rather, they want him to make concessions, using his concessions to pave the way for their own, and exploiting him for their purposes while posing as sincere advisers. This arises from their denial of the truth: their hearts no longer have any ultimate commitment to truth, whether truth to be sought or truth concerning what exists—neither in reporting it nor in commanding it, neither in belief nor in purposeful conduct.

Then He said:

“And do not obey any contemptible habitual swearer,”

and so forth. He mentioned four verses, each pair encompassing a category of corrupt, blameworthy character traits. Within each verse, He brought together related traits concerning statements and demands. The habitual swearer is paired with the contemptible person because a habitual swearer swears frequently, and an oath concerns either a statement or a demand: it affirms or denies, urges or restrains. A man resorts to frequent oaths in his statements only when he needs to be believed and his reports trusted. Someone who swears frequently also frequently lies in his pledges and is dependent on others; he is therefore among the most abased of people:

“A contemptible habitual swearer.”

He is a habitual swearer in his words and contemptible in his deeds.

---

## Page 8072

As for the faultfinder who goes about spreading malicious gossip:

Hamz (assailing) is stronger and harsher than lamz (faultfinding), whether through sound or movement. From this comes “hamzah,” a sharp sound from the throat resembling retching. Another example is thrusting with the heel, as in the hadith concerning Zamzam:

“It was caused by Gabriel’s thrust with his heel.”

The pattern fa''āl is an intensive form of the active participle. Thus, the hammāz is someone who goes to extremes in finding fault, both in kind and degree. The force is conveyed by the word’s form, fa''āl, while the kind is conveyed by its underlying substance, hamzah. Going about spreading malicious gossip is also a form of faultfinding, but it is done behind people’s backs: it is the faultfinding of someone weak and powerless. Thus, He mentioned both forceful and weak faultfinders, those who find fault in a person’s presence and those who do so in his absence.

As for:

“A withholder of good, a transgressor, sinful,”

wrongdoing is of two kinds: neglecting what is obligatory, which is withholding good, and encroaching upon others, which is what the transgressor does. Pairing the sinful person with the transgressor is like His saying:

“And do not cooperate in sin and transgression.”

As for the harsh brute marked by evil:

He is the overbearing, harsh, coarse person whose extreme tyranny and coarseness have made him notorious for evil, bearing a distinguishing mark like a sheep’s wattle.

It appears—and Allah knows best—that the contemptible habitual swearer and the faultfinder who goes about spreading malicious gossip belong to one category, concerning words and the deeds that follow from them. The withholder of good, the transgressor, the sinful person, and the harsh brute marked by evil belong to another category, concerning deeds and the words that follow from them. The former mainly concerns people’s honour, while the latter mainly concerns the sphere of

---

## Page 8073

rights concerning affairs, benefits, and the like.

He described him as unjust, miserly, and arrogant, as in His words:

“Indeed, Allah does not love anyone who is conceited and boastful.”

“Those who are miserly…” to the end of the verse.

His words:

“We shall brand him on the snout,”

are unrestricted, encompassing branding in the Hereafter and in this world as well. For Allah has given the righteous distinguishing marks, and He has given the wicked distinguishing marks.

The Most High says:

“Their mark is on their faces from the trace of prostration.”

And He says, apparently:

“If We willed, We could show them to you, and you would recognize them by their marks,” to the end of the verse.

He thus made the willing of this, and recognition through marks perceived by sight, conditional upon His will. Yet He swore that they would be recognized by their manner of speech, which consists of sounds perceived by hearing. This indicates that the hypocrites will inevitably be recognized through their voices and speech, in which their manner of speaking becomes apparent. This is clear and evident to those who observe people with discernment concerning speech and other expressions, through the contradictions, obscenity, and other things revealed in them.

As for what is in their hearts becoming apparent on their faces, this may or may not happen. This also indicates that a person’s inner thoughts are revealed more clearly through unguarded utterances than through facial features. For the tongue is the heart’s interpreter, so its disclosure of what the heart conceals is more definite. Moreover, the tongue communicates verbally, whereas the face communicates through its outward state. Speech encompasses the meanings within the heart more fully and extensively than outward appearance does. This is why those who preferred hearing to sight, such as Ibn Qutaybah and others, held that preference.

---

## Page 8074

The precise conclusion is that hearing has a wider range, whereas sight is more specific and more elevated. Although hearing perceives more, sight perceives more completely. Hence He swore that the Prophet would inevitably recognize them through hearing; recognizing them by sight, through their distinguishing marks, however, might or might not occur.

He, glorified be He, thus declared that He would certainly brand the person possessing these vile traits on his snout—that is, his nose, the prominent feature that first catches the eye when one looks at him—so that the mark would be apparent at first sight. This is evident in wicked oppressors whom people leave alone for fear of their evil and foul conduct: they bear a mark of evil by which they are recognized. The same applies to the openly sinful and those of dubious conduct.

His words,

“Indeed, We have tested them,”

and what follows explain the condition of the miserly and the punishment they suffer in this world before the Hereafter: the destruction of their wealth through flooding, fire, plunder, confiscation, its dissipation on sinful desires, or other punishments inflicted upon misers who withhold what is due. Nor do they take the initiative in acts of kindness. This is expressed in His words,

“A withholder of good.”

This is one of the two forms of wrongdoing, as they acknowledged about themselves when they said:

“Woe to us! We were indeed transgressors.”

Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“For a person of means to delay payment is wrongdoing.”

The passage also entails punishment for the wrongdoer who withholds what is due or oversteps the bounds of what is right, just as Allah punishes the one who withholds zakat—who is a withholder of good—and the one who consumes gains from usury and gambling, which constitutes consuming wealth wrongfully. Concerning each of the two, Allah has stated in His Book that He punishes him with the opposite of

---

## Page 8075

what he intended. Here He tells of the punishment of one who withholds what is due, and in al-Baqarah of the punishment of one who deals in usury. This punishment extends to those who employ stratagems to neglect this obligation and commit this forbidden act, as He relates in this surah, and as is witnessed in the punishments and exemplary penalties suffered by those who withhold financial dues and employ usurious stratagems. For when He, glorified be He, blesses a servant with some form of good and commands him to spend in that cause, but he is miserly, He punishes him with some form of evil through which he loses many times what he withheld, while his punishment in the Hereafter remains in store.

He then follows this with the punishment of the arrogant person—the brutal, ignoble man who is called to prostrate and obey but refuses. Thus the surah contains the punishment of both the one who abandons prayer and the one who withholds zakat. The one who abandons prayer is the sinful transgressor, brutal and ignoble; the one who withholds zakat is unjust and miserly.

He concludes the surah by commanding patience, which encompasses sublime character, in His words:

“So be patient with your Lord’s judgment.”

This explicitly commands patience in bearing both the harm inflicted by people and heaven-sent calamities. Patience in the former case is harder. The Companion of the Fish went away angry with his Lord because of the divine command. Hence He says:

“Those who disbelieve would almost strike you down with their eyes,”

and so forth. Thus the surah’s conclusion returns to its opening, in His words:

“By the grace of your Lord, you are not mad,”

and His words:

“And they say, ‘He is indeed mad.’”

Striking someone down with one’s gaze represents the utmost hatred, anger, and harm. Thus patience

---

## Page 8076

in the face of this is a form of forbearance: bearing the harm inflicted by people. Such forbearance repels their plotting and evil.

What He mentions in the account of the owners of the garden concerning generosity and liberality, together with what He mentions here concerning forbearance and patience, encompasses good character in its entirety. He likewise brings the two together in His words:

“Those who spend in prosperity and adversity,”

and the rest of the verse. As has been said:

Through forbearance and generosity, a young man rose to lead his people;
And becoming such a man is easy for you.

Showing kindness to people through wealth and practical help, and bearing the harm they cause, are akin to praiseworthy generosity. He likewise brings the two together in His words:

“Be forgiving, enjoin what is right, and turn away from the ignorant.”

Accepting what comes readily from people’s dispositions entails bearing the harm they cause. This takes two forms: relinquishing the rights you have over them. Thus, accepting what comes readily means not demanding the rights they have failed to fulfil towards you, and not forbidding them when they overstep the bounds in their treatment of you. And if you neither command nor forbid them in matters concerning (1).

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) This is the last passage found in the original.

---

## Page 8077

He also said:

The following is an explanation of verses that have proved difficult to understand, so much so that some books of Quranic commentary contain only mistaken interpretations of them.

Among them are His words:

“With which of you is the afflicted one?”

Many have been perplexed by this verse, but the correct interpretation is that transmitted from the early generations.

Mujāhid said: “Satan.” Al-Hasan said: “They have more claim to Satan’s company than Allah’s Prophet does.” He thus clarified the intended meaning, commenting on the wording in the manner customary among the early generations: concisely, yet with eloquence and understanding of the meaning.

Al-Dahhāk said: “The madman.” For someone possessed by Satan suffers from madness. Al-Hasan is also reported to have said: “The misguided one.” This is because, by calling him a madman, they did not mean someone who tears his clothes and babbles. Rather, they meant that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, opposed those whom they considered sensible, just as people say, “So-and-so has no sense.”

They levelled similar accusations against the followers of the prophets, as in His words:

“And when they saw them, they would say, ‘These people are truly astray.’”

The like occurs frequently within this community: people ridicule the believers and accuse them of madness and heinous things of which they themselves are more deserving of being accused.

Al-Hasan said: “I have indeed seen men whom, had you seen them, you would have called mad. Yet had they seen you, they would have said, ‘These are devils.’ Had they seen the best of you, they would have said, ‘These have no share of good.’ Had they seen the worst of you, they would have said, ‘These are people who do not believe

---

## Page 8078

in the Day of Reckoning.’”

Such statements are frequent in the words of the early generations: they describe the people of their time and their departure from the ways of their predecessors. What, then, are we to think of the people of our own time? Those who did not understand this said that the preposition bi- is redundant. Ibn Qutaybah and others held this view.

There are many such examples, as in His words:

“Tomorrow they will know who is the insolent liar.”

“Shall I inform you upon whom the devils descend?”

And the following verses.

“If you ridicule us, we will ridicule you just as you ridicule us.”

“Soon you will know whom a punishment will befall.”

To the end of the verse.

---

## Page 8079

He also said:

Section

Several learned scholars have discussed Allah’s words:

“On the Day a man will flee from his brother”

“and his mother and his father,”

asking: Why does He begin with the brother, when the Arabs customarily begin with what is most important?

When I was asked about this, I said:

In every context, one begins with whatever is appropriate to it. Sometimes this calls for beginning with what ranks higher, and sometimes with what ranks lower. Here, it is appropriate to begin with the lower, because the purpose is to describe his flight from his relatives in detail, one after another. Had the nearest relative been mentioned first, there would have been little benefit in mentioning the more distant, since it is understood that someone who flees from his nearest relative will flee from those more distant. Nor would the listener have gained a detailed sense of the severity of the ordeal. Thus, the passage begins with his rejection of the more distant relative, proceeding from there to the nearer. First, it says:

“A man will flee from his brother.”

This makes it clear that there is an ordeal severe enough to make him do so. He might flee from others, or he might not. Then it says:

“and his mother and his father.”

This makes it clear that the ordeal is even more severe, compelling him to flee from his parents. Then it says:

“and his wife and his children.”

This makes it clear that it is an overwhelming calamity, so severe that it compels him to flee

---

## Page 8080

from those from whom he would flee only in the direst distress: his wife and children. The expression “his companion” is more fitting than “his wife.”

I said:

This concerns statements of fact. Its counterpart among commands is His saying:

“Then compensation by fasting, charity, or sacrifice.”

And His saying:

“Its expiation is to feed ten needy people with food of the average quality you feed your own families, or to clothe them.”

Obligations are of two kinds. Some follow a prescribed sequence, in which the greater is placed first, followed by the next greatest, as with the expiations for zihār (declaring one’s wife to be like one’s mother), killing, and oaths. Others allow a choice. Here, He begins with the least burdensome to make clear that it is sufficient and in no way deficient. Even when He mentions the greater afterward, this is to encourage it, not to make it obligatory. It is better for the heart to move from the lesser deed to the greater than to be commanded to perform the greater and then be told of the lesser, only for the heart to disdain it.

Thus, when He begins with the greater in the compensation for killing game, we have two transmitted positions concerning whether a prescribed sequence must be followed. If we defend the well-known position, we say that He placed the greater first because the lesser is within one’s capacity, as He says:

“Or an expiation by feeding needy people, or its equivalent in fasting.”

Likewise, when He begins with the most severe of the prescribed punishments for those who wage war, we do not regard these as either a matter of choice or a prescribed sequence; rather, they correspond to the crimes committed. Nothing in the wording of the verse implies a choice, as some people imagine, for He did not say: “The obligation—or the recompense—is this

---

## Page 8081

or this, or this,” as He says: “Its expiation is this, or this, or this,” and as He says:

“Then compensation by fasting, charity, or sacrifice.”

Rather, He says: “Their recompense is only this, or this, or this.” This wording combines negation and affirmation: its meaning is that their recompense is nothing other than one of the three. Likewise, He says in the verse concerning alms:

“Alms are only for the poor and the needy.”

That is, they are only for these people. It has been established that such wording affirms for those mentioned what it denies to others. Thus, by excluding the permissibility of giving to those outside these categories, it affirms permissibility—not obligation or entitlement, as those who held that every category must be included understood from the apparent wording. Here, He excludes anything other than one of these punishments as a recompense, thereby establishing that the recompense of one who wages war is one of these punishments. Those who wage war constitute a group, not a single individual. The distinction between this verse and the other two verses thus becomes clear in several respects:

First: Those who wage war are referred to in the plural. Setting one plural against another entails distributing the members of one among the members of the other. If it were said, “The recompense of aggressors is either execution, amputation, flogging, crucifixion, or imprisonment,” this would not imply a choice among these punishments for every aggressor. Rather, the punishments would be distributed among the different categories of aggressors. The same applies when it is said, “The recompense of those who wage war is this, or this, or this, or this.” This differs from His saying:

“Its expiation,”

and His saying:

“Whoever among you is ill or on a journey—then an equal number of days.”

---

## Page 8082

Second: The purpose is to deny the permissibility of anything other than [that] (1) and to affirm its opposite: that the things mentioned are permissible in general. This encompasses both a choice between them and the prescription of a particular one. This differs from a statement intended solely to affirm something, for affirming it in wording that expresses a choice does indicate a choice.

This distinction is familiar in statements of simple affirmation and statements of restriction. Thus, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said to the claimant in a dispute:

“Your two witnesses, or his oath.”

In another wording:

“You are entitled to nothing from him beyond that.”

He thereby restricted the means of establishing the claim; the purpose was not to offer a choice.

Similarly, it is said: “What is required in a case of killing is retribution or blood money”; “Prayer is valid only with ablution or dry ablution”; “On Friday, either the noon prayer or the Friday prayer must be performed”; and “None may be allowed to remain in the abode of Islam except a Muslim or someone protected by a treaty.”

The reason is that, when part of the intended meaning conveyed by the wording is precisely what lies outside the matters mentioned, its meaning is an affirmation that entails a negation: namely, the common fact that one of these matters obtains. What they have in common encompasses both the prescription of a particular one and a choice between them. If, however, they are affirmed from the outset, then, were a particular one prescribed rather than a choice offered, without the wording indicating this, the statement would be misleading.

The third consideration is a subtle one. It may be said that “or” signifies an unqualified division into alternatives, just as we have said that “and” signifies an unqualified conjunction of the coordinated term with the term preceding it. As for sequence, it neither denies nor affirms it, for an expression that denotes

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I did not find it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-l-Tashīf (Safeguarding the Collected Fatwas from Omissions and Scribal Errors).
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’—book coordinator for al-Mawsū‘a al-Shāmila (the Comprehensive Encyclopedia).

---

## Page 8083

only what is common does not indicate the distinguishing feature. Likewise, “or” signifies an unqualified division into alternatives: simply that one of the two things obtains. This encompasses its obtaining through a choice between it and the other, through a prescribed sequence, or through an allocation whereby one applies in one circumstance and the other in another.

Similarly, they say that, in directives, “or” sometimes conveys permission, as in “Study grammar or jurisprudence,” and sometimes a choice, as in “Have fish or milk.” By “permission,” they mean that combining the two is permissible. In itself, the particle affirms only what these cases have in common: one of the two alternatives. Whether the other is also permitted or is forbidden is not indicated by the particle itself, but by the particular subject matter. For this reason, we combine killing and crucifixion, and, according to one reported view, combine it with amputation; “or” does not preclude this.

If, then, the particle “or” merely indicates that one of the things mentioned obtains, there are two approaches here.

The first is to say that, when used in a context of obligation, it conveys a choice, whereas in a context of permissibility, it conveys what the alternatives have in common. This is the well-known account given by grammarians discussing the meanings of particles: sometimes it means permission to do one of two things while forbidding the other, and sometimes permission to do either, even if the other is combined with it, as illustrated by their examples.

Accordingly, this verse concerns permissibility, since what is negated is permissibility. It follows, then, that

---

## Page 8084

what is affirmed is permissibility, as we explained concerning the verse on charitable alms, unlike the verse on expiation, which concerns obligation.

The second approach

One may say that there is no distinction between the two contexts—permissibility and obligation. Even where an obligation is concerned, combining the alternatives may be permissible, as when a person of means performs all the forms of expiation.

However, one may say that, in all these cases, “or” signifies an unqualified division into alternatives, the opposite of what “and” signifies. If no evidence indicates a prescribed sequence or a specifically designated alternative, one may perform any of the prescribed acts. This is because nothing indicates such specification or sequence, not because there is evidence ruling them out. The same applies to His words:

“Then the freeing of a slave.”

Freeing a particular slave suffices: that slave possesses the common attribute, and nothing requires a particular individual—not because any evidence points to that very individual.

If, however, evidence indicates a specifically designated alternative or a prescribed sequence, we accept it, just as we accept qualifying an unrestricted expression. Qualifying an unrestricted expression does not set aside the apparent meaning of the wording; rather, it adds another ruling to it. This is a sound approach in this matter and similar cases, for we must distinguish between what the wording affirms and what it negates.

Thus, if we hold that the penalties for those who wage war are specifically assigned on the basis of transmitted evidence or analogical reasoning, this is like holding that ablution must follow a prescribed sequence, that the slave to be freed must be a believer, and similar rulings.

---

## Page 8085

Surah al-Takwīr (The Folding Up)

Shaykh al-Islām said:

Section

His words:

“And when the girl buried alive is asked for what sin she was killed”

are evidence that no person may be killed except for an offense they have committed. Thus, neither a child nor an insane person may be killed, because they are exempt from moral accountability and therefore bear no sin. There should be no doubt that this is a reason for the prohibition against killing the children of those at war with the Muslims. As for the reason they share with women, it is that they are not combatants—according to the correct view, which is that of the majority—or that they come into the Muslims’ possession.

However, this alone cannot serve as the reason in the case of children. The verse entails condemnation of killing anyone, young or old, who has committed no offense. Questioning the girl is a rebuke to her killer.

As for His words in this surah:

“Indeed, it is the word of a noble messenger,”

through to His words:

“And it is not the word of an accursed devil,”

the messenger is Gabriel. This parallels what appears in Surah al-Shuʿarāʾ: the angels brought it down, not the devils. Lies and the like, by contrast, are brought down by the devils. Thus, a distinction is drawn between the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the habitual liar, the poet, and the soothsayer, and between the angel and the devil. The scholars are the heirs of the prophets.

---

## Page 8086

And Shaykh al-Islām said:

Concerning His words, Exalted is He:

“But you do not will unless Allah, Lord of the worlds, wills.”

He states that their willing depends upon His willing. Yet this does not necessarily entail their performing the act, for at most it means that He has made them willing. They do not perform the act until He wills it of them, as in His words, Exalted is He:

“So whoever wills may take heed of it.”

“But they will not take heed unless Allah wills.”

Even so, there must be the will that they perform the act before He wills, on His own part, to help them and grant them success.

Thus, there are four instances of willing here: willing to make clear, willing that they should will, willing that they should act, and willing to help them. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 8087

Surah al-A‘lā (The Most High)

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

In the work he wrote to Abū Ishāq al-Isfarāyīnī, Ibn Fūrak recounted what had happened to him, saying:

In the course of his remarks, the sultan said, “Do you not say that He is seen without being in any direction?”

I replied:

“Yes, He is seen without being in any direction, just as He has always seen Himself, neither in a direction nor from a direction. Others see Him as He sees and has seen Himself. Direction is not a prerequisite for seeing.”

I also said:

“The visible things that we can conceive of in our experience are indeed seen in a direction and a location. Yet judgment cannot be based solely on familiar experience without examination and inquiry. For just as we see things only in a direction and a location, so too we have only ever seen things that have colour, dimensions and volume, that can be measured and weighed, and that are never devoid of

---

## Page 8088

heat and moisture or dryness, unless what is seen is an accident that does not admit of doubling, composition, and the like. Nevertheless, none of these features is decisive.”

He said:

I then learned that throughout that day, that night, and the following day, the sultan kept repeating to himself in his assembly, “How can one conceive of something that is not in a direction?” A belief that first takes hold of the heart and upon which one is raised is difficult to uproot. Allah is the One whose help is sought. The Karrāmiyyah, however, rejoiced at his response.

When I returned home, I found a note which read:

“The master—may Allah preserve his well-being—maintains that the Creator is not in a direction. How, then, can He be seen without being in a direction?”

I wrote:

“The report concerning seeing Allah is authentic, and this vision will certainly occur, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, gave them glad tidings of it. The report also indicates that Allah is seen without being in a direction, for he, peace and blessings be upon him, said, ‘You will not be crowded together in seeing Him.’ This means that no single direction will gather you together when you see Him, for He is not in a direction.”

I added a lengthy discussion addressing the matter from every angle, filling both sides of the note. When it was returned to him, he sent it to the judge of the town, Abū Muhammad al-Nāsihī, and sought his legal opinion on what I had said. He gathered a group of Hanafīs and Karrāmīs. He himself—may Allah honour you—wrote that whoever says that Allah is seen without being in a direction is a misguided innovator. Abū Hāmid al-Mu‘tazilī wrote the same, as did a man from Bistām who was a tutor in the household of

---

## Page 8089

the army commander. They thus replied to him.

He then sent me the contents of that record bearing their handwriting, and wrote me a note saying:

“They have written this. What do you say about these fatwas?”

I replied: “These people should be consulted about questions of jurisprudence, concerning which a layperson is said to follow a scholar. As for knowledge of the fundamentals of religion and issuing fatwas concerning them, that is not their province. They themselves say, ‘We have no expertise in that.’”

I say: Their statement that “Allah is seen without directly beholding Him or facing Him” is a position unique to them among all the groups of the Muslim community. The majority of rational people agree that its falsity is self-evident. The mass-transmitted reports from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, refute them, such as his statement in the authentic hadiths:

“You will see your Lord just as you see the sun and the moon; you will experience no difficulty in seeing Him.”

And his words when the people asked him, “Will we see our Lord on the Day of Resurrection?” He said:

“Do you see the sun in a clear sky, with no clouds obscuring it?”

They replied, “Yes.”

“And do you see the moon in a clear sky, with no clouds obscuring it?”

They replied, “Yes.”

He said, “You will indeed see your Lord just as you see the sun and the moon.”

Thus he compared one act of seeing to another, not one object of sight to another; for the kāf—the particle of

---

## Page 8090

comparison—qualifies the act of seeing. In one wording recorded by al-Bukhārī:

“They will see Him directly.”

It is well known that we see the sun and moon directly, facing them; we must therefore see Him likewise. As for seeing what we neither behold directly nor face, this is inconceivable to reason, let alone comparable to seeing the sun and moon.

For this reason, their most astute thinkers came to deny the vision of Allah, saying:

“Our position is, in reality, that of the Muʿtazilah; for they interpreted this vision as a heightened disclosure and similar things concerning which we have no disagreement with the Muʿtazilah.”

As for his assertion that the report indicates that they will see Him without His being in a direction, and his explanation that “lā tudāmūna” means that no single direction will gather them together when they see Him, because He is not in a direction—this interprets the hadith in a way that its wording does not support and that none of the leading scholars ever proposed. Rather, it is an interpretation rejected by reason, revelation, and the Arabic language.

The words “lā tudāmūna” are transmitted without doubling the consonant, meaning: you will suffer no hardship in seeing Him, such as people experience when trying to see something beautiful, like the crescent moon. They may encounter hardship in seeking to see it when it becomes visible. He, glorified be He, however, will manifest Himself clearly, and they will see Him as the sun and moon are seen, without any hardship befalling them in seeing Him. This is the well-known reading. It is also reported as “lā tudāmmūna,” with the consonant doubled, meaning: you will not crowd together

---

## Page 8091

as people crowd together to see something difficult to discern, such as the crescent moon. The same applies to the readings “tudārrūna” and “tudārūna.”

But to transmit “lā tadāmmūna,” with the consonant doubled, and explain it as “no single direction will gather you together” is false, because tadāmm means people crowding together. It is a reciprocal verbal form, tafāʿul, like tamāss (mutual contact), tarādd (reciprocation), and similar forms. It is also transmitted as “lā tudāmmūna,” with an initial u-vowel and the consonant doubled, meaning that you will not crowd against one another. In either case, it derives from tadāmm: people crowding against one another. It does not mean that something else will not gather you together. That meaning is not expressed by “lā tadāmmūna,” for he did not say, “Nothing will gather you together.”

Furthermore, all those who see Him are in one direction, on the earth. Even supposing that the One seen is not in a direction, how can it be said, “No single direction will gather you together,” when they are all on the earth—the earth of the Resurrection—or in Paradise? Each of these constitutes a direction. For them themselves to exist without direction or place is impossible according to both sensory perception and reason.

As for his assertion, “He is seen without being in a direction, and likewise others see Him,” this is a false analogy. A person can see his own body, but he cannot see anyone else unless that person is in a direction relative to him: namely, in front of him, whether above him or below him.

---

## Page 8092

And the ordinary course of nature may be suspended for him, so that he sees those behind him. Thus the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I can indeed see you behind me.”

Another narration says, “Behind my back,” while a wording recorded by al-Bukhārī reads, “I can indeed see you behind me.” A wording in the two Sahīh collections reads:

“By Allah, I can indeed see behind me just as I see in front of me.”

Yet they are still in a direction relative to him: behind him. How, then, can someone’s seeing another be compared to his seeing himself? Moreover, comparing His seeing to our seeing is a false comparison, for His sight encompasses whatever He sees, unlike our sight.

These people affirmed the existence of something that cannot be seen, yet wished to defend the position of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamā'ah and the people of hadith. They thus combined two contradictory positions. Something that is neither within the world nor outside it, and cannot be pointed to, could not be seen with the eye even if its existence in external reality were possible. How, then, could it be seen when its existence is impossible? It is merely conceived in the mind, without any existence in external reality, and thus belongs to the realm of illusion and false imagination.

For this reason, they interpreted “encompassing” as seeing in His words:

“Vision does not encompass Him,”

just as the Mu'tazilah did. According to the Mu'tazilah, however, this is expressed in praise of Him, so He cannot be seen under any circumstances. These others said that He cannot be seen in this world, but can be seen in the Hereafter. Yet the verse unconditionally negates encompassing Him, not seeing Him, as Ibn Kullāb stated.

---

## Page 8093

and this is the more correct view. The verse would then affirm that He can be seen: He is seen but not encompassed, without being encompassed or confined. This is what makes it an expression of praise, for it describes His greatness: His servants’ eyes do not encompass Him, even though they see Him, whereas He encompasses their sight.

Ibn ‘Abbās—and ‘Ikrimah in his presence—said to someone who raised this verse as an objection:

“Do you not see the sky?”

He replied, “Yes.”

He asked, “Do you see all of it?”

Likewise, He said:

“They encompass nothing of His knowledge except what He wills.”

These people say that His knowledge is a single entity, no part of which can be encompassed apart from the rest. They therefore said: “They encompass nothing of what He knows.” This is not so. Rather, knowledge itself is a general category: they encompass as much of it as He wills, but do not encompass the rest.

He also said:

“He knows what is before them and what is behind them, but they do not encompass it in knowledge.”

The stronger of the two views is that the pronoun refers to “what is before them and what is behind them.” If they cannot encompass this in knowledge, although it is only part of the Lord’s creation, then it is all the more certain that they cannot encompass the Creator in knowledge.

Allah Most High said:

“None knows the hosts of your Lord except Him.”

He also said:

“Has there not reached you the news of those before you—the people of Noah, ‘Ād and Thamūd, and those after them? None knows them except Allah. Their messengers came to them with clear proofs, but they thrust their hands into their mouths”—to the end of the verse.

Thus, the statement “Vision does not encompass Him”—that is, it does not fully encompass Him—indicates that He

---

## Page 8094

is described as not being encompassed, while His being seen is affirmed. This is impossible according to their position, for they claim that this can apply only to something divisible, one part of which can be seen separately from another.

There would then be seeing without full apprehension or encompassing. Yet they hold that He can only be seen in a single, uniform manner, just as they say of His speech that it is a single entity, neither divisible nor multiple; and of faith in Him that it is a single entity admitting neither increase nor decrease.

As for full apprehension and encompassing, which go beyond mere seeing, their absence, according to them, is not due to the Lord’s greatness, but because His essence cannot admit them—just as the Muʿtazilah say that His essence cannot be seen. Moreover, neither they nor the Muʿtazilah wish to recognize any apprehension by the eyes distinct from seeing itself, whether seeing is affirmed or denied. For such a distinction invalidates both the Muʿtazilah’s denial that He can be seen and these others’ affirmation of seeing without direct beholding or face-to-face encounter.

Section

This is despite the fact that Ibn Fūrak is among those who affirm the attributes known through revelation, such as the Face and the two Hands, as well as coming and approaching, in agreement with Abū al-Hasan. For this is the position of Abū al-Hasan and his early followers.

---

## Page 8095

Ibn Fūrak said in a work he composed on the fundamentals of religion:

If the Jahmiyyah ask for evidence that the Eternal One is All-Hearing and All-Seeing, they are told: We agree that He is living and that He cannot be subject to impairments. A living being who is not afflicted with impairments preventing him from perceiving audible and visible things must hear and see.

If you ask, “Where is He?” our answer is: “He is in heaven,” just as He has informed us about Himself in revelation, saying—exalted is He who speaks:

“Do you feel secure from Him who is in heaven?”

This is also indicated by Muslims raising their hands toward Him in supplication. Were you to ask any of them, young or old, “Where is Allah?” they would say, “He is in heaven,” and would not object to the question being phrased with “where.” For the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, asked the slave woman who was presented for emancipation, “Where is Allah?” She replied, “In heaven,” gesturing upward. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said, “Free her, for she is a believer.”

Had this been an objectionable statement, he would not have judged her to be a believer and would have reproved her for it. What this means is that He is above heaven, because “in” means “above.” Allah Most High says:

“So travel freely in the land,”

meaning upon it.

He said:

If you ask, “How is He?” we tell you: “How” is a question about His attributes. He possesses the most exalted attributes. He is the All-Knowing, who possesses knowledge, and the All-Powerful

---

## Page 8096

who possesses power, and the Ever-Living, who possesses life, who has eternally been unique in possessing these attributes. He resembles nothing, and nothing resembles Him.

(I say:

This statement agrees with what al-Ashʿarī mentioned in his book al-Ibānah and with what Ibn Kullāb stated, as reported by Ibn Fūrak. However, Ibn Kullāb says that being above and separate are attributes known through reason. These others, by contrast, say that His being in heaven is an attribute known through revelation, like His coming and approaching. They state without qualification that He Himself is above the Throne, and regard this as an attribute of His essence.

Al-Ashʿarī refutes those who interpret istiwā—being established above—as dominion and subjugation, arguing that He has always held dominion over the Throne and everything else, whereas istiwā applies specifically to the Throne. If it meant dominion, it would be permissible to say, “He is established above everything, including the earth and other things,” just as one says, “He holds dominion over them.” Since Muslims agree that istiwā applies specifically to the Throne, this particular istiwā cannot mean universal dominion. And what authority is there for interpreting istiwā as subjugation and supremacy, which amount to dominion?

It therefore seems—and Allah knows best—that his conclusions on these questions varied, just as those of others did. Abū al-Maʿālī used to advocate figurative interpretation, then prohibited it and reported the early generations’ consensus that it was prohibited. Ibn ʿAqīl held differing views, and likewise

---

## Page 8097

did Abū Hāmid, al-Rāzī, and others. The inconsistency in Ibn Fūrak’s statements is further illustrated by what he said in another work:

“If someone asks, ‘Where is He?’ the reply is: He has no modality for us to describe, unless the question is, ‘What are His acts like?’ His acts include honoring whomever He wills and humbling whomever He wills, and He is the Maker of all things.”

Here he rejected inquiry into modality, whereas there he permitted it, saying: “Modality means an attribute, and He possesses attributes.” The same applies to asking what He is. In that work he said:

“If the Jahmiyyah ask, ‘What is He?’ they are told: ‘What’ may inquire about a genus or an attribute within the questioner himself. If you mean to ask about His attributes, the answer is knowledge, power, speech, might, and greatness.”

In the other work he said:

“If someone asks, ‘Tell us about the One you worship: what is He?’ the reply is: If you mean, ‘To what genus does He belong?’ He belongs to no genus. If by asking, ‘What is He?’ you mean, ‘Point Him out to me so that I may perceive Him with my senses,’ He is not present to the senses.

“If by asking, ‘What is He?’ you mean, ‘Guide me to Him through the wonders of His workmanship and the manifestations of His wisdom,’ then the evidence pointing to Him is established. If you mean, ‘What is His name?’ we say: He is Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful, the All-Powerful, the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing.”

---

## Page 8098

In this work, he affirmed that He is above the Throne, unlike His state before the Throne existed. He said:

“If someone asks, ‘Then tell us: where was He before He created?’ the reply is: ‘Where?’ presupposes a place, and places are created. He—glorified be He—has always existed, before creation and places, without being in a place; neither time nor duration applies to Him.

“If he asks, ‘Then what is He above now?’ he is told: He is established above the Throne, as He—glorified be He—said: ‘The Most Merciful is established above the Throne.’”

He also said:

“If someone asks, ‘Has the Creator always been All-Powerful, All-Knowing, Ever-Living, All-Hearing, and All-Seeing?’ the reply is: Yes. If he then asks, ‘Why, then, do you deny that He has always been a creator?’ he is told: If by saying, ‘He has always been a creator,’ you mean that creation has always existed alongside Him in His beginningless eternity, this is incorrect. For creation means that something did not exist and then came into being. How, then, could something that did not exist and then came into being have always existed?

“If you mean that the Creator has always existed and was able to create, then that is what we say. For the Creator has always existed, whereas creation did not exist and then came into being; and He has always had the power to create. This, then, is the answer.”

He said:

“If it is asked, ‘Since you say that He is a creator now, why do you deny that He has always been a creator?’ the reply is: That does not necessarily follow. For He is now established above

---

## Page 8099

His Throne; it does not follow that He has always been established above His Throne. What we have said is analogous to this. If it is said, ‘His establishment above the Throne is an act of His, and it is impossible for an act to have existed eternally,’ he said: The reply is, ‘Creation is also an act of His, and it is impossible for creation to have existed eternally.’”

This discussion merely sets out the position of those who say that He became established above the Throne after not having been so, yet affirm that the attribute of bringing into being and creating is eternal and that He has always been a creator. He therefore pressed them with the following argument:

“We say concerning creation what both we and you say concerning establishment above the Throne.”

This is a weak answer for several reasons:

First: In reality, he does not hold that He became established above the Throne after not having been so—as he discussed with the sultan. Rather, He is now as He was. Thus, the analogy with this is invalid.

Second: He has conceded that He has always had the power to create creation. This entails that the object of His power could exist in beginningless eternity. For if the object of that power were impossible, there would be no power to bring it about. How, then, can he affirm that He has always had power while deeming it impossible for the object of His power to have always been possible? Rather, according to him, the object of His power was impossible and then became possible without any newly arising cause necessitating that change.

---

## Page 8100

Third: As for his statement:

“For creation means that something did not exist and then came into existence. How, then, can something that did not exist and then came into existence have always existed?”

The reply is: Every created thing comes into being, preceded by its own nonexistence. Allah alone is eternal and beginningless. When it is said, “He has always been a creator,” this entails only that creation as a kind is beginningless. “His unceasing activity as Creator” does not entail that any particular created thing is beginningless. A distinction must therefore be drawn concerning individual created things that come into being after not having existed: no rational person says that any of these is beginningless. Whoever holds that something in the world—such as the celestial sphere or its matter—is beginningless regards it as created in the sense that it came into being after not having existed, but on the grounds that the Eternal brought it into existence. Yet He has always been acting and creating, and His unceasing activity as Creator is a necessary consequence of His existence. This does not affirm the beginninglessness of any created thing; rather, it entails that everything other than Him came into being. This is what the question put to him implies.

The fourth response is to say: The Throne came into being after not having existed, and He has remained established above it since it came into existence. As for creation, the discussion concerns creation as a kind. His argument that events without a first are impossible has already been shown to be weak. And Allah knows best.

In addressing the sultan, Ibn Fūrak intended to demonstrate his opposition to the Karrāmiyyah, just as in Nīshāpūr he sought to take action against the Mu'tazilah by demanding their repentance, and just as he declared them unbelievers in the presence of

---

## Page 8101

the sultan. Whoever fails to treat his opponents and disputants justly and to excuse their errors in independent reasoning, but instead introduces an innovation and shows hostility toward those who disagree with him about it, or declares them unbelievers, has wronged himself. The people of the Sunnah, knowledge, and faith know the truth and show mercy to creation; they follow the Messenger and do not introduce innovations. If someone exercises independent reasoning and makes an error for which the Messenger would excuse him, they excuse him.

The people of innovation, such as the Khawārij, introduce an innovation, declare those who disagree with them unbelievers, and deem it lawful to shed their blood. Each of these groups rejects the others’ innovations, yet is itself guilty of innovation, countering one innovation with another and one falsehood with another. His account of their debates with him before the vizier, session after session, likewise falls into this category. The Muʿtazilah and the Karrāmiyyah profess both truth and falsehood, both Sunnah and innovation, just as he too professes both truth and falsehood in following Abū al-Hasan. In matters of religious designations and rulings, and of divine decree, Abū al-Hasan followed the path of al-Jahm ibn Safwān—the path of the determinists and the extreme Murjiʾah. Thus, the former are determinist Qadariyyah, whereas the Muʿtazilah are Qadariyyah who deny divine decree. They consequently stood in utter opposition to one another on questions of justice and injustice and similar matters.

Allah loves speech grounded in knowledge and justice, and hates speech grounded in ignorance and injustice, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Judges are of three kinds: two judges are in the Fire, and one judge

---

## Page 8102

is in Paradise: a man who judges people’s cases without knowledge is in the Fire; a man who knows the truth but rules against it is in the Fire; and a man who knows the truth and rules accordingly is in Paradise.”

He, glorified be He, has forbidden speaking without knowledge in general, and has specifically forbidden speaking about Him without knowledge. He, the Most High, says:

“Do not pursue that of which you have no knowledge. Indeed, the hearing, the sight, and the heart—each of these will be questioned.”

He, the Most High, also says:

“Say: My Lord has only forbidden shameful deeds, whether openly or secretly committed, sin, unjust aggression, associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority, and saying about Allah what you do not know.”

He has commanded justice even toward the enemies of the Muslims, saying:

“Stand firm for Allah as witnesses in justice, and do not let hatred of a people lead you to be unjust. Be just; that is nearer to righteousness.”

Section

He, glorified be He, has described Himself as being on high. This is an attribute for which He is praised and magnified, because it is an attribute of perfection, just as He praised Himself as the Magnificent, the All-Knowing, the All-Powerful, the Almighty, the Forbearing, and the like, and as the Ever-Living

---

## Page 8103

the Self-Subsisting Sustainer of all, and the like among the meanings of His Most Beautiful Names. He cannot possess the opposites of these attributes. Thus He cannot be described by anything contrary to His life, His being self-subsisting and sustaining all, His knowledge, or His power—such as death, sleep, ignorance, incapacity, or weariness. Nor can He be described by the opposite of might, which is humiliation, or the opposite of wisdom, which is foolishness. Likewise, He cannot be described by the opposite of being on high, which is being below, or the opposite of being magnificent, which is being insignificant. Rather, He, glorified be He, is far above these deficiencies, which contradict the attributes of perfection established for Him. His possession of attributes of perfection excludes their opposites, which are deficiencies. Nothing is like Him in the attributes of perfection with which He is described. He is therefore free of any deficiency contrary to His perfection, and far above having any equal in any of His attributes. All that is meant by affirming His transcendence comes down to these two principles.

These two principles are indicated in Sūrat al-Ikhlās, which is equivalent to one-third of the Quran, in His words:

“Say: He is Allah, the One;
Allah, the Self-Sufficient Master.”

His name “the Self-Sufficient Master” encompasses the meanings of the attributes of perfection, as has been explained at length in the commentary on this surah and elsewhere. As related from Ibn ‘Abbās in Ibn Abī Talhah’s commentary, He is the One entitled to the attributes of supreme mastery, the All-Knowing

---

## Page 8104

whose knowledge is perfect, the All-Wise, whose wisdom is perfect, and so on, as already explained. His name “the One” implies that He has neither likeness nor equal:

“And there is none comparable to Him.”

We have explained elsewhere that every attribute by which Allah Most High describes Himself in negative terms must imply an affirmative meaning. Perfection lies in existence and affirmation; negation serves to deny whatever contradicts that perfection. When one contradictory—nonexistence and negation—is denied, the other—existence and affirmation—is necessarily established.

We have explained this regarding the Verse of the Throne and other Quranic passages. For example, His words:

“Neither drowsiness nor sleep overtakes Him”

imply the perfection of His life and His being self-subsisting and sustaining all. His words:

“Who can intercede with Him except by His permission?”

imply the perfection of His sovereignty. His words:

“They encompass nothing of His knowledge”

imply that He alone imparts knowledge, to the exclusion of all others. Oneness entails perfection, whereas partnership entails deficiency.

The same applies to His words:

“Preserving them both does not weary Him”; 

“No weariness touched Us”; 

“Vision cannot encompass Him”; 

“Not even an atom’s weight escapes Him.”

These and similar passages have been discussed at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 8105

The point here is that His being above all is one of His inseparable attributes of praise. He can therefore never be described by the opposite of being on high.

For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in the authentic hadith:

“You are the First, and nothing is before You; You are the Last, and nothing is after You; You are the Most High, and nothing is above You; You are the Most Near, and nothing is nearer than You.”

He did not say “beneath You.” We have discussed this hadith elsewhere.

That being so, those who oppose the Book, the Sunnah, and the way of the early generations do not affirm that He is on high to the exclusion of being below. Rather, they either describe Him as being both above and below, or in terms that necessarily entail this, or they deny that He is either above or below. They fall into two groups.

The Jahmiyyah, who say that He Himself is in every place, or that He is neither within the world nor outside it, do not describe Him as being on high to the exclusion of being below. For if He is in a place, places include both high and low ones: in a high place He would be high, and in a low place He would be low. Indeed, when they say that He is in every place, making all places locations in which He resides—containers and vessels—they have in reality made those places higher than Him. For a location encompasses what resides in it, and a container or vessel encompasses its contents; that which encompasses is above what it encompasses.

When the early generations, the imams, and all the other scholars of the Sunnah say, “He is above the Throne

---

## Page 8106

and that He is in heaven, above all things,” they do not mean that anything contains Him, confines Him, or serves as a place, container, or vessel for Him. Glorified and exalted is He above that! Rather, He is above everything and has no need of anything, while everything needs Him. He is high above everything, and through His strength and power He sustains the Throne and its bearers. Every created being needs Him, while He has no need of the Throne or any created being.

Some may understand expressions found in the Book and the Sunnah, such as His words, “Do you feel secure from Him who is in heaven?”, to mean that “heaven” refers to the elevated creation itself—the Throne and whatever is beneath it. They therefore say that “in heaven” means “above heaven,” just as He says, “I will surely crucify you on the trunks of palm trees,” where “on the trunks” translates an expression literally meaning “in the trunks”; and, similarly, “Travel throughout the earth,” meaning “over the earth.”

There is no need for this, however. Rather, al-samā’ (heaven) is a generic term for what is above, not restricted to any particular thing. Thus, His words “in heaven” mean “on high, not below.” He is the Most High, the Highest; His is the highest elevation, above the Throne. There is none there but Him, the Most High, the Highest—glorified and exalted is He.

As for those who say that He is everywhere, according to them He is in lowly, filthy, foul created things just as He is in elevated created things. The extremists among them are the proponents of union, who say, “Existence is one,” such as Ibn ‘Arabī al-Tā’ī, the author of “Fusūs

---

## Page 8107

al-Hikam” and “al-Futūhāt al-Makkiyya.” They say, “The necessary, eternal being is identical with the originated, contingent being.” Thus Ibn ‘Arabī says in “Fusūs al-Hikam”:

“Among His most beautiful names is ‘the Most High.’ Above whom, when there is none but Him? And beyond what, when He is nothing other than Himself? His elevation is therefore intrinsic to Him, and in respect of existence He is identical with existing things. Thus, the things called ‘originated beings’ are intrinsically exalted and are none other than Him.”

He goes on to say:

“The one who is intrinsically exalted is the one who possesses all attributes of existence and all relations of nonexistence, whether praiseworthy according to custom, reason, and revealed law, or blameworthy according to custom, reason, and revealed law. This is none other than the one named Allah.”

Thus, according to him, He is described by every blameworthy quality just as He is described by every praiseworthy quality. These people rank some created things above Him, for some created things, such as the heavens, are described as elevated but not low.
