Whatever is described as elevated but not low is superior to that which is not described as elevated, or which is described as both elevated and low.

Pharaoh said:

“I am your lord, the most high.”

Ibn ‘Arabī said:

---

## Page 8108

“Since Pharaoh occupied the position of ruler and caliph by the sword, legal convention permitted him to say:

‘I am your lord, the most high.’

That is: even if all are lords in some respect, I am the highest among them by virtue of the authority I have been given over you.

When the sorcerers recognized the truth of what he said, they did not deny it. Rather, they acknowledged it and said to him:

‘Decree whatever you wish to decree. You can only decree concerning this worldly life.’

Sovereignty, then, is yours. Thus Pharaoh’s statement was correct:

‘I am your lord, the most high.’”

Through this and similar arguments, they vindicate Pharaoh’s statement, “I am your lord, the most high,” while denying that Allah is high, let alone that He is the Most High. They say, “Higher than whom would He be, or higher than what?”

Likewise, the other Jahmiyya speak approvingly of the elevation of created things that are high, such as the heavens, Paradise, the stars, and the like. They know that what is high is superior to what is low, yet they do not describe their Lord as the Most High or the Exalted. Rather, they place Him among the lowest things just as they place Him among the highest.

The Jahmiyya who say, “He is neither within the world nor outside it, and He cannot be pointed to at all,” are closer to negation and nonexistence, just as the others are closer to indwelling in, and union with, created beings. These affirm an existent being, but in reality it is the created being, not the Creator; those, meanwhile, negate and affirm no existence whatsoever. Yet they

---

## Page 8109

affirm the existence of created beings and claim that they affirm the existence of the Creator.

If they say, “We say that He is high in power or rank,” the reply is: This presupposes that His very existence has been established. Yet you have not established an existent being whose existence can be known, let alone one who is powerful or of exalted rank.

If they say, “Allah existed before places and created beings were created, and He remains as He was, unchanged. He was not then above anything or high over anything, and so He is now,” the reply is: This is mistaken. Its invalidity becomes apparent first through a counterargument, and then through an analysis explaining its flaws.

As for the first approach, their position entails that He is not now high in power or rank, just as He was not in eternity past. For if His existence alone is supposed, there is no existent being over which He exercises power, dominance, or mastery, nor any existent being whose rank He surpasses. If, as they claim, no elevation over created beings arose for Him when they came into existence, then it follows that after their creation He has no power or mastery over anything, does not dominate His servants, and is no greater in rank than they are.

If they, along with all rational people, say that, when created beings exist, He is described in relational terms that do not apply to Him

---

## Page 8110

when He is conceived as existing alone, then it is clear that they are mistaken in treating the two situations as equivalent. All rational people agree that new relations and relational attributes may arise, such as being with another. The dispute concerns only whether new matters subject to His will may arise and subsist in His essence. It has been explained elsewhere that relations and relational attributes necessarily entail positive realities, and that their existence without such realities is impossible.

If a man is seated and someone moves to his right after having been to his left, one says of the seated man, “He is to his left.” An action has thus occurred on the part of the one who moved, changing the relation and relational attribute. Likewise, when someone who was beneath a roof comes to be above it, the relations of being below and above arise anew through that person’s new action.

If it is said, “The roof itself has not changed,” the reply is: This may be disputed. What is true of it when nothing is above it is not the same as when something is above it. Likewise, if it is said of the seated man, “He has not changed,” the reply is: This may be disputed. What is true of him when the other person is to his left is not the same as when that person is to his right. For the other person blocks that side, prompting the seated man to turn and producing other effects that were not there before.

Likewise, when someone acquires a brother or nephew through his father or brother begetting a child, positive realities have come into existence. This person develops affection and tenderness toward the newborn child that were not there before. These are the ties of kinship and family.

---

## Page 8111

This also makes clear the second answer, namely:

Being above and below, and similar attributes, necessarily entail a relation. The same applies to rising above, lordship, being the Creator, and the like.

When something other than Him exists, He is either above it or He is not, just as they themselves say: He is either above it in mastery or rank, or He is not. This differs from conceiving of Him as existing alone, for they do not then say that He subdues something, exercises power over it, or holds dominion over it. Likewise, He is not said to be above it.

If they say, “He possesses power and mastery,” this is conditional upon there being something other than Him; the same applies to His exaltedness in rank. The reply is: The same holds for His being above in His essence. He has always been above in His essence, but the manifestation of this is conditional upon the existence of something other than Him.

These inescapable implications leave them without an answer. What their position really amounts to is that He did not possess power in eternity past, but then came to possess it. They say that He has always possessed power, even though what He had power to do was impossible, and that action was not possible but then became possible. They thus affirm two contradictory propositions.

Section

As for those who describe Him as being both above and below, they are those who say, “He is above the Throne and also in every place,” and those who say, “When He descends each night, He

---

## Page 8112

leaves the Throne unoccupied, or some other created thing is greater than Him.” They say, “It is not impossible for the Creator to be smaller than the creation,” just as their teachers say, “It is not impossible for the Creator to be below the creation.” Such people do not describe Him as greater than everything. Indeed, according to their position, He is neither the Great, the Supremely Exalted, nor the Most High, the Magnificent.

He set out a detailed refutation of these people in “The Question of Descent.” When discussing the statement of the imams of the Sunnah, such as Hammād ibn Zayd, Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, and others, “He descends, yet the Throne does not become unoccupied by Him,” he cited the position of some later scholars affiliated with hadith and the Sunnah who denied this, and demonstrated the unsoundness of their position on both scriptural and rational grounds. These people stand at the opposite extreme from those who deny the descent.

If it is said, “The apparent meaning of the hadith of descent and similar reports does not admit figurative interpretation,” this is correct if “apparent meaning” refers to what these people and others like them understand: that He descends to a lower position and comes to be beneath the Throne, just as a person descends from the roof of his house to a lower level. According to their position, He would then no longer be the Most High or the Highest; rather, He would sometimes be above and sometimes below. Exalted is Allah far above what the wrongdoers say!

The same applies to the reports of His descent on the Day of Resurrection in canopies of clouds, and of His descent

---

## Page 8113

to the earth when He created it, and of His descent to speak to Moses, and other such reports—all belong to the same category, as in His words, Exalted is He:

“Do they await anything but that Allah should come to them in canopies of clouds?”

And His words:

“And your Lord comes, and the angels, rank upon rank.”

And His words:

“Do they await anything but that the angels should come to them, or your Lord should come, or some of your Lord’s signs should come?”

Those who deny and negate His attributes deny His coming altogether, saying, “There is nothing beyond what occurs within created things.” Those who hold that He indwells creation say that He comes in such a way that one place becomes unoccupied by Him and another becomes occupied: what is above the Throne becomes unoccupied by Him, and some created things come to be above Him. Thus, when He comes, according to their position, He would no longer be the Most High, the Highest, nor would He be the Most High, the Magnificent. This is especially so if they say that some created thing contains Him and is therefore greater than Him. Glorified and exalted is He, far above what both groups say!

The same applies to His words:

“Do you feel secure from Him who is in heaven?”

If anyone has said that He is inside heaven, his statement is worse than theirs. However, I know of no specific person associated with learning who has said this, such that I could report it as a position.

Whoever says, “He is in heaven,” means that He is on high, not that He is inside the celestial spheres, although some ignorant people imagine this. A group has supposed that this is the apparent meaning of the wording.

---

## Page 8114

As for the apparent wording, there is no doubt that, by common agreement, it is understood otherwise. Yet it is not conceded that this is what ordinary Muslims who use and hear this expression naturally understand, or that it is what the wording means in Arabic, as may be explained at length elsewhere.

He, Exalted is He, has said:

“Say, ‘No one in the heavens and the earth knows the unseen except Allah.’”

He thus excepted Himself, while “whoever is in the heavens and the earth” is the subject of “knows.” It cannot be said that this is a disconnected exception, because the excepted noun is nominative; had the exception been disconnected, it would have been accusative. Its nominative case is due to apposition, and its grammatical governor is the same as that of the expression to which it stands in apposition. This is equivalent to an elliptical exception construction, as though He had said, “No one knows the unseen except Allah.” It follows that He is included within “whoever is in the heavens and the earth.”

We have already explained that the word “heaven” encompasses everything that is high: it includes the heavens, the Footstool, the Throne, and whatever is above that. For this occurs within a negative statement, and He did not say here “the seven heavens”; rather, He used the unrestricted expression “the heavens.”

Since “heaven” may mean the clouds, the celestial sphere, what is above the world, or height in an unrestricted sense, “heavens” is the plural of “heaven.” No one in anything called “heaven,” and no one in anything called “earth,” knows the unseen except Allah.

---

## Page 8115

And He, Glorified is He, said, “Say, no one knows…” He used man (“who”), not mā (“what”). When rational and non-rational beings are mentioned together, the rational beings take precedence, and the whole is referred to using man. This makes the statement more emphatic: despite their knowledge and understanding, none of them knows the unseen except Allah. This is the absolute unseen, hidden from all created beings, concerning which He said:

“He discloses His unseen to no one.”

The relative unseen, however, comprises things that some created beings—angels, jinn, or humans—know and have witnessed. These are unseen only to those from whom they are hidden, not to those who have witnessed them. Among people generally, what one person witnesses may be hidden from another. It is therefore relatively unseen: unseen to those created beings from whom it is hidden, but not to those who have witnessed it. It is not the absolute unseen, hidden from all created beings without exception.

His words,

“Knower of the unseen and the seen,”

mean that He knows what is hidden from His servants, whether from all of them or from particular individuals, as well as what they have witnessed. He, Glorified is He, knows all of this.

Those who deny Allah’s being above creation and other such attributes acknowledge that their position is not based on what the prophets conveyed—neither the Book, nor the Sunnah, nor the statements of the early generations. Nor is it based on reason’s innate disposition or its self-evident knowledge. Rather, they say, “We have rational reflection on our side.”

As for Ahl al-Sunnah, who affirm that Allah is above creation, they say, “This is established by the Book, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus, together with the innate disposition Allah has instilled in His servants, the self-evident knowledge of reason, and rational reflection and inference.”

---

## Page 8116

But those who say that He descends without remaining above the Throne, that He is inside created things, and others like them, may say that their basis is transmitted evidence: what they have understood from the Quran, from authentic or unauthentic hadiths, or from the statements of the early generations. Their error lies in their approach: they confined themselves to what they understood from a single text, such as the hadith concerning His descent. They did not reflect on the passages in the Book and the Sunnah that describe His highness, greatness, and other attributes that preclude anything from being higher or greater than Him.

Nor did they reflect on the implications of the text itself, such as His descent to the lowest heaven when the last third of the night remains. Night occurs at different times: the night, its midpoint, and its last third occur for people in the east nearly a day before they do for those in the west. Their position would therefore entail that He is continually beneath the Throne, whereas He has stated that He rose over the Throne after creating the heavens and the earth. Their account contradicts His rising over the Throne and entails that He is not above the Throne, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

Section

“Al-A‘lā” (the Most High) follows the comparative and superlative pattern af‘al, like al-Akram (the Most Generous), al-Akbar (the Greatest), and al-Ajall (the Most Majestic). Hence, “The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said, when Abū Sufyān said:

---

## Page 8117

‘Rise high, Hubal! Rise high, Hubal!’ the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said, ‘Will you not answer him?’ They asked, ‘What should we say?’ He said, ‘Say: Allah is higher and more majestic.’”

Here, “al-A‘lā” (the Most High) appears with the definite article, as in “and your Lord is the Most Generous,” unlike the expression “Allāhu akbar” (Allah is greater), in which the comparative is indefinite. The former has its own distinctive meaning, and the latter has its own distinctive meaning, just as there are distinctions between highness, greatness, and magnificence. Although these attributes are closely related—indeed, inseparable—there are subtle differences between them. Thus, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said, relating from his Lord Most High:

“Magnificence is My lower garment, and greatness is My upper garment. Whoever contests either with Me, I shall punish.”

He thus likened greatness to the upper garment, which is above the lower garment.

For this reason, takbīr—saying “Allāhu akbar” (Allah is greater)—is the distinctive proclamation associated with prayer, the call to prayer, the Eids, and elevated places. It is one of the utterances that constitute the finest speech after the Quran: “Glory be to Allah,” “Praise be to Allah,” “There is no god but Allah,” and “Allah is greater,” as is authentically established from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace).

No transmitted report substitutes “Allāhu a‘zam” (Allah is more magnificent) for “Allāhu akbar” (Allah is greater). Accordingly, the majority of jurists hold that prayer can only be validly begun with the wording of takbīr. If someone were to say “Allāhu a‘zam,” the prayer would not be validly begun, because the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“The key to prayer is purification; takbīr marks entry into its sacred restrictions, and the salutation of peace marks release from them.”

This is

---

## Page 8118

the view of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, Abū Yūsuf, Dāwūd, and others. Nor would the prayer be validly begun with any other words of remembrance, such as “Glory be to Allah” or “Praise be to Allah.”

This is also because takbīr is the remembrance specifically associated with ascending, just as glorification is specifically associated with descending. Thus, the Sunan collections report that Jābir ibn ‘Abd Allāh said:

“When we were with the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), we would pronounce takbīr whenever we ascended and glorify Allah whenever we descended. Prayer was therefore arranged accordingly.”

When His words, “So glorify the name of your Lord, the Magnificent,” were revealed, he said, “Use this in your bowing.” And when “Glorify the name of your Lord, the Most High” was revealed, he said, “Use this in your prostration.”

It is authentically established that he would say while bowing, “Glory be to my Lord, the Magnificent,” and while prostrating, “Glory be to my Lord, the Most High.” He did not pronounce takbīr while in bowing or prostration. He did, however, combine glorification with praise and the declaration that there is no god but Allah. Thus, it is authentically established in the two Sahīh collections, on the authority of ‘Ā’ishah, that he (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“Would say while bowing and prostrating, ‘Glory be to You, O Allah, our Lord, and praise be to You. O Allah, forgive me,’ putting the Quran into practice.”

That is, he was putting into practice His words:

“So glorify your Lord with praise and seek His forgiveness. Indeed, He is ever Accepting of repentance.”

Thus, he combined glorification and praise.

---

## Page 8119

Likewise, he would combine glorification with the declaration that there is no god but Allah while bowing and prostrating, as reported in Sahīh Muslim from ‘Ā’ishah, who said:

“One night I missed the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) and thought that he had gone to one of his other wives. I searched for him, then returned, only to find him bowing or prostrating, saying, ‘Glory be to You, and praise be to You. There is no god but You.’ I said, ‘May my father and mother be sacrificed for you! I was preoccupied with one thing, while you were occupied with quite another!’”

All these hadiths show that he would glorify Allah while bowing and prostrating. Sometimes he would combine glorification with praise and the declaration that there is no god but Allah, and sometimes with supplication. It has not been reported that he pronounced takbīr while bowing or prostrating.

As for reciting the Quran in these two postures, it is authentically established that he said:

“I have been forbidden to recite the Quran while bowing or prostrating.”

Muslim narrated this through reports from ‘Alī and Ibn ‘Abbās. This is because the Quran is the speech of Allah, so it is to be recited only in an elevated posture. Takbīr, likewise, belongs to a state of rising.

The majority of scholars hold that glorifying Allah while bowing and prostrating is prescribed. It is reported from Mālik that he disliked doing so invariably, lest it be thought obligatory. They then differed over whether it is obligatory. The well-known position reported from Ahmad, Ishāq, Dāwūd, and others is that it is obligatory, whereas the position reported from Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfi‘ī is that it is recommended.

Among those who hold it obligatory are some who say that the specific wording required is “Glory be to my Lord, the Magnificent”

---

## Page 8120

and “Glory be to my Lord, the Most High,” because both have been commanded. This is the position of many scholars of Ahmad’s school. Others say that one may instead recite some of the transmitted remembrances.

The stronger view is that glorification specifically is required, whether expressed as “Glory be…” or “Glory be to You,” or something similar. This is because the Quran calls prayer “glorification,” indicating that glorification within it is obligatory. The Sunnah has explained that its place is in bowing and prostration, just as Allah calls prayer “recitation,” and the Sunnah has explained that the place for recitation is while standing. He also calls prayer “standing,” “prostration,” and “bowing,” and the Sunnah has explained the reason for this and the place of each. Likewise, glorification is to be offered while bowing and prostrating.

It has been reported that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) used to say, “Glory be to my Lord, the Magnificent” and “Glory be to my Lord, the Most High”; and that he used to say, “Glory be to You, O Allah, and praise be to You. O Allah, forgive me,” and “Glory be to You, and praise be to You. There is no god but You.”

Some versions narrated by Abū Dāwūd have: “Glory be to my Lord, the Magnificent, and praise be to Him.” Two positions are reported from Ahmad as to whether this addition is recommended.

In Sahīh Muslim, ‘Ā’ishah reports that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) used to say while bowing and prostrating:

“All-Glorious, All-Holy, Lord of the angels and the Spirit.”

The Sunan collections report that he used to say:

“Glory be to the Possessor of might, dominion, majesty, and greatness.”

All of these are expressions of glorification.

---

## Page 8121

It is reported that Mālik disapproved of consistently adhering to this. If he meant consistently using “Glory be to my Lord, the Most High and the Magnificent,” his position has some justification. But if he meant consistently offering glorification as such, it has no justification. I believe he meant the former. Indeed, what is reported from him is only that he disapproved of consistently using “Glory be to my Lord, the Magnificent,” lest it be thought obligatory. This implies that Mālik denied that this particular formula was obligatory—a strong and clear position. Glorification as such, however, is quite different: the Quran and Sunnah contain very numerous proofs that it is obligatory.

It is known that he (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) regularly offered glorification using varied expressions. His instruction, “Include this in your bowing and prostration,” indicates that these are the places for fulfilling this command; it does not mean that only this formula may be said, especially since it is established that he used other expressions.

Combining two formulas of glorification is hardly warranted, unlike combining glorification, praise, the declaration that there is no god but Allah, and supplication. These are distinct kinds of remembrance, whereas glorification is a single kind, so two of its formulas are not combined.

It is also established in an authentic report that he said:

“The best speech after the Quran

---

## Page 8122

is four phrases, all of which are from the Quran: ‘Glory be to Allah,’ ‘Praise be to Allah,’ ‘There is no god but Allah,’ and ‘Allah is the Greatest.’” This implies that these phrases are better than others. If glorification is treated as a single category, then “Glory be to Allah” and “Glory be to my Lord, the Most High” are equal. If its formulas are regarded as differing in merit, then “Glory be to Allah” is better, according to this hadith.

Furthermore, His words,

“Glorify the name of your Lord, the Most High,”

and

“So glorify the name of your Lord, the Magnificent,”

command one to glorify one’s Lord; they do not prescribe a particular formula. Thus, when someone says, “Glory be to Allah, and praise be to Him,” or “Glory be to You, O Allah, and praise be to You,” he has glorified his Lord, the Most High and the Magnificent. For Allah is the Most High and the Magnificent, and His name “Allah” implicitly encompasses the meanings of all His other names, although it does not explicitly express exaltedness and magnificence. His name “Allah” explicitly expresses divinity, and His name “Allah” is greater than His name “the Lord.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Abū Dharr reports that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) was asked, “Which speech is best?” He replied:

“That which Allah has chosen for His angels—or for His servants: ‘Glory be to Allah, and praise be to Him.’”

Thus, praise is offered while standing and when standing upright after bowing; glorification is offered in bowing and prostration; Allah’s greatness is proclaimed when moving between postures; and the testimony of faith is recited while sitting, including the declaration of Allah’s oneness. All four kinds of remembrance are therefore present in prayer.

---

## Page 8123

Al-Fātihah also contains praise and the declaration of Allah’s oneness. Praise and the declaration of His oneness are therefore an essential pillar required in the recitation; proclaiming Allah’s greatness is an essential pillar at the opening of prayer; and the final testimony of faith is an essential pillar while sitting, according to the well-known position reported from Ahmad, which is also the position of al-Shāfi‘ī. This sitting includes the testimony of faith, which encompasses the declaration of Allah’s oneness.

That leaves glorification. Ahmad considers it obligatory in bowing and prostration, and it is also reported from him that it is an essential pillar. This is a strong position, since the command to glorify Allah is established in the Quran and Sunnah. How, then, can invoking blessings upon the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) be considered obligatory, when no command specifically requires it within prayer, while glorification is not considered obligatory, despite the command to perform it in prayer and despite prayer itself being called “glorification”? Every component after which prayer is named is an essential pillar of it: prayer is called “standing,” “bowing,” “prostration,” and “recitation,” and it is also called “glorification.”

Nothing has been reported from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) that would waive its obligation when it is forgotten, unlike the first testimony of faith: it is reported that when he omitted it, he performed the prostrations of forgetfulness. It may, however, be argued that his not commanding the man who prayed incorrectly to recite glorification indicates that it is obligatory but not an essential pillar. A detailed discussion of these questions belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that glorification has been assigned specifically to the lowered posture, just as proclaiming Allah’s greatness has been assigned to rising. Thus, in his lowered and humbled state, the servant is reminded of the attributes possessed by

---

## Page 8124

the Lord, in contrast to his own state. Thus, in prostration he says, “Glory be to my Lord, the Most High,” and in bowing, “Glory be to my Lord, the Magnificent.”

“The Most High” encompasses all the meanings of highness, and He is the Most High in every such sense. People are agreed that He is above all things in the sense that He subdues them, has power over them, and governs them, as He says:

“Then each god would have taken away what it had created, and some would have prevailed over others.”

They are also agreed that He is exalted above every fault and deficiency: He is above all such things and free of them. As He, the Exalted, says:

“Do not set up another god alongside Allah, lest you be cast into Hell, blameworthy and banished.”

“Has your Lord favoured you with sons and taken daughters for Himself from among the angels? You are indeed making an outrageous claim.”

“We have certainly explained matters in various ways in this Quran so that they may take heed, yet it only increases their aversion.”

“Say: If there were other gods alongside Him, as they claim, they would surely have sought a way to the Lord of the Throne.”

“Glory be to Him! He is far exalted above what they say.”

Thus, He coupled His exaltedness above such things with the declaration of His freedom from all imperfection.

He, the Exalted, also says:

“Allah has never taken any offspring, nor is there any god alongside Him. Otherwise, each god would have taken away what it had created, and some would have prevailed over others. Glory be to Allah, far above what they attribute to Him!”

“Knower of the unseen and the seen! Exalted is He above what they associate with Him.”

And the jinn said:

“Exalted is the majesty of our Lord! He has taken neither a wife nor offspring.”

---

## Page 8125

And in the opening supplication:

“Glory and praise be to You, O Allah. Blessed is Your name, and exalted is Your majesty.”

In the two Sahīh collections, it is reported that he used to say at the end of his opening supplication:

“Blessed and exalted are You. I seek Your forgiveness and repent to You.”

He, glorified be He, has thus made clear that He is exalted above what the proponents of falsehood say and above what they associate with Him. He is exalted above having partners or offspring, just as He is glorified as being free of such things. His exaltedness above having a partner is His exaltedness above having any peer, rival or likeness: nothing can be like Him.

They have also mentioned superiority among the meanings of highness, as when one says, “Gold ranks higher than silver.” Denying that He has any likeness entails that He is higher than everything, with nothing like Him. This includes His being superior to and better than everything, just as He is greater than everything.

In the Quran:

“Say: Praise be to Allah, and peace upon His servants whom He has chosen. Is Allah better, or those they associate with Him?”

He also says:

“Is He who creates like one who does not create? Will you not then take heed?”

And He says:

“Is He who guides to the truth more worthy of being followed, or one who cannot find the way unless he is guided?”

And the magicians said:

“Allah is better and more enduring.”

He, glorified be He, makes clear in various passages that those worshipped besides Him are not like Him, as in His saying:

“Say: Who provides for you from heaven and earth, or who has control over hearing

---

## Page 8126

and sight? Who brings the living out of the dead and the dead out of the living? And who governs all affairs? They will say, ‘Allah.’ Then say: Will you not fear Him?”

“Such is Allah, your true Lord. What is there beyond the truth except error? How, then, are you turned away?”

“Thus your Lord’s word has come true against those who defiantly disobey: they will not believe.”

“Say: Can any of those you associate with Allah originate creation and then repeat it? Say: Allah originates creation and then repeats it. How, then, are you deluded?”

“Say: Can any of those you associate with Allah guide to the truth? Say: Allah guides to the truth. Is He who guides to the truth more worthy of being followed, or one who cannot find the way unless he is guided? What is the matter with you? How do you judge?”

“Most of them follow nothing but conjecture. Surely conjecture is no substitute for the truth. Indeed, Allah knows all that they do.”

He, exalted be He, also says:

“Is He who creates like one who does not create? Will you not then take heed?”

“If you tried to count Allah’s blessings, you could never enumerate them. Indeed, Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“Allah knows what you conceal and what you reveal.”

“Those they invoke besides Allah create nothing; they themselves are created.”

“They are dead, not living, and they do not know when they will be resurrected.”

Likewise, He says later in the surah:

“Allah sets forth an example: an owned slave who has no power over anything, and one whom We have granted good provision from Us, from which he spends privately and openly. Are they equal? Praise be to Allah! But most of them do not know.”

“And Allah sets forth an example of two men: one is mute, powerless over anything, and a burden on his master; wherever he sends him, he brings no good. Is he equal to one who commands justice and is himself on a straight path?”

Thus He, glorified be He, makes clear that He alone deserves worship, rather than those worshipped besides Him

---

## Page 8127

and that none is like Him. He explains the attributes of perfection that belong exclusively to Him and their absence from those worshipped besides Him. He also makes clear that He is exalted above the partners they associate with Him and their claims that He has children and partners.

He says:

“Say: Had there been other gods alongside Him, as they claim, they would have sought a way to the Lord of the Throne.”

They used to say that these gods interceded for them and that through them they drew near to Him. Yet they affirmed intercession without His permission, thus giving created beings authority over intercession. This is a form of associating partners with Allah.

For this reason, He, exalted be He, says:

“Those they invoke besides Him have no authority over intercession.”

No one but Allah has authority over intercession. Thus Ibn Abī Hātim narrated from al-Suddī concerning His words:

“They would have sought a way to the Lord of the Throne.”

He explained: “The fulfillment of needs would be sought from Allah.”

Maʿmar narrated from Qatāda concerning:

“They would have sought a way to the Lord of the Throne.”

“They would have sought to draw near to Him, though matters are not as they claim.”

Saʿīd narrated from Qatāda concerning:

“Had there been other gods alongside Him, as they claim.”

He explained: “Had there been other gods alongside Him, they would have acknowledged His superiority and preeminence over them, and would have sought means of drawing near to Him.”

It is also related from Sufyān al-Thawrī:

“They would have sought to seize His sovereignty.”

And from Abū Bakr al-Hudhalī, from Saʿīd ibn Jubayr:

“A way to overthrow His dominion.”

Al-Hudhalī, however, is a weak narrator.

---

## Page 8128

Thus, the exaltedness with which He describes Himself in His Book includes His transcendence above partners and children, which are unbefitting of Him: there is nothing like Him. This entails that the attributes of perfection belong to Him alone, and that no other resembles Him in any attribute of perfection. Rather, He is far exalted above anything resembling Him. It also includes His being above everything else, subduing it, having power over it, and exercising His will within it; and His being above all things, over His Throne. These, then, are three meanings contained in His name “the Most High.”

Affirming His exaltedness—His being above everything else, His power over it, and His subjugation of it—entails His lordship over it and His having created it; this, in turn, necessarily entails His perfection. His transcendence above equals entails that none is like Him in His attributes of perfection. Together, these encompass everything affirmed and negated concerning Him. In affirmation, He is described with attributes of perfection; in negation, He is declared free of deficiencies incompatible with perfection, and free of having any equal in His attributes of perfection. Both are indicated by Sūrat al-Ikhlās:

“Say, ‘He is Allah, One.’”

“Allah, the Eternal Refuge.”

His transcendence above partners entails that He alone is divine, and that none deserves

---

## Page 8129

worship except Him alone, as He says:

“Say, ‘If there were other gods alongside Him, as they claim, they would have sought a way to the Lord of the Throne.’”

That is, even if, as they claim, these gods intercede with Him without His permission and bring you near to Him without His permission, He alone—not they—is the Lord and the true God. They would themselves seek a way to Him by worshipping Him and drawing near to Him. This is the sounder of the two interpretations.

As He says:

“Indeed, this is a reminder, so whoever wills may take a way to his Lord.”

“But you do not will unless Allah wills.”

And He says:

“Indeed, it is a reminder.”

“So whoever wills may heed it.”

And He says:

“Those whom they invoke themselves seek a means of drawing near to their Lord, each seeking to be closest.”

Then He says:

“Glory be to Him! He is far exalted above what they say!”

He is thus exalted above there being any other god alongside Him, anyone interceding with Him except by His permission, or anyone drawing near to Him except by His permission. This, then, was what they claimed. They did not claim that their gods could resist Him or overpower Him.

Rather, this would follow from supposing another god who creates as He creates, even though they themselves did not make such a claim. As He says:

“Allah has never taken a son, nor has there ever been any god alongside Him. Otherwise, each god would have taken away what he created, and some would have prevailed over others.”

It is thus clear that His name “the Most High” entails that He possesses every attribute of perfection, that He is free of all contrary attributes of deficiency and of having any equal, and that there is no god but Him and no Lord besides Him.

---

## Page 8130

Section

The command to glorify Him also entails declaring Him free of every fault and evil and affirming His attributes of perfection. Glorification entails declaring Him free of imperfection and magnifying Him; magnifying Him necessarily entails affirming the praiseworthy attributes for which He is praised. Thus, it entails declaring His perfection, praising Him, proclaiming His greatness, and affirming His oneness.

Ibn Abī Hātim said:

My father narrated to us from Ibn Nufayl al-Harrānī, who narrated to us from al-Nadr ibn ‘Arabī, who said: A man asked Maymūn ibn Mihrān about “Subhāna Allāh” (Glory be to Allah). He replied:

“An expression by which Allah is magnified and declared free of all evil.”

He also said:

Abū Sa‘īd al-Ashajj narrated to us from Hafs ibn Ghiyāth, from Hajjāj, from Ibn Abī Mulayka, from Ibn ‘Abbās, who explained “Subhān” (glory) as Allah’s declaring Himself free of all evil.

It is also related through al-Dahhāk that Ibn ‘Abbās said concerning His words:

“Glory be to Him who took His servant by night”:

It expresses wonder.

Abū al-Ashhab related that al-Hasan said:

“Subhān” is a name that people cannot claim for themselves.

An explanation similar to that of Ibn ‘Abbās has been reported from several of the early generations: that it is

---

## Page 8131

Allah’s declaring Himself free of evil.

A hadith reported directly from the Prophet by a Successor has also been transmitted to this effect. This entails declaring Him free of committing evil deeds, just as it entails declaring Him free of blameworthy attributes. Negating imperfections necessarily entails affirming the attributes of perfection, which include His greatness, as Maymūn ibn Mihrān said:

“An expression by which Allah is magnified and declared free of evil.”

‘Abd ibn Humayd narrated:

Abū Nu‘aym narrated to us from Sufyān, from ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mawhab, from Mūsā ibn Talha, who said:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was asked about glorifying Allah. He replied: ‘Declaring Him free of evil.’”

He also said: Al-Dahhāk ibn Makhlad narrated to us from Shabīb, from ‘Ikrima, from Ibn ‘Abbās, who explained “Subhāna Allāh” (Glory be to Allah) as declaring Him free of imperfection.

Kathīr ibn Hishām narrated to us from Ja‘far ibn Burqān, who narrated to us from Yazīd ibn al-Asamm, who said:

A man came to Ibn ‘Abbās and said: “‘There is no god but Allah’—we understand this to mean that there is no god other than Him. ‘Praise be to Allah’—we understand this to mean that all blessings come from Him and that He is praised for them. ‘Allah is the Greatest’—we understand this to mean that nothing is greater than Him. But what does ‘Glory be to Allah’ mean?”

Ibn ‘Abbās replied: “What is there to question about it? It is an expression that Allah has approved for Himself, commanded His angels to utter, and to which the righteous among His creation have turned for refuge.”

---

## Page 8132

Section

His words:

“Who created and proportioned,”
“and Who ordained and guided.”

Coordination entails that the two conjoined elements share in what is stated, while differing either in identity or in attributes.

Difference in identity is common, as in His words:

“Indeed, those who believe, those who are Jews, the Sabians, the Christians, the Magians, and those who associate partners with Allah.”

Difference in attributes, however, is exemplified by the verse under discussion. The One who created and proportioned is the very One who ordained and guided; yet the name and attribute expressed in the first description differ from those expressed in the second.

Another example is His statement:

“He is the First and the Last, the Most High and the Most Near.”

Likewise, His words:

“Those who believe in the unseen,”

through to His words:

“And those who believe in what has been revealed to you and what was revealed before you.”

And His words:

“But those among them who are firmly grounded in knowledge, and the believers, believe in what has been revealed to you and what was revealed before you—those who establish prayer, give zakah, and believe in Allah and the Last Day.”

And His words:

“Successful indeed are the believers,”
“those who are humble in their prayers,”
“and those who turn away from idle talk.”

And His words:

“Except those who pray,”
“those who are constant in their prayers.”

---

## Page 8133

“and those in whose wealth there is a known right,” and the subsequent verses.

And His words:

“Indeed, the Muslim men and Muslim women, the believing men and believing women,” and the subsequent verses.

For whoever is truthful and patient but neither submits to Allah nor believes is not among those for whom Allah has prepared forgiveness and a great reward.

Attributes often occur without conjunction, as in His words:

“He is Allah, other than whom there is no deity, the Sovereign, the Most Holy, the Source of Peace, the Giver of Security, the Guardian.”

And His words:

“Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of mankind,
the King of mankind,
the God of mankind.”

They may also occur as successive predicates, as in His words:

“And He is the All-Forgiving, the Most Loving,
Lord of the Throne, the Glorious,
Doer of whatever He wills.”

Were “Doer” an adjectival qualifier, it would be definite. Rather, it is another predicate following the preceding ones.

His words, “He is the First and the Last,” likewise contain successive predicates, but with each attribute joined by a conjunction. The predicates of a subject may occur with or without conjunctions. When conjunctions are used, each name is mentioned independently; without conjunctions, the second in some sense completes the first. With conjunctions, the attributes serve only for praise and commendation, or for praise. Without conjunctions, they serve to distinguish indefinite nouns and may serve to clarify definite nouns.

---

## Page 8134

“And who brings forth pasture.”

He is described by each of these attributes and praised and extolled for it. Each of these attributes warrants such praise.

Section

Allah Most High says:

“Who created and proportioned.”

He mentions creation and proportioning without restriction, rather than confining them to mankind. Likewise, His subsequent words, “And who determined and guided,” are unrestricted. This unrestricted wording therefore excludes no created thing from its scope.

Moses, peace be upon him, made its comprehensive scope clear when he said:

“Our Lord is He who gave each thing its form, then guided it.”

He also mentions this specifically in relation to mankind, saying:

“O man, what has deceived you concerning your Lord, the Generous,
who created you, proportioned you, and balanced you?”

He combines unrestricted wording with wording restricted to mankind in the first revelation of the Quran, namely His words:

“Recite in the name of your Lord who created—
created man from a clinging substance.
Recite, and your Lord is the Most Generous—
who taught by the pen—
taught man that which he knew not.”

In all these verses—those with unrestricted wording, those with restricted wording, and those combining both—He mentions His creation, followed by His guidance and teaching, just as He says in this surah:

“Who created and proportioned,
and who determined and guided.”

---

## Page 8135

because all created things were created for an intended end, and must therefore be guided to the end for which they were created. Their well-being and intended purpose can only be fulfilled through their being guided to their ends. This helps demonstrate that Allah created things for a wise purpose and an end that they attain, as affirmed by the early generations, the majority of Muslims, and the majority of rational people.

A group, however, including Jahm and his followers, maintained that He did not create anything for any purpose. Abū al-Hasan al-Ashʿarī agreed with him, as did his followers among the jurists affiliated with the imams. They affirm that He possesses a will, but deny that He has a wise purpose that He wills. Some adherents of philosophy affirm His providence and wisdom but deny His will. Both positions are self-contradictory. The falsity of their views has been discussed at length elsewhere, along with the fact that their positions ultimately lead to a denial of realities.

For the former says:

“If He had a wise purpose for which He acted, He would necessarily have to will that purpose and benefit from it; yet He is exalted above that.”

The latter says:

“If He had a will, He would act to obtain some benefit, for will cannot be conceived of otherwise.”

Aristotle and his followers say:

“If He performed any act, that act would be for a purpose; yet He is exalted above that.”

---

## Page 8136

These people may therefore be asked: “Do these observable occurrences have an originator or not?” If they say, “No,” this is the height of obstinate denial. If they allow occurrences to arise without an originator, they should all the more readily allow them to arise through an originator who has no will. If they say, “They have an originator,” then the existence of an agent is established.

Once the existence of the Creator who brings things into being is established, He must act either with will or without it. If they say, “He acts without will,” this too is obstinate denial, for every motion in the world ultimately proceeds from will. Motions are either natural, forced, or voluntary. The source of motion is either within the moving thing or an external cause. Motion originating within the moving thing is either accompanied by awareness or not. Motion whose cause is external is forced; motion whose cause is internal and unaccompanied by awareness is natural; and motion accompanied by awareness is voluntary.

Forced motion depends on whatever forces it. Whatever moves by its own nature—such as water, air, and earth—is at rest in its proper place. But when forcibly displaced from that place, it seeks to return to it; thus its motion originates in an external force. This leaves voluntary motion as the only primary kind of motion. Every motion in the world therefore proceeds from will. How, then, could all occurrences and motions arise without will?

Moreover, if they allow great occurrences to arise from an agent without will, then it is all the more possible for them to arise from an agent who has will.

---

## Page 8137

Once it is established that He has will, it may be said: He either willed these occurrences for a wise purpose or willed them without any wise purpose. If they say, “Without any wise purpose,” this is obstinate denial. For will is intelligible only when the one who wills acts for a wise purpose that he intends to achieve through his action. Moreover, if they allow that He acts with will but without a wise purpose, then His acting with will for a wise purpose is all the more possible.

As for their statement, “This is conceivable only of one who derives benefit, and that entails need, whereas Allah is far above such a thing”: if they mean that this entails His needing another, or anything among His creatures, that claim is rejected and false. Everything besides Him needs Him in every respect. He is the One upon whom all depend, free of need of everything besides Himself, while everything besides Him needs Him. He is the Self-Subsisting, who sustains everything besides Himself. How, then, could He need another?

If, however, they mean that through creation a wise purpose is realized for Him, which itself also comes about by His will, there is nothing objectionable in this; rather, it is the truth.

If they say, “The wise purpose is pleasure,” the response is: The term “pleasure” has not been used in revelation; it is potentially misleading and ambiguous. Revelation does, however, state that He “loves” and “is pleased”

---

## Page 8138

and “rejoices at the repentance of those who repent,” and similar expressions. If what is intended is the meaning established by revelation and reason, then it is true.

If they say, “A wise purpose must be intended either for its own sake or for another wise purpose,” the response is: Things intended are of two kinds: those intended for their own sake and those intended for the sake of something else. Something may be an end and a wise purpose in relation to one created thing, while itself being created for another wise purpose. The matter must therefore ultimately lead to a wise purpose that the agent intends for its own sake. The Muʿtazilah and those who agree with them, such as Ibn ʿAqīl and others, affirm a wise purpose that does not pertain to His own being. The early generations, however, affirm a wise purpose that pertains to Him, as has been explained elsewhere. The point here is to discuss His words, Most High:

“Who created and proportioned, and Who determined and guided.”

Proportioning means making two things equal, as He says:

“The blind and the seeing are not equal.”

And His words, Most High:

“Come to a word equitable between us and you.”

The word sawāʾ (“equitable”) denotes the middle, because it is evenly situated between the sides. This is because justice is indispensable in creation and command. Two things that are alike must therefore be treated equally; if one is given an advantage over the other, the resulting work is spoiled. The same applies to things people make: when they erect a building, its walls must be level with one another, for if one were to raise

---

## Page 8139

one wall far above another, the structure would be spoiled. The roof beams must likewise be evenly fitted: if some fell short of the required length while others exceeded it, the work would be spoiled. Similarly, when one course of masonry is laid upon another, the courses must be level, as must the steps of a staircase. When channels and watercourses are made for irrigation, they too must be properly balanced and level. Likewise, clothes made for people must be measured to fit their bodies, neither too large nor too small. Food must also have its ingredients combined in balanced proportions, and the heat used to cook it must likewise be moderate. The same applies to the construction of ships.

This is why Allah said to David:

“And make the links of mail in due proportion.”

That is, do not make the rivet so thin that it fits loosely, nor so thick that it causes cracking; rather, make it the proper size. If this applies to what human beings make—which is itself part of what the Lord makes—then how much more does it apply to His mighty creations in whose making human beings have no part, such as the creation of man and all other animals, plants, the heavens and the earth, and the angels?

The celestial sphere, which He created and made round, has no gaps, as He, Most High, says:

“He who created seven heavens, one above another. You will see no inconsistency in the creation of the Most Merciful. Look again: do you see any cracks?”

“Then look again and again; your sight will return to you humbled and weary.”

And He, Most High, says:

“By the heaven of intricate design.”

And He says:

---

## Page 8140

“Have they not looked at the heaven above them—how We built it and adorned it, leaving no rifts in it?”

He, glorified be He, fashioned it in due proportion, just as He fashioned the sun, the moon, and other created things, balancing their parts. If one side of the heaven protruded inward or outward, it would contain rifts—that is, openings and cracks—and He would not have fashioned it evenly. It would be like someone building a dome without making it even. The same would apply if He made one side longer or shorter, or anything similar. Balance and proportion are therefore necessary in all created and manufactured things. Whenever corresponding parts are not made with balance and evenness, the result is defective. He, glorified be He, is:

“He who created and proportioned.”

Commenting on His words, “created and proportioned,” Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: “He gave their creation due proportion.” This is like His saying, Most High:

“Then He completed them as seven heavens in two days.”

Section

Then, when He has created a creature and given it due proportion, if He does not guide it to the fulfillment of the wise purpose for which it was created, it will fall into ruin. It must therefore subsequently be guided to that for which it was created.

---

## Page 8141

And that end must be known to the Creator. For the final cause comes first in knowledge and will, but last in existence and realization. Thus, the Creator must know what He has created: He has willed it and willed the end for which He created it, and will necessarily entails knowledge. It is impossible for a living being to will something of which it has no awareness. When a craftsman intends to make something, he knows it and wills it, and has determined in his mind what he will make, the end it is to attain, and what will bring it to that end.

Allah, glorified be He, decreed and recorded the destinies of created beings before creating them, as is established in Sahīh Muslim, from ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Amr, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah decreed the destinies of created beings fifty thousand years before He created the heavens and the earth, and His Throne was upon the water.”

Al-Bukhārī also relates from ‘Imrān ibn Husayn that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah was, and there was nothing before Him. His Throne was upon the water. He wrote everything in the Record and created the heavens and the earth.”

In another version: “Then He created the heavens and the earth.”

---

## Page 8142

Thus, while His Throne was upon the water, He, glorified be He, decreed all that He intended to create in this world until the Day of Resurrection. As related in the Sunan collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When Allah first created the Pen, He said, ‘Write.’ It asked, ‘What shall I write?’ He said, ‘Write whatever will happen until the Day of Resurrection.’”

The hadiths concerning His decreeing and recording what He intends to create are very numerous.

Ibn Abī Hātim related that al-Dahhāk was asked about His words:

“Indeed, We have created all things according to a decree.”

He replied: “Ibn ‘Abbās said: Allah decreed all destinies by His power, ordered all affairs by His wisdom, and knew what His servants would ultimately become, what He would create, and what would happen among His creation. Accordingly, Allah created Paradise and Hell. He appointed Paradise for His devoted servants, granted them knowledge, loved them, took them into His care, enabled them to do what is right, and protected them. He left the people of Hell, and Iblīs gained mastery over them, led them astray, and caused them to stumble.

“For everything He created what suited its constitution and the provision that would sustain it, whether on land or at sea. He gave the camel a constitution no part of which would suit another kind of animal. Likewise, for every animal Allah created features suited to its constitution. His creation is thus harmonious, not at odds with the purpose for which He created it.”

Ibn Abī Hātim said: My father narrated to us: Yahyā ibn Zakariyyā ibn Mihrān al-Qazzāz narrated to us:

---

## Page 8143

Hibbān ibn ‘Ubayd Allāh narrated to us, saying: I asked al-Dahhāk about this verse:

“Indeed, We have created all things according to a decree.”

Al-Dahhāk replied by relating the foregoing account from Ibn ‘Abbās.

He said: Abū Sa‘īd al-Ashajj narrated to us: Talhah ibn Sinān narrated to us, from ‘Āsim, from al-Hasan, who said:

“Whoever denies divine decree has denied the truth. Allah created His creation, appointed its term, allotted its provision, and decreed calamity, trial, and well-being. Whoever disbelieves in divine decree has disbelieved in the Quran.”

He said: Al-Hasan ibn ‘Arafah narrated to us: Marwān ibn Shujā‘ al-Jazarī narrated to us, from ‘Abd al-Malik ibn Jurayj, from ‘Atā’ ibn Abī Rabāh, who said:

“I came to Ibn ‘Abbās while he was drawing water from Zamzam, and the lower parts of his clothes had become wet. I said to him, ‘People have begun discussing divine decree.’

“He said, ‘Have they really done so?’

“I replied, ‘Yes.’

“He said, ‘By Allah, this verse was revealed concerning none but them:

“‘Taste the touch of Hell.’

“‘Indeed, We have created all things according to a decree.’

“‘Those are the worst people of this community. Do not visit their sick or offer the funeral prayer over their dead. If I see any of them, I will gouge out his eyes with these two fingers of mine.’”

He also said: ‘Alī ibn al-Husayn ibn al-Junayd narrated to us: Sahl al-Khayyāt narrated to us: Abū Sālih al-Haddānī narrated to us: Hibbān ibn ‘Ubayd Allāh narrated to us, saying: I asked

al-Dahhāk about His words:

Section

His words, glorified is He,

“And who determined and guided,”

imply that He determined what was to be for created beings and guided them to it. He knew what provision people and animals would need, so He created that provision and fashioned it, and He created living creatures, fashioned them, and guided them to that provision. He also guided other living beings to bring that provision to them. He created the earth and determined its need for rain, and He determined the clouds and the rain they carry. He created angels whom He guided to drive those clouds to that land

---

## Page 8145

so that it pours down the rain He determined. He determined the provision that would grow there and His servants’ need for that provision. He guided them to it and guided those who would bring it to them.

The Quran commentators have mentioned various forms of His determination and guidance. Ibn Jarīr, Ibn Abī Hātim, and others narrated with a sound chain of transmission that Mujāhid said concerning His words,

“Determined and guided”:

“He determined mankind’s wretchedness and felicity, and guided livestock to their pastures.”

Likewise, ‘Abd ibn Humayd narrated in his commentary:

“He guided mankind to felicity and wretchedness, and guided livestock to their pastures.”

He also said: Yūnus narrated to us, from Shaybān, from Qatādah, concerning:

“Determined and guided”:

“No, by Allah! Allah has never compelled any servant to commit a sin or follow misguidance, nor has He approved it for him or commanded him to do so. Rather, He approved obedience for you and commanded you to obey Him, and forbade you to disobey Him.”

I say: Qatādah mentioned this in connection with this verse to make clear that Allah determined whatever He determined of felicity and wretchedness, as al-Hasan, Qatādah, and other leading Muslim scholars stated. They did not dispute the foregoing point concerning Allah’s prior determination. Some of them differed only concerning the divine will and the creation of actions.

---

## Page 8146

Only those whom the Companions—including Ibn ‘Umar, Ibn ‘Abbās, and others—disavowed disputed Allah’s prior determination and its recording. Qatādah stated that Allah has not coerced anyone into disobedience. This is correct: Ahl al-Sunnah, who affirm divine decree, agree that Allah does not coerce anyone into disobedience as a ruler, judge, or another person coerces someone into acting against his will through punishment and threats. Rather, He, glorified be He, creates the servant’s will to act, his ability, and his action. He is the Creator of all things.

This statement of Qatādah might be thought to reflect the views of the Qadariyyah, and statements such as this might be thought to explain why he was accused of holding Qadarī beliefs. It was even said that Mālik disapproved of Ma‘mar narrating Quran commentary from him because he had been accused of such beliefs. Yet the statement itself is true, and no one among the early generations is known to have said, “Allah coerced anyone into disobedience.” Indeed, they went further and prohibited using the term jabr (compulsion), as did al-Awzā‘ī, al-Thawrī, al-Zubaydī, ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Mahdī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and others. They forbade saying, “Allah compelled His servants,” and said that this was an innovation in religion and conveyed a false meaning.

---

## Page 8147

Al-Awzā‘ī and others said: “The Sunnah uses jabala (to endow with an innate disposition), not jabara (to compel).”

For the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said to al-Ashajj of ‘Abd al-Qays: “You possess two qualities that Allah loves: forbearance and deliberation.”

He asked: “Are these two qualities I have cultivated, or two qualities I was created with?”

He replied: “Rather, they are two qualities you were created with.”

He said: “Praise be to Allah, who created me with two qualities that Allah loves.”

Al-Zubaydī and others said: “Only the powerless are coerced,” meaning compulsion in the sense of coercion, as when a woman is forced into marriage. “Allah is far too exalted and great to coerce anyone,” meaning that He creates the servant’s will and therefore has no need to coerce him. Thus, al-Zubaydī and a group of others denied “compulsion,” understanding it in this sense.

Al-Awzā‘ī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and others, however, disapproved of saying either “He compelled” or “He did not compel,” because “compulsion” may mean coercion, and Allah does not coerce anyone. Yet it may also mean that He is the Creator of the will, as Muhammad ibn Ka‘b said: “Al-Jabbār (the Compeller) is the One who compels His servants to do what He wills.” “Compulsion,” understood in this sense, is correct.

Mujāhid’s explanation of His words, “He determined and then guided”—“He guided humankind to felicity and misery”—shows that, in his understanding, this falls within His words: “He determined and then guided.”

---

## Page 8148

that is, He guided the felicitous to the felicity He had decreed, and guided the wretched to the misery He had decreed.

Similarly, Ibn Abī Hātim narrated that, concerning His words, “And We showed him the two ways,” Mujāhid said: “Misery and felicity.”

## Page 8149

This statement is broadly worded and includes a point on which there is agreement: through the messengers He has sent, the proofs and signs He has established, and the intellects He has granted them, He makes clear to people the paths of good and evil, as in His words:

“As for Thamūd, We guided them.”

As for including guidance in the sense of inspiration—meaning that He guided the believer to believe and do righteous deeds, thereby attaining felicity, and guided the disbeliever to the deeds through which he becomes wretched—some, such as Mujāhid and others, include this in the verse’s meaning. They also include it in His words:

“Indeed, We guided him to the way.”

‘Ikrimah and others exclude this from the meaning of this verse, although they affirm divine decree.

According to those who understand “guided” to mean only “made clear,” He has guided every servant to both the path of good and the path of evil—that is, He has made both paths clear to him.

Those who include felicity and misery in its meaning say that a distinction is involved here: this guidance is general and shared by all, while He has singled out the believer for guidance to the path of good and the disbeliever for guidance to the path of evil.

Those who do not include this in the verse’s meaning may cite a hadith reported by al-Hasan directly from the Prophet without naming an intermediary. He said: “It was related to us that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to say:

‘O people, there are only two paths: the path of good and the path of evil. What, then, makes the path of evil dearer to you than the path of good?’”

---

## Page 8150

They also argue that He did not call inspiring the wicked with the path of wickedness “guidance,” but rather called it misguidance, whereas Allah mentions His guidance as a blessing.

Those holding the other view may reply: This does not fall under guidance in its unqualified sense, but it does fall under guidance in a qualified sense, as in His words:

“Guide them to the path of Hellfire.”

The same applies to the expression “glad tidings,” as He says:

“Give them tidings of a painful punishment.”

And to the word “faith,” as He says:

“They believe in superstition and false objects of worship.”

These same two interpretations apply to His words:

“And inspired it with its wickedness and its righteousness.”

Some say this means making both clear to everyone. Others say that He inspired the wicked with wickedness and the righteous with righteousness. The latter interpretation is more evident in that verse, because “inspiration” commonly refers to inspiration within hearts, not to the outward clarification by which proof is established. When Husayn al-Khuzā‘ī embraced Islam, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, taught him to say:

“O Allah, inspire me to right guidance and protect me from the evil of my own soul.”

If inspiration meant outward clarification, this would already be available to both Muslim and disbeliever.

Ibn ‘Atiyyah said:

“‘Fashioned in due proportion’ means that He made things well-balanced and perfected them, so that they became properly ordered, indicating His power and oneness. The majority of Quran reciters read qaddara (‘determined’) with a doubled d, so it may be

---

## Page 8151

derived from divine decree and ordainment, or from determining measures and balancing things against one another.”

I say:

The two are inseparable. The prior determination is called “determination” because whatever occurs thereafter accords with the measure thus set, matching and corresponding to it.

He said:

“Al-Kisā’ī alone read it with an undoubled d. It may then refer to power, or to determining measures and balancing things against one another.”

I say:

This is the view of the majority: the two readings have the same meaning.

Ibn ‘Atiyyah said:

“His words, ‘then guided,’ encompass all forms of guidance in human beings and animals. Some commentators have singled out particular forms of guidance. Al-Farrā’ said: ‘It means that He guided and led astray; mentioning one suffices, since it indicates the other.’”

He said:

“Muqātil and al-Kalbī said: ‘He guided males to mate with females.’ It has also been said: ‘He guided the newborn at birth to suckle at the breast.’ Mujāhid said: ‘He guided people to good and evil, and livestock to pastures.’”

Ibn ‘Atiyyah said:

“These interpretations are examples. It is more accurate to understand the verse generally, encompassing every determination and every form of guidance.”

Abū al-Faraj ibn al-Jawzī mentioned these interpretations and others, listing

---

## Page 8152

seven interpretations:

“He decreed felicity and wretchedness, and directed people to right guidance and error.” This was Mujāhid’s view.

It was also said: “He provided every creature with what benefits it and guided it to that.” This was ʿAtāʾ’s view.

It was also said: “He determined the duration of the fetus’s stay in the womb, then guided it to emerge.” This was al-Suddī’s view.

It was also said: “He determined them to be male and female, and guided males to mate with females.” This was Muqātil’s view.

It was also said: “He determined, then guided and led astray.” The words “and led astray” were omitted because the wording indicates them. Al-Zajjāj reported this interpretation.

It was also said: “He determined provisions and guided creatures to seek them.” Another interpretation was: “He decreed sins, then guided people to repentance.” Al-Thaʿlabī reported both interpretations.

I say:

The interpretation reported by al-Zajjāj is al-Farrāʾ’s view. It is of the same kind as the explanation, “whether it benefits or does not benefit,” and the explanation, “garments that protect you against heat and cold.” The weakness of such interpretations has already been discussed. This is why none of the commentators expressed this view.

The sound interpretations are illustrative examples, as Ibn ʿAtiyyah said. Much of the early generations’ Quranic commentary is of this kind: they mention an example of a general category to draw attention to other instances, because the listener needs to know that example, or because it is the example familiar to him. They mention such examples in many passages, including His words:

“You will be called upon to face a people of great military might,”

and His words:

“And others from among them,”

and His words:

“Allah will bring forth a people whom He loves and who love Him,”

and His words:

“Among them are those who wrong themselves, those who take a middle course, and those who are foremost in good deeds.”

---

## Page 8153

and likewise their explanations of:

“By the even and the odd,”

and

“By the witness and what is witnessed,”

and other passages, and of His words:

“And in yourselves—will you not then see?”

and similar passages. Much of their commentary consists of illustrative examples.

This also includes their statement, “This verse was revealed concerning so-and-so.” They thereby give as an example the person concerning whom it was revealed—the one concerning whom it was first revealed and who occasioned its revelation. They do not mean that the verse applies exclusively to that person. Examples include the verse on mutual imprecation, the verse on accusations of unchastity, the verse on waging war, and others like them. No Muslim says that these apply exclusively to those who occasioned their revelation. Although some scholars say that general wording is restricted to the occasion that prompted it, they mean the kind of case that occasioned it, not that it is restricted to a single individual belonging to that category.

No Muslim says that the verse on zihār applies only to Aws ibn al-Sāmit, that the verse on mutual imprecation applies only to Āsim ibn Adī or Hilāl ibn Umayyah, or that the condemnation of disbelievers applies only to the disbelievers of Quraysh. Neither a Muslim nor any rational person would say such things. For it is necessarily known from the religion of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that he was sent to all humans and jinn, and through the Quran Allah Most High has addressed all

---

## Page 8154

humans and jinn, as He says:

“That I may warn you through it, and whoever it reaches.”

Thus, every human or jinn whom the Quran reaches has been warned through it by the Messenger. Warning means giving notice of something to be feared: the punishment that befalls whoever disobeys his commands and prohibitions. He has therefore informed everyone whom the Quran reaches that, unless they obey him, Allah Most High will punish them, and that, if they obey him, Allah Most High will honor them. Now that he has died, obedience to him consists in following what the Quran contains of Allah’s obligations and prohibitions, as well as what the Messenger made obligatory or prohibited through his Sunnah. For the Quran has made clear that obedience to him is obligatory and that Allah sent down to him the Book and wisdom. He said to His Prophet’s wives:

“And remember what is recited in your homes of Allah’s verses and wisdom.”

Section

Then He said:

“And Who brings forth pasture,”

“Then makes it dark stubble.”

When He, glorified is He, said, “He ordained and guided,” this included the provision He ordained for His servants and the animals, and His guiding them to it. Thus, He guided those who bring it to them.

This is part of the fullness of His favor toward His servants, as related in the tradition:

“Allah says:

---

## Page 8155

‘Indeed, a momentous state of affairs exists between Me and the jinn and mankind: I create, yet they worship others besides Me; I provide, yet they thank others besides Me.’” This meaning has also been related in explanation of His words:

“And in return for your provision, you deny the truth.”

That is, you make your gratitude and your thanks to your Lord consist in denying Allah’s favor and attributing provision to others besides Him, such as the stars. Thus, it is authentically reported from Ibn ‘Abbās that he said:

“Rain fell upon the people during the Prophet’s lifetime, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘This morning, some people are grateful, while others show disbelief.’ Some said, ‘This is Allah’s mercy,’ while others said, ‘Such-and-such star has indeed proved true.’ Then this verse was revealed: ‘I swear by the positions of the stars,’ continuing through to: ‘And in return for your provision, you deny the truth.’”

In Sahīh Muslim, Abū Hurayrah reports that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whenever Allah sends down a blessing from heaven, some people greet the morning disbelieving in it. Allah sends down rain, and they say, ‘Such-and-such star.’” In another version: “By such-and-such star.”

Ibn al-Mundhir narrated in his Tafsīr:

“Muhammad ibn ‘Alī—meaning al-Sā’igh—narrated to us; Sa‘īd—that is, Ibn Mansūr—narrated to us; Hushaym narrated to us, from Abū Bishr, from Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, from Ibn ‘Abbās, that he used to recite:

‘And in place of gratitude, you deny the truth,’

---

## Page 8156

meaning the stars. Whenever a people received rain, some of them would greet the morning in disbelief. They used to say, ‘We have been given rain by such-and-such a star,’ so Allah revealed:

‘And in return for your provision, you deny the truth.’”

Ibn Abī Hātim narrated from ‘Atā’ al-Khurāsānī, from ‘Ikrimah, concerning Allah’s words:

“And in return for your provision, you deny the truth.”

He said: “You attribute your provision to someone other than Allah, denying the truth and giving thanks to others besides Him.”

But in His words:

“Who brings forth the pasture,”

He specifically mentions bringing forth pasture—that on which animals graze—and states that He makes it blackened stubble. This mentions the sustenance of animals, yet human sustenance is more important than that. Both human and animal sustenance are included in His words:

“He ordained and guided.”

Moreover, animals do not feed on what has become blackened stubble; they feed on it only before that stage. He therefore—and Allah knows best—singled this out for mention because it is a likeness of the life of this world. For this surah encompasses the foundations of faith: faith in Allah and the Last Day, and faith in the messengers and the scriptures they brought, which also entails faith in the angels. It also mentions righteous deeds, which benefit one in the Hereafter, and corrupt deeds, which harm one there.

---

## Page 8157

Thus He, glorified be He, mentioned pasture after speaking of creation and guidance, to show the ultimate fate of some created things and that this illustrates the life of this world. Allah has also mentioned this in Surahs al-Kahf, Yūnus and al-Hadīd.

He, Most High, says:

“Set forth for them the likeness of the life of this world: it is like water We send down from the sky, by which the earth’s vegetation grows luxuriantly, only to become dry stubble scattered by the winds. Allah has power over all things.”

He, Most High, also says:

“The likeness of the life of this world is that of water We send down from the sky, by which the earth’s vegetation grows luxuriantly, providing food for people and livestock. Then, just as the earth takes on its adornment and is beautified, and its people think they have power over it, Our command comes upon it by night or by day, and We leave it like a field mown down, as though it had not flourished the day before. Thus We explain the signs in detail for people who reflect.”

“Allah invites to the Home of Peace and guides whom He wills to a straight path.”

He, Most High, also says:

“Know that the life of this world is only play, diversion, adornment, mutual boasting, and rivalry in wealth and children. It is like rain whose growth delights the farmers; then it withers and you see it turn yellow, and then it becomes debris. In the Hereafter there is severe punishment, and forgiveness from Allah and His good pleasure. The life of this world is nothing but deceptive enjoyment.”

He has also likened the destruction of those He destroyed to their being reaped, saying: “That is among the accounts of

---

## Page 8158

the towns We relate to you; some are still standing, while others have been mown down.” He also says:

“We have certainly created man in the best of forms, then We reduced him to the lowest of the low, except those who believe and do righteous deeds, for they will have an uninterrupted reward.”

Thus His words:

“And Who brings forth pasture, then turns it into darkened stubble”

are a likeness of the life of this world and the eventual fate of the disbelievers and those deceived by worldly life. They enjoy comfort, adornment and happiness, then come to misery in this world and the Hereafter, like the pasture He turns into darkened stubble.

Section

His words:

“So remind, if the reminder benefits. Those who fear will heed the reminder, but the most wretched will avoid it—he who will enter the greatest Fire.”

His words, “if the reminder benefits,” are like His words, “Indeed, the reminder benefits the believers.”

In His words, “if the reminder benefits,” the particle in (“if”) is conditional.

---

## Page 8159

Al-Māwardī also reported that it means mā. Here mā is a nominalizing particle used in a temporal sense: that is, “Remind for as long as it benefits—as long as it continues to benefit.” Its meaning is close to that of the conditional particle. But if anyone supposes that it is a negative particle, this is plainly mistaken. Allah does not deny that reminders are beneficial altogether, for He says:

“So turn away from them, for you are not to blame. And remind, for the reminder benefits”

—then He says, “the believers.” … (1).
