It is reported from … (2) that “So remind, if the reminder benefits” means “if the reminder meets with acceptance.” Muqātil explained it as: “So remind, for the reminder has indeed benefited.”

Another interpretation is: “Remind if the reminder benefits, and even if it does not.” This was the view of a group, beginning with al-Farrāʾ, whom several others followed, including al-Nahhās, al-Zahrāwī, al-Wāhidī and al-Baghawī. The latter mentioned no other interpretation.

They said that the second case was simply left unstated, as in His words, “garments to protect you from the heat,” where He meant both heat and cold. They offered this interpretation because they knew that he was obliged to convey the message to all people and remind them, whether they believed or disbelieved. Thus the obligation to remind was not confined to those whom

> **Footnotes:**
> (1, 2) Blank in the original.

---

## Page 8160

the reminder would benefit, as He says in another verse:

“So remind; you are only a reminder. You are not a controller over them.”

And He says:

“It is indeed a reminder for you and your people, and you will be questioned.”

And He says:

“And they say, ‘He is surely mad.’ Yet it is nothing but a reminder to all worlds.”

And He says:

“So that he may be a warner to all worlds.”

What they have said conveys a sound meaning. This is the view of al-Farrā’ and others like him, but none of the Quran commentators among the early generations expressed it. This is why Ahmad ibn Hanbal objected to certain statements made by al-Farrā’ and others like him, saying: “I used to think al-Farrā’ was a righteous man until I saw his book on the meanings of the Quran.”

The meaning they have stated is indicated by other verses and is an indisputable fact of the Messenger’s mission: Allah sent him to convey the message and give reminders to all humankind and jinn. But this is not the meaning of this verse. Rather, its meaning resembles His words:

“So remind with the Quran those who fear My warning.”

And His words:

“You are only a warner to those who fear it.”

And His words:

“You can only warn those who follow the reminder and fear the Most Merciful without seeing Him.”

And His words:

“It is nothing but a reminder to all worlds—for whoever among you wishes to follow the straight path.”

Thus, the Quran speaks in both general and particular terms. This is like His words:

“A guidance for the God-fearing,”

and other such passages.

---

## Page 8161

The reason for this is that teaching, reminding, warning, guidance, and similar activities involve both someone who acts and someone who receives. A person teaches and reminds another, but that other person may or may not learn and take heed. If he learns and takes heed, the teaching and reminding have been fully accomplished. If he neither learns nor takes heed, only one side of the process has been realized: action by the agent, without reception by the recipient.

In such a case, one may say: “I taught him, but he did not learn; I reminded him, but he did not take heed; I commanded him, but he did not obey.” Yet one may also say, “I did not teach him or remind him,” because the process was not fully accomplished and did not achieve its intended purpose. Thus, it is denied because it lacked completeness and failed to benefit the listener being addressed, even though the speaker delivering the address did benefit.

Accordingly, wherever believers are singled out as recipients of reminders, warnings, and the like, what is exclusive to them is the complete, beneficial form through which they attained felicity. Wherever the wording is general, everyone shares in receiving the warning through which the case is established against creation, whether they accept it or not.

This is the guidance mentioned in His words:

“As for Thamūd, We guided them, but they preferred blindness to guidance.”

Guidance here means clarification, showing the way, and direction offered generally to all. It is like warning and reminding in their general sense. In this sense, He has guided both the God-fearing and others, as He says:

“For every people there is a guide.”

As for His words:

“Guide us to the straight path,”

what is sought is the particular guidance

---

## Page 8162

in its complete form, through which one is truly guided, as in His words:

“A guidance for the God-fearing.”

And His words:

“Some He has guided, while others have deserved to go astray.”

And His words:

“Indeed, Allah does not guide those He leaves astray.”

And His words:

“Through it Allah guides those who seek His pleasure to the ways of peace.”

This occurs frequently in the Quran.

The same applies to warning. He says:

“We have made it easy in your tongue only so that you may give glad tidings thereby to the God-fearing and warn thereby a fiercely contentious people.”

And He, Most High, says:

“Is it astonishing to people that We have revealed to a man from among them: ‘Warn mankind and give glad tidings to those who believe’?”

Regarding warning in its particular sense, He says:

“You are only a warner to those who fear it.”

“You can only warn those who follow the Reminder and fear the Most Merciful without seeing Him.”

This is the particular warning: the complete, beneficial warning from which the one warned has benefited. Warning means informing someone of what is to be feared. Thus, he learns what is to be feared, becomes afraid, believes, and obeys.

Likewise, reminding has both a general and a particular sense. In its general sense, it means conveying the message to everyone. This is accomplished by communicating to them the message with which the Messenger was sent.

He, Most High, says:

“Say: ‘I ask you for no reward for this, nor am I one of the pretentious. It is only a reminder to all peoples.’”

And He, Most High, says:

“It is only a reminder to mankind.”

And He, Most High, says:

“It is only a reminder to all peoples.”

Then He says:

“For whoever among you wishes to follow the straight path.”

Thus, He mentions both the general and the particular.

---

## Page 8163

And taking heed is remembrance in its fullest sense: the person who receives the reminder remembers it and benefits from it. Of others, Allah, Most High, says:

“No new reminder comes to them from their Lord but they listen to it in play, their hearts distracted.”

And He, Most High, says:

“No new reminder comes to them from the Most Merciful but they turn away from it.”

Thus, the reminder has reached them and established the proof against them. Yet they have not listened to it with their hearts, so they have not understood it; or they have understood it but have not acted upon it. As He says:

“Had Allah known any good in them, He would have made them hear. But even if He had made them hear, they would have turned away in aversion.”

Reminding in its particular sense is complete and beneficial: it results in the person reminded taking heed. This is the reminder referred to in His words:

“So remind, if the reminder benefits. Those who fear will take heed, but the most wretched will avoid it.”

That is, he is kept from the reminder. Yet it is only the reminder in its particular sense that he is kept from. As for the reminder common to all, through which the proof is established, he and others have been reminded by it, and the proof has been established against them.

Allah, Most High, says:

“We never punish until We have sent a messenger.”

And He says:

“So that people may have no argument against Allah after the messengers.”

And He says of the people of the Fire:

“Whenever a group is cast into it, its keepers ask them, ‘Did no warner come to you?’ They say, ‘Yes, a warner did come to us, but we denied him and said: Allah has sent down nothing.’”

And He, Most High, says:

“O company of jinn and mankind, did there not come to you messengers from among yourselves

---

## Page 8164

relating My verses to you and warning you of the meeting of this Day of yours? They will say, ‘We testify against ourselves.’”

As for their illustrating this with His words,

“Garments that protect you from the heat,”

meaning, “and protect you from the cold,” there are two answers.

First, unlike the passage under discussion, this contains no conditional particle upon which the ruling depends. If a command is made conditional, yet the action is commanded just as much when the condition is absent as when it is present, mentioning the condition would lengthen the discourse, diminish its benefit, and mislead the listener.

The majority hold that the contrary implication of a condition constitutes evidence. Those who dispute this say that the wording leaves unaddressed the circumstances outside the stated condition. They do not say that the wording indicates what is left unstated just as it indicates what is explicitly stated. No one maintains that.

Second, His words,

“Protect you from the heat,”

retain their ordinary meaning; the verse makes no mention of cold. It is al-Farrā’ and others like him who say that “a coordinated term has been omitted.” The leading scholars criticized them for interpreting the Quran solely on the basis of their own conjecture and their understanding of some aspect of Arabic linguistic scholarship. Often, their interpretations do not fit the text. Nothing in the wording indicates any mention of cold. Yet Allah mentions in

---

## Page 8165

this surah His favors upon His servants. It is called the “Surah of Blessings.” At its beginning, He mentions the essential blessings that are indispensable and without which life cannot be sustained; later in the surah, He mentions the blessings that complete them. Protection from cold is among the essential blessings, so He mentions it at the beginning of the surah in His words:

“And He created livestock for you; in them you have warmth and other benefits.”

Warmth is that which keeps one warm and wards off cold. Severe cold causes death, unlike heat. Many people have died from cold, whereas death from heat is uncommon. Hence some Arabs have said, “Cold is misery; heat is discomfort.”

When, later in the surah, He mentions the blessings that complete His favors, He mentions shade and protection from heat, as well as weapons and protection from being killed. He says:

“Allah has provided you with shade from what He has created, and has provided you with shelters in the mountains. He has provided you with garments that protect you from the heat and garments that protect you in battle. Thus He completes His favor upon you, so that you may submit to Him.”

He states that He completes His favor, as He has explained in these verses, saying:

“Thus He completes His favor upon you, so that you may submit to Him.”

There is a distinction between shade and shelters, for shade is provided by trees

---

## Page 8166

and the like, which provide shade but not shelter, unlike caves in the mountains, which provide both shade and shelter.

This concerns places. Then, regarding clothing, He says:

“He has provided you with garments that protect you from the heat and garments that protect you in battle.”

This concerns clothing. Clothing and dwellings both protect people from the harm of heat, cold, and enemies, and both conceal them from the eyes of onlookers. Houses, in particular, provide them with rest, as He says:

“Allah has made your homes a place of rest for you, and has made for you dwellings from the hides of livestock, which you find light to carry when you travel and when you encamp.”

When He mentions the houses in which people live, He reminds them of His favor in making them places where they can rest from the weariness of movement. He also mentions that He has provided them with other dwellings that they carry with them and find light to carry when they travel and when they encamp. Thus He mentions both the heavy dwellings that cannot be carried and the light ones that can. It is therefore clear that the example they cited is evidence against them.

Thus His words,

“if the reminder is beneficial,”

retain their ordinary conditional meaning, as the early exegetes and the majority have stated. Al-Hasan al-Basrī said:

“A reminder for the believer and evidence against the disbeliever.”

---

## Page 8167

Accordingly, His words, Most High,

“if the reminder is beneficial,”

do not preclude conveying the Quran to a disbeliever, for several reasons.

First, He did not single out some people to the exclusion of others. Rather, He said:

“So remind.”

This is an unrestricted command to remind everyone. In saying,

“if the reminder is beneficial,”

He did not say, “if it benefits everyone.” Rather, He left the benefit unrestricted. Thus, He commanded that reminders be given if they bring benefit, and reminding people generally does bring benefit. Some people take heed and benefit from it; against others, the proof is established, and they deserve punishment on that account. They then become a lesson for others, so reminding them also brings benefit. Moreover, reminding them establishes the proof against them, making it permissible thereafter to punish them through jihad and other means. Thus, the reminder brings benefit.

Every reminder that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, gave the polytheists therefore brought some benefit, even if that benefit was for the believers who accepted it, took heed of it, and fought the polytheists against whom the proof had been established.

If it is asked: “On this basis, every reminder has brought some benefit. What purpose, then, does the qualification serve?”

---

## Page 8168

The reply is:

Rather, some reminders have brought no benefit at all: those that were not commanded. Such is the case with someone whom Allah has declared will not believe, such as Abū Lahab. After Allah revealed His words,

“He will burn in a Fire of blazing flame,”

he is not to be singled out for reminders; rather, the Prophet is to turn away from him. Likewise, he is to turn away from anyone who pays him no heed and does not listen to his words, as Allah said:

“So turn away from them; you are not to blame.”

Then He said:

“And remind, for the reminder benefits the believers.”

Thus, when he has conveyed the message to a people and the proof has been established against them, but they then refuse to listen to his words, he turns away from them. At that point, reminding them benefits no one. Likewise, when someone makes it plain that the proof has been established against him and that he will not accept guidance, the Prophet does not repeatedly convey the message to him.

The second point:

The command to remind calls for a full and beneficial reminder, just as it calls for the general reminder addressed to all. This full and beneficial reminder is specifically for the believers who benefit from it. Once they believe, he reminds them of what has been revealed. Whenever any part of the Quran is revealed, he reminds them of it, explains its meanings to them, and reminds them of what was revealed before.

This is unlike those of whom Allah said:

“What is the matter with them that they turn away from the reminder, as though they were

---

## Page 8169

startled wild donkeys fleeing from a lion.”

He does not remind such people as he reminds the believers, when the proof has already been established against them and they turn away from the reminder, refusing to listen. Hence Allah said:

“He frowned and turned away because the blind man came to him. Yet how could you know? Perhaps he might purify himself, or take heed and benefit from the reminder. As for him who considers himself self-sufficient, you give him your attention, though you are not accountable if he does not purify himself. But as for him who comes to you earnestly, fearing Allah, you neglect him.”

Thus, He commanded him to attend to those who came to him seeking purification and wishing to take heed.

He also said:

“He who fears will take heed,”

through to His words:

“He who purifies himself has certainly succeeded.”

Here He mentioned taking heed and purification, just as He mentioned them in the other passage. He commanded him to attend to those who turned towards him rather than those who turned away from him, for the former benefit from the reminder, unlike the latter.

He is therefore commanded to give those who benefit from the reminder a reminder specifically addressed to them, distinct from the general conveyance of the message by which the proof is established. As Allah said:

“So turn away from them; you are not to blame. And remind, for the reminder benefits the believers.”

He also said:

“Do not recite too loudly in your prayer or too quietly, but seek a way between.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Ibn ‘Abbās said: “When the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recited the Quran, the polytheists would hear him and revile the Quran, the one to whom it was revealed, and the one who brought it. Allah therefore said to him: ‘Do not recite it aloud, lest the polytheists hear it, and do not recite it too quietly

---

## Page 8170

for your Companions to hear it.’” He thus forbade him to let them hear it in a way that would cause more harm than benefit. Likewise, everything Allah commands must have benefits that outweigh its harms. Benefit is what is meant by good, and harm is what is meant by detriment. He is therefore commanded to remind only when the good predominates—that is, when the reminder brings about a benefit that outweighs its harm. This supports both the first and second points. Wherever harm predominates, he is forbidden to do what brings about that greater harm.

Benefit is not confined to everyone accepting the reminder: some accepting it is a benefit; establishing the proof against those who do not accept it is a benefit; his words becoming widely known and reaching those far away is a benefit; and their remaining with those who heard them until they convey them to those who have not heard them is a benefit. Thus, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, never gave any reminder except one that was beneficial. He never gave a reminder whose harm outweighed its benefit.

This is the position of the majority of Muslims, both earlier and later generations: whatever Allah commands must have a preponderance of good and benefit. As for anything whose harm outweighs its benefit, Allah does not command it.

As for Jahm and those of the Jabriyyah (determinists) who agree with him, they say that Allah may command something that contains no benefit or good whatsoever, but is instead purely harmful if carried out by

---

## Page 8171

the person commanded to do it. A group of later followers of the imams agreed with them on this. They followed the approach of the speculative theologians—Abū al-Hasan al-Ash‘arī and others—in matters of divine decree, thus championing the position of Jahm and the Jabriyyah.

The third point:

His words “the reminder” encompass both taking heed and giving a reminder. For He says:

“So remind, if the reminder benefits.”

This must therefore include the Prophet’s act of reminding. Then He says:

“Whoever fears Allah will take heed,”

“But the most wretched will avoid it.”

What the most wretched avoids is what the one who fears Allah does: taking heed. Thus, the pronoun referring to the reminder here encompasses taking heed. Otherwise, no one has avoided the mere act of reminding through which the proof was established.

However, avoiding it may mean that he did not listen or pay attention to it, as He says:

“Do not listen to this Quran, and make noise during its recitation.”

The proof was established by the presence of the Messenger conveying the message and their ability to listen and reflect, not by their actually listening. Among the disbelievers are those who avoided listening to the Quran and chose something else instead, just as many Muslims avoid listening to the statements of the People of the Book and others. They benefit only when they are reminded and then take heed, as He says:

“Whoever fears Allah will take heed.”

Thus, when He says:

“So remind, if the reminder benefits,”

“the reminder” may mean the very act of

---

## Page 8172

reminding, whether or not the hearer takes heed. His reminding is undoubtedly beneficial, as explained above, and this accords with the first interpretation.

Some have mentioned that this is intended to rebuke those of Quraysh who did not take heed. Ibn ‘Atiyyah said:

> People have differed over the meaning of His words:
>
> “So remind, if the reminder benefits.”
>
> Al-Farrā’, al-Nahhās and al-Zahrāwī said that it means “and even if it does not benefit.” Only one expression was mentioned because it indicates the other.
>
> He continued: Some discerning scholars have said that His words,
>
> “if the reminder benefits,”
>
> are a parenthetical remark between the two statements, intended to rebuke Quraysh—that is, if the reminder benefits these rebellious, defiant people. This resembles the poet’s words:
>
> You would have made yourself heard, had you called one alive—
> But there is no life in the one you call.
>
> All this is like saying to a man, “Speak to so-and-so and admonish him—if he will listen to you.” This is simply a rebuke to the person referred to.

I say: The person who said this is al-Zamakhsharī. This view contains some truth, but in another respect it is weaker than the other view. For it implies that he is commanded to remind those who neither accept nor benefit from the reminder, rather than those who accept it—as expressed in the words, “if the reminder benefits these rebellious, defiant people,” and in the verse of poetry he quoted.

---

## Page 8173

Moreover, the line he quoted is a statement about someone who addressed another. He says:

“You would have made yourself heard, had the one you called been alive.”

This is like His words:

“Indeed, those who disbelieve—it is all the same to them whether you warn them or do not warn them—they will not believe.”

And His words:

“Indeed, you cannot make the dead hear, nor can you make the deaf hear the call when they turn their backs and retreat.”

And His words:

“Say, ‘I warn you only by revelation.’ But the deaf do not hear the call when they are warned.”

These passages accord with the meaning of the poetic line: it is a statement concerning a particular case. The command to warn, however, is unrestricted and general. Even if it were restricted, believers would have a stronger claim to being singled out, as He says:

“So remind with the Quran those who fear My warning.”

And He says:

“And remind, for the reminder benefits the believers.”

The command is not confined to those who do not listen. How could it be, when He says immediately afterwards:

“He who fears will take heed.”

“But the most wretched will avoid it.”

The one who fears is among those whom He commanded him to remind, and he benefits from the reminder. How, then, can the sole purpose of this conditional clause be to censure those who do not listen?

As for someone saying, “Speak to so-and-so and admonish him, if he will listen to you,” people use this and similar expressions concerning someone whom they think will not accept what is said, but whom they nevertheless hope will accept it. They intend to rebuke him if he rejects it, not if he accepts it. Thus they say:

“Speak to him, if he will listen to you,” and “Speak to him, if he will accept it,” and “Advise him, if

---

## Page 8174

he will accept advice.” All such expressions fall into this category. They command full counsel that is accepted, if he will accept it, while also commanding that counsel be offered even if he rejects it, and censuring him in the latter case.

Likewise, His words:

“So remind, if the reminder benefits.”

He commands that everyone be reminded. If a person benefits, his taking heed is complete and beneficial. Otherwise, the basic act of reminding has taken place, establishing the proof against him and showing that he deserves blame and rebuke.

Moreover, He, glorified be He, only said:

“If the reminder benefits.”

He did not say, “Remind only those whom the reminder benefits,” as in His words:

“So remind with the Quran those who fear My warning.”

There, the command to remind is specific. It has come in both general and specific forms, just as the Quran addresses people with “O mankind,” which is general, and “O you who believe,” which is specific to those who believe in the Quran.

There He said:

“For the reminder benefits the believers.”

Here He said:

“He who fears will take heed.”

“But the most wretched will avoid it.”

He did not say, “He who fears will benefit.” For the benefit attained through reminding is broader than the taking heed of those who fear. When he reminds, the proof is established against everyone. As for the most wretched, who avoids the reminder, reminding him establishes the proof against him and makes him deserving of punishment in this world and the Hereafter.

---

## Page 8175

And in this Allah has wise purposes and benefits that are blessings for His servants. Everything Allah Most High decrees is a blessing He bestows upon His servants. Thus, after enumerating what He mentions, He says:

“So which of your Lord’s blessings will you both deny?”

After mentioning what He relates in Sūrat al-Najm, including the destruction of those who denied the messengers, He says:

“Then which of your Lord’s blessings do you doubt?”

Their destruction is therefore among our Lord’s blessings. His blessings testify to His mercy, wisdom, will, power, and lordship—glorified and exalted is He.

Among the benefits of reminding someone who avoids the reminder is that, once the proof has been established against him and he deserves punishment, the harm suffered by the believers is thereby lessened. For Allah destroys such people through punishment from Himself or at the believers’ hands. Through such a person’s destruction, faith triumphs and spreads, and others take heed—a tremendous benefit. His death is also hastened, leaving him less time in disbelief. Allah sent Muhammad as a mercy to all worlds, and through him mercy reaches everyone to the extent possible.

Moreover, by avoiding the reminder, such a person deserves the punishment threatened here, and this becomes a warning to others against doing as he did. The Exalted says:

“So We made it a deterrent for those present and for those who came after them.”

And the Exalted says concerning Pharaoh: “So We made them

---

## Page 8176

a precedent and an example for later generations.” And the Exalted says:

“In their stories there is certainly a lesson for people of understanding.”

Section

His words:

“Whoever fears will take heed,”

imply that everyone who fears will take heed. Fear may arise after the reminder or before it. His words “whoever fears” are unrestricted. Some people suppose this means that a person must first have experienced fear in order to take heed, but that is not so.

Rather, this is like His words:

“A guidance for the God-fearing,”

and His words:

“You are only a warner to those who fear it,”

and His words:

“So remind with the Quran those who fear My warning,”

and His words:

“You only warn those who follow the Reminder and fear the Most Merciful without seeing Him.”

A person only feared the warning after hearing it; he had received no such warning before hearing the Quran. Likewise, His words:

“You only warn those who follow the Reminder and fear the Most Merciful without seeing Him.”

A person only followed the Reminder and feared the Most Merciful after the Messenger had warned him. They may not have feared it before the warning, nor have been God-fearing before hearing

---

## Page 8177

the Quran; rather, it was through it that they became God-fearing.

This is like someone saying, “Only the blessed, the successful, or those with whom Allah is pleased listen to this,” or, “Only those whom Allah has guided embrace Islam,” and similar statements—even though these virtues and blessings come after embracing Islam and hearing the Quran.

Likewise, He says:

“This is enlightenment for mankind, and guidance and mercy for people who have certainty.”

In the corresponding passage, He says:

“But the most wretched will avoid it.”

Yet it is by avoiding it that a person becomes wretched.

This is like saying, “Only those who accept a warning are warned,” and, “Only those who act upon knowledge benefit from it.” Whoever listens to the Quran, believes in it, and acts upon it becomes one of the God-fearing for whom it is guidance. Whoever neither believes in it nor acts upon it is not among the God-fearing, nor among those guided by it.

Rather, it is as Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘It is guidance and healing for those who believe. As for those who do not believe, there is deafness in their ears, and it is blindness for them.’”

He did not mean that they were already believers and that, when they heard it, it became guidance and healing. Rather, when a disbeliever hears it and believes in it, it becomes guidance and healing for him, and he becomes one of those who believe in it after hearing it.

---

## Page 8178

And this is like His words concerning the blameworthy category:

“Through it He leads many astray and guides many, but He leads none astray through it except the defiantly disobedient, who break Allah’s covenant after it has been solemnly confirmed and sever what Allah has commanded to be joined.”

It is not necessary that they should have been defiantly disobedient before going astray. Rather, whoever hears it and rejects it becomes defiantly disobedient and goes astray. Sa'd ibn Abī Waqqās and others included followers of deviant inclinations, such as the Khawārij, among those described in this verse.

Sa'd used to say, “They are among ‘the defiantly disobedient, who break Allah’s covenant after it has been solemnly confirmed and sever what Allah has commanded to be joined.’” Yet neither 'Alī nor Sa'd nor other Companions regarded them as disbelievers.

Sa'd included them in this verse because of His words:

“But He leads none astray through it except the defiantly disobedient.”

They went astray through it because they distorted words from their proper places and interpreted it contrary to what Allah intended. They clung to its ambiguous passages and turned away from its clear passages and from the established Sunnah, which explains what Allah intends in His Book. Thus, they opposed the Sunnah and the consensus of the Companions, in addition to opposing the clear passages of Allah Most High’s Book. This is why many of the early generations included them among those who:

“Follow what is ambiguous in it, seeking discord and seeking its interpretation,”

and those:

“Who divided their religion and became sects.”

A detailed discussion of this belongs elsewhere. The point here concerns the verse, which indicates that everyone who fears Allah must

---

## Page 8179

take heed. A person may take heed and thereby come to fear Allah, or he may fear Allah and be prompted by that fear to take heed.

Qatādah expressed this meaning when he said: “By Allah, no servant has ever feared Allah without remembering Him.”

“But the most wretched will avoid it.”

Qatādah said: “No, by Allah, no servant turns away from this reminder out of disdain for it and hatred of it and its adherents unless he is plainly wretched.”

The unqualified mention of fear in the Quran encompasses fear of Allah and fear of His punishment in this world and the Hereafter. Allah Most High says:

“They ask you about the Hour: ‘When will it arrive?’ What have you to do with telling of it? Knowledge of its appointed time rests with your Lord. You are only a warner to those who fear it.”

And He Most High says:

“So remind with the Quran those who fear My warning.”

And He Most High says:

“It is Allah who has sent down the Book in truth, and the balance. And what will make you know? Perhaps the Hour is near. Those who do not believe in it seek to hasten it, while those who believe are fearful of it and know that it is the truth.”

And He says:

“They will say, ‘Previously, when we were among our families, we lived in fear. Then Allah bestowed His favor upon us and protected us from the punishment of the scorching Fire.’”

---

## Page 8180

Section

On His saying:

“Whoever feared the Most Merciful unseen and came with a heart turned to Him.”

In this verse, He says:

“He who fears will take heed.”

And in the account of Pharaoh, He says:

“Speak to him gently, so that he may take heed or come to fear.”

Thus, He links fearing to taking heed with the conjunction “or.”

He also says:

“For whoever wishes to take heed or show gratitude.”

In the account of the righteous, blind believer, He says:

“And what could make you know? Perhaps he might purify himself, or take heed and benefit from the reminder.”

And in Hā Mīm al-Mu’min, He says:

“That is because, when Allah alone was invoked, you disbelieved; but if partners are associated with Him, you believe. So judgment belongs to Allah, the Most High, the Most Great. It is He who shows you His signs and sends down provision for you from the sky. Yet none takes heed except one who turns to Him.”

Thus, He says:

“Yet none takes heed except one who turns to Him.”

---

## Page 8181

And He links turning to Him with fear in His saying:

“This is what you were promised—for everyone who repeatedly turns to Him and is mindful.”

“Whoever feared the Most Merciful unseen and came with a heart turned to Him.”

“Enter it in peace. This is the Day of Eternity.”

This is because whoever fears Allah must also place hope in Him and desire His mercy, and so turn to Him, love Him, and love worshipping and obeying Him. That is what saves him from what he fears and brings him what he loves. Such fear cannot exist in someone who is certain that he will be punished, for certainty of punishment is accompanied by despondency, despair, and utter hopelessness. This is not fear or apprehension. Fear and apprehension exist only alongside hope of deliverance.

For this reason, He says:

“You will see the wrongdoers fearful of what they have earned, yet it will befall them.”

Thus, whoever fears Allah turns to Him, as He says:

“And Paradise will be brought near to the righteous, not far away.”

“This is what you were promised—for everyone who repeatedly turns to Him and is mindful.”

“Whoever feared the Most Merciful unseen and came with a heart turned to Him.”

“Enter it in peace. This is the Day of Eternity.”

This occurs when fear and apprehension reach their fullness. In their initial stages, however, a person may fear punishment and the sin

---

## Page 8182

that brings it about, and so devote himself to seeking deliverance and safety while neglecting to seek mercy and Paradise. Alongside his evil deeds, he may perform good deeds that balance and offset them, thereby escaping the Fire without meriting Paradise. Rather, he will be among the people of the Heights. Although their ultimate destination is Paradise, they are not among those for whom Paradise has been brought near—that is, drawn close—since they did not attain fear of Allah and turn to Him. He thereafter sought what was good.

Section

As for His statement in the account of Pharaoh:

“Perhaps he may take heed or fear,”

and His saying:

“But what would make you know? Perhaps he might purify himself,

or take heed, and the reminder would benefit him,”

these do not contradict this verse. For He did not say in this verse, “Whoever takes heed will fear.” Rather, He stated that everyone who fears takes heed: either he takes heed and consequently fears—even though someone else may take heed without fearing—or his fear prompts him to take heed. Thus, fear necessarily entails taking heed, and everyone who fears takes heed.

As the Most High says:

“Only those of His servants who have knowledge fear Allah.”

Thus, none fears Him except

---

## Page 8183

one who has knowledge. Everyone who fears Allah therefore has knowledge. This is what the verse explicitly states. The early generations and most scholars said that it also indicates that everyone who has knowledge fears Allah, just as other verses indicate that everyone who disobeys Allah is ignorant.

As Abū al-‘Āliyah said:

“I asked the Companions of Muhammad about His saying:

‘The repentance accepted by Allah is only for those who do evil in ignorance.’

They told me, ‘Everyone who disobeys Allah is ignorant.’”

Mujāhid, al-Hasan al-Basrī, and other scholars among the Successors and those after them said likewise. This is because restriction has the force of an exception, and, according to the majority of scholars, an exception to a negation constitutes an affirmation. Thus, He denies fear to those who are not among the people of knowledge. These are the people who know Him, believe in what the messengers brought, and fear Him.

The Most High says:

“Is one who worships devoutly during the hours of the night, prostrating and standing, fearing the Hereafter and hoping for his Lord’s mercy, like one who does not? Say, ‘Are those who know equal to those who do not know?’”

He also affirms fear for the people of knowledge. Thus, everyone who has knowledge fears Him.

Whoever does not fear Allah is therefore not among the people of knowledge, but among the ignorant, as ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd said:

“Fear of Allah suffices as knowledge, and sufficient

---

## Page 8184

proof of ignorance is being deluded about Allah.” A man addressed al-Sha‘bī, saying, “O scholar!” He replied, “The scholar is only one who fears Allah.” Likewise, His saying,

“The one who fears will take heed,”

implies that everyone who fears Him must be among those who take heed. He has also mentioned that the most wretched avoids the reminder, making the one who fears the opposite of the most wretched. Thus it is said, “Everyone who takes heed fears.” More precisely, taking heed is a cause of fear; when it is complete, it necessarily produces fear. Likewise, knowledge is a cause of fear; when it is complete, it necessarily produces fear.

Accordingly, in His saying concerning Pharaoh,

“Perhaps he may take heed or fear,”

He distinguishes between the two, for the benefits this entails. One such benefit is that, if Pharaoh remembers that he is a created being, that Allah is his Creator, and that he himself is neither a god nor a lord—as he has been reminded—and remembers Allah’s kindness to him, this remembrance calls him to acknowledge Allah’s lordship, His oneness, and His favors toward him. It therefore calls for faith and gratitude, even if he supposes that Allah will not punish him. For the mere fact that something is true and beneficial calls for seeking it, even if one fears no harm

---

## Page 8185

from going without it. Thus believers hasten to perform voluntary and supererogatory acts of worship because of their benefit, even though there is no punishment for omitting them. Likewise, a person loves beneficial knowledge even if forgoing it causes him no harm. He may also love noble character and lofty pursuits because of the benefit and pleasure they bring in this world and the Hereafter, even if he fears no harm from neglecting them.

Thus, if he recalls Allah’s favors and His kindness toward him, this may lead him to acknowledge Allah’s right, His oneness, and His kindness toward him, and prompt him to thank Allah and hand Moses’ people over to him, even without fearing punishment. This may therefore come about through taking heed alone. He says:

“Or fear.”

As for fear itself, if Moses tells him of the punishment in this world and the Hereafter with which Allah has threatened him, this fear may move him to obey and submit, even if he does not take heed. Taking heed may occur without fear, and fear may occur without taking heed; both may also occur together, as is most often the case. Allah Most High says:

“Perhaps he may take heed or fear.”

Furthermore, a person’s recollection arises from knowledge he has acquired previously, and thus comes about through his reason alone, whereas his fear arises from the warnings he has heard. In the first case, he is among those who have a heart with which to understand; in the second, he is among those who have an ear with which to hear.

---

## Page 8186

Taking heed in a way that leads to good may come about through either of these, as Allah Most High says:

“How many generations We destroyed before them who were mightier than they and had ranged through the lands! Was there any escape?”

“Indeed, in that is a reminder for whoever has a heart or lends an ear with full attention.”

The second benefit:

Taking heed is a cause of fear, and fear results from taking heed. He therefore mentions taking heed, which is the cause, and fear, which is the outcome, even though the one entails the other. Likewise, He says:

“Indeed, in that is a reminder for whoever has a heart or lends an ear with full attention.”

And the people of the Fire say:

“If only we had listened or used our reason, we would not be among the inhabitants of the Blaze.”

He also says:

“Have they not traveled through the land so that they might have hearts with which to understand or ears with which to hear? It is not the eyes that are blind, but the hearts within the breasts.”

Either of these two ways brings salvation because each entails the other. Whoever listens to what the messengers have brought in a manner that enables him to understand what they have said will be saved.

Otherwise, hearing without understanding will not benefit him, as He says:

“Some of them listen to you, but when they leave your presence, they say to those who have been given knowledge, ‘What did he say just now?’ Those are the ones whose hearts Allah has sealed.”

He also says:

“Some of them listen to you. But can you make the deaf hear, even though they do not understand?”

And He says:

“Indeed, We have sent it down as an Arabic Quran so that you may understand.”

---

## Page 8187

Likewise, reason without listening to what the messengers have brought is of no benefit. The people of the Fire acknowledge that the messengers came to them, saying:

“Yes, a warner came to us, but we denied him and said, ‘Allah has sent down nothing.’”

Likewise, those who take heed of the remnants of those who were punished, concerning whom He says:

“Have they not traveled through the land so that they might have hearts with which to understand or ears with which to hear?”

They benefit only when they hear the accounts of those who were punished for denying the messengers and those who were saved for believing them, and thus listen to the messengers’ words and believe them.

The third benefit:

Fear is also a cause of taking heed, as explained above. Each may therefore be a cause of the other. A person may become afraid and consequently take heed; or he may recall frightening things and seek deliverance from them, then recall what he hopes will deliver him and act upon it.

If it is asked:

“Merely thinking that there is something to fear may give rise to fear, so how is it that He says:

‘Among His servants, only those who have knowledge fear Allah’?”

The answer is:

The soul has an overpowering desire from which mere supposition cannot turn it away. Only knowledge that punishment will inevitably occur can turn it away. As for someone who thinks that punishment will occur but has no certainty of it, he will not abandon his desire. Hence He says:

“But as for the one who feared standing before his Lord and restrained his soul from desire.”

---

## Page 8188

And the Most High says in condemnation of the disbelievers:

“When it was said, ‘Allah’s promise is true, and there is no doubt about the Hour,’ you said, ‘We do not know what the Hour is. We are merely guessing and have no certainty.’”

He describes the God-fearing as having certainty in the Hereafter. This is why the Lord swears that the punishment and the Hour will come. He also commands His Prophet to swear that the Hour will come and that the Quran is true, saying:

“Those who disbelieve claim that they will never be resurrected. Say, ‘Yes, by my Lord, you will surely be resurrected.’”

He also says:

“Those who disbelieve say, ‘The Hour will never come upon us.’ Say, ‘Yes, by my Lord, it will surely come upon you.’”

And He says:

“They ask you, ‘Is it true?’ Say, ‘Yes, by my Lord, it is indeed true.’”

Section

As for His saying, the Most High:

“None takes heed except one who turns to Him,”

it is true, just as He says. A person taking heed either recalls what leads to mercy, blessing, and reward—just as someone recalls what prompts him to ask—and thus turns to Allah; or he recalls what gives rise to fear and awe, in which case he must turn to Allah to escape what he fears. Hence it was said concerning Pharaoh:

“Perhaps he may take heed”—and thus turn to Allah—“or fear Him.”

---

## Page 8189

And likewise Moses said to him:

“Would you be willing to purify yourself, and let me guide you to your Lord, so that you may fear Him?”

Thus Moses brought the two together because they necessarily accompany one another.

Concerning the blind man, He said:

“How do you know? Perhaps he may purify himself, or take heed, and the reminder would benefit him.”

He thus mentioned benefiting from the reminder, just as He said:

“And remind, for the reminder benefits the believers.”

Benefit is of two kinds: receiving blessings and averting punishment. Averting punishment is itself a benefit, even if no other benefit accompanies it. Receiving benefits is itself a benefit, even when accompanied by fear of punishment; and having both is also a benefit. Thus benefit encompasses all three, and all three are attained through reminders, as the Most High said:

“And remind, for the reminder benefits the believers.”

And He said:

“How do you know? Perhaps he may purify himself, or take heed, and the reminder would benefit him.”

As for mentioning purification alongside taking heed, this parallels His mentioning fear alongside taking heed in the account of Pharaoh.

Purification means faith and righteous deeds through which a person’s soul becomes pure, as He said in this surah:

“Successful indeed is the one who purifies himself, remembers the name of his Lord, and prays.”

And He said:

“Successful indeed is the one who purifies it, and a failure is the one who corrupts it.”

---

## Page 8190

And He said:

“He is the One who sent among the unlettered a Messenger from among themselves, reciting His verses to them and purifying them.”

And He said:

“And woe to the polytheists, who do not give zakah.”

And Moses said to Pharaoh:

“Would you be willing to purify yourself, and let me guide you to your Lord, so that you may fear Him?”

He coupled purification with “or take heed, and the reminder would benefit him” for several reasons:

First: Purification is attained by obeying the Messenger’s command, even if the person does not recall knowledge received from him. As He said:

“Reciting His verses to them and purifying them.”

Then He said:

“And teaching them the Book and wisdom.”

Having the verses recited to them and being purified are common to all believers, whereas being taught the Book and wisdom is particular to some of them. Likewise, purification is common to everyone who believes in the Messenger. Taking heed, however, pertains specifically to someone who possesses knowledge that he recalls, and who, through that recollection, comes to know for himself what others do not know.

Second: His words, “or take heed, and the reminder would benefit him,” encompass benefit, whether small or great, whereas purification is more specific than that.

Third: Taking heed is a cause of purification. When a person takes heed, he experiences fear and hope and thus purifies himself. He therefore mentioned the outcome and its cause; He mentioned action and knowledge, each of which necessarily entails the other.

---

## Page 8191

For he does not purify himself until he takes heed of what he hears from the Messenger, as He said:

“Whoever fears Allah will take heed.”

Thus, every believer must have some fear and take heed. When he takes heed, he benefits; that benefit may become complete, so that he purifies himself.

His words:

“For whoever wishes to take heed or wishes to be grateful”

also admit considerations similar to these. A grateful person may thank Allah for His blessings even without feeling fear, whereas taking heed may entail fear. Moreover, taking heed entails fear of punishment and seeking reward, so that one acts with the future in mind, whereas gratitude concerns past blessings.

Furthermore, taking heed involves recalling previously acquired knowledge, including recalling Allah’s blessings upon oneself; it is therefore a cause of gratitude. Recollecting the cause and its originator.

Furthermore, gratitude leads to an increase in blessings. Taking heed may be for this purpose, or it may arise from fear of punishment. The reverse may also be the case: a grateful person may offer the gratitude required of him so as not to be ungrateful and thus be punished for failing to give thanks through the withdrawal of the blessing and other punishments.

---

## Page 8192

and one who takes heed may recall what Allah has prepared for those who obey Him, and so obey Him in pursuit of His mercy. Furthermore, taking heed may lead to fulfilling the obligations by which one wards off punishment, while gratitude may be offered in pursuit of more of His bounty. Thus, it is related in the two Sahīh collections:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stood in prayer until his feet became swollen. He was asked, ‘Do you do this when Allah has forgiven your past and future sins?’ He replied, ‘Should I not be a grateful servant?’”

He also said, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Let none of you wish for death. If he does good, he may increase in goodness; and if he does evil, perhaps he may be given the chance to make amends.”

The believer always has blessings from his Lord that call for gratitude, and sins for which he needs to seek forgiveness. In the foremost supplication for forgiveness, he says:

“I acknowledge before You Your blessing upon me, and I acknowledge my sin. So forgive me, for none forgives sins but You.”

He understands the full truth of Allah’s words:

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself.”

Whatever good befalls him consists of Allah’s blessings and therefore calls for gratitude. Whatever calamities befall him are due to his sins and call for him to remember those sins, prompting repentance and seeking forgiveness. Allah has made “the night and the day succeed one another for whoever wishes to take heed,” so that he may repent

---

## Page 8193

and seek forgiveness for his sins, “or wishes to show gratitude” to his Lord for His blessings. Every blessing Allah bestows upon His servant, and whatever He grants him in replacement, is a blessing from Allah to him. Whenever he considers what his Lord has done, he gives thanks; whenever he considers himself, he seeks forgiveness.

Taking heed may involve recalling one’s sins and one’s Lord’s punishment. It may also include recalling His favors and blessings, for this prompts gratitude. Allah Most High says in several places:

“Remember Allah’s blessing upon you.”

Thus, He has commanded that His blessings be remembered. The one who takes heed remembers both his Lord’s blessings and his own sins. Furthermore, He mentions gratitude because it is an end in itself, for gratitude endures in this world and the Hereafter. He mentions taking heed because it is the foundation of seeking forgiveness, giving thanks, and other acts. Thus, He mentions both the beginning and the ultimate end. This understanding brings together the interpretations that have been offered. And Allah, glory be to Him, knows best.

Section

Taking heed is a comprehensive expression encompassing everything Allah has commanded us to recall. As He says:

“Did We not give you lives long enough for whoever wished to take heed to do so? And the warner came to you.”

That is, the proof was established against you through the warner who came to you and through your being granted lives long enough to take heed.

---

## Page 8194

He, glory be to Him, has commanded that His blessings be remembered in several places, as when He says:

“Remember Allah’s blessing upon you and what He has sent down to you of the Book and wisdom.”

The purpose of remembering these blessings is to give thanks for them, as He says:

“Wherever you set out from, turn your face toward the Sacred Mosque. This is indeed the truth from your Lord, and Allah is not unaware of what you do.”

“Wherever you set out from, turn your face toward the Sacred Mosque; and wherever you may be, turn your faces toward it, so that people may have no argument against you, except those among them who do wrong. So do not fear them; fear Me, so that I may complete My blessing upon you and that you may be guided.”

“Just as We have sent among you a Messenger from among yourselves, reciting Our verses to you, purifying you, teaching you the Book and wisdom, and teaching you what you did not know.”

“So remember Me; I will remember you. Give thanks to Me, and do not be ungrateful to Me.”

His words,

“Just as We have sent among you a Messenger from among yourselves,”

encompass everyone addressed by the Quran. The same applies to His words:

“There has certainly come to you a Messenger from among yourselves. Your suffering weighs heavily upon him; he is deeply concerned for you, and compassionate and merciful toward the believers.”

The Messenger is thus from among those addressed by these words, since the kāf is the pronoun of address. Since these words were addressed first to Quraysh, then to the Arabs, and then to all other nations, their scope is accordingly specific or general.

The Quran also contains passages specifically concerning Quraysh, such as His words:

“For the accustomed security of Quraysh,”
“their accustomed security on the journey of winter

---

## Page 8195

and summer.”

And His words:

“Indeed, it is a reminder for you and your people.”

It also contains passages that encompass all the Arabs while referring specifically to them, such as His words:

“He is the One who sent among the unlettered a Messenger from among themselves, reciting His verses to them.”

“The unlettered” encompasses all the Arabs, as distinct from the People of the Book. Then He says:

“And others among them who have not yet joined them.”

This encompasses everyone who enters Islam after the Arabs entered it, until the Day of Resurrection, as stated by Muqātil ibn Hayyān, ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn Zayd, and others. His words, “And others among them,” mean that they belong to them in religion, not in lineage; had they belonged to them in lineage, they would have been included among the unlettered.

This is like His words, Most High:

“And those who believed afterwards, emigrated, and strove alongside you—they are of you.”

It is established in the authentic collection that when this verse was revealed, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was asked about those people and said:

“Were faith suspended at the Pleiades, men of Persian descent would reach it.”

This indicates that these people are included; it does not preclude the inclusion of people from other nations. Since they belong to them, they are included in His words:

“Allah has certainly bestowed favour upon

---

## Page 8196

the believers when He sent among them a Messenger from among themselves.”

This favour extends to all believers, Arab and non-Arab, earlier and later generations alike. The Messenger is one of them because he is a human being and a believer. He belongs more specifically to the Arabs, being an Arab who came speaking their language, and more specifically still to Quraysh.

This particular connection establishes the proof against them, but confers no superiority except through faith and piety, for Allah says:

“Indeed, the most noble of you in the sight of Allah is the most righteous of you.”

Thus, the Ansār were superior to those of Quraysh who were granted their freedom, although the Ansār belonged neither to Rabīʿah nor to Mudar, but to Qahtān. Most hold that they were descendants of Hūd, not descendants of Abraham. It has also been said that they were descendants of Ishmael, based on the hadith concerning Aslam, when he said:

“Shoot, for your forefather was an archer.”

Aslam belongs to Khuzāʿah, and Khuzāʿah descends from Abraham. There is further discussion of this, but this is not the place for it. The point is that the Ansār were more distantly related to the Messenger than any of Rabīʿah and Mudar, numerous as these tribes were. Nevertheless, they were superior to the majority of Quraysh, except for the first and foremost among the Emigrants, who included both Qurashīs and non-Qurashīs. The forerunners numbered fourteen hundred in all, excluding those who emigrated to Abyssinia.

---

## Page 8197

Thus, His words:

“There has certainly come to you”

refer specifically to Quraysh and the Arabs, then extend to all other human beings, since the Quran addresses them. The Messenger is one of their own, meaning that he is neither an angel from whom they would be unable to receive instruction, nor a jinn.

This then extends to the jinn, because the Messenger was sent to both humans and jinn, and the Quran addresses both communities. The Messenger belongs to them collectively, as Allah says:

“O company of jinn and mankind, did there not come to you messengers from among you?”

He thus described the messengers He sent as belonging to both kinds, although they were human. Humans and jinn share the qualities of being alive, endowed with speech, and subject to commands and prohibitions. They eat, drink, have sexual relations, produce offspring, and receive nourishment and grow through eating and drinking. These qualities are common to them both and distinguish them from the angels, who neither eat nor drink, nor have sexual relations or produce offspring. Thus, the Messenger is among the noblest of the two communities, in view of the qualities they share that distinguish them from the angels. Accordingly, the Messenger was sent to both communities, but not to the angels.

Likewise, His words:

“Allah has certainly bestowed a favour upon the believers when He sent among them a Messenger from

---

## Page 8198

among themselves,” are like His words:

“Remember Allah’s favour upon you and what He has sent down to you of the Book and wisdom.”

And His words:

“Just as We have sent among you a Messenger from yourselves, reciting Our verses to you, purifying you, teaching you the Book and wisdom, and teaching you what you did not know.”

Then He said:

“So remember Me; I will remember you. And be grateful to Me, and do not be ungrateful to Me.”

The point is that He commanded people to remember His blessings and give thanks for them. In several places, He said:

“O Children of Israel, remember My favour which I bestowed upon you.”

And He said to the believers:

“And remember when you were few, and He increased your numbers.”

Remembering His blessings is thus part of the remembrance they were commanded to observe.

They were also commanded to recall the accounts of the earlier prophets, as He said:

“And mention Abraham in the Book.”

“And mention Moses in the Book.”

“And mention Ishmael in the Book.”

“And mention Idrīs in the Book.”

He also said:

“And remember Our servant David, a man of strength.”

“And remember Our servants Abraham, Isaac and Jacob.”

“And remember Ishmael and Elisha.”

They were also commanded to remember the reward and punishment they had been promised. Allah Most High said:

“Indeed, We singled them out for a special quality: remembrance of the abode of the Hereafter.”

---

## Page 8199

Among the things they were commanded to remember are Allah’s signs, from which they infer His power and the resurrection, as in His words:

“Man says, ‘When I am dead, will I really be brought forth alive?’”

“Does man not remember that We created him before, when he was nothing?”

He also said to Moses:

“And remind them of the days of Allah.”

These encompass both the days of His blessings and the days of His punishments, so that they may give thanks and take heed. Hence He said:

“Indeed, in that are signs for everyone who is steadfast and grateful.”

Remembering blessings inspires gratitude, while remembering punishments calls for patiently doing what is commanded, even if the soul dislikes it, and refraining from what is forbidden, even if the soul desires it, lest one suffer the punishment that befell others.

Section

As for His words:

“But the most wretched will avoid it,”

“Who will burn in the greatest Fire,”

“Then neither die therein nor live.”

He also said in Sūrat al-Layl:

“So I have warned you of a blazing Fire,”

“None will burn in it except the most wretched,”

“Who denied and turned away.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained what is meant by this burning in the authentic hadith

---

## Page 8200

which Muslim narrated from Abū Sa'īd al-Khudrī, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“As for the inhabitants of the Fire who truly belong there, they neither die therein nor live. But there are people whom the Fire afflicts because of their sins—or he said, their misdeeds—and causes to die. Once they have become charcoal, permission for intercession is granted, and they are brought in group after group and scattered along the rivers of Paradise. Then it is said, ‘O inhabitants of Paradise, pour water over them.’ They then sprout as a seed sprouts amid flood-borne debris.”

A man among those present remarked, “It is as though the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had lived in the desert.”

In another narration cited by Ibn Abī Hātim, he stated: It was related from 'Abd al-Samad ibn 'Abd al-Wārith, who said: My father narrated to us: Sulaymān al-Taymī narrated to us, from Abū Nadrah, from Abū Sa'īd, who reported:

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, delivered a sermon and came to this verse:

“Then neither die therein nor live.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“As for its inhabitants who truly belong there, they neither die therein nor live. But those who are not inhabitants of the Fire are caused to die by it. Then the intercessors will stand forth and be permitted to intercede for them, and they will intercede. They will then be brought to a river called al-Hayāh (Life), or al-Hayawān (Life), and will sprout as flood-borne seeds sprout amid the debris carried by a torrent.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, thus explained that this burning is for the inhabitants of the Fire who truly belong there. As for those who do not belong there, the Fire afflicts them because of their sins, and Allah causes them to die therein until they become charcoal. Then intercession is made for them, and they emerge and are brought

---

## Page 8201

to the River of Life, where they sprout as seeds sprout amid flood-borne debris. This meaning is widely transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—indeed, it is established through mass transmission in numerous hadiths in the two Sahīh collections and elsewhere, narrated by Abū Saʿīd, Abū Hurayrah, and others.

These reports refute two groups. They refute the Khawārij and the Muʿtazilah, who say, “Monotheists will remain therein forever.” This verse is evidence against them, and against those extreme Murjiʾah who are reported to have said, “No monotheist will enter the Fire.” For the Prophet’s report that monotheists will emerge from it after entering it disproves the claims of both groups.

It also refutes those who say, “It is possible that Allah will not admit any monotheist into the Fire,” as maintained by a group among the Shīʿī Murjiʾah and the Murjiʾah among speculative theologians who profess adherence to the Sunnah. These are those who suspend judgment among the followers of Abū al-Hasan and others, such as al-Qādī Abū Bakr and others. For the mass-transmitted texts establish that some monotheists will enter the Fire and subsequently leave it.

As for the claim that “No monotheist will enter it,” I do not know it to be reliably established from any particular individual such that I could attribute it to him. It has, however, been reported from Muqātil ibn Sulaymān

---

## Page 8202

and he said: Those who held this view cited this verse as evidence. Two answers have been given to them.

One is the answer offered by a group that includes al-Zajjāj. They said that this refers to a particular fire. Yet His subsequent words,

“But the most God-fearing will be kept far from it,”

would then offer little promise, since someone spared that fire could still be made to enter another.

Others answered that they will not burn in it eternally. This is closer to the truth. More precisely, burning here means burning in the full, unqualified sense: remaining in it forever, continually subject to its torment. As for someone who enters and then leaves, this is a kind of burning, but not burning in that full sense—especially if he has died therein and the Fire has not consumed him entirely. For it is established that it does not consume the parts of the body that touch the ground in prostration. And Allah knows best.

Section

Allah, glorified be He, mentions Abraham and Moses together—may Allah bless them both and all the other messengers—in a number of contexts, as in His words:

“Indeed, this is in the earlier scriptures—the scriptures of Abraham and Moses.”

---

## Page 8203

And in the lengthy hadith of Abū Dharr:

“I said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, how many books did Allah reveal?’ He replied, ‘One hundred and four books: thirty scrolls to Seth, fifty to Idrīs, ten to Abraham, and ten to Moses before the Torah. He also revealed the Torah, the Gospel, the Psalms, and the Criterion.’”

Later in the hadith, he asks, “Do we have anything of what was contained in the scrolls of Abraham?” He replies, “Yes,” and recites Allah’s words:

“Successful indeed is the one who purifies himself, remembers the name of his Lord and prays. Yet you prefer the life of this world, though the Hereafter is better and more enduring. Indeed, this is in the earlier scriptures—the scriptures of Abraham and Moses.”

Self-purification means becoming pure and blessed by abandoning evil deeds, which brings purity and growth to the soul. As Allah says:

“Successful indeed is the one who purifies it.”

Thus zakāh (purification) is sometimes explained as growth and increase, and sometimes as cleansing and removal. In truth, it combines both: removing evil and increasing good. This is righteous action and doing good. Yet it avails only with sincere devotion to Allah and worship of Him alone, without any partner, which is the foundation of faith. This is expressed in His words:

“And remembers the name of his Lord and prays.”

These three things may be said to resemble the three that Allah brings together in various places in the Quran, as in His words at the beginning of al-Baqarah:

“A guidance for the God-fearing, who believe in the unseen, establish prayer, and spend out of what We have provided for them.”

And likewise His words:

---

## Page 8204

“But if they repent, establish prayer, and give zakāh, then let them go their way.”

“But if they repent, establish prayer, and give zakāh, then they are your brothers in religion.”

It may also be said that they resemble the two qualities mentioned in the verse, “Whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day and does righteous deeds,” and in His words:

“And who is better in religion than one who submits himself to Allah and does good?”

Here, however, self-purification in the verse is broader than spending, for it means abandoning evil deeds, at the foundation of which is abandoning the association of partners with Allah. Thus, the first stage of self-purification is to purify oneself of associating partners with Allah, as He says:

“And woe to those who associate partners with Allah, who do not give zakāh.”

And He says:

“He recites His verses to them and purifies them.”

There is also purification from major sins, which brings piety to completion, as He says:

“So do not declare yourselves pure; He knows best who is mindful of Him.”

And He says:

“Have you not seen those who declare themselves pure? Rather, Allah purifies whom He wills, and they will not be wronged so much as the thread of a date stone.”

Thus, to declare someone pure is to attest to their piety. Self-purification also includes cleansing oneself, giving charity, and doing good, as He says:

“Take charity from their wealth to cleanse them and purify them thereby.”

The words “remembers the name of his Lord” may mean faith in Allah, and “prayer”:

---

## Page 8205

deeds. For someone who does not pray may nevertheless remember the name of his Lord.

Some jurists say that this means mentioning His name at the beginning of prayer. This, Allah knows best, is why self-purification is mentioned first in this verse. Some of the early generations would invoke this verse when giving the alms of Eid al-Fitr before the Eid prayer. One of them—I believe it was Yazīd ibn Abī Habīb—considered it recommended to give charity before every prayer for this reason.

Since Allah mentions prayer before sacrifice in His words:

“So pray to your Lord and sacrifice,”

and mentions self-purification before prayer in His words:

“Successful indeed is the one who purifies himself, and remembers the name of his Lord and prays,”

the Sunnah is to give charity before the prayer on Eid al-Fitr and to slaughter the sacrifice after the prayer on the Eid of Sacrifice.

It also seems—and Allah knows best—that fasting may be included in the self-purification mentioned in the verse. For Allah says:

“Fasting has been prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, so that you may become mindful of Allah.”

The purpose of fasting is therefore piety, which is part of the meaning of self-purification.

In the hadith of Ibn ʿAbbās:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made the alms of Eid al-Fitr obligatory as a purification for the fasting person from idle and obscene speech, and as food for the needy.”

---

## Page 8206

Thus, charity completes the purification achieved through fasting. Both are forms of self-purification that precede the Eid prayer. These two statements therefore encourage the faith and righteous deeds that Allah has commanded.

His words affirm belief in the Last Day:

“But you prefer the life of this world, whereas the Hereafter is better and more lasting.”

These are the foundations mentioned in His words:

“Indeed, those who believe, the Jews, the Christians and the Sabians—whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day and does righteous deeds—will have their reward with their Lord. No fear will be upon them, nor will they grieve.”

He also says:

“Indeed, this is in the earlier scriptures, the scriptures of Abraham and Moses.”

And He also says:

“Have you seen the one who turned away, gave a little, and then stopped giving? Does he possess knowledge of the unseen, so that he sees? Or has he not been informed of what is in the scriptures of Moses and of Abraham, who fulfilled his duty: that no bearer of burdens will bear the burden of another; that a person will have only what he strives for; that his striving will be seen; and that he will then be rewarded for it with the fullest recompense?”

Moreover, Abraham is the bearer of the faith and the leader of the community. Allah Most High says:

“Then We revealed to you: Follow the faith of Abraham, inclining to the truth; he was not one of the polytheists.”

He also says:

“Who would turn away from the faith of Abraham except one who makes a fool of himself?”

And He says:

“Who is better in religion than one who submits himself to Allah, does good, and follows the faith of Abraham

---

## Page 8207

inclining to the truth?” He also says:

“Indeed, Abraham was a leader, devoutly obedient to Allah, inclining to the truth.”

And He says:

“I will make you a leader for the people.”

Moses received the Scripture, was spoken to by Allah, and brought the Law. No scripture has descended from heaven that offers better guidance than his Scripture and the Quran.

For this reason, Allah mentions the two scriptures together in several places, as in His words:

“Say: Who sent down the Scripture that Moses brought as a light?”

Through to His words:

“And this is a blessed Book which We have sent down.”

And His words:

“They said: Two works of magic.”

Through to His words:

“Say: Then bring a scripture from Allah that offers better guidance than these two, and I will follow it.”

And the words of the jinn:

“Indeed, we have heard a Book sent down after Moses, confirming what came before it.”

And His words:

“Say: Have you considered: if it is from Allah and you disbelieve in it, while a witness from the Children of Israel has testified to something like it?”

And the words of the Negus:

“Indeed, this and what Moses brought come forth from the same niche.”

Of Moses it is said:

“And Allah spoke directly to Moses.”

And of Abraham:

“And Allah took Abraham as an intimate friend.”

The foundation of this intimate friendship is worshipping Allah alone, and worship is the utmost love and humility. Moses received the Scripture and was spoken to by Allah. Thus, those who disbelieved in the messengers denied the reality of Abraham’s intimate friendship with Allah and Allah’s speaking to Moses. When polytheistic innovations emerged in this community, al-Ja‘d ibn Dirham denied this

---

## Page 8208

, so the Muslims put him to death when the governor of Iraq, Khālid ibn ‘Abd Allāh, offered him as a sacrifice, saying:

“Offer your sacrifices; may Allah accept them. I am sacrificing al-Ja‘d ibn Dirham, for he claimed that Allah did not take Abraham as an intimate friend and did not speak directly to Moses.”

Then he descended and slaughtered him.

When Allah sent His Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, He sent him to all the people of the earth. They were essentially of two kinds: the unlettered and the People of the Book. The unlettered traced their affiliation to Abraham, for they were his descendants and the custodians of his House, and they retained some of his rites. The People of the Book had their foundation in the Scripture of Moses. Both groups had made alterations and changes. Thus, the Prophet restored the religion of Abraham after it had been distorted, and brought the Book that stands as a guardian over the scriptures preceding it, confirming them and clarifying the matters disputed and whatever had been distorted or concealed in the earlier Scripture.

Section

Abraham and Moses upheld the foundation of religion: acknowledging Allah, worshipping Him alone without any partner, and disputing with those who disbelieved in Allah. As for Abraham, Allah said of him:

“Have you not considered the one who argued with Abraham about his Lord

---

## Page 8209

because Allah had given him kingship? When Abraham said, ‘My Lord is the One who gives life and causes death,’ he said, ‘I give life and cause death.’ Abraham said, ‘Allah brings the sun from the east, so bring it from the west.’ Thus the disbeliever was confounded. And Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people.”

Allah also relates that Abraham asked Him to bring the dead to life, so Allah commanded him to take four birds. Thus he affirmed creation and resurrection—the beginning and the return—and belief in Allah and the Last Day. The unbelievers among the Sabians and polytheists, including philosophers and others like them, reject both of these or one of them. It was to people of their kind that Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, was sent. Some of them deny the existence of the Creator; others deny His attributes; still others deny that He creates, saying instead that He is a cause. Most of them deny the resurrection of the dead. They are polytheists who worship the heavenly bodies above and earthly idols below.

Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend—may Allah’s blessings be upon him—refuted all of this. He affirmed the lordship of his Lord, as in this verse. He affirmed sincere devotion to Him alone and rejected associating partners with Him, as in Sūrat al-An‘ām and elsewhere. He also affirmed resurrection after death. Firmly established in his religion were his love for Allah and Allah’s love for him, Allah having taken him as an intimate friend.

---

## Page 8210

Then he disputed with the polytheists over their worship of beings lacking the attributes of perfection. He said to his father:

“My father, why do you worship that which neither hears nor sees and cannot avail you at all?”

He said to his father and his people:

“What do you worship?”
“They replied, ‘We worship idols and remain devoted to them.’”
“He said, ‘Do they hear you when you call upon them, or benefit or harm you?’”

The passage continues:

“They are enemies to me, except the Lord of all worlds, who created me and guides me, who feeds me and gives me drink. And when I am ill, it is He who heals me, and who will cause me to die and then bring me to life.”

And so on to the end of the passage.

He also said:

“I have turned my face towards the One who created the heavens and the earth, upright in faith, and I am not one of the polytheists.”

And he said:

“I disavow whatever you worship, except the One who created me, for He will surely guide me.”
“He made this an enduring declaration among his descendants, so that they might return.”

Thus Abraham called people to the natural disposition: worshipping Allah alone, without any partner. This is Islam in its universal sense, affirming for Allah the attributes of words, and refuting those who worship beings bereft of them.

Having reproached them for worshipping beings that have no knowledge and neither hear nor see, he said:

“Our Lord, You know whatever we conceal and whatever we reveal. Nothing on earth or in heaven is hidden from Allah.”
“All praise is for Allah, who has granted me Ishmael and Isaac in my old age. Indeed, my Lord hears supplication.”

---

## Page 8211

And when he reproached them for worshipping beings that can avail them nothing, neither benefiting nor harming them, he said:

“Who created me and guides me,
who feeds me and gives me drink.
And when I am ill, it is He who heals me,
who will cause me to die and then bring me to life,
and who, I hope, will forgive my sin on the Day of Judgment.”

A person needs to secure benefit for his heart and body and ward off harm from them. This encompasses both religious and worldly affairs. In religion, the benefit is guidance, the harm is sin, and the removal of that harm is forgiveness. This is why affirming Allah’s oneness and seeking His forgiveness are coupled in numerous passages. For the body, the benefit is food and drink, the harm is illness, and the removal of that harm is healing.

He declared that his Lord gives life and causes death, and that He originated the heavens and the earth. His giving life is a perfection beyond that of His being living. His having originated the heavens and the earth entails His sustaining them and maintaining their existence—a perfection beyond that of His being self-subsisting. Concerning the stars, he said:

“I do not love those that set.”
