For that which sets disappears at one time and appears at another, and thus does not watch over

---

## Page 8212

its worshipper at all times. Those who worship things other than Allah, such as the stars and the like, and take them as idols make requests and supplications when they rise. When they set, however, these worshippers obtain neither what they seek nor what they desire. They secure no benefit, ward off no harm, and derive no benefit from their worship at such times. Thus he explained the deficiencies of the gods worshipped besides Allah and the perfection of his Lord, the Originator of the heavens and the earth: He is the Creator, the Originator, the All-Knowing, the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing, the Guide, the Provider, the Giver of Life and the Causer of Death.

He called his Lord by the Most Beautiful Names, which signify His attributes of perfection, saying:

“Who will recite Your verses to them, teach them the Book and wisdom, and purify them. Indeed, You are the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

He also said:

“Whoever follows me is indeed of me; and whoever disobeys me—surely You are All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

And he said:

“I will ask my Lord to forgive you. Indeed, He has always been gracious to me.”

He thus described his Lord as possessing wisdom and mercy, in keeping with the meaning of intimate friendship, as he said:

“Indeed, He has always been gracious to me.”

Moses, peace be upon him, disputed with Pharaoh, who denied divine lordship and messengership, and said:

“I am your lord, the most high.”

And:

“I know of no god for you other than myself.”

His story is recounted repeatedly and at length in the Quran, so there is no need to elaborate on it here. Moses likewise affirmed Allah’s lordship and His attributes of perfection, and rejected associating partners with Him.

---

## Page 8213

And when his people took the calf for worship, Allah explained to them the attributes of deficiency that are incompatible with divinity, saying:

“After Moses had gone, his people made from their ornaments a calf—a bodily form that lowed. Did they not see that it neither spoke to them nor guided them to any way? They took it for worship and were wrongdoers.”

He also said:

“They said, ‘This is your god and the god of Moses, but he forgot.’”

“Did they not see that it could give them no answer and had no power to harm or benefit them?”

“Aaron had already told them, ‘My people, you are only being tested by it. Your Lord is indeed the Most Merciful.’”

Thus He described it as something that, although it made a lowing sound, neither spoke to them nor answered them, neither guided them to any way nor had any power to harm or benefit them.

Likewise, Allah, glorified be He, spoke through Muhammad concerning the association of partners with Him, both generally and specifically, saying:

“Do they associate with Him those who create nothing and are themselves created?”

“They can neither help them nor help themselves.”

“If you call them to guidance, they will not follow you. It makes no difference whether you call them or remain silent.”

“Those you invoke besides Allah are servants like you. So invoke them, and let them answer you, if you are truthful.”

“Do they have feet with which to walk, hands with which to grasp, eyes with which to see, or ears with which to hear? Say, ‘Call upon your partners, then plot against me and give me no respite.’”

---

## Page 8214

And He posed rhetorical questions emphatically denying them the means of complete perception—hearing and sight—and complete action—the hand and foot. Likewise, He, glorified be He, spoke of those He loves, who draw near to Him through voluntary acts of worship, in the report transmitted from Him by His Messenger:

“My servant continues to draw near to Me through voluntary acts of worship until I love him. When I love him, I become his hearing with which he hears, his sight with which he sees, his hand with which he grasps, and his foot with which he walks.”

Section

The people of the Sunnah and the Community, who follow Abraham, Moses, and Muhammad—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon them all—affirm what these prophets affirmed: Allah’s speaking, His love, His mercy, and all His most beautiful names and highest attributes. They declare Him free from resemblance to lifeless bodies. For Allah said:

“We placed upon his throne a body; then he turned in repentance.”

And He said:

“We did not make them bodies that ate no food.”

And He said:

“A calf—a bodily form that lowed.”

Thus He described the bodily form as lacking life, for lifeless things neither hear nor see nor speak, nor are they of any avail.

As for the people of innovation and misguidance, such as the Jahmiyyah and others like them, they followed

---

## Page 8215

the path of the enemies of Abraham, Moses, and Muhammad, who denied that Allah truly spoke to Moses and took Abraham as an intimate friend.

Allah also spoke to Muhammad and took him as an intimate friend, just as He took Abraham as an intimate friend, and raised him many degrees beyond that.

They followed Pharaoh, who said:

“O Hāmān, build me a lofty tower so that I may reach the ways—the ways of the heavens—and look upon the God of Moses. Indeed, I think he is a liar.”

They also followed the polytheists of whom it is said:

“When they are told, ‘Prostrate to the Most Merciful,’ they say, ‘And what is the Most Merciful? Should we prostrate to what you command us?’”

They followed those who deviated concerning Allah’s names. They deny the reality of His being the Most Merciful, or that He shows mercy, speaks, loves His servants or is loved by them, or that He is above the heavens. They claim that whoever affirms these attributes of Him has likened Him to bodies perceptible to the senses—namely, living creatures such as human beings—and that this amounts to likening Allah to His creation.

Yet they themselves have likened Him to lifeless bodies in matters of deficiency and defect—a likeness which the divine scriptures and innate reason show to be defective and deficient, indeed one that entails His nonexistence.

As for those who affirm His attributes, even if it were supposed that their statements involved some likeness, it would not be a likeness to something deficient or defective, nor would it concern an attribute of deficiency or defect. Rather, it would concern what is known to be the utmost perfection, whose possessor has majesty and honor.

---

## Page 8216

Thus, the people of the Sunnah affirm His existence and the perfection of His existence, whereas those others describe Him as lacking perfection of existence or as not existing at all. They both liken Him to creation and negate His attributes: both their likening and their negation are contrary to reason and revelation.

As regards reason, they have likened Him to nonexistence and lifeless bodies. As regards revelation, they have equated the attributes conveyed by the messengers with the very attributes of created beings—even though the likeness they claim the scriptural texts convey is less extreme than the likeness they themselves assert.

Their negation, judged by reason, is a negation of the attributes that necessarily entails the nonexistence of His very being. Consequently, many of them have been driven to deny His being altogether. They have followed Pharaoh’s path, acknowledging only the existence of created things, although they may dissemble by professing meaningless words or engaging in acts of worship with no object of worship.

As for their negation of revelation, they have denied the meanings contained in Allah’s scriptures and distorted words from their proper places. Or they have said, “We are like the unlettered, knowing the Scripture only as mere recitation,” or, “Our hearts are veiled.” Concerning the Book and the Sunnah brought by the Messenger, they have said much the same as the disbelievers said:

---

## Page 8217

“Our hearts are veiled against what you call us to, our ears are deaf, and between us and you is a barrier,” and, “They said, ‘O Shuayb, we do not understand much of what you say.’”

Likewise, these people say, “We do not understand much of what the Messenger says.” They speak like those who listen to the Messenger, then, upon leaving him, “say to those given knowledge, ‘What did he say just now?’”

They have become like those of whom it was said:

“When you recite the Quran, We place a hidden barrier between you and those who do not believe in the Hereafter.”

“And We place coverings over their hearts, preventing them from understanding it, and deafness in their ears. When you mention your Lord alone in the Quran, they turn their backs in aversion.”

Reflect, then, on what Allah has related about the enemies of the messengers—their claims not to understand and their rejection—and you will find something of this among those who turn away from Allah’s remembrance and from reflecting on His Book, and follow what the devils recite and inspire in their allies. May Allah guide us to a straight path.

For this reason, these Jahmiyyah, who negate the divine attributes and resemble the disbelievers and polytheists among the Sabians and others who deny the Creator’s existence or His attributes, sometimes accuse the people of knowledge and faith, who adhere to the Book and the Sunnah, of resembling the Jews because of what is in the Torah

---

## Page 8218

and the books of the prophets concerning the divine attributes, and because of the likenesses to created beings that some Jews have innovated, which must be denied of Allah. At other times, they accuse them of resembling the Christians because the Christians affirm the attributes of life and knowledge, and because of their innovations: that the hypostases are substances and that the hypostasis of the Word united with human nature.

These accusations appear in their statements before the time of Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal and during his lifetime. Accounts of them also appear in his statements and those of his companions. He mentioned this in his book, Refutation of the Jahmiyyah and the Heretics, reporting their claim, “If you affirm the attributes, you have adopted the Christians’ position,” and refuting it. His Responses to Questions also records that a group said to him: Whoever says, “The Quran is uncreated or is within people’s hearts,” has adopted the Christians’ position.

Likewise, the Jahmiyyah accuse those who affirm the divine attributes of being the Jews of this community. This appears in the statements of both the earlier and later Jahmiyyah, including those of Abū Abd Allāh Muhammad ibn Umar al-Rāzī, the Jahmī determinist—although at times he may go so far as outright polytheism and the worship of celestial bodies and idols. He composed his well-known book on magic and the worship of celestial bodies and idols, even though he frequently declares such practices forbidden and prohibits them, following the Muslims and the followers of the Scriptures and the prophetic message. He supports Islam and its adherents in many passages, just as he sows doubt among its adherents and among those other than

---

## Page 8219

its adherents in most passages. He may also support those outside Islam in some passages. His prevailing tendency is toward casting doubt and perplexity rather than certainty and clear exposition. There are several replies to these people.

First:

Resembling Jews and Christians is objectionable only in matters that contradict the religion of Islam, the texts of the Quran and Sunnah, and scholarly consensus. Otherwise, it is well known that the religion of the messengers is one, and that the Torah and the Quran emanate from a single source.

Allah has called upon the People of the Book as witnesses in several places, even saying:

“Say, ‘Have you considered: what if it is from Allah and you disbelieve in it, while a witness from the Children of Israel testifies to its like and believes, yet you remain arrogant?’”

Thus, when He calls upon the People of the Book to testify to something like what the Muslims affirm, this constitutes proof and evidence. This is part of the wisdom behind allowing them to remain upon payment of the jizyah (poll tax). One therefore rejoices when a position derived from the Quran and Sunnah agrees with what the People of the Book transmit from the messengers who preceded them. Such agreement is among the signs of prophethood, the proofs of the prophetic message, and the evidence that the messengers agree.

Second:

The resemblance they allege is unfounded. For Ahl al-Sunnah

---

## Page 8220

do not agree with the Jews and Christians in the religious beliefs and practices they have innovated.

For this reason, in explaining the error in Ibn al-Khatīb’s statement, I said that he had understood neither the position of the adherents of hadith and Sunnah, whether Hanbalīs or others, nor the position of the Christians. I explained this in the relevant discussion, just as Imām Ahmad explained the distinction between the position of Ahl al-Sunnah and the Christians’ innovated doctrine, and likewise explains the distinction between the position of Ahl al-Sunnah and the Jews’ innovated doctrine.

Third:

Even if those who affirm Allah’s attributes are assumed to resemble the Jews or Christians, those who negate and deny His attributes resemble the unbelievers and polytheists among the Christians and others. It is certainly known that resembling the people of the two Scriptures is better than resembling unbelievers outside the People of the Book who deny divine lordship and prophethood, and others like them.

For this reason, it has been said:

“The one who likens Allah to His creation is dim-sighted, while the one who denies His attributes is blind.”

This is why, during the Prophet’s lifetime—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the believers rejoiced at the Christians’ victory over the Magians, just as the polytheists rejoiced at the Magians’ victory over the Christians. Reflect on this, for it is useful in various contexts. Allah knows best.

For this reason, the Mu‘tazilah and others like them among the Qadariyyah are the Magians of this community. They regard those who affirm Allah’s attributes as the Christians of this community, and incline toward the Jews because of their agreement

---

## Page 8221

with them in many matters more than with the Christians, just as a group of Sufis and ascetics incline more toward the Christians than toward the Jews. If, then, those who affirm Allah’s attributes are closer to the Christians, while their opponents are closer to the Magians and polytheists, it becomes clear that those who affirm Allah’s attributes are followers of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and his Companions, who rejoiced at the Christian Romans’ victory over the Magian Persians, whereas those who deny His attributes are closer to the polytheists who rejoiced at the Magians’ victory over the Christians.

---

## Page 8222

Surah al-Ghāshiyah (The Overwhelming Event)

Shaykh al-Islām said:

Section

Regarding His words:

“Has there come to you the account of the Overwhelming Event?
Faces on that Day will be humbled,
toiling and weary,
burning in a scorching Fire,
given drink from a boiling spring.”

There are two interpretations of these verses. The first is that they mean: faces humbled, toiling and weary in this world will burn in a scorching Fire on the Day of Resurrection. This refers to devotees among the disbelievers, such as monks and devotees of idols. These verses have sometimes also been interpreted as referring to followers of innovation, such as the Khawārij.

The second interpretation is that on the Day of Resurrection they will be humbled—that is, abased—and will toil and grow weary. I say: This is the correct interpretation, for several reasons.

First, on this understanding, the adverbial expression relates to what immediately follows it. That is: on the Day of the Overwhelming Event, faces will be humbled, toiling, weary and burning. On the first interpretation, however, it can relate only to

---

## Page 8223

His words “will burn.” The word “humbled” would then qualify “faces,” but would be separated from the noun it qualifies by an intervening expression connected to another, later adjective. The intended order would thus be: “Humbled faces, toiling and weary, will on that Day burn in a scorching Fire.” Yet assuming such a rearrangement departs from the normal rule, which is to retain the discourse in its existing structure and order rather than change its sequence. Moreover, such a rearrangement may only be assumed when there is a contextual indication supporting it. It is impermissible when it creates ambiguity, since it would confuse the addressee. Clearly, there is no contextual indication here supporting such a rearrangement; rather, the context indicates the opposite. To intend such a rearrangement in wording like this would therefore run counter to clarity, and requiring the addressee to understand it would be to demand the impossible.

The second reason is that Allah mentions both the faces of the wretched and the faces of the blessed in this surah, saying thereafter:

“Faces on that Day will be blissful,
pleased with their striving,
in a lofty Garden.”

Clearly, He describes them as enjoying bliss on the Day of Resurrection, not in this world, since the latter would not constitute praise. The discourse must therefore be consistent, with the two groups described in parallel rather than differently. Accordingly, the faces of the wretched are likewise described as they will be in the Hereafter.

The third reason is that a parallel to this division occurs in His words:

“Faces on that Day will be radiant.”

---

## Page 8224

looking at their Lord.”

“And faces on that Day will be gloomy,
expecting a crushing calamity to befall them.”

And His words:

“Faces on that Day will be bright,
laughing and rejoicing.
And faces on that Day will be dust-stained,
covered in darkness.
Those are the disbelievers, the wicked.”

All of this describes the condition of their faces in the Hereafter, not in this world.

The fourth reason is that the Quran does not describe faces in terms of deeds; rather, it mentions the signs visible upon them, as in His words:

“Their mark is on their faces.”

And His words:

“If We willed, We could show them to you, and you would recognize them by their marks.”

And His words:

“You recognize disapproval in the faces of those who disbelieve. They almost assault those who recite Our verses to them.”

This is because toil and weariness do not pertain to faces alone, unlike visible marks and signs.

The fifth reason is that if His words, “humbled, toiling and weary,” were taken to describe them in this world, this wording would convey no censure. Indeed, it is closer to praise. At most, it describes a quality shared by worshippers among the believers and worshippers among the disbelievers, and censure cannot rest on a shared quality. Had a quality exclusive to the disbelievers been intended, it would have said, for example: “humbled before idols, toiling for other than Allah, wearying themselves in obedience to Satan.” Nothing in the wording indicates that this description applies exclusively to disbelievers or that it is blameworthy. Nor does the Quran condemn this description as such or threaten punishment for it. Thus, interpreting it in this sense departs from the Quran’s established manner of address.

---

## Page 8225

The sixth reason is that this description applies only to some disbelievers, yet there is no justification for such a restriction. Those disbelievers who do not engage in worship are more numerous, and those among them who transgress their own religion face a more severe punishment in this world and the Hereafter. Those among them who refrain from what is agreed to be forbidden and fulfill what is agreed to be obligatory will not receive the same punishment as those who invoke another god alongside Allah, take a life Allah has made sacred except with just cause, and commit unlawful sexual intercourse. Thus, if, on this interpretation, unbelief and punishment are more extensive and severe among the excluded category, this restriction would be the very opposite of what is called for.

The seventh reason is that this wording would initially discourage worship and religious devotion. If it were then qualified as referring to the worship of disbelievers and religious innovators—although the wording contains no such qualification—this would amount to an attempt to rectify the wording by supplying something it does not mention.

---

## Page 8226

Surah al-Balad

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said concerning the words of the Most High:

“Have We not given him two eyes,
a tongue and two lips,
and shown him the two ways?”

Guidance resides in the heart. These three organs are constantly moving and engaged in deeds that count either for a person or against him. This is unlike what moves within the body, to which neither reward nor punishment attaches, and unlike the other outward organs, which are mostly still and move little compared with these three.

These are the three referred to in the saying reported from Jesus son of Mary, peace be upon him:

“One whose silence is reflection, whose speech is remembrance, and whose gaze draws lessons.”

A hadith recorded by Ibn Abī Hātim describes the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as frequently silent, constantly reflective, and continually sorrowful. Silence and reflection pertain to the tongue and the heart, respectively. As for sorrow, what is meant is not the pain caused by missing something desired or experiencing something disliked, for that is prohibited and was not characteristic of him. Rather, what was intended was his concern and alertness regarding the matters that lay ahead of him. This involves both the heart and the eye.

---

## Page 8227

The two Sahīhs also contain the report of Ibn ‘Abbās concerning him:

“Whenever he rose at night to pray, he would look towards the sky and recite the ten verses at the end of Surah Āl ‘Imrān.”

Thus he combined remembrance, observation, and reflection. Observation encompasses both the heart’s contemplation and the eye’s gaze. Likewise, remembrance with the tongue must be accompanied by remembrance in the heart.

Since observation is the starting point and remembrance the culmination—for observation precedes perception and knowledge, whereas remembrance follows them—He mentioned the organ of observation before the organs of remembrance, and concluded by mentioning His guidance of the sovereign that brings these faculties together, which itself observes and remembers. This also explains why the speculative theologians concern themselves with the contemplation that leads to knowledge, while the Sufis concern themselves with the remembrance that reinforces knowledge.

He, glorified be He, mentioned the tongue and the lips because they are the organs that articulate speech. As for the air, throat, lining of the palate, uvula, and teeth, they form a connected system, with the movement of each linked to that of the others, like the other parts of the palate. The tongue and lips, however, are separate.

Furthermore, since the lips are at the outermost point, they articulate the letters that bring things together: bā’, fā’, mīm, and wāw. Bā’ and fā’ are the two letters expressing causation: bā’ always conveys attachment and causation, while fā’ likewise conveys immediate succession and causation. Through causes, things are connected to one another.

---

## Page 8228

As for mīm and wāw, they convey gathering and encompassing. Do you not see that mīm represents a plurality of addressees in all five pronominal forms: the attached and independent nominative and accusative pronouns, and the genitive pronoun? Examples are (antum: you), (‘alimtum: you knew), (iyyākum: you, as an object), (‘ilmukum: your knowledge), and (bikum: with you). It likewise represents a plurality of third persons in all five forms. Any pronoun is either first, second, or third person; singular, dual, or plural; and nominative, accusative, or genitive. Thus it encompasses them all without restriction.

Unrestricted plurality is expressed by mīm itself, whereas plurality specifically numbering two is expressed by adding the dual marker, alif, as in antumā (you two), ‘alimtumā (you two knew), and the remaining forms. Accordingly, wāw was added to express unrestricted plurality, producing ‘alayhimū (upon them) and antumū (you), just as alif was added for the dual. Those who omit it do so for ease of pronunciation, and because the absence of a marker is itself a marker. Mīm thus became common to both, with the distinction supplied by alif, or by its absence together with wāw.

As for wāw, it marks third-person plural pronouns in forms such as qālū (they said) and the like. As for the attached forms, such as iyyākum and hum (they), both dialectal forms are used.

Since wāw came to complete the independent nominative pronoun, and yā’ to complete the feminine pronoun, yā’ came to mark the feminine generally, in all its states, because the feminine follows the masculine. The singular, both masculine and feminine, precedes the dual and plural, for what is single precedes what is composite. Alif then became the general marker of the dual, in explicit nouns and pronouns alike, just as wāw marks the masculine plural. And yā’ was made the marker of both the accusative and the genitive

---

## Page 8229

in explicit dual and plural nouns; for explicit nouns precede pronouns and are stronger than them. Thus yā’ had a greater claim than alif to occur there: where greater strength was required, wāw was used, and where an intermediate degree was required, yā’ was used.

As for explicit plural nouns, wāw marks the sound masculine plural, just as alif marks the dual. Hence it is pronounced even where there is no case inflection. In the accusative and genitive, however, each is changed to yā’ to distinguish the cases. This is because explicit nouns throughout Arabic refer to the third person rather than the person addressed. Wāw is the strongest of the weak letters, and damma—the short vowel corresponding to it—is the strongest vowel, owing to the gathering it involves and its final position. Wāw was therefore assigned to the plural. Alif is the lightest of the weak letters and was therefore assigned to the dual, since yā’ had already been assigned to the feminine in the singular nominative, which is the basic form, as when you say: … (1).

Mīm occurs in expressions such as Allāhumma (O Allah) to indicate all the names. For the labial sound encompasses the strength extending from the first articulation point of the letters to the last. It is like a final seal containing everything in what preceded it, and more. Thus it encompasses the strong letters and was made to encompass nouns, both explicit nouns and pronouns, and to join individual words and clauses. Wāw and fā’ are both coordinating conjunctions, and fā’ links one clause to another.

Since nūn is close to al-fīha and is nasal, it was assigned to the feminine plural

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in the original.

---

## Page 8230

because it ranks below the masculine plural. He used the dual for the eyes and the lips because the eyes are the heart’s sentinels, and no bodily organs are more closely connected to the heart than the eyes. Hence He mentions them together in His words:

“And We turn their hearts and eyes away.”

“In which hearts and eyes will turn about.”

“And when eyes grew wild and hearts reached the throats.”

“Hearts that Day will tremble.”

“Their eyes will be downcast.”

This is also because both possess sight: the heart’s outward vision is through the eyes, while its inward vision is through itself alone. Likewise, the tongue is the male, and the two lips are its female counterpart.

## Page 8231

Surah al-Shams (The Sun)

Shaykh al-Islam Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section

Concerning the words of Allah Most High:

“By the sun and its brightness,”
“and the moon when it follows it,”
“and the day when it reveals it,”
“and the night when it covers it.”

The feminine pronoun in “reveals it” and “covers it” has no preceding referent other than the sun. This therefore implies that the day reveals the sun and that the night covers it. “Revealing” means uncovering and making apparent, while “covering” means concealing and enveloping.

It is well known that night and day are periods of time. When an action is attributed to time, as when one says, “This period—or this day—brings cold, causes cooling, or makes the earth yield vegetation,” and similar expressions, what is meant is that this occurs during it. Likewise, time is described as distressing, harsh, ill-fated, cold, hot, pleasant, or disagreeable, whereas what is intended is a description of what occurs within it. Thus, a thing’s acting and its being described in a certain way are understood in the manner appropriate to it—each thing according to its own nature.

---

## Page 8232

Thus, the day reveals the sun and the night covers it, even though the sun’s appearance causes the day and its disappearance causes the night.

Allah has indicated this in His words:

“By the sun and its brightness.”

He thus attributes the brightness to the sun. This brightness encompasses the entire day, as He says:

“Or the heaven? He built it, raised its height and proportioned it, darkened its night and brought forth its daylight.”

He also says:

“By the morning brightness, and by the night when it grows still.”

As for His words:

“By the heaven and the One who built it,
and by the earth and the One who spread it,
and by a soul and the One who fashioned it,
then inspired it with its wickedness and its righteousness,”

it has been said that mā is a nominalizing particle, so that the meaning is: “By the heaven and Allah’s building of it, by the earth and Allah’s spreading of it, and by a soul and Allah’s fashioning of it.” The agent must be included in the sentence. The verbal noun cannot here be construed as expressing the action alone, as in “and its construction,” because the agent is mentioned in the clauses “and the One who built it” and “and the One who spread it.” The verb necessarily has an agent in the sentence, as well as an object. Both agent and object must therefore be included in the expanded wording.

However, if mā is nominalizing, it is a particle containing no pronoun. The subject pronoun in banāhā (“built it”) would then refer to someone not mentioned, but known from the context. The expanded wording would be: “By the heaven and Allah’s building of it.” This departs from both the default construction and the apparent meaning.

---

## Page 8233

The second view is that it is a relative pronoun, meaning “the One who built it” and “the One who spread it.” Mā (“what”) has a general, indeterminate sense, suitable for things that do not possess knowledge and for the attributes of those who do, as in His words, Exalted is He:

“I do not worship what you worship, nor do you worship what I worship.”

And His words:

“Then marry women of your choice.”

This meaning also applies to His words:

“And by the One who created the male and the female.”

Besides being the apparent meaning and the default reading of the wording, this interpretation is also fuller in meaning. Swearing by the agent includes swearing by his action, unlike swearing by the action alone. Moreover, most oaths in the Quran are by entities, whether acting or otherwise. He swears by the action itself, as in His words:

“By those ranged in ranks,
then those who drive away with force,
then those who recite the Reminder.”

And His words, “By those who draw out,” and “By those sent forth,” and similar passages.

Sometimes He, glorified is He, swears by created things themselves, and sometimes by their Lord and Creator, as in His words:

“By the Lord of heaven and earth.”

And His words:

“And by the One who created the male and the female.”

At other times, He swears both by them and by their Lord. In this surah, He swore by a created thing and its action; He also swore by a created thing without its action, then swore by its Maker.

---

## Page 8234

For He said:

“By the sun and its morning brightness,
and by the moon when it follows it,
and by the day when it reveals it,
and by the night when it covers it.”

Thus He swore by the sun, the moon, the night, and the day, and by their effects and actions, just as He distinguished between them in His words:

“And among His signs are the night and the day, the sun and the moon.”

He also said:

“Each floats in an orbit.”

For it is through the actions and effects of these things that the well-being of the children of Adam and all other living creatures is sustained.

He said:

“By the sun and its morning brightness.”

He did not say “its daytime” or “its light,” because “morning brightness” denotes both light and heat, and it is through light and heat that the well-being of His servants is sustained.

Then He swore by heaven, earth, and the soul. Without mentioning any action of theirs alongside them, He mentioned their Maker, saying:

“And by the One who built it,”

“And by the One who spread it,”

“And by a soul and the One who fashioned it.”

It was not fitting to swear by the soul’s actions, because it performs both righteous and wicked deeds, whereas He, glorified is He, swears only by what is held in high esteem among His creatures. Yet He conveyed implicitly in the oath that He is the Creator of its actions, saying:

“And by the One who fashioned it,
then inspired it with its wickedness and its righteousness.”

Since He has made clear that He is the Creator of the actions of the servant, whose agency, choice, and power are most evident, then all the more certainly must He be the Creator of the actions of the sun, the moon, the night, and the day.

---

## Page 8235

As for heaven and earth, they have no manifest activity held in such esteem as to warrant an oath, apart from what is manifest in the sun, the moon, the night, and the day. Heaven and earth are greater than the sun, the moon, the night, and the day, while the soul is the noblest of created living beings. It was therefore fitting to swear by the Maker of these mighty things. His swearing by their Maker also drew attention to His being the Maker of what they contain: the sun, the moon, the night, and the day. Thus, the passage includes oaths by the Maker of these creatures, by the creatures themselves, and by their effects and benefits for the children of Adam.

He concluded the oath with the soul, which was the last of created beings, for Allah created Adam on Friday, last of all created beings. He made clear that He creates all its actions, thereby indicating that He creates all the actions of everything else.

Alongside His declaration that His creation encompasses all existing things in their various ranks—even the servant’s actions, which fall into righteousness and wickedness—He, glorified is He, also made clear that actions are divided into good and evil, and those who perform them into the successful and the unsuccessful, the blessed and the wretched. This entails commands and prohibitions, promises and warnings.

This therefore refutes the Magian Qadariyyah, who exclude the actions of His servants from His creation and inspiration, and the polytheistic Qadariyyah, who nullify His commands and prohibitions, His promises and warnings, by appealing to His decree and predestination.

---

## Page 8236

It has been said concerning His words:

“Successful indeed is the one who purifies it,”
“and failed indeed is the one who corrupts it,”

that the pronoun refers to Allah, meaning: “Successful indeed is the one whose soul Allah purifies, and failed indeed is the one whose soul Allah corrupts.” This contradicts the apparent meaning and departs considerably from the Quran’s characteristic manner of expression. The more fitting wording would then have been: “Successful indeed is she whom Allah purifies, and failed indeed is she whom He corrupts.” This is weak. Moreover, His words, “And inspired it with its wickedness and its righteousness,” explain divine predestination. There is therefore no need to mention it again immediately afterwards in such a short surah.

For this reason, only this verse, rather than the latter passage, is reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as evidence establishing predestination. Thus, in Sahīh Muslim, Abū al-Aswad al-Du’alī reported:

‘Imrān ibn Husayn said to me, “Tell me about what people do today and strive to accomplish: is it something already decreed for them and settled by a prior decree, or is it something they face anew in response to what their Prophet has brought them and the proof established against them?”

I replied, “Rather, it is something already decreed for them and settled for them.”

He said, “Would that not then be injustice?”

I was greatly alarmed by this and said, “Everything is Allah’s creation and belongs to Him. He is not questioned about what He does, but they will be questioned.”

He said to me, “May Allah have mercy on you! I asked you this only to safeguard your understanding. Two men from Muzaynah came to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, tell us about what people do today and strive to accomplish: is it something already decreed for them and settled concerning them as part of a decree

---

## Page 8237

already determined, or is it something they face anew in response to what their Prophet has brought them and the proof established against them?’

He replied, ‘No. Rather, it is something already decreed for them and settled for them. This is confirmed in the Book of Allah, Mighty and Majestic:

“By the soul and the One Who fashioned it,
and inspired it with its wickedness and its righteousness.”’”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, thus explained that Allah’s words, “And inspired it with its wickedness and its righteousness,” confirm what he had stated concerning the decree. The hadith concerns the prior decree contained in Allah’s knowledge, His writing, and His speech; only the extreme Qadariyyah deny this. The Quranic passage, however, concerns Allah’s creation of people’s actions, which goes further, for the Magian-like Qadariyyah deny this. Thus, the Quranic passage establishes what the hadith states and more. This is why the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, presented it as confirmation. This can be explained in several ways.

First: once a person knows that Allah is the One Who inspires wickedness and righteousness, and that this involves neither injustice, as the Iblīs-like Qadariyyah claim, nor any contradiction of divine commands and prohibitions, promises and threats, as the polytheistic Qadariyyah claim, he has no grounds relating to predestination for disputing that Allah recorded and decreed this before it came into existence. This is why most of the Qadariyyah who deny the creation of human actions have acknowledged the prior decree. No member of the Qadariyyah has affirmed that Allah creates people’s actions while denying, on grounds of predestination, that Allah is their Creator.

---

## Page 8238

Second:

Once it is established that Allah creates a person’s actions and inspires wickedness and righteousness, these actions are part of His creation. The doubt that arose among the Qadariyyah, concerning which the two men from Muzaynah questioned the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, relates specifically to human actions for which people are rewarded or punished. They did not deny, on grounds of predestination, that Allah decreed what He Himself creates before its existence. Those among them who denied this did so only when the matter of human actions became unclear to them.

These people say that Allah decrees things before they exist, except for human actions, felicity and wretchedness. They maintain that He should not know these until they occur, because they consider it reprehensible for a ruler to issue a command when he knows that the person charged with it will not obey him and that it will instead cause that person harm. Various authors writing on the principles of jurisprudence and other subjects have reported the Muʿtazilah’s disagreement on this point. They say:

“Allah may command a person to do something He knows that person will not do, contrary to the position of the Muʿtazilah.”

This is because some among the Muʿtazilah dispute this, although most do not; only one group among them disagrees.

Since the Quran establishes that He inspires the soul with its wickedness and its righteousness, these are part of what He brings into being. Thus, the Qadariyyah’s objection to His having decreed this before its existence no longer stands, just as they have no objection to His predetermining the entities and attributes He creates. As for those among the deniers of the divine attributes, or some of them, who deny His predetermination in knowledge, such people

---

## Page 8239

have a different basis for their position, unrelated to the divine attributes.

Third:

He has inspired wickedness and righteousness, and He is the Creator of a person’s actions. He must therefore know what He creates before creating it, as He says:

“Does He who created not know?”

For an agent acting by choice wills what he does, and willing necessarily entails conceiving what is willed. This constitutes knowledge of the intended action. Since His creation of a thing necessarily entails His knowledge of it, this is the foundation of prior divine predestination. As for what Allah, glorified be He, has taught through His speech and His Books, there is no dispute about it. This is clear in all things, in this matter and others.

For when He, glorified be He, inspires wickedness and righteousness, the one who inspires must distinguish between wickedness and righteousness and know that this act He intends to bring about is wickedness, while that act He intends to bring about is righteousness. Otherwise, He could not inspire wickedness and righteousness. This makes clear how apt was the statement of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that the verse confirms what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had reported concerning prior divine predestination.

His words, glorified be He,

“And inspired it with its wickedness and its righteousness,”

indicate the divine law just as they indicate predestination. For had He said, “And inspired it with its actions,” as people say

---

## Page 8240

“the Creator of people’s actions,” there would have been no distinction in this between good and evil, what is loved and what is hated, or what is commanded and what is forbidden. Rather, it would have provided an argument for the polytheists among the antinomians and the Jabriyyah, who reject commands and prohibitions, and moral goodness and badness; for He created people’s actions. But when He said:

“And inspired it with its wickedness and its righteousness,”

these words distinguished between the good that is commanded and the evil that is forbidden, establishing that actions fall into good and evil, although He, exalted is He, is the Creator of both categories. This is the Quran’s approach in numerous passages: it mentions the believer and the disbeliever, their good and evil deeds, and His promise and warning; and it mentions that He is the Creator of both categories, as in His words:

“He leads astray whom He wills and guides whom He wills,”

and similar passages.

It is concerning this principle that the Jabriyyah and the Qadariyyah have gone astray.

The Magian Qadariyyah said: Actions fall into good and evil because of qualities inherent in them, and the servant himself brings them into being, independently of Allah’s power and without His creating them.

The Jabriyyah, by contrast, said: Rather, the servant is compelled to act. Compulsion is a reality that necessitates the occurrence of his actions whenever the causes Allah creates are present, and makes their occurrence impossible whenever any of those causes is absent. If he is compelled, an action cannot be good or evil by virtue of any quality inherent in it.

---

## Page 8241

This is the approach of Abū Abd Allāh al-Rāzī and others among the Jabriyyah who deny that actions are intrinsically divided into good and evil. The former is the approach of Abū al-Husayn al-Basrī and others among the Qadariyyah who maintain that the servant alone brings his action into being, that knowledge of this is either self-evident or inferential, and that actions are intrinsically divided into good and evil, knowledge of which is self-evident.

Abū al-Husayn is the leading authority among the later Muʿtazilah, possessing greater intellect and merit than most of his peers. Yet his knowledge of the Prophetic traditions, the meanings of the Quran, and the way of the early generations is limited. On this issue, he and Abū Abd Allāh al-Rāzī stand at opposite extremes, although each possesses some truth that the other lacks.

Abū al-Husayn claims that it is self-evident that the servant brings his action into being. Al-Rāzī likewise claims that it is self-evident that a newly originated, contingent action depends upon a determining factor whose presence necessitates the action’s occurrence and whose absence makes its occurrence impossible. Indeed, both speak truthfully concerning the self-evident knowledge they describe. Each side then believes that this self-evident knowledge invalidates what the other claims to be self-evident, but that is not so. Rather, both are truthful and correct concerning the self-evident knowledge they describe. Each errs only in denying what the other affirms regarding creation. For there is no incompatibility between the servant’s bringing his action into being and the fact that

---

## Page 8242

this bringing into being is possible through the will of Allah Most High. Hence, the position of the adherents of the pure Sunnah is that the servant truly performs his action, just as Abū al-Husayn maintained was self-evident. They do not say that he does not truly act, or that he does not act at all, as those inclined toward compulsion say, such as the school of Abū Abd Allāh al-Rāzī.

At the same time, they maintain that Allah is the Creator of both this agent and his action, that He is the One who made him truly act, and that He creates the actions of His servants. In this they agree with those who affirm this among the Asharīs—the school of al-Rāzī—and others, rather than with the Qadariyyah, such as Abū al-Husayn and his followers, who say that Allah did not create the actions of His servants.

For this reason, the imams, such as Imam Ahmad and earlier imams such as al-Awzāī and others, explicitly rejected unqualified statements either affirming or denying compulsion. Thus, one should neither say, “Allah compelled His servants,” nor say, “He did not compel them.” The term “compulsion” has multiple meanings and is ambiguous. Saying “He compelled them” suggests that Allah compels them to do good and evil without their choosing; saying “He did not compel them” suggests that they do whatever they wish without His choosing. Both are erroneous. We have discussed this at length elsewhere.

The point here is that these two groups believed divine decree and revealed law to be incompatible, just as the Magians and polytheists believed. They therefore said: “If He creates the action, it is impossible

---

## Page 8243

for the act to be good in itself and merit reward, or evil and merit punishment.”

The Qadariyyah then said: “But actions do fall into these categories; therefore, He does not create them.”

The Jabriyyah said: “But He does create them; therefore, actions do not fall into these categories.”

However, those among the Jabriyyah who acknowledge the messengers recognize this distinction solely on the basis of the Lawgiver’s commands and prohibitions. They say: “He may command whatever He wills, without any inherent quality in it being the reason, and prohibit whatever He wills, without any inherent quality in it being the reason.” Regarding both His creation and His commands, they say: “He does whatever He wills and decrees whatever He wishes.”

As for someone overcome by personal opinion or desire, he casts off the Lawgiver’s authority once he sees actions as compelled. Such people say what the polytheists say:

“Had Allah willed, neither we nor our forefathers would have associated partners with Him, nor would we have forbidden anything.”

Whoever acknowledges revealed law, commands and prohibitions, and moral good and evil, but not divine decree or Allah’s creation of actions—as is the position of the Mutazilah—belongs to the Magian Qadariyyah, who resemble the Magians. The Mutazilah bear considerable resemblance to the Magians and the Jews.

Whoever acknowledges divine ordainment and decree, Allah’s creation of actions, and His all-encompassing lordship, but denies right and wrong, guidance and misguidance, and good deeds and evil deeds, bears a resemblance to the polytheists and the Sabians.

---

## Page 8244

Such was also the case with Jahm ibn Safwān and his followers when he debated the people of India, just as it was with the Mutazilah when they debated the Persian Magians. The Magians, however, are closer to the truth than the polytheists. Whoever denies divine commands and prohibitions, or does not acknowledge them, is an outright polytheist and disbeliever, greater in disbelief than the Jews, Christians and Magians. This is found among many antinomian speculative theologians and Sufis, and others like them. Such people did not emerge during the era of the Companions and their Successors because they lived so close to the time of prophethood. Rather, those who emerged were the Magian Qadariyyah, whose doctrine holds commands and prohibitions, reward and punishment in high regard. They are therefore closer to the Quran, the Sunnah, the Messenger and the religion than those who nullify commands and prohibitions, for the latter are among the worst of creation.

As for the Qadariyyah who resemble Iblīs, they acknowledge that commands and prohibitions come from Allah, and also acknowledge that divine ordainment and decree come from Him. Yet they say:

“This involves ignorance and injustice. Its contradictory nature makes it ignorance and folly, while punishing the servant for what He has created within him makes it injustice.”

Such was the case with Iblīs, who said:

“Because You have led me astray, I will surely make evil seem attractive to them on earth

---

## Page 8245

and will surely lead them all astray.” He thus acknowledged that Allah had led him astray, then treated this as a motive requiring him, in turn, to lead Adam’s descendants astray. Iblīs was the first to show enmity towards Allah, to transgress with regard to His creation and command, and to oppose an explicit revealed text with analogical reasoning.

For this reason, some of the early generations said:

“The first to reason by analogy was Iblīs.”

Allah commanded him to prostrate before Adam, but he objected to this command, saying, “I am better than him,” and refused to prostrate. He was thus the first to show enmity towards Allah. He was ignorant and unjust: ignorant of the wisdom in Allah’s command, and unjust through his arrogance, which combined rejection of the truth with contempt for others.

Then, in saying to his Lord, “Because You have led me astray, I shall surely do such and such,” he made Allah’s action—His leading him astray—a justification and a motive for leading the children of Adam astray. In this, he impugned Allah’s action and command, alleging that it was reprehensible: “So I too shall do what is reprehensible.” He thus drew an analogy between himself and his Lord and likened himself to his Lord. In this way, he sought to imitate divine lordship, as is established in Sahīh Muslim, on the authority of Jābir, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Iblīs sets up his throne upon the sea, then sends out his troops. Those who cause the greatest turmoil are closest to him in rank. One of them comes and says, ‘I persisted with him until he did such and such.’ Then another comes and says, ‘I persisted with him until I separated him from his wife.’ He then embraces him, draws him close, and says, ‘You are the one! You are the one!’”

---

## Page 8246

The Qadariyya sought to declare Allah free of foolishness, and their intention in this was good. For He, glorified be He, is exalted far above what the wrongdoers—Iblīs and his hosts—say. He is a just Judge. Yet their understanding proved inadequate, and a measure of ignorance led them to believe that declaring Him free of such defects required denying His power over His servants’ actions, His creation of those actions, and the all-encompassing scope of His will. Thus, while they disputed with Iblīs and his party on one point, Iblīs gained mastery over them from another direction.

This is among the gravest perils of disputing about religion without knowledge or without adhering to the truth. This is the speculative theology condemned by the early generations: its practitioner counters falsehood with falsehood and innovation with innovation.

Then various groups who affirmed Allah’s all-encompassing power debated with them, seeking to establish that Allah is the Creator of all things; that whatever Allah wills comes to pass, and whatever He does not will does not come to pass; and that He has power over all things. Their capacity and knowledge likewise proved inadequate. They believed that this could not be established unless they denied Allah’s love and good pleasure, the good and bad qualities by which He has distinguished certain actions from others, and His wisdom and mercy. Accordingly, they deemed every act possible for Him, without holding Him to be above injustice or any other act.

Some went still further, entirely nullifying commands and prohibitions, promises of reward and threats of punishment. These inclined towards irjā (the Murji’ite doctrine), just as the former inclined towards the Wa‘īdiyya’s emphasis on threatened punishment.

The Wa‘īdiyya said:

---

## Page 8247

“Every evildoer will remain in the Fire forever, never to leave it.” The Khawārij further said: “He is an unbeliever.”

The extremist Murji’a denied that anyone among the people of the qiblah would be punished. Those who openly avowed unbelief denied altogether the threatened punishment in the Hereafter, as various groups among the Ittihādiyya, the philosophizers, the Qarāmita and the Bātiniyya do. These determinist Murji’a showed greater disbelief in commands and prohibitions, promises of reward and threats of punishment than the Mu‘tazila, who upheld the doctrines of the Wa‘īdiyya and the Qadariyya. As for the moderate determinist Murji’a, who acknowledge commands and prohibitions, promises of reward and threats of punishment, and that some among the people of the qiblah will enter the Fire, they are the closest to Ahl al-Sunnah.

Al-Tirmidhī narrated that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The Qadariyya and the Murji’a were cursed on the tongues of seventy prophets, of whom I am the last.”

Nevertheless, the Mu‘tazila among the Qadariyya are sounder than the Jabriyya, the Murji’a and their like in their knowledge and practice of the Sharia. Their discussions of the principles of jurisprudence and of obedience to commands and prohibitions are better than those of the Murji’a among the Ash‘arīs and others. The latter’s discussions of the principles of jurisprudence are severely deficient; likewise, they fail to give due weight to acts of obedience and disobedience.

Yet in the fundamentals of religion, they are sounder than the former, for they affirm aspects of Allah’s attributes, power and creation that the former do not affirm. And this category ranks higher.

---

## Page 8248

For this reason, the Murji’a were generally better than the Qadariyya. Indeed, even jurists from Kufa and elsewhere embraced the doctrine of the Murji’a, whereas none of the jurists or imams of the early generations adhered to Mu‘tazilism.

Section

If, then, error concerning divine decree has arisen sometimes through denying divine decree and Allah’s creation of acts, sometimes through denying the revealed Law and the threatened punishment, and sometimes through attributing injustice to the Lord, then this surah contains a refutation of all these groups. His statement, Exalted is He:

“Then inspired it with its wickedness and its righteousness”

affirms divine decree through the words “Then inspired it.” It also affirms the servant’s actions by ascribing wickedness and righteousness to his own soul, making it clear that the soul itself is wicked or righteous. Furthermore, the words “its wickedness and its righteousness” affirm the distinction between good and evil, and between commands and prohibitions.

His subsequent statement:

“He has succeeded who purifies it, and he has failed who corrupts it”

affirms the servant’s actions, the promise of reward and the threat of punishment, through the success of whoever purifies his soul and the failure of whoever corrupts it. This explicitly refutes the Magian Qadariyya and the Jabriyya concerning the revealed Law or the servant’s actions. They are those who deny the truth.

---

## Page 8249

As for those who accuse the Creator of injustice, He has demonstrated His justice through His words:

“By the soul and Him who proportioned it.”

Proportioning means giving a balanced form. He thus makes clear that He is just in fashioning the soul into which He inspired its wickedness and its righteousness. He then mentions the punishment of those who rejected His messengers and transgressed, and that He does not fear the consequences of His retribution against those who oppose His messengers. This makes clear that whoever denies either of these truths will face Allah’s retribution, and He does not fear its consequences, just as He exacted retribution upon Satan and his forces. Such a person’s complaints of injustice against his Lord and his imputing folly to Him only bring about his own destruction; he will not harm Allah in the least.

“For the servants will never be able to harm Allah, nor will they ever be able to benefit Him. Even if the first and last of them, humans and jinn alike, had the heart of the most righteous man among them, that would add nothing to His dominion. And even if the first and last of them, humans and jinn alike, had the heart of the most wicked man among them, that would diminish His dominion in no way.”

Thus, when ʿImrān ibn Husayn asked Abū al-Aswad al-Duʾalī about this to gauge his understanding—“Would that constitute injustice?”—he explained that this was not injustice on His part and expressed fear in view of His words:

“Glory be to Him! Exalted is He far above what they say!”

He also mentioned the hadith of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his citing this verse as evidence. It has thus become clear that the Qadariyya who engage in false disputation either deny what

---

## Page 8250

the Lord has reported concerning His creation or His commands, or accuse Him of injustice in His judgment. Yet He, glorified be He, is truthful and just, as He, Most High, says:

“The word of your Lord has been fulfilled in truth and justice. None can change His words, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing.”

For speech is either performative or declarative. His declarations are true, never false; His performative speech—His commands bringing things into being and His legislative commands—is just, never unjust. The Magian Qadariyya denied what He reported concerning His creation and His legislation in matters of religion, while the Iblis-like Qadariyya accused Him of injustice in the two taken together, or in each separately.

It has thus become clear that those within the Muslim community who divide and disagree do so because they abandon some of the truth with which Allah sent His Prophet, embrace falsehood contrary to it, and share in falsehood that contradicts what the Messenger brought. This is akin to the unbelievers’ opposition to the believers, as He, Most High, says:

“Those messengers—some We have favored above others,”

through to His words:

“Had Allah willed, they would not have fought one another, but Allah does whatever He wills.”

When they share in a falsehood by which they oppose the believers who follow the messengers, they forget a portion of what they were reminded of. He then casts enmity and hatred among them, and they disagree with one another over another truth brought by the Messenger. Thus, one group believes in part of it and rejects another, while the other group believes in what the first rejects and rejects what the first believes. In this situation, both of these divided, opposing groups are blameworthy. This is characteristic of most

---

## Page 8251

division and disagreement within this community and others. This is one such instance. They shared the view that the Lord’s creation of the servant’s actions is incompatible with those actions being classified as good or bad. They shared this premise in their disputation, although it is neither true in itself nor supported by any sound proof. It is one of the two premises on which al-Rāzī relies in discussing the question of judging actions good or bad. In his al-Mahsūl and elsewhere, he held that the servant is compelled to act, that the action of someone compelled cannot be bad, and that therefore none of the servants’ actions can be bad.

This argument for denying such badness is rooted in the argument of the polytheists who denied the messengers and said:

“Had Allah willed, neither we nor our forefathers would have associated partners with Him, nor would we have forbidden anything.”

By affirming divine decree, they denied the wrongfulness of associating partners with Allah and of forbidding good things that Allah had not forbidden. Yet those who invoke compulsion to negate rulings, if they acknowledge the revealed law, are not like the polytheists in every respect. Thus, the speculative theologians who acknowledge the revealed law are not like the polytheists, even though they share in some of the polytheists’ falsehood. Nevertheless, among the speculative theologians and Sufis are groups so dominated by belief in compulsion that

---

## Page 8252

they come to disbelieve in commands and prohibitions, promises and threats, reward and punishment, whether in speech or in their disposition and conduct. This most often occurs with actions that accord with their desires. By this they seek to escape blame and punishment, yet it only increases their blame and punishment—like someone seeking refuge from scorching ground in a fire.

No one can consistently put this view into practice. In their dealings with one another, human beings cannot do without wanting certain things and commanding them, and hating other things and forbidding them. Whoever seeks to treat alike what is loved and what is hated, what is pleasing and what is displeasing, justice and injustice, knowledge and ignorance, misguidance and guidance, sound judgment and error, can never maintain that position. Rather, when something happens that he dislikes and that harms him, he rushes to ward it off and punish its perpetrator by whatever means he can, even to the point of transgression.

They are thus among the most unjust of people: they judge reprehensible acts of injustice and indecency differently depending on whether they are committed by themselves or others, or by those they favor or those they do not. They invoke divine decree in their own defense, but not in defense of their adversaries. You will find that when one of them does something for which he is praised, the outlook of the Qadarites comes to dominate his heart: he regards himself, rather than Allah, as the one who brought it about, and forgets Allah’s favor to him

---

## Page 8253

in inspiring him with piety.

Such a person is among the most unjust of all creatures, as Abū al-Faraj Ibn al-Jawzī said:

“When obeying Allah, you are a Qadarite; when disobeying Him, you are a fatalist. Whatever doctrine suits your desires, you adopt.”

Those who act justly are the opposite. When they perform a good deed, they thank Allah for it, knowing that He is the One who has endeared faith to them and beautified it in their hearts, and that He is the One who has made disbelief, defiance, and disobedience hateful to them:

“And those who, when they commit an indecent act or wrong themselves, remember Allah and seek forgiveness for their sins—and who forgives sins except Allah?—and do not knowingly persist in what they have done.”

Thus they followed the example of their father when he sinned:

“Then Adam received words from his Lord, and He accepted his repentance. Indeed, He is the Accepter of repentance, the Most Merciful.”

And he said:

“Our Lord, we have wronged ourselves. If You do not forgive us and have mercy upon us, we will surely be among the losers.”

Each of them says, “I acknowledge Your favor upon me, and I acknowledge my sin,” as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“The best way of seeking forgiveness is for a servant to say: ‘O Allah, You are my Lord; there is no god but You. You created me, and I am Your servant. I hold to Your covenant and promise as best I can. I seek refuge in You from the evil of what I have done. I acknowledge Your favor upon me, and I acknowledge my sin. So forgive me, for no one forgives sins but You.’”

As also stated in the authentic hadith: “Allah Most High says: ‘O My servants, it is only your deeds that are returned

---

## Page 8254

to you. Whoever finds good, let him praise Allah, and whoever finds evil, let him blame no one but himself.’” They also affirm what Allah Most High says:

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself.”

Ibn al-Qayyim—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

In this surah, Allah, glorified be He, mentioned Thamūd alone among the nations that rejected their messengers. Shaykh al-Islām Abū al-Abbās Taqī al-Dīn Ibn Taymiyyah said:

This—and Allah knows best—is an instance of drawing attention to the greater through the lesser. For none of the nations that rejected their messengers was less sinful or more lightly punished than they were (*), since He did not mention concerning them the sins He mentioned concerning ‘Ād, Midian, the people of Lot, and others. Thus, when He mentioned them alongside ‘Ād, He said:

“As for ‘Ād, they were arrogant in the land without right and said, ‘Who is mightier than us in strength?’ Did they not see that Allah, who created them, was mightier than them in strength? Yet they continued to reject Our signs.”

“As for Thamūd, We guided them, but they preferred blindness to guidance.”

Likewise, when He mentions them alongside the nations that rejected their messengers, He does not attribute to them the tyranny, arrogance, and evil deeds that He attributes to those others, such as sodomy, giving short measure and weight, and spreading corruption in the land, as in Surahs Hūd and al-Shu‘arā’ and elsewhere. Thus, besides associating partners with Allah, the people of Lot committed indecencies that

> **Footnotes:**
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd said (p. 139):
> This was mentioned by Ibn al-Qayyim, may Allah have mercy on him, in his book al-Tibyān fī aqsām al-Qur’ān (An Exposition of the Oaths in the Quran), pp. 19–20.

---

## Page 8255

none before them had committed. Besides associating partners with Allah, ‘Ād were tyrannical and arrogant, indulged in worldly luxury, exercised brutal force, and said:

“Who is mightier than us in strength?”

Besides associating partners with Allah, the people of Midian dealt unjustly in matters of wealth; and Pharaoh’s people spread corruption in the land and exalted themselves.

Each nation’s punishment was commensurate with its sins and crimes. ‘Ād were punished with a fierce, raging wind that nothing could withstand. The people of Lot were subjected to forms of punishment inflicted on no other nation. He combined their destruction with pelting them with stones from the sky, obliterating their sight, overturning their dwellings upon them—turning them upside down—and causing the earth to swallow them into the lowest depths. He punished the people of Shu‘ayb with fire that consumed both them and the wealth they had acquired through injustice and aggression. As for Thamūd, He destroyed them with the mighty blast, and they died instantly.

If this was His punishment of these people, whose sin, besides associating partners with Him, was hamstringing the she-camel Allah had made a sign for them, then whoever violates Allah’s prohibitions, treats His commands and prohibitions with contempt, slaughters His servants, and sheds their blood will suffer a more severe punishment. Whoever considers the state of the world, past and present, and the punishments inflicted upon those who spread corruption in the land, shed blood unjustly, stir up strife, and disdain what Allah has made inviolable, will know that deliverance in this world and the Hereafter belongs to those who believe and are mindful of Allah.

---

## Page 8256

Surah al-‘Alaq (The Clinging Clot)

The shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

The first revelation sent down to the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained the foundations of religion: the rational proofs establishing the existence and oneness of the Creator, the truthfulness of His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the Resurrection—both its possibility and its occurrence.

We have discussed this principle several times previously, explaining that the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, set out the rational and revealed proofs through which people are guided to their religion and to what brings them salvation and happiness in this world and the Hereafter. We have also explained that those who have devised foundations contrary to some of what he brought have laid the foundations of their own religion, not his. These foundations are invalid according to both reason and revelation, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

He also explained that many who associate themselves with religious knowledge and devotion are either deficient or negligent in their knowledge of what he brought in the way of

---

## Page 8257

revealed and rational proofs. One group has devised principles that contradict what he brought in both respects. Another group recognized this as innovation and turned away from it, identifying themselves with the Sunnah because they were free of the former group’s innovation. Yet they have neither followed the Sunnah properly nor upheld the revealed and rational proofs he brought.

Indeed, regarding the matters he conveys through revelation concerning his Lord and the Last Day, the most they do is believe his words without understanding what he has conveyed. They may even say that he himself did not know the meaning of what he conveyed, because they consider this to be the interpretation of the ambiguous passages, which Allah alone knows.

As for rational proofs, they may not even conceive that he brought rational foundations establishing what he conveys, such as the proofs establishing Allah’s oneness and attributes. Some acknowledge that he brought this in general terms, but do not know his proofs. They may even suppose that an argument they themselves use—such as inferring from the creation of the human being that his constituent substances had a beginning—is the Messenger’s own argument.

Many of these people believe that some of these matters cannot be known through reason, such as the Resurrection, the goodness of monotheism, justice and truthfulness, and the evil of polytheism and injustice

---

## Page 8258

and falsehood. The Quran sets out the rational proofs for these matters and rebukes those who fail to reason from them. It explains that through reason one can recognize the Resurrection, the goodness of worshipping Him alone and thanking Him, and the evil of associating partners with Him and being ungrateful for His blessings, as I have discussed at length elsewhere.

Many people know this by their innate disposition, yet deny reason’s ability to discern good and evil when the fundamentals of religion are presented according to the approach of Jahm’s negationist, determinist followers. This is true of most of what the proponents of falsehood say: their innate disposition leads them to say things that contradict their innovated beliefs.

Abū ʿAbd Allāh Ibn al-Jadd al-Aʿlā mentioned that he heard Abū al-Faraj Ibn al-Jawzī recite these two well-known verses at one of his preaching gatherings:

Suppose no messengers announcing resurrection had come to us, and Hell’s blazing fire had not been kindled—
Is it not an obligation, a duty owed, for servants to feel shame before their Benefactor?

Here he explicitly states that it is an obligation and a duty owed for created beings to feel shame before their Creator, who bestows blessings upon them. This explicitly affirms that thanking Him is an obligation and His due, even if there were no threat of punishment and no

---

## Page 8259

message announcing recompense. This demonstrates that thanking Him is obligatory and due, even supposing there were no punishment. This is a disputed matter, which we have discussed elsewhere, explaining that this is the correct position.

The consequence of committing what is forbidden is a decline in rank and the loss of many blessings one previously enjoyed, even if one is not punished with harm. It also shows that what is obligatory and what is due can be known self-evidently. Thus, someone who neglects an obligation or commits an evil deed, even if not subjected to painful punishment such as the Fire, is deprived of blessings and the means of obtaining them as recompense. Such is the recompense of someone who fails to give thanks for a blessing and instead shows ingratitude for it: to be deprived of it. Gratitude preserves blessings and brings their increase. Ingratitude, once the proof has been established, entails punishment; before that, it diminishes blessings rather than increasing them. Nevertheless, a messenger must be sent, whereupon one becomes deserving of either bliss or punishment, for there is no abode other than Paradise or Hell. Allah Most High says:

“We have certainly created man in the finest form,
then We returned him to the lowest of the low,
except those who believe and do righteous deeds, for theirs is an unending reward.”

This is discussed at length in various places.

The point here is that these fundamental principles were explained in the earliest revelation of the Quran. For the first Quranic revelation was:

“Read in the name of your Lord,”

according to the overwhelming majority of

---

## Page 8260

scholars. It has also been said that the first revelation was:

“O you wrapped in your cloak.”

This was reported from Jābir. The first view, however, is more sound. The hadith of ‘Ā’ishah in the two Sahīh collections makes clear that the first revelation was “Read in the name of your Lord,” which was revealed to him while he was in the cave of Hirā’, and that “The Cloaked One” was revealed later. This is indeed the fitting sequence, for His words “Read” command recitation, not the conveyance of the message; through this he became a prophet. His words “Arise and warn” command him to warn; through this he became a messenger charged with warning.

The two Sahīh collections relate, through al-Zuhrī, from ‘Urwah, that ‘Ā’ishah said:

“The revelation to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, began with true dreams during sleep. Every dream he saw would come true as clearly as the break of dawn. Then solitude was made dear to him. He would go to the cave of Hirā’ and devote himself to worship there for several nights before returning to his family, taking provisions for that purpose. Then he would return to Khadījah and replenish his provisions for a similar stay, until the truth came to him while he was in the cave of Hirā’.

“The angel came to him and said, ‘Read.’ He replied, ‘I cannot read.’ He related: ‘Then he seized me and squeezed me until I could bear no more. Then he released me and said, “Read.”

---

## Page 8261

I replied, “I cannot read.” Then he seized me and squeezed me a second time until I could bear no more. Then he released me and said, “Read.” I replied, “I cannot read.” Then he seized me and squeezed me a third time until I could bear no more. Then he released me and said:

“Read in the name of your Lord who created—
created man from a clinging substance.
Read, and your Lord is the Most Generous,
who taught by the pen—
taught man what he did not know.”’

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, returned with these words, his heart trembling. He went to Khadījah bint Khuwaylid and said, ‘Wrap me up! Wrap me up!’ They wrapped him up until his fear subsided. He told Khadījah what had happened and said, ‘I feared for myself.’

Khadījah said to him, ‘No! By Allah, Allah will never disgrace you. You maintain ties of kinship, bear others’ burdens, honour guests, provide people with what they lack, and give help where it is right to do so in times of hardship.’

Then Khadījah took him to Waraqah ibn Nawfal ibn Asad ibn ‘Abd

---

## Page 8262

al-‘Uzzā, Khadījah’s cousin. He had embraced Christianity during the pre-Islamic era. He could write in Hebrew and would write out passages from the Gospel in Arabic, as much as Allah willed him to write. He was an elderly man who had become blind.

Khadījah said to him, ‘Cousin, listen to your nephew.’

Waraqah asked him, ‘My nephew, what do you see?’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, told him what he had seen.

Waraqah said to him, ‘This is the bearer of revelation who was sent down to Moses. If only I were a young man then! If only I were alive when your people drive you out!’

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, asked, ‘Will they drive me out?’

He replied, ‘Yes. No one has ever brought anything like what you have brought without being met with hostility. If I live to see that day of yours, I will give you steadfast support.’”

Soon afterward, Waraqah died, and revelation ceased for a time. Ibn Shihāb al-Zuhrī said, ‘I heard Abū Salamah ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān say that Jābir ibn ‘Abd Allāh had told him that he heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, speaking

---

## Page 8263

about the pause in revelation:

“While I was walking, I heard a voice. I looked up towards heaven, and there was the angel who had come to me at Hirā’, sitting on a chair between heaven and earth. I went on until I fell to the ground. Then I came to my family and said, ‘Wrap me up! Wrap me up!’ So they wrapped me up. Then Allah Most High revealed, ‘O you wrapped in your cloak, arise and warn,’ down to His words, ‘And shun uncleanliness.’”’

This makes clear that Sūrat al-Muddaththir was revealed after that pause, and that this occurred after he saw the angel who had first come to him at Hirā’. He had thus seen the angel twice.
