This explains the hadith of Jābir transmitted through another chain, as the two compilers narrated from Yahyā ibn Abī Kathīr, who said:

“I asked Abū Salamah ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān about the first passage of the Quran to be revealed.

“He replied, ‘O you wrapped in your cloak.’

“I said, ‘People say it was “Read in the name of your Lord who created.”’

“Abū Salamah said, ‘I asked Jābir ibn ‘Abd Allāh about that and said to him just what you have said. Jābir replied, “I will tell you only what the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, told us. He said: ‘I stayed in retreat at Hirā’. When I had completed my retreat, I descended, and someone called out to me. I looked to my right but saw nothing. I looked to my left but saw nothing. I looked ahead of me but saw nothing. I looked behind me but saw nothing. Then I raised my head and saw something. I went to Khadījah and said, “Cover me up and pour cold water over me.” So they covered me up and poured cold water over me.

---

## Page 8264

He said: Then the following was revealed: “O you wrapped in your cloak, arise and warn, and magnify your Lord.”’”’”

This hadith agrees with the preceding one: Sūrat al-Muddaththir was revealed after he had descended from the mountain, while he was walking, and after the angel had called out to him. In the other narration, he explained that this angel was the one who had come to him at Hirā’. ‘Ā’ishah explained that “Read” was revealed at that time in the cave of Hirā’.

It seems, however, that he did not know that “Read” had been revealed then. He only knew that the Prophet had seen the angel before that; yet he could have seen him without hearing from him. ‘Ā’ishah’s hadith, however, provides additional information: the angel’s command that he recite “Read.”

In al-Zuhrī’s narration, he called this the “pause in revelation,” an expression also found in ‘Ā’ishah’s hadith. Al-Zuhrī may therefore have paraphrased Jābir’s hadith, calling the interval between the two sightings the “pause in revelation,” as ‘Ā’ishah explained it. Otherwise, if Jābir himself called it the “pause in revelation,” how could he say that no revelation had yet come down?

In any event, al-Zuhrī possessed both ‘Urwah’s hadith from ‘Ā’ishah and Abū Salamah’s hadith from Jābir. Should he and Yahyā ibn Abī Kathīr differ, al-Zuhrī has broader knowledge and a stronger memory. Yahyā, however, mentioned that he had asked Abū Salamah about the first revelation. Jābir thus related what he knew, not having known what had been revealed before that, whereas ‘Ā’ishah established and clarified the matter.

---

## Page 8265

And the verses of “Read” and “The Cloaked One” make this clear. The two hadiths agree with one another, with the Quran, and with reason’s indication that this is the appropriate sequence. If the first revelation was:

“Read in the name of your Lord who created—
created man from a clinging clot.
Read, and your Lord is the Most Generous,
who taught by the pen—
taught man that which he knew not,”

then the first verse affirms the Creator, exalted is He, as does the second. Both also provide evidence for the possibility of prophethood and for the prophethood of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

As for the first, He said, “Read in the name of your Lord who created,” then said, “Created man from a clinging clot.” He thus mentioned creation in general, then singled out the creation of man, stating that He created him from a clinging clot. This is something known to everyone: all people know that a human being comes into being in his mother’s womb and develops from a clinging clot. These are the descendants of Adam. The word “man” here is a generic noun encompassing all people; it does not include Adam, who was created from clay. The purpose of this verse is to present evidence for the Creator, exalted is He, and reasoning can proceed only from premises

---

## Page 8266

known to the person drawing the inference. The aim is to direct people and guide them, and they all know that human beings are created from clinging clots. As for Adam’s creation from clay, that is known only through what the prophets reported or through other evidence. Hence some disbelievers among the Dahriyyah and others who do not acknowledge prophethood deny it. This differs from the mention of his creation in other surahs, where it is mentioned in connection with what establishes prophethood. This surah, however, was the first revelation, and prophethood is established through it. It therefore does not mention what is known through transmitted accounts, but instead presents evidence known through reason, observation, and—for those who have not seen clinging clots—mass-transmitted reports.

He, glorified is He, mentions the creation of man from clinging clots: ‘alaq is the plural of ‘alaqah, meaning a small piece of blood. Before that stage, it was a drop of semen. Such a drop may be discharged outside the womb, as when someone has a wet dream, or it may enter the womb and then be expelled before becoming a clot. At the clot stage, it has thus become the starting point of human creation, and it is known that the drop has become a clot from which a human being is to be created. He says in Surah al-Qiyāmah:

“Was he not a drop of semen emitted?
Then he became a clinging clot, and He created and proportioned him,
and made from him the two sexes, male and female.
Is He not able

---

## Page 8267

to give life to the dead?”

Here He mentions this as evidence that the second creation, which will be from dust, is possible. Hence He says elsewhere:

“O mankind, if you are in doubt about the Resurrection, then indeed We created you from dust, then from a drop of semen.”

In Surah al-Qiyāmah, He adduces man’s creation from a drop of semen as evidence, since this is known to everyone. In Surah al-Hajj, He mentions man’s creation from dust, since this has been established by conclusive evidence. Mentioning the original creation provides stronger evidence that re-creation is possible.

Here, however, the purpose is to present evidence establishing the existence of the Creator, exalted is He, in the first place. He therefore mentions that He created man from clinging clots. Man develops from the blood clot into a lump of flesh—a piece of flesh like meat chewed in the mouth—then develops further and takes shape, as He, exalted is He, says:

“Then from a lump of flesh, formed and unformed, so that We may make things clear to you.”

The womb may expel it before it has taken shape. Thus He makes clear to people the beginning of their creation, which they see with their own eyes. This evidence—man’s creation from clinging clots—is shared by all people. People are the ones drawing the inference, yet they themselves are the evidence, the proof, and the sign. Man is both the evidence and the one who reasons from it, as He, exalted is He, says:

“And in yourselves—do you not see?”

And He says:

“We will show them Our signs on the horizons and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth.”

This is like His statement in another verse:

“Or were they created by nothing, or are they themselves the creators?”

---

## Page 8268

And this is evidence that man knows from his own self and recalls whenever he reflects on himself and on the fellow human beings he sees. He thus reasons from it to the original creation and the resurrection, as Allah, exalted is He, says:

“Man says, ‘When I am dead, will I really be brought forth alive?’”

“Does man not remember that We created him before, when he was nothing?”

And He, exalted is He, says:

“He presents an example to Us, forgetting his own creation. He says, ‘Who will give life to bones when they have crumbled away?’”

“Say, ‘He who brought them into being the first time will give them life, and He has full knowledge of every creation.’”

Likewise, Zechariah, astonished at having a child in old age, said:

“How can I have a son when my wife is barren and I have reached extreme old age?”

“He said, ‘So it will be. Your Lord says: It is easy for Me; I created you before, when you were nothing.’”

It does not say, “It is easier for Him,” as He says concerning the original creation and the resurrection:

“It is He who originates creation, then repeats it, and that is easier for Him.”

He, glorified is He, says:

“He created man from clinging clots,”

after saying:

“Who created.”

He thus speaks of creation in unrestricted terms, encompassing every created thing, and then singles out the creation of man. Everything known to have come into existence is therefore included in His words, “Who created.”

After creation, He mentions teaching: teaching by the pen and teaching man what he did not know. He singles out this teaching, from which one may infer the possibility of prophethood. Here the wording is not “guided,” which would refer to the general guidance encompassing human beings

---

## Page 8269

and all other animals, as He says elsewhere:

“Glorify the name of your Lord, the Most High,”
“Who created and proportioned,”
“And Who determined and guided.”

And as Moses said:

“Our Lord is He who gave each thing its form, then guided it.”

For this particular teaching necessarily entails general guidance, but the converse does not hold. It bears more directly on establishing prophethood, since prophethood is a kind of teaching. Making a human being a prophet is no greater than making a clinging clot into a living, knowing, rational human being, endowed with hearing, sight, and speech, who has acquired various kinds of knowledge. Likewise, originating creation is no easier for Him than repeating it. How could the One capable of the original creation be incapable of resurrection? And how could the One capable of this teaching be incapable of that teaching, when He knows all things and no one encompasses any of His knowledge except what He wills?

He, glorified is He, first says:

“Who taught by the pen.”

He thus speaks of both teaching and those taught in unrestricted terms, without singling out any particular class of learners. This encompasses the teaching of angels and others among humans and jinn, just as creation encompasses them all. He mentions teaching by the pen because it entails teaching writing. Writing corresponds to spoken expression, which is communication and speech. Spoken expression, in turn, signifies the intelligible meanings within the heart; thus, all knowledge within hearts is included.

Everything has a reality of its own, existing outside the mind; then

---

## Page 8270

the mind and heart conceive it, then the tongue expresses it, and then the pen writes it down. Thus, it has external, mental, verbal, and written existence: existence in external reality, in minds, on tongues, and through fingertips. Yet the first is the thing itself, whereas the other three are representations corresponding to it. The first is the created thing, while the other three convey knowledge of it. He therefore mentions creation and teaching so as to encompass all four levels, saying:

“Read in the name of your Lord who created,”
“Created man from a clinging clot.”
“Read, and your Lord is the Most Generous,”
“Who taught by the pen,”
“Taught man what he did not know.”

People have disputed whether essences are made or not, and whether the essence of each thing is something additional to its existence. This has been discussed at length elsewhere, where the correct position has been explained: there is only what is conceived in the mind and what exists in external reality. If by ‘essence’ one means what is conceived in the mind, and by ‘existence’ what is in external reality—or vice versa—then essence is distinct from existence, if what is in external reality differs from what is in the mind. But if ‘essence’ refers to what is in the mind, or in external reality, or both, and ‘existence’ is used likewise, then existence in external reality is the very essence existing in external reality. The same applies in the mind: the one is the other. There are not two things in external reality.

---

## Page 8271

He—glorified be He—knows what is in minds and has created what exists in external reality; both are made by Him. Yet He brings about what exists externally by creating it, and what exists in the mind by teaching it. He is the One who “created,” “created man from a clinging clot,” and He is “the Most Generous,” “who taught by the pen,” “taught man what he did not know.”

His words, “taught by the pen,” include His teaching the recording angels and His teaching the writing of the revealed Books. Through the pen, He taught how to write down His speech which He revealed, such as the Torah and the Quran. Indeed, He Himself wrote the Torah for Moses.

The fact that Muhammad was an unlettered prophet makes what he brought all the more miraculous and extraordinary, and demonstrates all the more clearly that the teaching he received surpasses all other teaching. As the Most High said:

“You did not recite any Scripture before it, nor did you write it with your right hand; otherwise, those who follow falsehood would have doubted.”

Others know what others have written—such is people’s knowledge of what they write—whereas Allah taught him this through what He revealed to him. This speech revealed to him is a sign and proof of his prophethood, for neither mankind nor jinn can produce it:

“Say, ‘If mankind and jinn gathered to produce the like of this Quran, they could not produce its like, even if they supported one another.’”

“Or do they say, ‘He fabricated it’? Say, ‘Then produce a surah like it and call upon whomever you can besides Allah, if you are truthful.’”

And in the other verse: “Then produce ten surahs

---

## Page 8272

like it that you have fabricated, and call upon whomever you can besides Allah, if you are truthful.”

“But if they do not respond to you, then know that it was revealed with Allah’s knowledge and that there is no deity except Him. Will you then submit?”

Section

We have discussed at length elsewhere the methods people use to establish the existence of the Creator and the truth of prophethood. We have shown that every method containing something contrary to the Sunnah is rationally invalid, just as it is contrary to revelation. The well-known method among speculative theologians is to infer the temporal origination of bodies from the temporal origination of accidents. We have examined this method in several places and shown that it contradicts both revelation and reason.

Many people know that it is an innovation in religion but do not know that it is rationally unsound. Some suppose that it is sound according to both reason and revelation, and that it was the method of Abraham, Allah’s close friend, peace be upon him. The invalidity of this has been demonstrated elsewhere.

The point here is that a group of speculative thinkers who affirm the divine attributes wished

---

## Page 8273

to follow the path of the Sunnah, yet they knew no method other than this. They therefore argued from the creation of man, but instead of treating his creation as evidence, as the verse does, they treated it as something requiring proof. They supposed that the temporal origination of the accidents of the drop of semen was known self-evidently and through sense perception. As for its constituent atoms, they believed that all bodies are composed of indivisible atoms, and that creating man and other things consists merely in bringing accidents into existence in those atoms by combining and separating them, rather than bringing an entity itself into existence. Thus, they sought to establish by inference that man is created.

Once they had established that he is created, they said that he has a Creator. They argued that he is created by appealing to accidents: the drop of semen, the clot, and the lump of flesh are never free of temporally originated accidents. For, in their view, these consist of atoms that are sometimes combined and sometimes separated. They are therefore never free of combination and separation, both of which are temporally originated. Thus, man is never free of temporally originated events, and whatever is never free of such events is itself temporally originated, since a beginningless succession of events is impossible.

This is the method al-Ash‘arī followed in *Gleams in Refutation of the People of Innovation*, on which his followers wrote numerous commentaries, and likewise in his *Epistle to the People of the Frontier*. He cited the words of Allah Most High:

“Have you considered the semen you emit?”

“Is it you who create it, or

---

## Page 8274

is it We who create?” He thus argued that man is created because he consists of atoms that are never free of combination and separation. Since these atoms are never free of temporally originated events, they are themselves temporally originated. This method depends on all bodies being of this nature.

This is the well-known method followed by the Jahmiyyah, the Mu‘tazilah, and those who followed them among later scholars affiliated with the four schools and others among the followers of Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad. It was set out by al-Qādī and Ibn ‘Aqīl, among others; by Abū al-Ma‘ālī al-Juwaynī and the author of *The Supplement*, among others; by Abū al-Walīd al-Bājī and Abū Bakr ibn al-‘Arabī, among others; and by Abū Mansūr al-Māturīdī and al-Sābūnī, among others.

However, those who argued from the creation of man applied this argument specifically to man, supposing that this was the Quran’s method. They elaborated at length and in minute detail, going so far as to argue that man’s very entity and constituent atoms are created, because they supposed that sense perception and self-evident reason establish only the temporal origination of accidents, not of atoms. They claimed that, in everything Allah brings into being—clouds, rain, crops, fruit, man, and animals—He brings into existence only accidents, namely, the combination and separation of atoms that already existed.

---

## Page 8275

They also claimed that no one knows, through observation or self-evident reason, that other concrete entities come into being; rather, this can only be known by reasoning as they did. They said:

“These are temporally originated accidents inhering in atoms, and those atoms have never been free of accidents, since atoms cannot be devoid of accidents.”

They then said:

“Whatever is never free of temporally originated events is itself temporally originated.”

They based this on the premise that bodies consist of individual, indivisible atoms. They also said that bodies cannot be transformed into one another.

The majority of rational people among the early generations and the various classes of scholars, as well as most speculative thinkers, disagree with their affirmation of indivisible atoms. They affirm that bodies can be transformed into one another and maintain that the Lord continually brings concrete entities into being, as the Quran indicates.

Thus, this method is invalid both rationally and according to revelation; indeed, it obstinately defies reason. For it is self-evident to everyone that man is created and originated, coming into existence after not having existed. Everyone knows that he came into being in his mother’s womb after not having existed, and that his very being came into existence. As the Exalted says:

“I created you before, when you were nothing.”

And the Exalted says:

“Does man not remember that We created him before, when he was nothing?”

---

## Page 8276

This is not something that requires proof, for it is clearer and more evident than the argument offered to establish it, even if that argument were sound. How much more so when it is false!

Their claims that only accidents come into being and that man is composed of indivisible atoms are both false. Their truth is not known; rather, their falsity is known. It is known that man’s very substance—and likewise that of other beings—comes into existence from the material from which he was created, namely clinging substance, as He says:

“He created man from a clinging substance.”

The claim that man is composed of indivisible atoms is not correct. Even if it were correct, it could only be known through subtle arguments that cannot constitute the foundation of religion, which consists of primary premises. Those premises must be clear, fundamental, and self-evidently known.

Their method thus entails denying what is known—namely, that concrete entities come into being, something known to people—and affirming what is not known, but is in fact false: that bringing these entities into being consists merely of combining and separating atoms, and that it is nothing more than bringing accidents into being.

For this reason, the leading scholars of the religion condemned their use of the method of substances and accidents in this manner and explained that they were introducing innovations in this regard. Indeed,

---

## Page 8277

they demonstrated their misguidance on both scriptural and rational grounds. The statements of the early generations and the leading scholars against them have been set out at length elsewhere, for they are numerous. The Quran bases its argument on what people already know:

“He created man from a clinging substance.”

Yet these people approached this known fact and claimed that it was not known, but doubtful. They then claimed to present the proof by which it would become known. However, they offered an invalid argument that does not establish that man came into existence. It is taken for a proof, but is merely a specious argument with false implications. Thus, they denied what is known through reason and, furthermore, through revelation, while claiming to offer a method established by reason, although it is invalid according to both reason and revelation. They thereby resembled those of whom Allah says:

“If only we had been listening or reasoning, we would not be among the companions of the Blaze.”

Likewise, in establishing prophethood and its possibility, and resurrection and its possibility, they turned away from the guiding path that yields certain knowledge—the path by which Allah guided His servants—to a path that breeds doubt, specious arguments, and bewilderment.

Hence it has been said:

“The ultimate outcome for proponents of innovation among speculative theologians is doubt, and among Sufis, ecstatic excesses.”

Moreover, this path has false implications that contradict reason and revelation. They insisted on these implications, which led them into sophistry in rational matters and Qarmatian esotericism in matters known through revelation. They also spoke

---

## Page 8278

about the proofs of prophethood, resurrection, and Lordship, advancing arguments they claimed were proofs but which, upon examination, are no proofs at all. Hence, they attack one another’s proofs. Whenever they do offer a sound proof, it agrees with what the Messenger brought, even if the wording differs. Thus, one of them may offer a proof, whether sound or unsound, only for another to attack it and claim to offer something better, while actually offering something inferior. This frequently happens with their definitions: some attack the definitions of others, then offer definitions that are equivalent or inferior. Yet all these definitions belong to the same category and are valid if their purpose is to distinguish the thing defined from other things.

As for those who claim that definitions convey a conception of the essence of the thing defined, as the logicians maintain, they are mistaken and misguided, as has been explained at length elsewhere. A definition merely identifies the thing defined and points to it, much as a name does, but it sets out in detail what the name conveys in general terms. It is therefore a kind of indication, as has been explained at length elsewhere. The purpose here is simply to draw attention to the distinction between a path that yields knowledge and certainty, such as the one explained in the Quran, and the paths of the proponents of innovation that do not, and that are invalid according to both revelation and reason.

---

## Page 8279

Section

Those who built the foundation of their religion upon the argument from accidents, using them to infer that bodies came into existence, have fallen into considerable inconsistency, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

Besides opposing the law revealed from heaven, each of them inevitably also contradicts clear reason and obstinately denies the obvious, becoming one of those who neither listen nor reason. A proposition has necessary consequences; if it is false, it may entail other falsehoods whose falsity is evident. Its proponent seeks to establish those consequences, thereby revealing his opposition to sensory experience and reason.

Such are those who affirmed indivisible atoms and maintained that motions do not intrinsically differ as fast or slow, since, in their view, motion is divided just as the moving body is, and likewise time and its parts. For them, a motion and a moving body are each a single indivisible unit. Thus, if two moving bodies are equal, yet one moves faster than the other, they say: “That is only because periods of rest intervene.”

They maintained that when a millstone, a wheel, or any circular object rotates, its circumference and a smaller ring within it move for the same length of time, although the circumference has more parts. The movements of those parts must therefore be more numerous, and hence take more time; yet they do not;

---

## Page 8280

so they claimed that the parts separate and then rejoin. This is an obstinate denial of the obvious, akin to “al-Nazzām’s leap.” Likewise, those who maintained that an accident does not persist for two successive moments contradicted sensory experience and what rational people know by the self-evident dictates of reason. Everyone knows that the colour of his body a moment ago is this same colour, and likewise the colour of the sky, mountains, wood, paper, and other things. Among the considerations that drove them to this was their supposition that, if both persisted, neither could be brought out of existence. For they were perplexed about how Allah causes things to perish when He wills to do so, just as they were perplexed about His bringing them into existence. Their perplexity concerning annihilation is even more evident.

One says: “He creates annihilation that inheres in no substrate; it is the opposite of those things, and they perish through their opposite.” Another says: “He deprives them of accidents altogether, or of the persistence without which they cannot endure; thus they perish because the condition of their continued existence is lost.”

One cause of this is their supposition—or the supposition of some among them—that things which come into existence need Allah only when they are brought into existence, not while they continue to exist. Yet they affirmed that He has the power to annihilate them, and so they contrived these false doctrines. These people do not argue for the Lord’s everlasting existence on the basis of the world’s dependence upon Him, but rather on the grounds that He is beginningless, and whatever is necessarily beginningless cannot cease to exist. Apart from that, in their view, an enduring thing has no need of the Lord while it continues to exist.

---

## Page 8281

These people are worse than those who asked Moses, “Does your Lord sleep?” Allah gave them an illustration with two glass vessels. After Moses had remained awake for several nights, He commanded him to hold the two vessels. When he held them, sleep overcame him, and they shattered. Allah thus showed him that, were drowsiness or sleep to overtake Him, the world would collapse. Yet according to these people’s view, were drowsiness or sleep to overtake Him, what already endures would not cease to exist.

Some of them, however, say that an enduring thing depends upon accidents being brought into existence in succession, since, in their view, an accident does not persist for two successive moments. It is on this basis that such a person argues that, were drowsiness or sleep to overtake Him, the accidents through which bodies persist would not come into existence—not because, in his view, bodies themselves depend upon Him while they continue to exist.

Likewise, they say that the divine will does not pertain to what is beginningless or to what continues to exist. In their view, power likewise does not pertain to what continues to exist. Nor, according to them, can incapacity properly be spoken of with respect to what continues to exist or is beginningless, since they hold that incapacity can only concern something over which power is possible.

These people say that the reason for dependence upon the Creator is simply having come into existence. Others among the philosophers say that it is simply contingency, and claim that something beginningless and eternal, which has always existed and will always exist, depends upon the Maker. Thus the former claims that an enduring thing which came into existence has no such dependence, while the latter claims that an enduring, beginningless thing does have such dependence. Both views are

---

## Page 8282

unsound, as has been explained at length elsewhere. The truth is that everything besides Allah has come into existence and depends upon Him at all times. He sustains it and brings it to nonexistence, just as He brings it into being and originates it. Thus He brings things into existence from dust and other things, then causes them to perish and transforms them into dust and other things. Many of these people have claimed that everything besides Allah will cease to exist and then be restored.

Some of them have said: “This is possible, but establishing it depends upon revelation, and revelation has neither affirmed nor denied it.” This is their conception of the resurrection. Yet neither the Book nor the Sunnah has taught this, nor has reason established it. Rather, the Book and the Sunnah make clear that Allah transforms the world from one state to another: He splits the heaven, makes the mountains like wool, rolls up the sun, and does the other things He has foretold in His Book. He has not stated that all things will cease to exist and then be restored.

Some of them further say that these things will cease to exist thereafter, because an endless succession of events is impossible, as the Jahmiyyah maintain. This is among the views for which the early generations and the leading scholars condemned them, as has been discussed elsewhere.

They said this only to carry through the implications of their claim that a perpetual succession of originated events is impossible. They said: “Whatever must have a beginning must also have an end.” This has been explained at length, and the unsoundness of this principle has been demonstrated.

---

## Page 8283

Section

He, glorified be He, sometimes mentions the creation of man in general terms and sometimes describes it in detail, as when He says:

“We created man from an extract of clay. Then We placed him as a drop of sperm in a secure resting place. Then We made the drop into a clinging clot, then made the clot into a lump of flesh, then made the lump into bones, then clothed the bones with flesh, then brought him into being as another creation. So blessed is Allah, the best of creators.”

He then mentions the two returns—the lesser and the greater—saying:

“Then, after that, you will surely die. Then, on the Day of Resurrection, you will be raised.”

Some people ask: Why does the emphatic lām occur in the statement about death, which is witnessed, but not in the statement about resurrection, which is unseen and therefore calls for emphasis?

The reason—and Allah knows best—is that the purpose of mentioning death and resurrection is to announce recompense and the return, the first stage of which is death. Thus He draws attention to belief in the return and preparation for what comes after death. He says only, “you will be raised,” not “you will be recompensed”; nevertheless, it is known that resurrection is for recompense.

This also draws attention to how man is overpowered and humbled. He says: “After this

---

## Page 8284

all, you will indeed die and be returned to the lowest of the low—except those who believe and do righteous deeds.” As He says:

“We have certainly created man in the finest form.”

“Then We returned him to the lowest of the low.”

“Except those who believe and do righteous deeds, for they will have an uninterrupted reward.”

This return takes place through death, for man descends to the lowest of the low, except those who believe and do righteous deeds. As He says:

“No! Indeed, the record of the wicked is in Sijjīn.”

And He says:

“Indeed, the record of the righteous is in Illiyyīn.”

There are two interpretations of His words, “the lowest of the low.” One is that they refer to decrepit old age; the other is that they refer to punishment after death. The latter is what the verse definitively indicates, for He places man among the lowest of the low, with the exception of the believers.

People fall into two categories: after death, the disbeliever is punished among the lowest of the low, while the believer is in Illiyyīn. As for the first interpretation, it is open to question. Not everyone other than the believers reaches decrepit old age and is thus returned to the lowest of the low. Rather, many disbelievers die before reaching decrepitude, while many believers do reach it. Even if the believer’s condition in old age is better than the disbeliever’s, the believer’s condition is likewise better than the disbeliever’s in youth. Thus, interpreting the return to the lowest of the low as occurring at the end of life, and restricting it to disbelievers, is a weak interpretation. Hence some have said that, on this interpretation, the exception is disjunctive; this, too, is

---

## Page 8285

weak. A disjunctive exception cannot occur in an affirmative statement. Were this permissible, anyone could claim that any exception he wished was disjunctive. Moreover, in a disjunctive exception, the category excepted is not part of the original category, whereas believers are part of humankind.

Some have explained the verse, according to the first interpretation, as meaning that when a believer becomes incapable of performing deeds, he continues to be credited for what he used to do.

Ibrāhīm al-Nakhaʿī said:

“When a believer becomes so old that he can no longer perform deeds, Allah credits him for what he used to do. This is what He means by His words, ‘For they will have an uninterrupted reward.’”

Ibn Qutaybah said:

“The meaning is: ‘Except those who believe’ while they possess strength and ability. Their reward is not diminished in old age, even if they become incapable of acts of obedience. Allah knows that, had He not taken away their strength, they would not have ceased doing good, so He continues to grant them its reward.”

In reply, one may say: This also holds true during youth, when a young person becomes unable to act because of illness or travel. As reported in the two Sahīh collections, Abū Mūsā narrated that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When a servant falls ill or travels, Allah credits him with the deeds he used to perform while healthy and resident.”

Others have explained it by citing the report that Ibn ʿAbbās said:

“Whoever recites the Quran will not be reduced to decrepit old age.”

In reply, one may say: This applies specifically to someone who recites the Quran, whereas the verse excepts those who believe and do righteous deeds, whether they recite the Quran or do not

---

## Page 8286

recite it. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in an authentic hadith:

“The believer who recites the Quran is like a citron: its taste is pleasant and its fragrance is pleasant. The believer who does not recite the Quran is like a date: its taste is pleasant, but it has no fragrance.”

It may also be said: Senescence is not peculiar to human beings; other animals likewise become decrepit as they age. Moreover, although an elderly person’s body grows weak, his intellect is stronger than a young person’s. Even supposing that some of his faculties decline, this does not amount to being reduced to the lowest of the low. Allah, glorified be He, describes old age only in terms of weakness, as when He says:

“Then, after strength, He brought weakness and grey hair.”

And:

“Whoever We grant a long life, We reverse his development.”

Thus, He returns him to a state of weakness. It is well known that a child is not among the lowest of the low; the same holds for an elderly person, with even greater reason. Rather, those in the lowest of the low are those who will be in Sijjīn, not in Illiyyīn, as Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, the hypocrites will be in the lowest depths of the Fire.”

Further clarification comes in His words:

“What, then, makes you deny the Judgment?”

This must be connected to what precedes it, since it is introduced by the particle fa (“then”). If the preceding passage referred only to a person’s being reduced to decrepit old age, rather than to what happens after death, there would be no reference here to judgment and recompense, unlike

---

## Page 8287

the case in which the passage states that, after death, man is reduced to the lowest of the low, except for the believer who does righteous deeds. This conveys that Allah will judge His servants after death, honouring the believers and humiliating the disbelievers.

Moreover, He, glorified be He, has affirmed this with solemn oaths: by the fig, the olive, Mount Sinai, and this secure city. These are the places from which Muhammad, the Messiah, and Moses came, and where Allah sent these messengers as bearers of glad tidings and warnings. Such an oath would not concern mere senescence, which everyone knows, but rather unseen matters that are confirmed by oaths. For Allah’s oaths concern tidings of the unseen.

The very subject by which the oath is sworn—the sending of these messengers—confirms what the oath affirms: reward and punishment after death, since the messengers conveyed this. It also encompasses recompense in this world, such as Allah’s destruction of those disbelievers whom He destroyed. Through their destruction in this world, He reduced them to the lowest of the low. This also draws attention to the loss of blessings when sins are committed, as in the case of someone brought low in this world as recompense for his sins.

---

## Page 8288

His words,

“What, then, makes you deny the Recompense after this?”

refer to recompense for deeds in this world, the intermediate realm after death, and the Hereafter. For He has sworn by the places of these messengers, who were sent with clear signs indicating Allah’s commands and prohibitions, His promises and warnings, bringing glad tidings to the believers and warning the disbelievers. By these places, He has sworn that man, after being created in the best form, will have an unending reward if he believes and does righteous deeds; otherwise, he will be among the lowest of the low. Thus, the surah explains the message entrusted to these messengers by whose places He swore.

Swearing by the places of their trials honours them, for if a person’s place is honoured on his account, that person is even more deserving of honour. Hence correspondence uses expressions such as, “To the lofty and exalted assembly, residence, or the like.” The place is mentioned with deference and veneration, while its occupant is intended.

Thus, His words,

“What, then, makes you deny the Recompense after this?”

indicate that the preceding passage has explained what should preclude denial of the Recompense. There are two interpretations of His word yukadhdhibuka.

One is that it addresses man, as stated by Mujāhid, ‘Ikrimah, and Muqātil. Al-Baghawī mentions no other interpretation. ‘Ikrimah said: “He is saying: What, after this, makes you deny these things that I have done for you?”

And it is reported from Muqātil:

---

## Page 8289

“What, then, makes you deny recompense?” He claimed that it was revealed concerning ‘Ayyāsh ibn Abī Rabī‘ah.

The second interpretation is that it addresses the Messenger, and this is more evident. Man has only been spoken about, not directly addressed. It is to the Messenger that the Quran was revealed, and he is the one addressed in these surahs, as in His words:

“Your Lord has neither forsaken you nor despised you.”

And His words:

“Have We not expanded your breast for you?”

And His words:

“Read in the name of your Lord.”

When man is addressed, he is told:

“O man, what has deceived you concerning your Lord, the Generous?”

“O man, you are striving hard towards your Lord.”

Moreover, if we suppose that this addresses man, it must address mankind as a whole, as in His words, “O man, you are striving.” According to these interpreters, however, it addresses only the disbeliever who denies the Recompense.

Furthermore, His words, “calls you a liar, after this, concerning the Recompense,” mean “declares you to be a liar.” This is the familiar usage in Arabic. The expression kadhdhaba ghayrahu, meaning “he accused another of lying and declared him a liar,” is well known, and the Quran abounds in this usage. Whenever Allah mentions the deniers’ rejection of the messengers, or rejection of the truth and the like, this is what He means.

---

## Page 8290

Yet this verse presents a difficulty because He says:

“Calls you a liar, after this, concerning the Recompense.”

Here He mentions both the person accused of lying and the matter denied. This is uncommon, though a similar construction occurs in His words:

“They have indeed declared you liars in what you say.”

In most passages, however, He mentions only one of the two: either the person accused of lying, as in His words:

“The people of Noah denied the messengers,”

or the matter denied, as in His words:

“Rather, they deny the Hour.”

Mentioning both the person accused of lying and the matter denied together is uncommon. This is why this verse proved confusing to those who took it as addressing man and interpreted His words, “What, then, makes you a liar?” as meaning, “What makes you a denier?” Others expressed it as, “What makes you a liar?”

Ibn ʿAtiyya said: “The majority of exegetes said that the person addressed is the disbelieving man. That is: What makes you a liar concerning the Recompense, setting up rivals to Allah and claiming that there is no resurrection, after these proofs?”

(I say: Neither interpretation is known in Arabic usage—that one should say, “Kadhdhabaka means ‘he made you one who is disbelieved.’” Rather, kadhdhabaka means “he declared you a liar.” To say “he declared you a liar” means that he deemed you untruthful in what you reported, just as the disbelievers deemed the messengers untruthful in what they reported and thus accused them of lying. This interpreter, however, says:

---

## Page 8291

“He made you a liar concerning the Recompense.” He thus takes the man’s lying to consist in his associating partners with Allah and denying the Resurrection. Yet this is the opposite of being a denier. The former interpretation makes him a denier of the Recompense; the latter makes him a liar concerning it. The first is unsound in terms of Arabic usage, and the second is unsound in meaning. For the Recompense is the requital that the disbeliever denied. The disbeliever denied it; he did not himself accuse anyone of lying concerning it.

Furthermore, when referring to someone who conveys a report, it is not established usage to say, “Kadhabtu bihi” (“I lied concerning him”); rather, one says, “Kadhdhabtuhu” (“I declared him a liar”). Moreover, the established meaning of declaring someone a liar is to accuse him of lying, not to cause lying to arise in him. All of this is strained interpretation unknown in the language; established usage is, rather, the opposite. He did not say, “Fa-mā yukadhdhibuk” (“What, then, declares you a liar?”), nor did He say, “Fa-mā kadhdhabak” (“What, then, declared you a liar?”). This is why the scholars of Arabic adopted the first position.

Ibn ʿAtiyya said:

“There is disagreement over who is addressed in His words, ‘What, then, calls you a liar?’ Qatāda, al-Farrāʾ and al-Akhfash said that it is Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Allah said to him: ‘What, then, declares you a liar in what you report about requital and resurrection—which is the Recompense—after this lesson, reflection upon which establishes the truth of what you have said?’”

He said: “According to this interpretation, ‘the religion’ may also encompass the entirety of his revealed law and religion.”

---

## Page 8292

I say:

On the assumption that Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the one addressed, there are two interpretations. The first is Qatāda’s. He said concerning “What, then, makes you deny the Recompense after this?”: “That is, be certain, for clarification has come to you from Allah.” This is how Ibn Abī Hātim transmitted it from him through a reliable chain of transmission.

Al-Mahdawī likewise cited it:

“‘What, then, makes you deny the Recompense after this?’ That is, be certain, in light of what has come to you from Allah, that He is the wisest of judges. Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the one addressed.”

He attributed this interpretation to Qatāda, then said:

“It has also been said that the meaning is: ‘What, then, makes you deny, O you who doubt Allah’s power?’—meaning the disbelievers. What induces you to do so after His power has become clear to you?”

He said: “Al-Farrā’ said: ‘Who, then, can declare you a liar concerning reward and punishment?’ This is the interpretation preferred by al-Tabarī.”

(I say:

This interpretation transmitted from Qatāda is what caused Mujāhid to recoil from the view that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was being addressed. As various scholars, including Ibn Abī Hātim, transmitted from al-Thawrī, from Mansūr, who said:

“I asked Mujāhid: ‘In “What, then, makes you deny the Recompense after this?”, did He mean the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?’

“He replied: ‘Allah forbid! He meant man.’”

Mujāhid was right to hold the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, above being told, “What, then, makes you deny?”—meaning, “Be certain and do not deny.” For if he were told, “Do not deny,”

---

## Page 8293

that would have been of the same kind as commanding him to believe and fear Allah, and forbidding him from doing what Allah has forbidden. But as for saying, “What, then, makes you deny the Recompense after this?”, he never denied the Recompense. Rather, he is the one who proclaimed it and believed in it. He is “the one who brought the truth and believed in it.” How, then, could he be asked, “What makes you deny the Recompense after this?” This interpretation is therefore unsound in both wording and meaning.

Moreover, the wording I have seen transmitted from Qatāda through a chain of transmission does not explicitly support this interpretation. Rather, he may have intended it as an address to man, for concerning “What, then, makes you deny the Recompense after this?”, he said: “Be certain, for clarification has come to you.” Every human being is addressed by this. If this is what Qatāda intended, the meaning is sound. However, they reported him as saying that this is addressed to the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. On that understanding, the meaning is false. The Messenger cannot be asked, “What, then, makes you deny the Recompense?”—even if the mind should entertain such an interpretation—for there are indications here that demonstrate its unsoundness. This is why Mujāhid sought refuge in Allah from it.

The correct interpretation is that stated by al-Farrā’, al-Akhfash and others. It is the interpretation preferred by Abū Ja‘far Muhammad ibn Jarīr al-Tabarī and other scholars, as mentioned above. Abū al-Faraj ibn al-Jawzī likewise cited it from al-Farrā’, saying:

“It is an address

---

## Page 8294

to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and means: ‘Who can deny your account of reward and punishment after it has become clear to him that We created man as We have described?’ This was stated by al-Farrā’. He said that ‘the Recompense’ means requital.”

(I say: Several others said likewise. Ibn Abī Hātim narrated from al-Nadr ibn Arabī concerning “Who, then, can deny your account of the Recompense after this?” that it means the reckoning. In al-Awfī’s commentary, transmitted from Ibn Abbās, it means Allah’s judgment.

I say: He said “Allah’s judgment” because of His words, “Is Allah not the most just of judges?” He, glorified be He, judges between the one who affirms the Recompense and the one who denies it. On this interpretation, mā (“what”) in His words “What, then” describes persons. He did not use man (“who”), because mā refers to attributes rather than particular individuals, and that is what is intended here. This is like His words, “Marry such women as seem good to you,” His words, “I do not worship what you worship,” and His words, “By the soul and the One who fashioned it.”

It is as though it were said: “What kind of person, then, denies the Recompense after this?” That is, anyone with this description and these qualities is ignorant and wrongs himself. Allah will judge between His servants concerning their disagreements about this momentous news. As for His word “after,” it has been said that it means “after the proofs of the Recompense that He has mentioned.”

---

## Page 8295

Yet it may be asked: Has He done more than announce it and state that people are of two kinds—those in the lowest of the low and those who will have an unfailing reward? He has thus mentioned glad tidings and warnings, and the messengers were sent as bearers of glad tidings and warners. Whoever rejects you after this, his judgment rests with Allah, the most just of judges, while you have conveyed what you were obliged to convey.

His words, “Who, then, can deny your account?” do not mean that no such rejection occurs, for it has indeed occurred. Rather, it may be said that they express astonishment at it, as in His words:

“If you are astonished, then astonishing is their saying, ‘When we have become dust, will we really be created anew?’”

It may also be said that this belittles the denier’s importance and diminishes his standing because of his ignorance and wrongdoing, just as one might say, “Who is so-and-so?” or “Who but an ignorant person would say this?” Yet He uses mā (“what”), because it indicates the person’s qualities, which are what is intended; his individual identity is of no concern. It is as though He said: “What sort of person, then—what ignorant person—denies your account of the Recompense after this? He is indeed among those who will be returned to the lowest of the low.”

His words, “Is Allah not the most just of judges?” indicate that He is the Judge between the one who denies the Recompense and the one who believes in it. The decision in this matter belongs to Him, glorified and exalted be He.

---

## Page 8296

The wonders of the Quran never cease. Allah, glorified be He, has made His intent clear with perfect precision. Confusion arises, however, for those who are not firmly grounded in understanding the evidence and what it indicates. This surah, like others, contains inexhaustible wonders. Among them is that His statement,

“Who, then, can deny your account of the Recompense after this?”

mentions both the Messenger who is denied and the Recompense that is denied. The surah encompasses both matters. It includes an oath by the places associated with the messengers, which testify to their greatness and to the signs they brought—signs that establish their truthfulness and require belief. They foretold the Resurrection mentioned in this surah. Allah has sworn to its occurrence, just as He does elsewhere, and just as He commanded His Prophet to swear to it in such statements as:

“Those who disbelieve claim that they will never be resurrected. Say, ‘Yes, by my Lord, you will surely be resurrected.’”

And:

“Those who disbelieve say, ‘The Hour will never come upon us.’ Say, ‘Yes, by my Lord, it will surely come upon you.’”

Since the surah encompasses both matters, He mentions both kinds of denial, saying:

“Who, then, can deny your account of the Recompense after this?”

And Allah, glorified be He, knows best.

Moreover, he bears no sin for that, whereas the Quran intends to make clear that this return is a recompense for his sins. Hence He says, “Except those who believe and do

---

## Page 8297

righteous deeds.” As He says:

“Indeed, mankind is in loss, except those who believe and do righteous deeds and counsel one another to truth and counsel one another to patience.”

Here, however, He mentions only loss, so He describes those excepted as counselling one another to truth and patience, in addition to their faith and righteousness. There, He mentions “the lowest of the low,” which is punishment. A believer who acts righteously will not be punished, even if he has neglected certain things and thereby lost them; had he preserved them, he would have gained rather than lost. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that He, glorified be He, mentions the creation of man both in general terms and in detail. At times, He mentions giving him life, as in His statement, Most High:

“How can you disbelieve in Allah when you were lifeless and He gave you life? Then He will cause you to die, then bring you back to life, and then to Him you will be returned.”

This is like the statement of His intimate friend, peace be upon him:

“My Lord is the One who gives life and causes death.”

For the creation of life, together with all that it entails and presupposes, is greater and more clearly demonstrates power, blessing, and wisdom.

---

## Page 8298

Section

His words:

“Recite, and your Lord is the Most Generous, who taught by the pen, taught man that which he knew not.”

After declaring that He created, He named and described Himself as generous, indeed as the Most Generous, to make clear that He bestows blessings upon His creatures and brings them to praiseworthy ends. As He says elsewhere:

“Who created and proportioned, and who determined and guided.”

And as Moses, peace be upon him, said:

“Our Lord is He who gave each thing its form, then guided it.”

And as His intimate friend, peace be upon him, said:

“Who created me, and He guides me.”

Creation thus encompasses the beginning, while generosity encompasses the culmination. Likewise, in the Mother of the Quran, He says:

“Lord of the worlds,”

then says:

“The Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.”

The word karam (nobility and generosity) encompasses all fine and praiseworthy qualities. It does not mean merely giving; rather, giving completes its meaning, for doing good to others brings fine qualities to their perfection. Karam is the abundance and ready availability of goodness. Hence the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not call grapes al-karm (‘the noble one’), for al-karm is the believer’s heart.”

---

## Page 8299

They called grapes “al-karm” (“the noble one”) because they are the most beneficial of fruits: they are eaten fresh or dried, and pressed to make various products. Grapevines are also more widespread than date palms, growing in most countries, whereas date palms grow only in hot countries. Thus, concerning human sustenance, Allah says:

“Let man consider his food: We poured down water abundantly, then split the earth open, and caused grain to grow in it, and grapes and herbage, and olives and date palms, and gardens of dense foliage, and fruit and pasture, as provision for you and your livestock.”

He thus mentioned grapes first. And in describing Paradise, He says:

“Indeed, the righteous will have triumph: gardens and grapevines.”

Nevertheless, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade calling grapes al-karm, saying:

“Al-karm is the believer’s heart.”

For nothing in this world contains more or greater goodness than the believer’s heart. The quality of karam (nobility) is ascribed to anything fine and praiseworthy. Allah Most High says:

“Have they not looked at the earth—how many noble kinds We have caused to grow in it?”

Ibn Qutaybah said: “Of every fine kind.” Al-Zajjāj said: “Zawj means ‘type,’ and karīm means ‘praiseworthy.’” Others explained “of every kind” as “of every class and variety,” and “noble” as “fine,” referring to plants eaten by people and livestock. One says “a noble date palm” when its fruit is good, and “a noble she-camel” when its milk is abundant.

---

## Page 8300

It is also reported from al-Sha‘bī:

“People are among the things that grow from the earth: whoever enters Paradise is noble, and whoever enters the Fire is base.”

The Quran indicates that some people are noble in Allah’s sight and He honors them, while others He humiliates. He, Most High, says:

“Indeed, the most noble of you in the sight of Allah is the most righteous of you.”

He, Most High, also says:

“Whomever Allah humiliates, none can honor. Indeed, Allah does whatever He wills.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal:

“Beware of taking the choicest of their possessions, and beware the supplication of the oppressed, for there is no barrier between it and Allah.”

The choicest possessions are those dear to their owners because they need them and benefit from them, whether livestock or other property.

He, glory be to Him, has declared that He is “the Most Generous,” using the superlative with the definite article. This indicates that He alone is the Most Generous, unlike saying, “Your Lord is more generous,” which would not indicate exclusivity. His saying “the Most Generous” does indicate exclusivity. Nor did He say, “more generous than such-and-such.” Rather, He left the name unrestricted to make clear that He is the Most Generous absolutely, without qualification. This indicates that He possesses the utmost generosity, beyond which there is nothing greater and in which there is no deficiency.

Ibn ‘Atiyyah said: “Then He, Most High, said to him, ‘Read, and your Lord is the Most Generous,’ by way of

---

## Page 8301

reassurance, as though He were saying: ‘Proceed with what you have been commanded to do. Your Lord is not like these other lords; rather, He is the Most Generous, untouched by any deficiency. He will support you and grant you victory.’”

I say: Some of the early Muslims said:

“None of you should offer Allah what he would be ashamed to offer someone he esteems, for Allah is the most generous of the generous.”

That is, He is more deserving of honor than anything else, since He is more generous than anything else. He, glory be to Him, is the Possessor of Majesty and Honor. He therefore deserves to be revered and honored. Reverence entails exaltation, while honor entails praise and love.

This resembles what has been said in describing the believer: he has been endowed with sweetness of character and an awe-inspiring presence. In the report of Hind ibn Abī Hālah describing the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, it says:

“Whoever saw him at first sight stood in awe of him, and whoever came to know him through close association loved him.”

This is because sovereignty and praise belong to Him, glory be to Him. This has been discussed at length elsewhere, and it has been explained that Ahl al-Sunnah describe Him as possessing divine power, wisdom, and mercy. They are the ones who worship and praise Him. It has also been explained that worship must be His due alone, to the exclusion of all else, and that worship entails the utmost humility and the utmost love.

---

## Page 8302

and that the real implication of the position of the Jahmiyyah and those who agree with them in denying that He loves is that He does not deserve to be worshipped, just as their claim that He acts without wisdom or mercy implies that He is not to be praised. They describe Him only as possessing power and irresistible might. This entails reverence alone, not honor, love, or praise. Yet He, glory be to Him, is the Most Generous. Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, your Lord’s punishment is severe. Indeed, it is He who originates creation and brings it back.”

Then He says:

“And He is the All-Forgiving, the All-Loving, Owner of the Throne, the Glorious, Doer of whatever He wills.”

And Shuayb said:

“Seek your Lord’s forgiveness, then turn to Him in repentance. Indeed, my Lord is Merciful, Loving.”

In the first revelation, He described Himself as the One who created and as the Most Generous. The Jahmiyyah affirm nothing beyond His being the Creator, despite their shortcomings even in affirming this. They do not describe Him as possessing generosity, mercy, or wisdom. Even when they use these terms, they do not intend their meanings. Rather, they use them because they occur in the Quran, then deviate concerning His names and distort words from their proper places.

At times they say that wisdom is power; at other times, that it is will; and at still other times, that it is knowledge. Yet wisdom, although it includes and entails these, is something more

---

## Page 8303

than that. Not everyone who possesses power or will is wise, nor is everyone who possesses knowledge wise unless he acts upon his knowledge.

Ibn Qutaybah and others said: Wisdom is knowledge and acting upon it. It is also sound speech. Thus, it encompasses sound speech and upright, righteous action. The Lord Most High is the wisest of judges and the wisest of the wise. The precision evident in His creation is proof of His knowledge. They, along with all the other groups, infer knowledge from this precision. Yet it indicates knowledge only if the agent is wise and acts for a wise purpose. They, however, say that He does not act for a wise purpose, but acts solely by a will that singles out one of two equivalent alternatives without any reason necessitating that choice. This contradicts wisdom; indeed, it is foolishness. He has declared Himself free of this in His words:

“Had We intended to take a pastime, We would have taken it from what is with Us—if We were to do so.

“Rather, We hurl the truth against falsehood, and it crushes it, whereupon it vanishes. And woe to you for what you attribute to Us!”

He has also stated that He created the heavens and the earth and all that is between them only in truth, and that He

---

## Page 8304

did not create them in vain, and that such is the supposition of those who disbelieve. He says:

“Did you think that We created you without purpose?”

He also says:

“Does man think that he will be left without responsibility?”

That is, left unattended, neither commanded nor forbidden. This rhetorical question rebukes anyone who considers such a thing possible for the Lord. Yet the Jahmiyyah who espouse absolute determinism consider it possible for Him. They do not declare Him above any act, even if it is reprehensible. Nor do they ascribe to Him what His generosity, mercy, wisdom, and justice necessarily entail, whereby it would be known that He does what befits these attributes and does not do what contradicts them. Rather, they consider it possible for anything within His power either to occur or not to occur. They affirm one of these alternatives with certainty only on the basis of a revealed report or an established pattern, despite their inconsistencies in reasoning from reports—the reports of the messengers—and from the Lord’s customary ways. This has been explained at length elsewhere, including in discussions of the prophets’ miracles, the sending of messengers, commands and prohibitions, the resurrection, and similar matters pertaining to His acts and decrees that proceed from His will.

For these proceed from His wisdom and His mercy, and His will necessarily entails both. He wills only with a will that encompasses wisdom. He is more merciful to His servants than a mother is to her child, as is established in the authentic hadith in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah is indeed more merciful to His servants than a mother is to her child.”

---

## Page 8305

Thus, in reality, they do not acknowledge that He is the Most Generous. The will they affirm is supported by neither revelation nor reason. For no will is known to favor one thing over another without a cause that necessitates that preference. Whoever among the Jahmiyyah and Muʿtazilah says, “An agent capable of acting favors one of two alternatives within his power over the other without any determining factor,” is obstinately denying the obvious.

The examples they give—a hungry person taking one of two loaves, or a fleeing person taking one of two roads—are evidence against them. Such a choice occurs only when one alternative has an advantage: either it is easier for him, it is the one that occurred to him and that he pictured, or he thought it more beneficial. One must therefore have some advantage over the other, whether in terms of his ability or his conception and awareness. He then gives preference to willing that option, while he does not will the other. How, then, can it be said that his will favored one over the other without any determining factor, or that he favored willing this over willing that without any determining factor? This is impossible, and its impossibility becomes evident once it is properly conceived.

But when they advanced invented doctrines concerning the origin of creation, contradicting both revelation and reason, they found themselves compelled to resort to this obstinate denial, as has been explained at length elsewhere. This also gave the philosophers an opening against them from another direction. Thus, they neither defended Islam nor defeated the philosophers.

---

## Page 8306

Sound reason clearly establishes that if one who is capable of acting neither wills an act nor performs it, and then comes to will and perform it, something must have occurred to bring this about. The discussion here concerns two issues.

The first concerns action and speech in general: did He begin acting and speaking by His will after not having done so, or has He always acted and spoken by His will? This is discussed at length under the questions of divine speech and actions, in connection with the Quran and the world's coming into existence.

The second concerns willing and performing a particular act, as in His words, Most High:

“His command, when He wills a thing, is only to say to it, ‘Be,’ and it is.”

And His words:

“So your Lord willed that they should reach maturity and recover their treasure.”

And His words:

“When We will to destroy a town, We command its affluent inhabitants, but they transgress within it; then the sentence against it becomes justified, and We destroy it utterly.”

And His words:

“When Allah wills harm for a people, there is no averting it.”

And His words:

“If Allah touches you with harm, none can remove it except Him; and if He wills good for you, none can repel His bounty.”

And His words:

“Say, ‘Have you considered those you invoke besides Allah? If Allah willed harm for me, could they remove His harm? Or if He willed mercy for me, could they withhold His mercy?’”

When He, glorified be He, wills any such thing, people hold various views concerning this.

It has been said that the will is single, eternal, and beginningless, and that only its relation to what is willed is renewed

---

## Page 8307

and its relation to all things willed is the same, yet one of the distinctive properties of the will is that it determines particulars without a determining factor. This is the view of Ibn Kullāb, al-Ashʿarī, and their followers.

Many rational thinkers say that the falsity of this is self-evident. Indeed, Abū al-Barakāt went so far as to say, “No rational thinker has held this view.” He was unaware that it was the view of a large group of speculative thinkers and theologians.

Its invalidity is evident on several grounds: in treating the will for this as different from the will for that; in making the will determine particulars by its very nature; and in positing nothing new arising when temporal events occur that could account for a particular determination or its absence. Rather, only a relation devoid of actual existence is renewed. This is not anything real, so nothing new has arisen. Temporal events thus occur and take their particular forms without any newly arising cause or determining factor.

The second view is that of those who, like the preceding group, affirm a single eternal will, but say that as new acts occur, particular wills arise within His essence through that eternal will. This is the position of the Karrāmiyyah and others. They are closer to the truth insofar as they affirm wills pertaining to particular acts. Nevertheless, they face the same difficulties as the preceding group: they affirm temporal events without a newly arising cause, and particular determinations without a determining factor; and they make that will a single will relating to all newly arising wills

---

## Page 8308

and also held that its particular determinations are intrinsic to it. They did not posit anything newly arising when the new wills come into existence that would enable those wills to determine the occurrence of events.

The third view is that of the Jahmiyyah and Muʿtazilah, who deny that will subsists in Him. They either deny will altogether, interpret it as nothing other than command and action, or affirm that a will comes into existence without inhering in any subject, as the Basrans hold. All these views, too, are known to be invalid.

The fourth view is that He has always willed through successive acts of will. Will as a kind is therefore eternal, whereas He wills each particular thing only at its appointed time. He, glorified is He, decrees things and records them, then subsequently creates them. When He decrees them, He knows what He will do and wills to do it at a future time, but He does not will to do it at that moment. When its time comes, He wills to do it. The former is resolve, and the latter is intention.

There are two views on whether He may be described as having resolve. The first disallows this, as held by al-Qādī Abū Bakr and al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā. The second permits it, and this is the more correct view. A number of the early generations recited, with a dammah (u-vowel):

“When I have resolved, then put your trust in Allah.”

And in the hadith

---

## Page 8309

authenticated on the authority of Umm Salamah:

“Then Allah resolved on my behalf.”

Likewise, in Muslim’s introduction:

“He resolved on my behalf.”

Whether or not this is called “resolve,” when He, glorified is He, decrees things, He knows that He will do them at their appointed times and wills to do them at those times. When the time comes, there must be both the will to perform the particular act and the act itself, and He must know what He is doing.

There are two well-known views concerning whether His knowledge of what He is doing is the same as His prior knowledge of what He would do, and whether His knowledge that He has done it is the same as that earlier knowledge. Reason and the Quran indicate that this involves something additional, as He says, “so that We may know,” in more than ten passages. Ibn ʿAbbās explained: “Only so that We may see.”

Thus, His willing a particular thing is favored by His knowledge of the consideration within it that favors His willing it. Will therefore follows knowledge. That particular thing’s possession of those favoring attributes exists only in knowledge and conception; it has no external existence. This is where those who said, “The nonexistent is a thing,” went wrong: they affirmed that the object of will existed externally. As for someone who does not affirm it as a thing in knowledge, or who holds that there is only a will

---

## Page 8310

that is single, together with a single knowledge, the objects of knowledge and will have no representation in knowledge in their view. These people thus deny that it is a thing in knowledge and will, while the others affirm that it is a thing in external reality.

That representation in knowledge and will came into being after not having existed. It comes into being by His will and power, just as He brings into being events distinct from Himself by His will and power. He determines what He will do, then does it. His assigning them one attribute rather than another, and one measure rather than another, is due to considerations within Himself that call for this. Thus, He wills only what His own self calls for Him to will, on account of a consideration that calls for it; and He favors one object of will over another only on that account.

He cannot favor something merely because He has the power to bring it about, for He had that power before willing it, and He also has power over other things. Singling this out as the object of His will therefore cannot be due to the power that applies equally to it and to other things. Nor can will single out one of two like things over the other without a determining factor. Rather, one who wills chooses one thing rather than another only because of a consideration in both the one willing and the object of will. The one willing must be more inclined toward it, and the object of will must contain something that makes that inclination prevail.

---

## Page 8311

The Quran and Sunnah affirm divine decree, His determining all matters before He creates them, and their being recorded in a Book. This is a fundamental principle that affirms His knowledge and will concerning everything that will come to pass. It also resolves many difficulties that have led various groups astray in this area concerning questions of knowledge and will.

Belief in divine decree is therefore one of the foundations of faith, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, stated in the hadith of Gabriel:

“Faith is to believe in Allah, His angels, His Books, His messengers, and resurrection after death, and to believe in divine decree, both its good and its evil.”

Ibn Umar and other Companions disavowed those who denied divine decree. Nevertheless, a group of speculative theologians and others affirm nothing in divine decree beyond eternal knowledge and an eternal will. When they affirm its being written down, they say that this records only part of that.

As for those who say that He determined these matters at that time—as related in Sahīh Muslim from Abd Allāh ibn Amr, from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, who said:

“Allah determined the decrees of all creation fifty thousand years before He created the heavens and the earth, and His Throne was upon the water”—

this has been discussed at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 8312

This is like His saying:

“And when your Lord declared that He would surely send against them, until the Day of Resurrection, those who would inflict grievous torment upon them.”

And His saying:

“I will surely fill Hell with you and all those among them who follow you.”

And His saying:

“Were it not for a word that had already gone forth from your Lord and an appointed term, punishment would have been inevitable.”

And His saying:

“Our word has already gone forth to Our servants, the messengers: they will surely be helped, and Our forces will surely prevail.”

And His saying:

“Had it not been for a prior decree from Allah, a tremendous punishment would have befallen you for what you took.”

The written record itself cannot have existed from eternity.

In a hadith transmitted by Hammād ibn Salama, from al-Ash‘ath ibn Abd al-Rahmān al-Jarmī, from Abū Qilāba, from Abū al-Ash‘ath al-San‘ānī, from Shaddād ibn Aws, the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Allah wrote a book two thousand years before He created the heavens and the earth. From it He sent down two verses with which He concluded Sūrat al-Baqara.”

Al-Tirmidhī narrated it and described it as singular.

Allah, glorified is He, sent down the Quran on the Night of Decree from the Preserved Tablet to the House of Honour in the lowest heaven. Many works on the foundations of religion and speculative theology contain innovated views but omit the position conveyed by the Quran and Sunnah. Thus al-Shahrastānī, despite his writings on religions and sects, lists, on the question of speech

---

## Page 8313

and will, among other matters, views that do not include the position indicated by the Quran and Sunnah, although some come closer to it.

Before him was Abū al-Hasan, whose book on disagreements among Muslims is among the most comprehensive works. In it, he exhaustively surveyed the views of the people of innovation. Yet when he presented the position of the people of Sunnah and hadith, he gave only a general account, without elaboration. He also reworked parts of it, presenting what he himself believed to be their position without this having been transmitted from any of them.

The position closest to his own is that of Ibn Kullāb. Ibn Kullāb’s position, however, is mingled with that of the Jahmiyyah: it combines the position of the people of Sunnah with that of the Jahmiyyah. The same applies to al-Ashʿarī’s position on the divine attributes. As for divine decree and faith, his position is that of Jahm.

As for the position he reported from the people of Sunnah and hadith, concerning which he said, “We affirm everything we have mentioned of their position, and we adopt it,” this comes closest to the truth among the positions he mentioned. Some of it he reported accurately from them; other parts he reworked and mixed with views of Jahm concerning the divine attributes and divine decree, since he himself believed those principles to be sound. He was eager to defend the people of Sunnah and hadith and agree with them, so he sought to

---

## Page 8314

reconcile the views he had adopted from the former with what he transmitted from the latter. For this reason, some say of him that he moved from explicit avowal to obfuscation. As some put it: “They are the female Jahmiyyah, while those are the male Jahmiyyah.”

His followers who understood and accepted his views on those fundamental principles openly expressed the opposition to the people of Sunnah and hadith that their position necessarily entailed. They did not stand in awe of the people of Sunnah and hadith, revere them, or believe their doctrines to be sound, as he had done. Both groups—the people of Sunnah and the Jahmiyyah—say that this is contradictory. From the Sunni perspective, however, his agreement with the people of hadith is praiseworthy, while his agreement with the Jahmiyyah is blameworthy; from the Jahmī perspective, his agreement with the people of hadith is blameworthy, while his agreement with the Jahmiyyah is praiseworthy. Thus, his later followers, such as Abū al-Ma‘ālī and others, were more overtly Jahmī and more pronounced in their negation of the divine attributes than their predecessors.

These are subtle matters in which Allah forgives those who err after exercising scholarly judgment. Yet what is correct is what the Messenger conveyed; the truth can never lie in anything contrary to it. And Allah knows best.
