One of the greatest principles, affirmed by the Quran in very many passages, as well as by the hadiths, all of Allah’s other scriptures, and the words of the early generations, and also indicated by

---

## Page 8315

clear rational truths, is the affirmation of His volitional attributes: for example, that He speaks by His will and power, with speech that subsists in His essence, and that His action, which He performs by His will, likewise subsists in His essence. Affirming this principle counters the misguided sectarians who deny it. The Quran and hadith, as well as the statements of the early generations and the imams, abound in affirmations of it. The unalloyed truth is what the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—conveyed; the truth cannot lie in anything contrary to it.

Yet complete guidance is attained through knowing this and understanding it clearly. Disagreement sometimes arises from misunderstanding and insufficient knowledge, and sometimes from ill intent. People disagree concerning knowledge and will, regarding their multiplicity and their being brought into existence. It is well known that, with regard to the willing of things that subsists in the soul, one cannot say, “Knowing this is knowing that,” or, “Willing this is willing that.” Such a claim is obstinate denial of the obvious. Distinguishing one instance of knowledge from another, or one act of willing from another, does not mean that one is separate from the other. Rather, even attributes of different kinds, such as knowledge

---

## Page 8316

and power and will, are not separate from one another when they subsist in a single subject. Rather, they share the same subject, just as taste, colour and fragrance subsist in a single citron, or in other fruits and things of this kind. Thus, when one speaks of “instances of knowledge and acts of willing,” there is no physical separation between one and another; rather, it is a single kind subsisting in the soul.

When someone comes to know one thing after knowing another, this kind has increased and become more abundant—or, if you wish, greater. Here, the increase in quantity does not exceed the increase in quality. Rather, one speaks of “abundant knowledge and great knowledge,” with greatness referring to its strength, the nobility of what is known, and similar considerations.

Thus the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said to Ubayy ibn Kaʿb:

“Do you know which verse of the Book of Allah that you know is the greatest?”

He replied: “Allah—there is no deity except Him, the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all.”

He said: “May your knowledge bring you joy, Abū al-Mundhir!”

Salmān wrote to Abū al-Dardāʾ: “Goodness does not lie in having abundant wealth and children. Rather, goodness lies in having abundant knowledge and great forbearance.”

The addition of knowledge to knowledge, willing to willing, and power to power resembles the joining of continuous bodies, as when water is added to water and its amount increases. Yet this is a continuous quantity, not a discrete one, unlike dirhams.

---

## Page 8317

Thus, when it is said, “Instances of knowledge and acts of willing have multiplied,” this describes an increase in their amount: they are more abundant and greater than before. It does not mean that there are separate, countable entities, as some people may understand it. Hence, “knowledge” is a generic noun and is hardly ever pluralized in the Quran. Rather, it says:

“Then whoever disputes with you concerning him after knowledge has come to you.”

Here, the generic term is used. Likewise, the Quran does not speak of “waters”; rather, it uses the generic term “water”:

“And We sent down purifying water from the sky,”

and similar expressions. Knowledge is likened to water, as in the Prophet’s saying—may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The guidance and knowledge with which Allah has sent me are like rain that falls upon a land…”—to the end of the hadith.

Allah also says:

“He sent down water from the sky, and valleys flowed according to their capacity,”

through to His words:

“Thus does Allah set forth examples.”

Whatever the Lord, Exalted is He, has created, He sees; and He hears the voices of His servants. All rational people agree that what is nonexistent cannot be seen.

The Sālimiyyah, such as Abū Tālib al-Makkī and others, did not say that it is seen as something subsisting independently. They said only that the Lord sees it within Himself, even though it has no existence in the nonexistent thing itself. Thus, they take the object of vision to be its conceptual representation subsisting within the knower

---

## Page 8318

, which is not sheer nonexistence.

Although they erred in some of what they said, they did not say that sheer nonexistence, which is nothing at all, can be seen; no rational person would say this. In reality, when something is seen, it is only its conceptual representation that is seen, not the thing itself. When this question was raised, Abū al-Shaykh al-Asbahānī instructed people to refrain from discussing it. Before a thing comes into existence, it is not seen; after it ceases to exist, it is not seen. It is seen only while it exists. This is perfection in seeing. Likewise, the voices of His servants are heard when they exist, not after they have faded away or before they arise. Allah, Exalted is He, says:

“And say, ‘Work, for Allah will see your deeds, as will His Messenger and the believers.’”

He also says:

“Then We made you successors in the land after them, so that We might see how you would act.”

Section

Allah, Exalted is He, sent the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—as guidance and mercy to all worlds. Just as He sent him with knowledge, guidance, and proofs drawn from reason and revelation, He also sent him to show kindness and mercy to people without seeking anything in return, and to be patient in the face of the harm they inflict

---

## Page 8319

and to endure it. Thus He sent him with knowledge, generosity, and forbearance—a knowledgeable guide, generous and benevolent, forbearing and forgiving. Allah, Exalted is He, says:

“And indeed, you guide to a straight path—the path of Allah, to whom belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on earth. Surely, to Allah all matters return.”

He, Exalted is He, also says:

“A Book We have sent down to you so that you may bring people out of darkness into light, by their Lord’s permission, to the path of the Almighty, the Praiseworthy.”

And He, Exalted is He, says:

“And thus We have revealed to you a spirit by Our command. You did not know what the Scripture was, nor faith, but We made it a light by which We guide whom We will of Our servants.”

There are many similar passages. He also says:

“Say, ‘I ask you for no reward for it.’”

And He says:

“Say, ‘Whatever reward I may have asked of you is yours. My reward rests with Allah alone.’”

And He says:

“Say, ‘I ask you for no reward for it.’”

He thus possesses knowledge, gives guidance, reforms hearts, and directs them toward what will set them right in this world and the Hereafter, without seeking anything in return. This is the description of all the messengers; each says:

“I ask you for no reward for it.”

Hence the man mentioned in Surah Yā-Sīn said:

“O my people, follow the messengers. Follow those who ask you for no reward and are themselves rightly guided.”

This is also the path of those who follow him, as Allah says:

“Say, ‘This is my path: I call to Allah with clear insight—I and those who follow me.’”

---

## Page 8320

As for those who oppose them, Allah says of those who claim affiliation with them while practicing religious innovation:

“Indeed, many rabbis and monks devour people’s wealth unjustly and turn people away from the path of Allah.”

These people took their wealth and barred them from Allah’s path—the opposite of what the Messenger did. What, then, of those who are worse than these, such as pagan scholars, sorcerers, and soothsayers? They devour people’s wealth unjustly and turn people away from Allah’s path even more than the rabbis and monks do. Yet Allah, Glorified is He, says:

“Indeed, many rabbis and monks…”

Thus, not all of them are like this. Rather, He says elsewhere:

“And you will find the nearest of them in affection to the believers to be those who say, ‘We are Christians.’ That is because among them are priests and monks, and because they are not arrogant.”

He also says in describing the Messenger:

“Nor does he withhold knowledge of the unseen.”

There are two readings here. According to the reading bi-zanīn (“suspected”), the meaning is that he is not suspected of falsehood concerning the unseen; rather, he is truthful and trustworthy in what he reports. According to the reading bi-danīn (“withholding”), the meaning is that he is not miserly, imparting it only in return for payment, like those who demand payment for what they know. Thus, Allah describes him as speaking the truth, neither lying nor concealing it. He has described the People of the Book, by contrast, as making it into sheets which they display while concealing much, and as exchanging it for a paltry price.

---

## Page 8321

Along with both of these, He strengthened him with patience to bear their abuse. He also enabled him to give them what they desperately need, without recompense, even as they subject him to coercion and harm him for it. This is greater still than offering beneficial medicine to the sick and administering it without recompense while they cause him harm, as a compassionate father does. Indeed, he is the father of the believers. Likewise, He described his community by saying:

“You are the best community brought forth for mankind.”

Abū Hurayrah said: “You are the best people for mankind: you bring them in chains until you bring them into Paradise.”

Thus they strive, sacrificing their lives and wealth for the benefit and betterment of people, even though those people, in their ignorance, resent it. As Ahmad said in his address:

“Praise be to Allah, who has appointed, in every age without messengers, a remnant of the people of knowledge who call those who have strayed to guidance and patiently endure their abuse. Through Allah’s Book they bring the dead to life, and through Allah’s light they give sight to the blind. How many whom Iblīs had slain have they brought to life, and how many lost wanderers have they guided! How good their effect on people, and how harmful people’s effect on them!”

And so his words continue. Such is the case, and to Allah belongs praise—abundant, pure, and blessed praise. He, Glorified is He, recompenses people for their deeds, and Allah aids His servant as long as the servant aids his brother.

---

## Page 8322

So He bestows His favors upon the Messenger as a reward for their goodness, and all of it comes from Him. He is the Most Merciful, the Ever-Merciful, the Munificent, the Generous, the Compassionate, the Bestower of blessings. His are all blessings and bounty; His is all beautiful praise. To Him belongs praise—abundant, pure, and blessed praise. He, Glorified is He, loves noble character and detests base traits. He loves penetrating insight when doubts arise, and sound judgment when desires assail.

It has also been said:

He may love courage even when shown in killing snakes, and generosity even when shown by giving a handful of dates. The Quran tells us that He loves those who do good and those who are patient. This is generosity and courage.

Section

His saying, “The Most Generous,” entails that generosity is an attribute of His own, that He is the most generous, and that He is beneficent toward His servants. He therefore deserves praise for His excellent attributes and His beneficence.

There are three interpretations of His saying, “Possessor of Majesty and Honor.”

One interpretation is:

Worthy of reverence and honor. This is like saying that He is “Worthy of being feared,” meaning the One who deserves to be

---

## Page 8323

feared.

Another interpretation is: He is worthy of being revered in Himself and of bestowing honor upon those devoted and obedient to Him.

A third interpretation is: He is worthy of being revered in Himself, and it befits Him to bestow honor.

Al-Khattābī mentioned these three possibilities, and Ibn al-Jawzī quoted his words as follows:

Abū Sulaymān al-Khattābī said: “Jalāl (majesty) is the verbal noun corresponding to jalīl (majestic). One says, ‘jalīl bayyin al-jalālah wa-l-jalāl’ (majestic, manifestly possessed of majesty). Ikrām (honoring) is the verbal noun of akrama, yukrimu (to honor). The meaning is that He honors those devoted and obedient to Him, and that Allah deserves to be revered and honored, neither denied nor disbelieved in.” He said: “The meaning may also be that He honors those devoted to Him and raises their ranks.”

(I say: This is what al-Baghawī mentioned when he said: “‘Possessor of Majesty’ means greatness and grandeur; ‘and Honor’ means that He honors His prophets and His close servants through His kindness, alongside His majesty and greatness.”

Al-Khattābī said: “It is also possible that one of the two—majesty—is attributed to Allah as His attribute, while the other is attributed to the servant as an action. This is like His saying, Most High:

‘He is worthy of being feared and is the One who forgives.’

“One of the two, forgiveness, thus refers to Allah, while the other, fear of Him, refers to His servants.”

I say: The first interpretation comes closest to the intended meaning, although jalāl here

---

## Page 8324

is not the verbal noun of jalla jalālan (to be majestic), but a noun standing in place of the verbal noun in ajalla ijlālan (to revere). This is like the saying of the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“Part of revering Allah is honoring an elderly Muslim, a bearer of the Quran who neither goes to excess concerning it nor neglects it, and a just ruler.”

He thus made honoring these people part of jalāl toward Allah—that is, revering Allah. This is like His saying:

“And Allah has caused you to grow from the earth a [progressive] growth.”

Likewise, one says kallamahu kalāman (he spoke to him) and a‘tāhu ‘atā’an (he gave him a gift). Kalām and ‘atā’ are nouns standing in place of the verbal nouns taklīm (speaking) and i‘tā’ (giving). Jalāl is coupled with ikrām (honoring), and it is the verbal noun of a transitive verb; so too is ikrām.

Among the sayings of the early generations is:

“Revere Allah too greatly to say such a thing.”

In the report concerning Moses, he says: “My Lord, I find myself in a state in which I revere You too greatly to mention You.” He replied:

“Remember Me in every state.”

If He deserves reverence and honor, it necessarily follows that He possesses in Himself the qualities that warrant them. Similarly, when one says, “The Deity is He who deserves to be taken as God—that is, worshipped,” He is in Himself worthy of the qualities that warrant this. And when it is said:

“He is worthy of being feared,”

He possesses in Himself the qualities that warrant His being feared. Another example is the saying of the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), when he raised his head from bowing, after

---

## Page 8325

saying, “Our Lord, to You belongs all praise: praise filling the heavens, the earth, all that lies between them, and whatever else You will. You are worthy of praise and glory—the truest words a servant can say—and we are all Your servants. O Allah, none can withhold what You give, and none can give what You withhold, nor can worldly fortune avail its possessor before You.”

That is, He deserves to be praised and glorified in Himself. His servants cannot encompass all the praise due to Him; He is as He has praised Himself. Likewise, He is worthy of reverence and honor. He—glorified be He—reveres Himself and honors Himself, while His servants cannot encompass all the reverence and honor due to Him.

Reverence is akin to magnification, while honoring is akin to love and praise. This is like the saying, “To Him belongs dominion, and to Him belongs all praise.” Thus, to Him belong reverence and dominion, and to Him belong honor and praise.

Prayer is founded upon glorifying Allah while bowing and prostrating, praising Him and affirming His oneness while standing and sitting, and proclaiming His greatness when changing postures. As Jābir said:

“We were with the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace). Whenever we ascended, we proclaimed Allah’s greatness, and whenever we descended, we glorified Him. Prayer was patterned accordingly.”

Narrated by Abū Dāwūd. While bowing, one says:

“Glory be to my Lord, the Magnificent.”

And the Prophet said

---

## Page 8326

(may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“I have been forbidden to recite the Quran while bowing or prostrating. When bowing, magnify the Lord; when prostrating, strive earnestly in supplication, for then your supplications are likely to be answered.”

When he raises his head, he praises Allah, saying:

“Allah hears whoever praises Him. Our Lord, to You belongs all praise.”

Thus, he praises Him during this standing just as he praises Him during the first standing when reciting the Mother of the Quran. Praising Allah and affirming His oneness take precedence over magnification alone. This is why al-Fātihah encompasses these meanings: its beginning is praise and its middle is glorification. Then, while bowing, one magnifies the Lord, whereas while standing, one praises Him, extols Him, and glorifies Him. This shows that magnification alone is subordinate to His being praised and worshipped. For He loves to be praised and worshipped, and magnification must accompany this, since it necessarily entails magnifying Him.

Magnification, however, may be detached from praise and worship, according to the principle of the Jahmiyyah. Such magnification is not commanded, nor does it make a servant a believer, a worshipper, or obedient.

Abū ʿAbd Allāh Ibn al-Khatīb al-Rāzī assigns majesty to attributes of negation and honor to affirmative attributes, calling the former “attributes of majesty” and the latter “attributes of honor.” This, however, is his own terminology, not what is meant in His words:

---

## Page 8327

“And the Face of your Lord, possessed of majesty and honor, will remain.”

And His words:

“Blessed is the Name of your Lord, Possessor of Majesty and Honor.”

In the codex of the people of Syria, the wording is: “Blessed is the Name of your Lord, possessed of majesty and honor,” with dhū al-jalāl wa-l-ikrām. This is the reading of Ibn ‘Āmir. Thus, the name itself is described as possessing majesty and honor. In all other codices and in the reading of the majority, the wording is dhī al-jalāl (“possessed of majesty”), referring to the One named Himself.

In the earlier verse, “And the Face of your Lord, possessed of majesty and honor, will remain,” it is His Face—glorified is He—that is described as possessing these qualities. This necessarily entails that He Himself possesses majesty and honor. For if His Face possesses majesty and honor, this points to His being so, just as, if His name possesses majesty and honor, this points to the One named.

This makes clear that what is meant is that He deserves to be revered and honored. For the name itself is glorified and mentioned, while what is intended is the One named. The name itself does nothing: it neither bestows honor nor performs any other act. Hence, the Quran does not attribute any acts or blessings to the name.

Rather, it says:

“Glorify the Name of your Lord, the Most High.”

“Blessed is the Name of your Lord.”

And similar expressions. For Allah’s name is blessed, and blessing is attained through it. The servant glorifies the Name of his Lord, the Most High, saying:

“Glory be to my Lord, the Most High.”

And when His words were revealed: “Glorify the Name of

---

## Page 8328

your Lord, the Most High,” he said: “Include this in your prostration.” So they said, “Glory be to my Lord, the Most High.” Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not say, “Glory be to the Name of my Lord, the Most High.” Rather, his saying, “Glory be to my Lord, the Most High,” glorifies His name with the intention of glorifying the One named, not merely the name itself. This is like His words:

“Say, ‘Call upon Allah or call upon the Most Merciful. Whichever name you call upon, to Him belong the Most Beautiful Names.’”

The supplicant says, “O Allah,” or “O Most Merciful,” intending the One named. His words “Whichever” mean whichever of the two names you invoke; invoking the name is invoking the One it names.

This is what those among Ahl al-Sunnah who said, “The name is the One named,” intended. They meant that when a name is invoked or mentioned, it is the One named who is intended. When someone praying says, “Allah is the Greatest,” he has mentioned his Lord’s name, intending the One named. They did not mean that the utterance itself is the actual entity existing outside the mind. The absurdity of this is obvious to anyone who understands it: if that were so, anyone who said “fire” would have his tongue burned. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The point is that majesty and honor are like sovereignty and praise, and like love and veneration. This applies to both affirmative and negative attributes, for every negation entails

---

## Page 8329

an affirmation. Pure negation, however, contains no praise. This exposes the error of those who assign one of the two to negation and the other to affirmation, especially those among the Jahmiyyah who deny love of Him and affirm no attributes of His that call for love and praise. Rather, they affirm only what entails subjugation, such as power. Thus, they have believed in part and disbelieved in part, and have deviated concerning His names and signs to the extent that they have rejected the truth. This has been discussed at length elsewhere.

Section

In the first revelation, Allah Most High says:

“Read in the name of your Lord who created.”

And He says:

“Read, and your Lord is the Most Generous.”

In both places, He refers to Himself through a possessive construction that makes the expression definite and indicates that He is known to those addressed. For the Lord Most High is known to the servant without inferring His existence from the fact that He created. Although created things constitute evidence and point to the Creator, He is known through the natural disposition before this inference is made. Knowledge of Him is innate, implanted in the natural disposition—necessary, self-evident, and primary. And His command, “Read,” although addressed first to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is

---

## Page 8330

an address to everyone, whether His words,

“Read, and your Lord is the Most Generous,”

are addressed to humanity in general—with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, being the first to hear this address, or one of those it addresses—or specifically to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as has been suggested regarding comparable passages. For example, His words,

“Whatever good comes to you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself,”

have been understood as addressed either to him or to humanity generally. There are other such examples. Even if the address is understood as directed to him, it is established that commands and prohibitions addressed to him also address his community, unless there is evidence that they apply exclusively to him.

This makes clear that the words of Allah Most High,

“So if you are in doubt about what We have revealed to you, ask those who have been reading the Scripture before you,”

also encompass others besides him. Indeed, many commentators have said: “The address is directed to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, but others are intended.” That is, they are the ones intended to ask because of their doubts, whereas he was not intended to ask, since he had no doubt.

There is no question that this does not preclude his being addressed and intended by the address. Indeed, this is what the wording explicitly states, so it is not permissible to say that the address did not include him. Moreover, the address does not command him to ask unconditionally; rather, he is commanded to do so if he has any doubt. This does not entail that he had any doubt, nor that he was commanded to ask

---

## Page 8331

unconditionally; rather, he was commanded to do so if such doubt existed. A ruling dependent on a condition does not apply when that condition is absent.

Likewise, concerning His words,

“The truth is from your Lord, so never be among those who doubt,”

and His words,

“And do not obey the disbelievers and the hypocrites,”

and similar passages, many commentators say that the address is directed to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, but others are intended. That is, others might doubt and obey those people, and so are forbidden to do so, whereas he would neither doubt nor obey them. Yet even assuming this to be so, he too is addressed by these words and is subject to this prohibition.

Allah, glorified be He, has forbidden him to commit what He has made unlawful: associating partners with Him, speaking about Him without knowledge, wrongdoing, and acts of indecency. Through Allah’s prohibition of these things and his obedience to Allah in this regard, he became deserving of an immense reward. Were it not for the prohibition and his obedience, he would not have deserved that reward.

It is not necessary that someone who receives commands and prohibitions be a person whose obedience is in doubt and who might disobey the Lord—or disobey Him altogether and never obey Him. Rather, Allah commanded the angels while knowing that they would obey Him, and He commands the prophets while knowing that they will obey Him. Likewise, everything in which the believers obey Him is something He has commanded them to do, while knowing that they will obey Him.

---

## Page 8332

Nor should it be said, “He has no need of the command.” Rather, it is through the command that he becomes obedient and deserving of an immense reward. A prohibition, however, presupposes that he is capable of doing what is forbidden and that, were he to will it, he would do it, so that when he refrains from it, he may be rewarded for doing so. It may also presuppose the presence of a motive urging him to do it, in which case he is forbidden to do it. Through the prohibition and Allah’s help in obeying it, he refrains from what he has been forbidden, even when a motive urging him towards it is present.

Likewise, concerning His words,

“Ask the Children of Israel,”

it has been said that this is a command to the Messenger, intended to include both him and the believers. It has also been said that it is a command to everyone accountable before Allah. Thus, His command in this surah,

“Read,”

is like His command at its conclusion,

“Prostrate and draw near,”

and His words,

“So do not oppress the orphan,”

“And do not rebuff the one who asks,”

“And proclaim the blessings of your Lord.”

This encompasses the entire Muslim community. His words,

“O you wrapped in garments,”

“Stand in prayer at night, except for a little,”

were addressed to all the believers. The same applies to His words,

“O you covered in your cloak,”

“Arise and warn,”

when the Messenger was commanded to convey the warning revealed to him. This is a collective obligation. The Muslim community must therefore convey what was revealed to him and warn as he warned. Allah Most High says:

“Why, then, should not a group from each section of them go forth to gain understanding of the religion and warn their people when they return to them, so that they may

---

## Page 8333

be on their guard.” When the jinn heard the Quran,

“they returned to their people as warners.”

Since this is so, every person has knowledge of his Lord within his heart. When he is told,

“Read in the name of your Lord,”

he knows the Lord in whose name he is commanded to read, just as he knows that he is a created being. The existence of a created being necessarily implies a Creator and points to Him. This has been discussed at length elsewhere, where it has been explained that acknowledging and recognizing the Creator is innate and self-evident within human souls. Some people, however, may experience something that corrupts their innate disposition, so that they need rational inquiry to acquire this knowledge. This is the view of most people, including the most accomplished rational thinkers: knowledge is sometimes attained immediately and sometimes through rational inquiry, as several leading speculative theologians have acknowledged.

This verse also indicates that rational inquiry is not the first obligation. Rather, the first thing Allah enjoined upon His Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was:

“Read in the name of your Lord.”

He did not say, “Engage in rational inquiry and seek proof until you come to know the Creator.” Likewise, this surah was the first thing the Prophet conveyed. Those to whom it was conveyed were therefore addressed by this verse before anything else, yet it did not command them to engage in rational inquiry or seek proof. Many speculative theologians, however, have maintained that the soul can acknowledge the Creator and affirm His existence only through rational inquiry.

---

## Page 8334

Many of them further restricted this to a particular form of rational inquiry: examining accidents and their inseparability from bodies, such that bodies cannot exist without them. They said:

“Whatever is never free of events that come into being, or does not precede such events, must itself have come into being.”

Some of them then believed this premise to be self-evident—indeed, necessarily known. They did not distinguish between a particular, bounded event and events considered as a kind, occurring continuously one after another, either because they thought the latter impossible or because it had never occurred to them. Yet even if it is said to be impossible, knowledge of its impossibility is not self-evident.

What is self-evident is only that an event with a definite beginning is like any other event that comes into being. As for events reckoned from a definite point in time, whatever does not precede them must itself have come into being. Whatever is never free of them does not precede them and must therefore have come into being. For if it does not precede them, it must exist either alongside them or after them. In either case, it must have come into being.

If, however, one supposes a perpetual succession of events, one after another, this must be said to be either possible or impossible. But knowledge of its impossibility requires proof. No known group of rational thinkers has said:

“Knowledge that this is impossible is self-evident and necessary, requiring no proof.”

---

## Page 8335

Indeed, many people do not fully conceive of this. Whenever they conceive of an event that comes into being, they posit a starting point in their minds, then imagine something before it, then something before that—but only to finite limits, according to what their minds envisage. This is like the mind imagining one number after another: whatever it imagines is finite. Some people, when told of “beginningless eternity,” or that “this existed in beginningless eternity,” conceive of it in this way. This is mistaken. “Beginningless eternity” has no beginning, just as “endless eternity” has no end. Whatever limit the mind points to, beginningless eternity lies beyond it. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that those who said that knowledge of the Lord can be attained only through rational inquiry, and then said that it can be attained only through this particular inquiry, are the Jahmī and Qadarī speculative theologians and their followers. The early generations of the Muslim community, its leading scholars, and the majority of scholars, both speculative theologians and others, have agreed that these people are mistaken in making this particular form of inquiry obligatory and in claiming that knowledge of the Lord depends upon it. For it is necessarily known from the religion of the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that he neither made this obligatory upon the Muslim community nor commanded them to undertake it. Indeed, neither he nor any of the community’s early generations even followed this method to attain such knowledge. Now, concerning this rational inquiry—this argument—people hold three views.

---

## Page 8336

One view is that it is obligatory and that knowledge depends upon it, as these people maintain.

Another view is that knowledge can be attained without it, but that it offers another path to knowledge. This is held by many who consider this method valid but do not regard it as obligatory, such as al-Khattābī, Judge Abū Yaʿlā, and Abū Jaʿfar al-Simnānī, the judge of Mosul and teacher of Abū al-Walīd al-Bājī. Al-Simnānī used to say: “Making rational inquiry obligatory is a remnant of Muʿtazilism that remained with Shaykh Abū al-Hasan al-Ashʿarī.”

These are the people who do not regard this particular rational inquiry as obligatory. Some of them do not regard rational inquiry as obligatory at all, such as al-Simnānī, Ibn Hazm, and others. Others regard it as obligatory in general, such as al-Khattābī and Abū al-Faraj al-Maqdisī. Judge Abū Yaʿlā sometimes adopts one position and sometimes the other; indeed, at times he even maintains that this particular form of inquiry is obligatory, as do Abū al-Maʿālī and others.

Among those who consider rational inquiry obligatory, some say that it is the first obligation. Others say: “Rather, it is the knowledge that must be attained through it.” This is a merely verbal dispute.

Similarly, some have said that the first obligation is the intention to undertake rational inquiry, as expressed by Abū al-Maʿālī. Others among them have said: “Rather, it is the doubt that precedes it,” as Abū Hāshim maintained. These and other views have been discussed at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 8337

and it has been shown that all these views are mistaken, contrary to the Quran, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the early generations and the leading scholars—indeed, they are also rationally unsound. This verse is among the evidence for that, for the first obligation Allah imposed upon His Messenger and the believers was what He commanded in His words:

“Recite in the name of your Lord who created.”

Those who maintain that knowledge can be attained only through rational inquiry argue: “If it could be attained otherwise, the obligation to attain it would lapse.” Judge Abū Bakr and others have stated this.

They may be answered as follows: Nothing in the accounts of the messengers that Allah has related to us indicates that any of them made this knowledge obligatory. Rather, all nations already possessed it.

Most messengers, however, began their call by commanding people to worship Allah alone and none besides Him, as Allah relates concerning Noah, Hūd, Sālih, and Shuayb. Their peoples acknowledged the Creator, but they associated partners with Him, worshipping others besides Him. Such was also the case with the Arabs among whom Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent.

Some unbelievers professed denial of the Creator, such as Pharaoh, who said:

“O chiefs, I know of no god for you other than myself. So, Hāmān, fire bricks for me and build me a lofty tower, so that I may look upon the God of Moses; indeed, I think he is one of

---

## Page 8338

the liars.” He also said:

“I am your lord, the Most High.”

And he said to Moses:

“If you take a god other than me, I will surely imprison you.”

He also said:

“O Hāmān, build me a lofty tower so that I may reach the pathways—the pathways of the heavens—and look upon the God of Moses; indeed, I think he is a liar.”

Yet Allah commanded Moses to say what He relates in the Quran:

“And when your Lord called Moses, ‘Go to the wrongdoing people—the people of Pharaoh. Will they not fear Allah?’ He said, ‘My Lord, I fear that they will deny me, and my chest tightens and my tongue is not fluent, so send for Aaron. They also have a charge against me, so I fear they will kill me.’ He said, ‘No! Go, both of you, with Our signs. We are with you, listening. Go to Pharaoh and say, “We are messengers from the Lord of all worlds: send the Children of Israel with us.”’ Pharaoh said, ‘Did we not raise you among us as a child, and did you not spend years of your life among us? And you committed that deed of yours, and you were ungrateful.’ Moses said, ‘I did it then, when I was among those who were astray. So I fled from you when I feared you; then my Lord granted me wisdom and made me one of the messengers.’”

Pharaoh said, in denial and rejection:

“And what is the Lord of all worlds?”

Moses said:

“The Lord of the heavens and the earth and everything between them, if you have certainty.”

“Pharaoh said to those around him, ‘Do you not hear?’ Moses said, ‘Your Lord and the Lord of your forefathers.’ Pharaoh said, ‘This messenger of yours who has been sent to you is surely mad.’ Moses said, ‘The Lord of the east and the west and everything between them’”—and the following verses.

---

## Page 8339

Some people have supposed that Pharaoh’s question, “And what is the Lord of all worlds?” was a question about the Lord’s essence, like someone asking for the definitions of things: “What is a human being? What is an angel? What is a jinni?” and so forth.

They said: “Since the One being asked about has no essence, Moses turned from answering the question to explaining how He may be known, saying: ‘The Lord of the heavens and the earth.’” This view, advanced by some later scholars, is false. Pharaoh’s question expressed denial and rejection. He was not asking about the essence of a Lord whose existence he acknowledged; rather, he denied and rejected Him. Hence, later in the exchange, he said:

“If you take a god other than me, I will surely imprison you.”

He also said:

“Indeed, I think he is a liar.”

His question thus expressed denial and rejection, as though he were saying: “The worlds have no Lord who could send you. Who, then, is this?” He was denying Him. Moses therefore made clear that Pharaoh and those present knew Him, and that His signs were manifest and clear, leaving no grounds for denying Him: “You merely deny with your tongues what you know in your hearts.” As Moses said to Pharaoh elsewhere:

“You know well that none but the Lord of the heavens and the earth has sent down these signs as clear proofs.”

And Allah Most High said:

“They denied them, though their souls were convinced of them, out of wrongdoing and arrogance. So see what was the end of those who spread corruption.”

---

## Page 8340

Nor did Pharaoh ask, “And who is the Lord of all worlds?” For “who” asks about someone’s particular identity: it is used by someone who knows that the one being asked about belongs to the category of beings endowed with knowledge, but is unsure of that individual’s identity. Thus, one might ask a messenger known to have come from a human being, “Who sent you?”

“What,” however, asks about a description. He was saying: “What sort of thing is this? What is this that you have called ‘the Lord of all worlds’?” He said this in denial and rejection of Him. Since his question expressed denial, Moses answered that He was too well known to be denied and too evident to be doubted or questioned. He said:

“The Lord of the heavens and the earth and all that lies between them, if you have certainty.”

He did not say, “Certainty about such-and-such,” but left it unrestricted. Whatever certainty you have about anything, the foremost certainty is certainty about this Lord, as the messengers said to their peoples:

“Can there be any doubt about Allah?”

If you say, “We have no certainty about anything; rather, we have been deprived of all knowledge,” this is the claim of universal skepticism, and anyone making it is plainly lying. For knowledge is inseparable from every human being: every rational human being must possess some knowledge. For this reason,

---

## Page 8341

the intellect has been defined as self-evident knowledge, which no rational person lacks.

Pharaoh’s statement, “Your messenger who has been sent to you is surely mad,” exemplifies the slanders that those who reject the messengers direct against them. Whenever the messengers departed from the customs that their opponents considered praiseworthy, they accused them of madness. They openly professed denial of the Creator, or misgivings and doubt about Him. Such was the condition of the people generally and the character of their religion, which they considered good. Their only god, whom they obeyed, was Pharaoh. Thus he said:

“Your messenger who has been sent to you is surely mad.”

Moses therefore made it clear to him: it is you who have been deprived of beneficial reason, and you are more deserving of this description. He said:

“The Lord of the east and the west and all that lies between them, if you use your reason.”

For reason necessarily entails certain, self-evident knowledge, foremost among which, in the innate human disposition, is acknowledgment of the Creator. Having first explained that whoever is certain of anything is certain of Him—and certainty about anything is inseparable from reason—he then explained that acknowledging Him is inseparable from reason.

What is praiseworthy, however, is beneficial knowledge upon which its possessor acts. If he does not act upon it, he is said to have no reason. It is likewise said of someone who does not follow what he knows with certainty:

---

## Page 8342

“He has no certainty.” For certainty can mean knowledge firmly established in the heart, and it can also mean acting upon that knowledge. Thus, only someone in whose heart both knowledge and action are firmly established is described as “certain.” Pharaoh’s people did not follow what they knew, so they had neither reason nor certainty.

Moses’ words imply both points: if you have certainty, you already know Him; and if you have reason, you already know Him. If you claim that you have neither certainty nor reason, and that the same is true of your people, then you are admitting that you lack the distinguishing quality of a human being. Someone in such a condition is unfit to make the claim to divinity that you make. Moreover, this claim of yours is false, for you are certain of Him, as the Most High said:

“They denied them, though their souls were certain of them, out of injustice and arrogance.”

You also possess reason by which you know Him, but your desire for supremacy and corruption on earth prevents you from following what reason requires. In this sense, you have no reason, just as the inhabitants of the Fire said:

“If only we had listened or used our reason, we would not be among the inhabitants of the Blaze.”

The Most High also said of the disbelievers:

“Or do you think that most of them listen or use their reason? They are only like cattle; rather, they are further astray.”

The Most High said of Pharaoh and his people:

“He made fools of his people, and they obeyed him. Indeed, they were a people

---

## Page 8343

who were defiantly disobedient.” A person who is easily swayed is a fool who does not act upon his knowledge but follows his desires. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that none of the messengers began calling his people by saying: “You are commanded to seek knowledge of the Creator, so reflect and reason until you come to know Him.” They were not initially charged either with acquiring this knowledge itself or with examining the proofs leading to it, since their hearts already knew and acknowledged Him. Every child is born upon the innate disposition, but influences arise that alter it. When a person is reminded, he recalls what is within his innate disposition. Hence Allah said when addressing Moses:

“Both of you speak to him gently, so that perhaps he may be reminded”

of the knowledge within his innate disposition by which he knows his Lord, recognizes His blessings and kindness towards him, and knows his own dependence upon Him. This calls him to faith.

“Or become fearful”

of the punishment of which he is warned; this too calls him to faith. As the Most High said:

“Invite to the way of your Lord with wisdom and good admonition.”

Wisdom makes the truth known, so that it is accepted on account of its truth, without resistance. Whoever encounters resistance from his own desires is admonished through encouragement and warning. Knowledge of the truth calls the one who possesses it to follow it, for truth is beloved to the innate disposition. It is dearer to it, more exalted in its eyes, and more delightful to it than falsehood, which has no reality. The innate disposition does not love such falsehood.

---

## Page 8344

If the truth and knowledge of it do not move him to follow it, he is warned of the consequences of denial and disobedience and the punishment they entail, for the soul naturally fears punishment. Every living being flees what harms it, unlike what benefits it. Some people, however, follow their desires, choosing what is inferior over what is superior. Likewise, some deny the punishment of which they are warned or deliberately disregard it so that they can do what they desire. For if a person believed in it and kept it in mind, he would not urge his soul to pursue its desires. There must therefore be some heedlessness and ignorance for him to follow them. Hence everyone who disobeys Allah is ignorant, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

The purpose here is to draw attention to the fact that His words,

“Read in the name of your Lord,”

indicate that the Lord is already known to those addressed and that the innate disposition acknowledges Him. This is also indicated by His words,

“And when your Lord brought forth from the children of Adam, from their loins, their descendants and made them testify concerning themselves,”

and the rest of the verse, which has been discussed at length elsewhere. Likewise, the messengers’ words,

“Can there be any doubt about Allah?”

express a negation: there is no doubt about Allah. This is a question calling for acknowledgment: it calls upon the nations to affirm what they already acknowledge—that there is no doubt about Allah. Thus, it is a question calling for acknowledgment.

---

## Page 8345

For when an interrogative particle precedes a negative particle, the question calls for acknowledgment, as in His words:

“Have We not expanded your breast for you?”

“Have We not given him two eyes?”

“Has the news of those before them not reached them?”

There are many similar examples. Pharaoh’s question, by contrast, expresses denial rather than calling for acknowledgment: it contains only an interrogative particle, and the context indicates that it expresses denial.

If it is asked:

“If knowledge and acknowledgment of Him are inherent in every innate disposition, how can so many speculative thinkers, both Muslim and non-Muslim, deny this, when they claim to be the very ones who establish rational proofs concerning theological questions?”

The first answer is:

The first people known in Islam to deny this knowledge were the adherents of the speculative theology that the early generations unanimously condemned: the Jahmiyyah and the Qadariyyah. The early generations of this ummah regarded them as among the most misguided and ignorant sects. Yet many of their principles spread among later thinkers who agree with the early generations on many matters in which their own Jahmī predecessors opposed those generations. Some people thus came to suppose that this position originally came from Muslim scholars. That is not so: it first came from those whom the leading authorities of the religion and the Muslim scholars condemned.

The second answer is:

A person may possess knowledge, intentions, and other attributes without knowing that they are present within him. For an attribute’s presence in the soul is distinct from

---

## Page 8346

the person’s awareness that it is present within him. Something’s existence within a person or elsewhere is distinct from his knowledge of it. This is like his bodily attributes: some he does not see, such as his face and the back of his neck; others he sees if he deliberately looks at them, such as his abdomen, thigh, and upper arms. His upper arms may bear moles and other marks, or exhibit other conditions, which he has neither seen nor known about. Yet if he deliberately looked, he would see them. Some people cannot see such things because their eyesight has been affected by dimness of vision, blindness, or some other impairment.

Likewise, he may know some attributes of his soul but not others. Yet if he deliberately reflected on the state of his soul, he would recognize them. Some, however, he would not recognize even upon reflection, because of the corruption of his insight and the impairments that have affected it.

What makes this clear is that voluntary actions are inconceivable without an intention present within the person’s soul. Anyone who performs a voluntary action while knowing what he is doing must intend it. Someone who eats, drinks, or dresses while knowing that he is doing so must intend to do so. A voluntary action cannot occur without an intention. If he has conceived of the action he performs and has actually performed it, he must intend it, having conceived of it. If he intends it and has conceived of it, it is impossible for him not to intend what he has conceived of and performed.

---

## Page 8347

Thus, when a person stands to pray, knowing that it is the noon prayer, it is impossible for him to perform that prayer while knowing this, without having forgotten it, yet not intend to perform it. The same applies to fasting: if he recognizes that tomorrow is a day of Ramadan and wishes to fast Ramadan, it is impossible for him not to intend to fast that day. Likewise, if he announces his entry into Hajj, knowing that this is what he is doing, it is impossible for him not to intend Hajj. Similarly, if he knows that he is performing ablution for prayer and is actually doing so, it is impossible for him not to intend ablution.

There are many such examples. We find a great many scholars, let alone ordinary people, trying to summon intention through words they utter. They labor over verbal formulas and repeatedly doubt whether the intention is present, descending into a kind of obsessive doubt whose sufferers resemble the insane.

Yet intention is simply the will to act—one’s purpose. It is present within them, for it is present within everyone who prays in that mosque or congregational mosque, and everyone who performs ablution at that ablution facility. The former recognize this within themselves and have not developed obsessive doubts, whereas the latter suppose that intention is absent from their hearts and seek to bring it about within them.

---

## Page 8348

Yet they know that stating the intention aloud is not obligatory; what is obligatory is simply that the will to act be present in the heart. It is already present, but despite this they believe it is not. If one of them is told, “The intention is already present in your heart,” he will not accept it because of the false belief he holds, which contradicts his innate disposition.

Likewise, love for Allah and His Messenger is present in the heart of every believer. So long as he is a believer, he cannot banish it from his heart. Signs of his love for Allah and His Messenger become apparent when someone begins to insult and disparage the Messenger, or to insult Allah and speak of Him in an unworthy manner. The believer becomes far angrier at this than he would if his own father and mother were insulted.

Nevertheless, many adherents of speculative theology and rationalist thought have denied love in relation to Allah, saying, “It is impossible for Him to love or to be loved.” They made this a fundamental tenet of religion, adding, “In opposition to the advocates of divine indwelling”—as though only the advocates of divine indwelling affirmed that Allah loves. Yet it is well known that this is the religion of the prophets and messengers, the Companions, their successors, and all believers. The Quran and Sunnah establish this, as we have explained at length elsewhere.

This love for Allah and His Messenger is present in the hearts of most of those who deny it—indeed, in the heart of every believer—even if he denies it because of a misconception that has occurred to him.

---

## Page 8349

Likewise, knowledge of Allah is present in these people’s hearts. Those who denied love for Him are the very ones who said, “Knowledge of Him can be attained only through rational reflection.” Thus they denied the knowledge and love of Him present in their innate dispositions and hearts. This denial may then prevent them from recognizing these within themselves. The knowledge and love once present in a person’s heart may also disappear, for the innate disposition can become corrupted: it may disappear, or it may remain present yet go unseen.

“For indeed, it is not the eyes that are blind, but the hearts within the breasts.”

Allah Most High also says:

“So direct your face toward the religion, inclining to truth—in accordance with the natural disposition with which Allah created mankind. There is no changing Allah’s creation. That is the upright religion, but most people do not know. Turn to Him in repentance, fear Him, establish prayer, and do not be among those who associate partners with Him.”

In the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“Every child is born upon the innate disposition. Then his parents make him a Jew, a Christian, or a Magian, just as an animal gives birth to an intact offspring. Do you see any mutilation in it?”

Abū Hurayrah would then say: “Recite, if you wish: ‘The natural disposition with which Allah created mankind.’”

The innate disposition necessarily entails knowing Allah, loving Him, and singling Him out as the most beloved of all things

---

## Page 8350

to the servant. This is the affirmation of Allah’s oneness, and this is the meaning of the statement, “There is no deity worthy of worship except Allah.” As an explanatory wording states: “Every child is born upon this religion.” Another narration reads: “Upon the religion of Islam.”

In Sahīh Muslim, ‘Iyād ibn Himār reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah Most High says: ‘I created My servants as pure monotheists, but the devils led them astray, forbade them what I had made lawful for them, and commanded them to associate with Me that for which I had sent down no authority.’”

He thus states that He created them as pure monotheists. This entails knowing the Lord, loving Him, and affirming His oneness. These three are encompassed by pure monotheism, which is the meaning of the statement, “There is no deity worthy of worship except Allah.”

For this good word, which is “like a good tree whose root is firmly fixed and whose branches reach into heaven,” affirms knowing Him and acknowledging Him. It also affirms loving Him, for a deity is an object of worship who deserves to be worshipped, and this is the highest degree of love. It further affirms that there is no deity worthy of worship except Him. Thus it encompasses knowledge, love, and the affirmation of His oneness.

Every child is born upon the innate disposition: the pure monotheism upon which He created them. But the child’s parents corrupt this, making him a Jew, a Christian, a Magian, or one who associates partners with Allah.

---

## Page 8351

Likewise, they make him a Jahmī, causing him to deny the knowledge of the Lord, love for Him, and affirmation of His oneness that are within his heart. He then seeks that knowledge through evidence, while denying love altogether. The affirmation of Allah’s oneness that encompasses love is denied by those who do not understand it; only His oneness as Creator is affirmed. The polytheists used to acknowledge this oneness and this association of partners with Allah. Thus, his parents make him a polytheist, a Jew, a Christian, or a Magian. This hadith and people’s views concerning it have been discussed at length elsewhere.

Another illustration of how much of a person’s inner state can remain hidden from him, without his awareness, is that many people harbour a latent love of leadership without knowing it. Indeed, a person may be sincere in his worship while his faults remain hidden from him. Much has been said about this, and it is well known. This is why it has been called “the hidden desire.”

Shaddād ibn Aws said:

“O remaining Arabs! What I fear most for you is showing off and the hidden desire.”

Abū Dāwūd al-Sijistānī was asked, “What is the hidden desire?”

He replied, “Love of leadership.”

It is hidden: concealed from others and often even from the person who harbours it. The same is true of love of wealth and physical form: a person may love such things without knowing it. His soul remains at ease as long as what he loves is present; but when he loses it, what emerges from

---

## Page 8352

his soul’s anguish and devastation reveals the love that was already there. Love necessarily entails awareness; thus, the soul necessarily has an awareness of certain things. Yet a person may be unaware of this within himself, especially since Satan conceals things from him. His sins, too, remain as a stain upon his heart. Allah Most High says:

“No! Rather, their hearts have been stained by what they used to earn.”

“No! Indeed, that Day they will be veiled from their Lord.”

Al-Tirmidhī and others relate, through al-Qa‘qā‘ ibn Hakīm, from Abū Sālih, from Abū Hurayrah, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When a servant sins, a black mark is placed upon his heart. If he repents, desists, and seeks forgiveness, his heart is polished clean. But if he sins further, the mark grows until it covers his heart. That is the stain of which Allah says: ‘No! Rather, their hearts have been stained by what they used to earn.’”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “A good and authentic hadith.”

Another example is His saying, Most High:

“They say, ‘Our hearts are covered.’ Rather, Allah has cursed them for their disbelief, so little do they believe.”

He also says:

“Indeed, when those who fear Allah are touched by a suggestion from Satan, they remember, and at once they see clearly.”

When those who fear Allah are afflicted by this passing suggestion that circles about their hearts, they recall what they already knew. The suggestion then vanishes, and they see the truth that was already known to them, but which the suggestion had prevented them from seeing. Allah Most High says:

“But their brethren draw them further into error, and never desist.”

Thus, the brethren of

---

## Page 8353

the devils are drawn further into their error by the devils. “And never desist”: the devils never desist from supporting and encouraging them, nor do those people desist from their error. In that state of error, they fail to perceive what they already know and what is firmly rooted in their innate disposition; instead, they forget it. Thus, the messengers come only to remind the innate disposition of what it already knows, to strengthen and reinforce that knowledge, and to remove whatever alters the innate disposition. The messengers were sent to affirm and perfect the innate disposition, not to change or transform it. Perfection is attained through the innate disposition perfected by the revealed law.

Section

This forgetfulness—a person’s forgetting himself and what lies within him—results from his forgetting his Lord and what He has revealed. Allah Most High says:

“And do not be like those who forgot Allah, so He made them forget themselves. It is they who are defiantly disobedient.”

He also says concerning the hypocrites:

“They forgot Allah, so He abandoned them.”

And He says:

“Thus Our signs came to you, but you forgot them; and so today you will be forgotten.”

His words, “And do not be like those who forgot Allah, so He made them forget themselves,” indicate that their forgetting Allah caused them to forget themselves, and that when they forgot Allah, He punished them by making them forget themselves.

---

## Page 8354

Their forgetting themselves entails their turning away, their heedlessness, and their loss of awareness of what they previously knew about their own condition. It also entails their neglecting their own best interests. Thus, they do not remember themselves in a way that benefits and reforms them; had they remembered Allah, they would have remembered themselves. This is the reverse of the saying:

“Whoever knows himself knows his Lord.”

Some people attribute this to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Yet these are not the Prophet’s words, nor does this saying appear in any of the books of hadith, nor is any chain of transmission known for it. However, the following is reported in some earlier books—if the report is authentic:

“O human being, know yourself, and you will know your Lord.”

Whether the meaning of this statement is sound or unsound, its wording cannot be used as authoritative proof, for it has not been established as the statement of anyone protected from error. If, however, it is interpreted in a sound sense, the soundness of that meaning is known regardless of whether this wording conveys it or not. The statement that is firmly established is what appears in the Quran, namely His words:

“And do not be like those who forgot Allah, so He made them forget themselves.”

This indicates that forgetting the Lord necessarily leads to forgetting oneself. Accordingly, whoever remembers Allah and does not forget Him will remember himself. For if he

---

## Page 8355

were to forget himself regardless of whether he remembered Allah or forgot Him, his forgetting himself would not be caused by his forgetting his Lord. Since the verse indicates that a person’s forgetting himself is caused by his forgetting his Lord, it follows that whoever remembers his Lord does not experience this forgetfulness of himself. Remembrance includes recalling what one already knows. Thus, whoever recalls what he knows of his Lord recalls what he knows of himself.

He was born with the innate disposition that entails knowing his Lord, loving Him, and affirming His oneness. If he does not forget his Lord, whom he has known, but instead remembers Him in a manner that entails loving Him, knowing Him, and affirming His oneness, he remembers himself. He then perceives the knowledge of Allah, love for Him, and affirmation of His oneness that were previously within him.

When the adherents of innovation—the Jahmiyyah and others like them—turned away from the prescribed remembrance of Allah, which was present in the innate disposition and brought by the revealed Law, and which includes knowing Him, loving Him, and affirming His oneness, they forgot Allah in this respect. He therefore made them forget themselves in this respect: they forgot the innate knowledge, innate love, and innate affirmation of His oneness that were within them.

A number of Quran commentators have said:

“They forgot Allah” means that they neglected Allah’s command; “so He made them forget themselves” means their own best interests, since they had sent ahead no good for themselves. This is the wording used by a number of them, including al-Baghawī.

The wording used by others, including Ibn al-Jawzī, is:

“When they failed to obey Him.”

Both said that “They forgot Allah” means that they neglected Allah’s command.

---

## Page 8356

Such interpretations also occur frequently in the words of those who offer a general statement conveying a meaning indicated by the verse, without explaining it as fully as it deserves. Their statement, “They neglected Allah’s command,” means that they neglected to obey Him; the first is thus made identical to the second. Yet Allah, glorified is He, said:

“And do not be like those who forgot Allah, so He made them forget themselves.”

Here there are two things: their forgetting Allah, and then their forgetting themselves, which was their punishment.

If it is said, “The second is identical to the first, but merely elaborates on what was stated generally, as in His words, ‘How many towns We have destroyed! Our punishment came upon them by night or while they were taking their midday rest.’ Here, the two describe the same thing,” the answer is:

He did not say, “They forgot Allah, so they forgot their own best interests,” such that one could say that the two are identical. Rather, He said:

“They forgot Allah, so He made them forget themselves.”

Thus, He caused them to forget themselves. If this were identical to the first, He would have mentioned something that excused them, rather than something by which He punished them.

If the second were identical to the first, then “They forgot Allah” would mean that they neglected to obey Him, and He was the One who made them forget to do so. The unsoundness of this interpretation, both in wording and in meaning, is evident.

If, however, it were said that “They forgot Allah” means that they forgot His command, and “so He made them forget” means that He made them forget to act in obedience to Him—that is, to call that obedience to mind—this would be closer to the meaning, and the first instance of forgetting would retain its literal sense. For whoever forgets Allah’s command itself does not obey Him.

---

## Page 8357

But they interpreted forgetting Allah as abandoning His command. His command, which is His speech, is not within their power such that they could abandon it; what they abandon is acting upon it. Thus, “command” here means what is commanded.

Unless it is said that by abandoning His command they mean abandoning faith in it: when they abandoned faith, He consequently caused them to abandon acting upon it. This, too, is a weak interpretation. If abandoning faith means only abandoning affirmation of its truth, that alone suffices as disbelief and sin. Abandoning action upon it should not then be regarded as the punishment; rather, the former is more grievous. If abandoning faith means abandoning both affirmation and action, then this is abandoning obedience, as discussed above.

Their error arose from seeking to interpret the servant’s forgetting in light of what has been said about the Lord’s forgetting. Since the latter was interpreted as abandonment, they interpreted the former in the same way. This is unsound, for forgetting, as the opposite of remembering, can undoubtedly occur in a servant. A person turns away from what he has been commanded to do until he forgets it and no longer remembers it. There is therefore no need to interpret his forgetting as abandonment while remaining mindful and aware.

As for the Lord, exalted is He, nothing that contradicts His attributes of perfection can apply to Him—glorified and exalted is He. Yet interpreting His forgetting the disbelievers as mere abandonment is open to question. Moreover, this interpretation was offered concerning His words, exalted is He:

“Thus did Our signs come to you, but you forgot them.”

---

## Page 8358

that is, you abandoned acting upon them. Here, however, He says, “They forgot Allah,” and one does not say of Allah, “They abandoned Him.”

Section

His words, “Who created” and “Created man from a clinging substance,” explain how He is made known through what is already known of creation in general and the creation of man in particular. This is among the ways in which the innate disposition recognizes Him, as explained earlier.

Once a person recognizes that He is the Creator, it is necessarily known that the Creator must possess power. Indeed, every action performed by an agent requires strength and power—even the actions of inanimate things. The falling of stones and water, and the movement of fire, occur through a force within them. Likewise, plants move through a force within them, and every living creature, whether an animal or otherwise, acts through a power within it. The same applies to human beings and others.

Creation is the greatest of all acts, for none but Allah has the power to create. Thus, the power to create is greater than every other power and has no equal among the powers of created beings. Teaching by the pen also necessarily entails power. Thus, both creation and teaching necessarily entail power.

---

## Page 8359

Likewise, both necessarily entail knowledge. Anyone who teaches another must himself know what he teaches; otherwise, it would be impossible for him to teach someone else what he himself does not know. Thus, He who taught all things—human beings and others—what they did not know must all the more know what He taught them. Creation also necessarily entails knowledge, as the Most High says:

“Does He who created not know, while He is the Subtle, the All-Aware?”

This is because creation necessarily entails will. Bringing something into being with particular characteristics and a particular measure, rather than otherwise, can only occur through a will that selects one possibility over another. Will, in turn, necessarily entails knowledge: one wills only what one is aware of and conceives in one’s mind. Will without awareness is impossible.

Moreover, the very act of creating man brings into being this human being, one of the wonders of creation. His creation displays such precision and perfection as to astound the mind. An act performed with precision and perfection can proceed only from one who knows what he has done. This is self-evident. Creation thus indicates knowledge in both these ways.

In Surah al-Mulk, He says:

“And He is the Subtle, the All-Aware.”

This explains the subtle wisdom found in created things, which involves bringing matters to their intended ends by the subtlest means. As Joseph, peace be upon him, said: “Indeed, my Lord is Subtle in what

---

## Page 8360

He wills.” This necessarily entails knowledge of the intended end and of the path leading to it. The same applies to His full awareness. A detailed discussion would be lengthy; the purpose here is merely to draw attention to what is contained in the verses that were the first to be revealed.

Once it is established that He is powerful and knowing, this necessarily entails that He is living. Likewise, will necessarily entails life. A living being who does not hear, see, or speak would be characterized by the opposite attributes: blindness, deafness, and muteness. This is impossible with respect to the Lord Most High. He must therefore possess hearing, sight, and speech.

Will either serves a wise purpose or does not. If it does not serve a wise purpose, it is foolishness, and He is far exalted above that. He must therefore be wise. Furthermore, He either intends to benefit His creatures and show them kindness, intends merely to harm and torment them, or intends neither, but wills whatever He wills, regardless of whether it brings benefit or harm. The second possibility would entail evil and injustice, from which the Lord is far exalted; the third would entail foolishness and frivolity. It follows necessarily that He, Most High, is merciful just as He is wise, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 8361

Section

There are several ways of establishing His attributes of perfection. One is what we have already indicated: action necessarily entails power and other attributes. Some speculative thinkers begin by establishing power, others knowledge, and others will. These are the methods followed by many speculative theologians.

These attributes are inferred from action as such. This is the approach of those who do not distinguish between one effect and another, such as Jahm ibn Safwān and his followers. They affirm neither wisdom nor mercy, since action as such does not necessarily entail either. Yet they establish knowledge on the basis of well-ordered, perfectly executed acts. Likewise, mercy is established through beneficial acts, and wisdom through praiseworthy ends.

However, they contradict themselves when they infer knowledge from order and precision, since these indicate knowledge only if the agent acts for an intended end. They say that He acts without a wise purpose, yet then infer knowledge from the orderliness of His acts. This is a contradiction. They likewise contradicted themselves concerning miracles, which they regarded as demonstrating a prophet’s truthfulness, either

---

## Page 8362

on the basis of self-evident knowledge of this, or because their failure to demonstrate it would imply incapacity. Yet they demonstrate it only if the agent intends to manifest them as proof of the prophets’ truthfulness. Thus, when they say that He does nothing for any purpose, they contradict themselves.

Another method of establishing the attributes is to infer the cause from its effect: whoever produces something perfect has a greater claim to perfection. The third method is a fortiori reasoning, based on precedence and superiority: if perfection is established for a contingent, created being that has come into existence, then the necessarily existent, eternal Creator has a greater claim to it. The Quran employs all three methods.

An example of inferring from an effect the greater perfection of its cause is His statement, Most High:

“They said, ‘Who is greater than us in strength?’”

Allah, Most High, said:

“Did they not see that Allah, who created them, is greater than them in strength?”

Likewise, all the strength and might found in created beings indicate that Allah is greater in strength and might. Whatever knowledge they possess indicates that Allah is more knowledgeable, and whatever knowledge and life they possess indicate that Allah has a greater claim to knowledge and life.

---

## Page 8363

And this is a method acknowledged by rational people generally. Even the philosophers say: “Every perfection in an effect comes from its cause.” As for a fortiori reasoning, examples include His statement:

“To Allah belongs the highest description,”

and His statement:

“He sets forth for you an example drawn from yourselves: do you have, among those whom your right hands possess, any partners in what We have provided for you, such that you are equal in it, fearing them as you fear one another?”

These and similar passages indicate that every perfection free of deficiency established for an originated, created, contingent being belongs with greater reason to the eternal, necessarily existent Creator, because He has a greater claim to perfection by virtue of His superiority. The earlier method, by contrast, rests on His being the One who made that being perfect and bestowed those attributes upon it.

His name “the Most High” is explained in these two senses. It is explained as meaning that He is higher than all others in rank, and thus has a greater claim to the attributes of perfection. It is also explained as meaning that He is high above them in subjugation and mastery: that is, He exercises power over them, while they are subject to His power. This entails His being their Creator and Lord. Both meanings entail that He Himself is above everything, with nothing above Him, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“You are the First, and nothing is before You; You are the Last, and nothing is after You. You are the Manifest, and nothing is above You; You are the Inward, and nothing is nearer than You.”

---

## Page 8364

Thus, nothing is before Him or after Him, above Him or nearer than Him, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, declared in praising his Lord. Otherwise, if He were supposed to be beneath any created thing, that would be an imperfection, and that thing would be higher than Him.

And if it were said that He is neither within the world nor outside it, that would amount to denying Him. He is exalted above this. This is what it means for Him to be the Most High, the Highest. Moreover, when used without qualification, the expressions “the Most High” and “highness” are used in the Quran only in this sense, which necessarily entails the other two senses. They are not used to signify mere power or mere superiority.

The term “highness” conveys being above. Other verbs, too, indicate being above when construed with the preposition denoting elevation, as in His statement:

“Then He rose over the Throne.”

This indicates His being high above the Throne. The early generations explained “rising” in terms that convey ascending above the Throne, as al-Bukhārī records in his Sahīh from Abū al-Āliyah concerning His words, “Then He rose”: he said, “He ascended.”

Ibn Abī Hātim and others likewise transmitted this through their chains of narration. He transmitted it through Ādam ibn Abī Iyās, from Abū Ja‘far, from Abū al-Rabī‘, from Abū al-Āliyah: concerning “Then He rose,” he said, “He ascended.”

---

## Page 8365

Al-Bukhārī also said:

“Mujāhid said concerning His words, ‘Then He rose over the Throne’: ‘He rose above the Throne.’”

Yet one may speak of being “high above something” (‘alā ‘alā) or “exalted beyond something” (‘alā ‘an). The latter occurs in several places in the Quran, though with the verb ta‘ālā (“He is exalted”), as in His words:

“Glorified and exalted is He, far above what they say!”

“Knower of the unseen and the seen; exalted is He above what they associate with Him.”

A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere. The point here is that each statement—that He created, and that He is the Most Generous, who taught by the pen—indicates these two ways of affirming the attributes, just as it directed us to the first way: inferring them from His acts.

His words, “the Most Generous,” entail that He surpasses all others in generosity. Generosity is a comprehensive term encompassing every excellent quality. This therefore entails that He has a greater claim to every praiseworthy quality, and praiseworthy qualities are attributes of perfection. Thus, He is more deserving of being described as beneficent towards creation and merciful, and of being attributed with wisdom, power, knowledge, life, and all such perfections.

The same applies to His words, “He created.” The Creator is eternal, without beginning, self-sufficient, necessarily existent in Himself, and the Self-Subsisting Sustainer. It is known that He has a greater claim to the attributes of perfection than a created, originated, contingent being. This is from the standpoint of a fortiori reasoning. As for reasoning from the effect, the Creator of another being

---

## Page 8366

that He has made living, knowing, capable, hearing and seeing has a greater claim to be living, knowing, powerful, hearing and seeing.

And “the Most Generous,” “who taught by the pen,” “taught man what he did not know.” He thus made man knowledgeable, and one who possesses knowledge must be alive. He also honoured him by granting him power, hearing and sight. The Most Generous, who made another knowledgeable, has a greater claim to possess knowledge Himself. The same applies to all other attributes of perfection and praiseworthy qualities.
