This is reasoning from a particular created being, whereas the first argument reasons from creation in general. Thus, this establishes the attributes as a matter of necessary knowledge, without strained argumentation. The same applies to reasoning from superiority and a greater claim to perfection: the Lord has a greater claim to perfection than the created being.

These methods are so evident that non-Muslims, whether followers of religious traditions or otherwise, also employ them. Christians, for example, used this method to affirm that Allah subsists in Himself so that He may speak. However, they called Him a “substance” and went astray by making the attributes three—the hypostases.

They said:

“We found that things are divided into substances and things that are not substances. Since substance is the higher of the two kinds, we said: ‘He is a substance.’ Then we found that substances are divided into living and non-living, and that the living is more perfect, so we said: ‘He is living.’ We found that living beings are divided into the nātiq (endowed with reason or speech) and the non-nātiq, so we said: ‘He is nātiq.’”

---

## Page 8367

Likewise, it may be said to them regarding the other attributes of perfection: things are divided into those that possess power and those that do not, and those that possess power are more perfect. We have discussed at length what is correct and what is mistaken in their statements in the book we entitled The Correct Response to Those Who Altered the Religion of the Messiah. The purpose here is to draw attention to how this verse, and these verses—which were the first to be revealed—point to the foundations of religion.

His saying, “Taught man what he did not know,” indicates His power to teach man what He has taught him, despite the various deficiencies found in humankind. If He is able to impart such knowledge, then all the more is He able to teach the prophets what He has taught them. This is included in His saying, “Taught man what he did not know,” for the prophets are human beings.

These verses thus indicate all the rational foundations, for the possibility of prophethood is the furthest point that reason can establish. As for the existence of the prophets and their signs, this is known through mass-transmitted reports. Moreover, His saying, “Taught man what he did not know,” includes the affirmation that He taught the prophets what He taught them. These verses therefore indicate both the possibility and the actual occurrence of prophethood.

---

## Page 8368

We have mentioned elsewhere that affirming His transcendence rests on two principles. The first is that He is free from any imperfection that contradicts His perfection. Whatever establishes His perfection also establishes His freedom from any imperfection that contradicts it. This makes clear that His freedom from imperfection is known through reason, contrary to the claim of a group of speculative theologians that it can be known only through revelation.

We have explained elsewhere that the rational methods they have followed—inferring the temporal origination of bodies from accidents—establish neither His existence, nor any of His attributes of perfection, nor His freedom from any imperfection. Thus, they have no basis for ruling out any imperfection in His case. They themselves acknowledge that, unlike attributes, any action is possible for Him. Yet their approach to the attributes is unsound and contradictory, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

The second principle is that nothing is like Him in His attributes of perfection. The Quran is replete with affirmations of these two principles: it affirms the attributes of perfection in detail and declares Him free from any likeness to others. Glory be to Him! Exalted is He far above what the wrongdoers say.

---

## Page 8369

Section

His words, “In the name of your Lord who created,” and His words, “Who taught by the pen,” “taught man what he did not know,” establish His actions and His speech. Creation is His action, and teaching includes teaching what He has revealed, as He says:

“The Most Merciful taught the Quran, created man, and taught him articulate speech.”

His words, “by the pen,” include teaching His speech, which is written with the pen. The fact that this was revealed in the opening of the surah in which He sent down His speech and taught His Prophet His speech, which is written with the pen, shows that the verse encompasses this. For the occasion prompting unrestricted and general wording must necessarily fall within its scope.

If, then, this indicates that He created and spoke—and He has said, “He created man”—it is known both through reason and through the wording that the created human being is distinct from the Lord’s act of creating him. The same applies to His creation of anything else. Those who disputed this did so only because of a misconception that occurred to them, as discussed later and elsewhere. Otherwise, they do not dispute that khalaqa (“he created”) is a verb with a verbal noun, as in: khalaqa (“he created”), yakhluqu (“he creates”), khalqan (“creation”). The human being is the object of the action denoted by the verbal noun—the “created being”—not the action itself.

---

## Page 8370

A verbal noun may, however, be used to denote the object of the action, as in “a dirham of the ruler’s minting.” An example is His statement, “This is Allah’s creation,” meaning here: this is what Allah has created. The discussion is not about khalq (“creation”) in the sense of “a created thing,” but about khalq in the sense of the action itself, expressed by the verbal noun, as in khalaqa (“he created”), yakhluqu (“he creates”), khalqan (“creation”). Likewise, He says:

“Your creation and resurrection are only like those of a single soul.”

“He creates you in your mothers’ wombs, stage after stage of creation.”

“I did not make them witnesses to the creation of the heavens and the earth, nor to their own creation.”

Since creation is His action, it occurs by His will, for it is impossible for His action to occur without His will. Whatever occurs by His will cannot be beginningless in its individual instance, although its kind may be beginningless. Since the creation of anything that comes into being must have a complete cause necessitating its occurrence, it follows that He has always been characterized by voluntary acts subsisting in Him. Yet if it is established that another created thing existed before this one, it is thereby established that He is characterized by one act of creation after another. Likewise with speech: He speaks by His will.

It is impossible for Him to lack the attribute of speech and then acquire it, for two reasons:

First, this would deny His perfection, and speech is an attribute of perfection.

Second, it is impossible for this to come about. For one who lacks speech cannot

---

## Page 8371

make himself capable of speech. One who lacks knowledge cannot make himself knowledgeable, and one who is not alive cannot make himself alive. These attributes are thus inseparable from His essence.

Likewise, one who is not a creator cannot make himself a creator. For if He were unable to create, making Himself a creator would be an even greater act, and would therefore be impossible all the more certainly: making Himself a creator entails the existence of something created. For this reason, since He is able to make a human being an agent, He is the Creator of what that human being does. Thus, if He were to make Himself a creator, He would be the Creator of whatever He made Himself create. If, then, it is supposed that His being a creator in beginningless eternity is impossible, it would be impossible for Him to make Himself a creator in any way whatsoever. The assertion that action was impossible for Him in beginningless eternity entails its being impossible for Him always.

Yet the verse establishes that He created. It is therefore known that He has always been able to create, that creation has always been possible for Him, and that creation has always been possible and within His power. This invalidates the foundational premise of the Jahmiyyah. Moreover, if He has the power to do so, that which necessitates it is not something separate and external to Him; rather, it is from Himself. It is therefore impossible for Him to make Himself willing after not having been so. It follows that He has always possessed will and power. When power and will are present, the object of that power must necessarily exist.

---

## Page 8372

And the speculative theologians who dispute this say, “He has always had power over what will come to be.”

They are told: “Power can exist only if its object is possible. If His power is everlasting, has it always been possible for Him to bring about what lies within His power?”

They say: “No; rather, possibility—the possibility of action—came into being.”

This contradicts their affirmation of power. If, instead, they say, “Rather, the possibility exists,” it becomes clear that action has always been possible. This establishes the possibility of infinitely many things within the Lord’s power existing.

Thus, if He has always possessed power, action has always been possible, and this possible action has actually occurred in the time that continues without end, then consider what necessitates the existence of things within His power as a class—His will, for example. Either its existence in beginningless eternity is impossible, in which case action must also be impossible, although we have already shown that it is possible. Moreover, if its existence were impossible, it would always remain impossible, since there is nothing there to make it possible, let alone bring it into existence. It is well known that its coming into existence after not existing must have something that necessitates it.

If, on the other hand, its existence in beginningless eternity is possible, then the existence of this possible thing depends on nothing other than His essence, and His essence is sufficient to bring it about. It follows that He has always possessed will. The same applies to all attributes of perfection: once their possibility in beginningless eternity is established, it follows that

---

## Page 8373

they have existed from beginningless eternity. For if they did not exist, their existence would be impossible, since in beginningless eternity there is nothing besides Himself to necessitate their existence. If they are possible, and He Himself is their fully sufficient cause, their existence from beginningless eternity must be necessary.

This is among the things demonstrating that He has always been living, knowing, powerful, willing, speaking, and acting, since nothing but His essence necessitates these things, and His essence alone suffices for them. It follows that the kind has no beginning and that He has always spoken whenever He wills. Individual instances of the kind, however, occur successively, in accordance with possibility and wisdom.

For this reason, it has been explained elsewhere that, in reality, no possible thing has its existence and nonexistence equally balanced. Rather, either what necessitates its existence is present, making its existence necessary, or it is absent, making its existence impossible. Whatever attributes the Lord possesses, His possession of them is necessary; whatever attributes He does not possess, His possession of them is impossible. Whatever He wills comes to be, and its existence is necessary; whatever He does not will does not come to be, and its existence is impossible. Thus, a possible thing necessarily exists when the fully sufficient determining factor is present, and its existence is impossible without it.

His words, exalted is He,

“Read in the name of your Lord who created—created man from a clinging substance,”

and His words,

“Read, and your Lord is the Most Generous, who taught by the pen,”

demonstrate that He possesses the attributes of perfection and has always possessed them. The statements of the early generations concerning this are numerous. This is how they explained His words, “And Allah has always been

---

## Page 8374

Almighty, All-Wise,” and similar verses. Al-Bukhārī related this from Ibn ‘Abbās in his Sahīh, and Ibn Abī Hātim narrated it through several chains of transmission. When Ibn ‘Abbās was asked, “His words, ‘And Allah was’—do they suggest something that once was and then passed away?” he replied, “He described Himself thus, and He has always been so.”

This is Ibn Abī Hātim’s wording, transmitted through Abū Mu‘āwiyah, from al-A‘mash, from al-Minhāl, from Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, from Ibn ‘Abbās. Ibn ‘Abbās said, “Thus He was, and He has always been so.”

Another report is transmitted through ‘Amr ibn Abī Qays, from Mutarrif, from al-Minhāl, from Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, from Ibn ‘Abbās. He said: A man came to him and said, “I have heard Allah say, ‘And Allah was’—as though referring to something that once was?”

Ibn ‘Abbās replied, “As for His word ‘was,’ He has always been and will always be, and ‘He is the First and the Last, the Most High and the Most Near, and He has knowledge of all things.’”

Another report is transmitted through ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Maghrā, from Mujammi‘ ibn Yahyā, from his paternal uncle, from Ibn ‘Abbās. He said: A Jew said, “You claim that Allah was Almighty, All-Wise. What, then, is He today?”

Ibn ‘Abbās replied, “He was, in Himself, Almighty, All-Wise.”

These statements of Ibn ‘Abbās make clear that He has always possessed the attributes expressed by the predicate of kāna (“was”), and never

---

## Page 8375

ceases to be so, and that these attributes are intrinsic to Him. He has therefore always possessed them in Himself, given that they are inseparable from His very being; hence He will always possess them, for they are intrinsic to Him.

Ahmad ibn Hanbal said:
“Allah has always possessed knowledge and speech and has always been forgiving.”

He also said:
“Allah has always been One who speaks whenever He wills.”

Section

Just as the first verse revealed of the Quran indicates this, so does the greatest verse in the Quran, but more expansively and fully. This is the Verse of the Throne, as established in the Sahīh:

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said to Ubayy ibn Ka‘b, “Abū al-Mundhir, do you know which verse of Allah’s Book that you know is the greatest?”

He replied, “Allah—there is no deity except Him, the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all existence.”

He said, “May your knowledge bring you joy, Abū al-Mundhir!”

Here He begins the verse with the word “Allah,” which is greater than His words “and your Lord.” For this reason, He also begins the greatest surah in the Quran with it, saying:

“All praise is for Allah, Lord of all worlds.”

---

## Page 8376

And He said:

“Allah—there is no deity except Him, the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all existence.”

This is because the polytheists had taken a god other than Him, even though they acknowledged that He is the Creator. In His words “created,” He did not explicitly deny the existence of another creator, since that was already understood. No one has ever affirmed another creator in the unrestricted sense—one who created everything, mankind and all else. Divinity, however, is a different matter. Allah Most High said:

“They said, ‘Burn him and support your gods, if you are going to act.’”

And He Most High said:

“The leaders among them went off, saying, ‘Carry on, and remain steadfast in worshipping your gods. Indeed, this is something intended.’”

And He Most High said:

“Do you truly testify that there are other gods besides Allah? Say, ‘I do not testify.’ Say, ‘He is but One God.’”

And He Most High said:

“Say, ‘If there were gods alongside Him, as they claim, they would surely have sought a way to the Lord of the Throne.’”

Thus, they sought other gods alongside Him, but did not affirm another creator alongside Him. He therefore said in the greatest of verses:

“Allah—there is no deity except Him, the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all existence.”

He mentions this in three places in the Quran, each of which contains one of the three foundations of religion: Allah’s oneness, the messengers, and the Hereafter. These are the foundations with which He sent all the messengers. He tells us that the polytheists disbelieve in them, as in His words:

“And do not follow the desires of those who deny Our signs, who do not believe in the Hereafter, and who set up equals to their Lord.”

---

## Page 8377

So here He said:

“Allah—there is no deity except Him, the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all existence.”

He coupled this with the affirmation that there is no deity except Him. In Āl ‘Imrān, He added:

“He has sent down to you the Book in truth, confirming what came before it, and He revealed the Torah and the Gospel previously, as guidance for mankind, and He revealed the Criterion.”

This is belief in the scriptures and the messengers.

And He said in Tā Hā:

“On that Day, intercession will be of no benefit except from one whom the Most Merciful has permitted and whose words He approves. He knows what is before them and what is behind them, but they do not encompass Him in knowledge. And all faces will be humbled before the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all existence. Whoever bears wrongdoing will have failed.”

Section

Among the greatest foundations is a person’s knowledge of how Allah has described Himself. This includes the attributes pertaining to His actions, as in His words in this surah:

“Who created.”

“Created man from clinging clots.”

Creation is mentioned in many places, as are His other actions. These are of two kinds. One is an action that takes a direct object, such as “created,” which necessarily implies something created. The same applies to “provided,” as in His words:

“Allah is the One who created you, then provided for you, then will cause you to die, then will bring you to life

---

## Page 8378

—can any of those you associate with Him do any of these things?”

The same applies to guiding, leading astray, teaching, raising up, sending, and addressing. Likewise, He tells us:

“He completed them as seven heavens in two days.”

“He fashioned them into seven heavens.”

And He says:

“We built the heaven with might.”

“He who made the earth a resting place for you and the sky a canopy, and sent down water from the sky, bringing forth fruits thereby as provision for you.”

And in another verse:

“He who made the earth a settled abode for you and the sky a canopy, formed you and perfected your forms, and provided you with good things.”

Such passages are very numerous in the Quran.

Examples of intransitive actions include His words:

“Then He rose towards the heaven.”

“Then He rose over the Throne.”

“Are they waiting for anything but for Allah to come to them in canopies of clouds?”

“Are they waiting for anything but for the angels to come to them, or for your Lord to come, or for some of your Lord’s signs to come?”

And His words:

“And your Lord comes, with the angels, rank upon rank.”

As for the first kind, Muslims agree that it is attributed to Allah and that He is the One who creates and provides; this is not an attribute of any of His creatures. But does an act—namely, creation—subsist in Him, or is the act identical with what is done, and creation identical with what is created? Those who affirm that He possesses attributes hold two views on this. As for

---

## Page 8379

those among the Jahmiyyah and Muʿtazilah who deny the attributes, they deny all the more that any act subsists in Him. Some of them, however, distinguish creation from the created thing, holding creation to be either a quality subsisting in the created thing or an infinite succession of qualities, as maintained by Maʿmar ibn ʿAbbād. Others hold that creation subsists without a locus, as in the view of some that it is the utterance “Be!” without a locus, or the Basrans’ view that it is a volition without a locus. They adopt these positions to avoid admitting that temporally originated events subsist in Him, although some of them accept this consequence, as did Abū al-Husayn and others.

The majority, who affirm the attributes, hold two views concerning acts. Some say that no act subsists in Him; rather, the act is identical with what is done. This is the position of a group that includes al-Ashʿarī and those who agree with him, both among his followers and outside their ranks, such as Ibn ʿAqīl and others. It was also the earlier of al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā’s two positions. They divide the attributes into attributes of essence, of meaning, and of action. Yet this classification has no basis in reality, for according to them, acts do not subsist in Him, so He is not characterized by them; rather, statements are made about Him in terms of them.

This classification accords with the position of those who say: Attributes are the statements that

---

## Page 8380

are made about Him, not qualities subsisting in Him, as the Jahmiyyah and Muʿtazilah maintain.

Thus, when they say that attributes are divided into attributes of essence and attributes of action, they mean that the statements made about Him sometimes concern His essence and sometimes concern created things. They recognize no attributes subsisting in Him. Whoever understands attributes in this way can divide them into three categories: attributes of essence, of meaning, and of action. But for those who understand attributes to mean what subsists in Him, this classification is incompatible with their own principles. They nevertheless adopted it from the former group, despite differing from them over what is meant by attributes. This classification appears in the writings of Abū al-Hasan and those who agree with him, such as al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā, Abū al-Maʿālī, al-Bājī, and others.

The second view is that acts subsist in Him. This is the position of the early Muslim generations and the majority of those who affirm the attributes. Al-Bukhārī states in The Creation of Human Acts that the scholars unanimously agree on this: there is a Creator, an act of creation, and a created thing. Al-Baghawī presents it as the position of Ahl al-Sunnah, and Abū Nasr Muhammad ibn Ishāq al-Kalābādhī states in An Introduction to the Doctrines of Sufism that it is the position of the Sufis. It is also the position of the Hanafis and is well known among them; they call it

---

## Page 8381

“takwīn” (bringing into being).

This is also the position of the Karrāmiyyah, the Hishāmiyyah, and similar groups, as well as the early followers of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad. It is also the later of al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā’s two positions.

Now, if it is said that the act of creation is distinct from the created thing and subsists in the Lord, is it a beginningless act of creation inseparable from the Lord’s essence, even though created things have a beginning, as the followers of Abū Hanīfah and others maintain? Or is it an act of creation that originated in His essence when the genus of created things originated? Or is it one act of creation after another? There are three views. One or the other of these views is held by the imams of the Sunnah and hadith and the majority of their scholars. It is also the position of groups among the speculative theologians, including the Karrāmiyyah, the Hishāmiyyah, and others. Thus, whoever says, “He speaks by His will and choice, with speech that subsists in His essence,” can say, “He acts by His choice and will, with an act that subsists in His essence.”

Among those who affirm that matters subject to His choice subsist in His essence, some accept the argument from accidents as valid, along with its use to establish that bodies have a beginning. These include the Karrāmiyyah and later Hanafi, Māliki, Hanbali, and Shāfiʿī scholars. Others do not accept it as valid, such as the imams of the early Muslim generations and the imams of the Sunnah and hadith, including Ahmad ibn Hanbal, al-Bukhārī, and others.

---

## Page 8382

This issue is referred to as “the question of causation”: does it have real existence or not? Is causation distinct from both the cause and the effect, or not? Al-Rāzī’s statements on this differ, as has been discussed at length elsewhere.

The principal argument of those who say that the act of creation is identical with the created thing, and that causation is simply the existence of the effect—affirming nothing beyond this—is as follows: if creation and causation were something distinct from the created thing itself and the effect, they would either subsist in a substrate or not. The latter is invalid, since qualities cannot subsist independently.

This is a reply to a group among the Muʿtazilah who said that it subsists independently.

They continue: if it subsists in a substrate, it must subsist either in the Creator or in something else. The latter is invalid, because if it subsisted in something else, that other thing would be the creator, rather than Him.

This is a reply to a second group, who say that it subsists in the created thing. If it subsists in the Creator, it must be either beginningless or originated. If it were beginningless, this would entail that the created thing is beginningless, since the act of creation and the created thing necessarily accompany one another. An act of creation without a created thing is impossible, as is causation without an effect. If it is originated, this is invalid for two reasons. First, it would entail that originated events subsist in Him. Second, that originated act of creation would require another act of creation, entailing an infinite regress. Muʿammar ibn ʿAbbād accepted the infinite regress and posited an act of creation for the act of creation, and another act of creation for that act of creation

---

## Page 8383

but not within Allah’s essence, and held that all this occurred at one and the same time. This, then, is their principal argument. Each group that disagreed with them rejected one of its premises. Those who allowed creation to subsist independently or in the created thing rejected those two premises. As for the majority, each answered according to his own position.

Some said:

Rather, creation and bringing into being are beginningless, just as, according to you, will is beginningless. Affirming that will is beginningless does not entail the prior existence of what is willed. Likewise, creation and bringing into being are beginningless without this entailing the prior existence of the created thing.

This objection is binding upon the Kullābiyyah, whether Ashʿarīs or others, and they have no answer to it. It does not, however, bind those who deny that a particular act of willing is beginningless—indeed, deny that will is beginningless, as the Jahmiyyah and Muʿtazilah maintain—or who affirm that will as a kind is beginningless, as maintained by the leading scholars of Ahl al-Hadīth and those philosophers, theologians, and others who agree with them.

But the proponent of this view is told:

This beginningless bringing into being must either occur by His will or not occur by His will. If it occurs without His will, it follows that He created the creation without willing it. If it occurs by His will, it follows that something beginningless is willed, which is false. Were this valid, it would be possible for the world to be beginningless while also being created

---

## Page 8384

through a beginningless act of creation, by a beginningless will. It is known that this is false. Hence, all those who say, “The Quran is beginningless,” say that He spoke independently of His will and power. A willed effect must therefore have a beginning, and likewise a willed act must have a beginning.

Moreover, those who dispute with them say:

Will entails what is willed, and creation entails what is created. The foregoing argument applies against both groups, for will and creation are relational in nature: the existence of will without something willed, or creation without something created, is impossible.

But the disputant says:

Will and creation exist, while the willed, created thing comes later.

These people are then told: You say that will exists—or that creation exists together with will—while neither what is willed nor what is created exists. Then, after an infinite span of hypothetical time, the willed, created thing comes into existence without any cause. This is false by the most elementary dictates of reason. For will or creation already existed together with power. If this constituted a complete cause, it would entail the existence of the effect, and the effect would necessarily exist when the complete cause existed. For the effect is “contingent,” and a contingent thing must exist upon the existence of the determining factor

---

## Page 8385

in its complete form. For otherwise, the thing would remain contingent even after the determining factor existed, admitting either existence or nonexistence, and would then require a further determining factor. This entails an infinite regress, which ends only when there exists a complete determining factor that necessitates the outcome.

Here people disagreed. One group, including Muhammad ibn al-Haysam al-Karrāmī and Mahmūd al-Khwārizmī, held that a contingent thing becomes more likely to occur, but its occurrence does not become necessary.

Most of the Muʿtazilah and Ashʿarīs said:

Rather, its occurrence does not become more likely. Instead, an agent possessing power—or possessing both power and will—gives preference to one of two equivalent alternatives without a determining factor.

Others recognized that this consequence was unavoidable. They therefore acknowledged that, when the complete determining factor exists, the effect must exist, and that, when the complete motive is present together with power, the act must occur. Abū al-Husayn al-Basrī, al-Rāzī, al-Tūsī and others acknowledged this. Many of the earlier speculative theologians affirmed a necessitating will, holding that will entails the existence of what is willed.

The philosophers raised this objection against the speculative theologians, but maintained that the effect accompanies the exercise of causal influence, so that the two exist together in time. Many people know only this view and that view

---

## Page 8386

—al-Rāzī and others, for example—and thus remain bewildered over this fundamental principle, one of the greatest foundations of knowledge, religion, and speculative theology. We have discussed this at length elsewhere and explained that a third view is correct: the view held by the leading scholars. It is that the complete exercise of causal influence necessarily entails the existence of its effect immediately afterward, neither simultaneously with it nor after a delay. Those speculative theologians who maintain that there is a delay are mistaken, while those who maintain simultaneity, such as the philosophers, are even more mistaken. Their position entails impossibilities that we have explained elsewhere.

This third view is supported by both revelation and reason. Allah Most High says:

“His command, when He wills a thing, is only to say to it, ‘Be,’ and it is.”

People of sound reason say, “I cut it, and it was severed; I broke it, and it broke,” and, “He divorced the woman, and she became divorced; he emancipated the slave, and he became free.” Thus, emancipation and divorce take effect immediately after the acts of emancipation and divorce: the effect is neither delayed nor simultaneous. The same applies to becoming broken or severed in relation to the acts of breaking and cutting.

This helps explain that, when the act of creation occurs, the created thing must exist immediately afterward. Thus one says, “Allah brought the thing into being, and it came into being.” Its coming into being follows immediately upon Allah’s bringing it into being, neither simultaneously with that act nor after a delay.

---

## Page 8387

Likewise, complete will, together with power, necessarily entails the existence of what is willed and lies within that power. He wills to create, and the act of creation occurs through His will and power. The act of creation then necessarily entails the existence of the created thing, even if that act of creation comes about through another cause and follows immediately upon it. This involves only the effect’s existence immediately after its fully operative cause, and an infinite succession of effects. Both are true, and Allah knows best.

As for the created thing, it can only be separate from Him; nothing created subsists in Him. Rather, will itself, together with power, entails the act of creation, just as it entails speech. The act of creation does not require another act of creation; rather, it requires that through which it occurs, namely, the preceding will. When He creates one thing, He wills to create another. Whatever He wills comes to pass, and whatever He does not will does not come to pass.

Those who say that the act of creation comes into being—such as the Hishāmiyyah and the Karrāmiyyah—say:

We affirm that temporally originated events subsist in Him. There is no evidence that this is invalid. Rather, reason and transmitted revelation—the Quran, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the early generations—establish its reality, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 8388

Nor is it possible to affirm that Allah governs this world without accepting this, as those philosophers closest to the truth, such as Abū al-Barakāt, author of al-Muʿtabar, and others, have acknowledged.

As for their statement, “It follows that the act of creation requires another act of creation,” those who accept this, such as the Karrāmiyyah and others, have answered them: “You maintain that created things separate from Him come into being without any cause coming into being at all. In that case, to maintain that the act of creation through which created things come into being itself comes into being without any cause coming into being is closer to reason and transmitted revelation.” This is a reply they must accept on this assumption—the assumption that voluntary acts subsist in Him.

The Karrāmiyyah call what subsists in Him hādith (“temporally occurring”), but do not call it muhdath (“brought into being”). For example, concerning the speech He utters, whether the Quran or otherwise, they say, “It occurs in time,” but do not permit saying, “It is brought into being.” For what “occurs in time” occurs through His power and will, like an “act.” What is “brought into being,” however, requires an act of bringing it into being. It would therefore follow that an act of bringing into being, distinct from what is brought into being, subsists in His Essence. That act would itself require another act of bringing into being, thus entailing an infinite regress.

Those other than the Karrāmiyyah among the leading authorities in hadith, the Sunnah, and theology do, however, call it “brought into being,” as He says:

“No reminder comes to them anew from their Lord.”

---

## Page 8389

And in the two Sahīh collections, Ibn Masʿūd reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah introduces whatever commands He wills, and among what He has newly introduced is that you must not speak during prayer.”

What He newly introduced was the prohibition against their speaking during prayer. As for their statement, “What is brought into being requires an act of bringing it into being, and so on,” this entails an infinite succession of effects, such as His speaking one utterance after another—for Allah’s words have no end—and His having always spoken whenever He wills. This is the position of the leading authorities of the Sunnah, and it is the truth supported by revelation and reason.

The same applies to His actions, for acting and speaking are attributes of perfection. One who speaks is more perfect than one who does not speak, and one who creates is more perfect than one who does not create. The Most High says:

“Is He who creates like one who does not create? Will you not then take heed?”

Thus, He has always possessed the attributes of perfection and been characterized by honor and majesty. This resolves the various difficulties and makes it clear that what the messengers have conveyed about Allah is among the truest of statements, and that rational proofs point only to what accords with the Messenger’s teachings. Yet error arose because many people were ignorant of what the Messenger conveyed

---

## Page 8390

and followed arguments of their own devising which they thought rational, though they were based on ignorance. Thus they erred concerning both revelatory and rational proofs and fell into disagreement:

“Indeed, those who differ over the Scripture are in extreme dissension.”

This has been discussed at length elsewhere in connection with divine speech and actions, citing such statements of the early generations and leading scholars on this fundamental issue as were readily available. The purpose here is to draw attention to the grounds underlying these positions.

This point was clarified by the leading authorities of the Sunnah, such as Imam Ahmad and others. In his Refutation of the Jahmiyyah, he discussed Allah’s words:

“Indeed, We have made it an Arabic Quran.”

He explained that Allah’s “making” may mean “creating,” as in His words:

“and made darkness and light,”

or it may mean “an act that is not creation.” His words, “Indeed, We have made it an Arabic Quran,” belong to this latter category. For creation, and comparable acts that are not creation—such as His speaking the Quran and other words, His speaking to Moses and others, and His descending, coming, arriving, and the like—all take place through His power and will, and through other acts that subsist in His essence and are not acts of creation.

This is how al-Bukhārī and other leading authorities of the Sunnah answer the Karrāmiyyah when they ask, “Must not whatever is brought into being have an act of bringing it into being?” He replies, “Yes, and that act of bringing into being

---

## Page 8391

is an act, not an act of creation.” And we accept “an infinite regress.” For the impossible kind of regress is the simultaneous existence of the members of an infinite series: a creator of the creator, and a creator of that creator; or an act of creation having another act of creation, which in turn has another act of creation, all at the same instant.

This is impossible for several reasons. One is the existence of an infinite number of things at a single instant, which is absolutely impossible. Another is that everything mentioned would be “brought into being,” not “contingent,” with nothing among them existing through itself to terminate the regress. In that case, it is all the more impossible.

This differs from saying, “Before this speech there was speech, and before this act there was an act.” This is possible according to most rational thinkers, including the leading authorities of the Sunnah, the leading philosophers, and others. Thus, to say, “He brought this newly occurring speech into being within Himself,” is intelligible. It is equivalent to our saying, “He spoke it.” This is the meaning of His words:

“Indeed, We have made it an Arabic Quran.”

That is, We spoke it in Arabic and sent it down in Arabic.

This is also how the early generations explained it, including Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, who related from Mujāhid that he explained “We have made it an Arabic Quran” as “We uttered it in Arabic.” Ibn Abī Hātim cited this in his commentary on the Quran, reporting that Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh said: “It was related to us from Mujāhid and other Successors that ‘Indeed, We have made it an Arabic Quran’ means ‘Indeed, We uttered it and described it.’” He also cited it

---

## Page 8392

on the authority of Ahmad ibn Hanbal, from al-Ashja‘ī, from Sufyān al-Thawrī, who explained His words, “We have made it an Arabic Quran,” as: “We have made it clear as an Arabic Quran.” A person distinguishes between his own speech and movement and his causing something else to move.

Sufyān ibn ‘Uyaynah and others among the early generations argued that the Quran is uncreated on the grounds that Allah created things by saying “Be!” If “Be!” were created, it would follow that He created one created thing by means of another, entailing an invalid infinite regress. For if He creates only by saying “Be!”, and “Be!” were created, it would follow that He could create nothing. This is an impossible circular dependence: He creates nothing until He says “Be!”, yet He does not say “Be!” until He creates it. Thus He could create nothing.

This is an infinite regress in the very basis of causation and action. It is like saying, “He does not act until He acts,” which entails that He does not act; or, “He does not create until He creates,” which entails that He does not create.

But if it is said, “He said ‘Be!’; before that ‘Be!’ there was another ‘Be!’, and before that ‘Be!’ there was another ‘Be!’,” this is not impossible. Such a regress concerns individual instances of causation, not causation as such. Likewise, in the future He will say “Be!” after “Be!” and create one thing after another without end.

Thus He creates fully realized created things through His act of creation. His act of creation is His act that subsists in Him, and this occurs only through His power and will.

---

## Page 8393

If it is said that this act subsisting in Him requires another act, distinct from power and will, to bring it into existence—for if power and will alone were sufficient, they would suffice to bring the created thing into existence; yet since an act of creation is indispensable, this act of creation comes into existence after not having existed and subsists in Him—then its fully sufficient cause necessarily entails it and is followed by it, just like the fully sufficient cause of speech arising in His essence.

When a human being speaks, his speech, both its wording and its meaning, is preceded by another act. There must be a movement followed by the production of the letters that constitute speech. That movement is what makes the speech Arabic or non-Arabic, and it is an act subsisting in the agent. This newly occurring act of making likewise comes about through a fully sufficient cause preceding it.

The Lord’s essence is what necessitates all of this. It necessitates the second act on condition that the first has ended, not while the first is occurring. Its necessitating the second is an act subsisting in Him after the first. His essence necessitates this causal act, then another, then another. Every causal act is caused by what precedes it and is a condition for what follows it. None of these acts is created, even though they are “temporally originated.”

If someone says, “I call this ‘creation,’” his disagreement is merely verbal. He is told: Those who said, “The Quran is created,” did not mean this, nor was this what the early generations and the imams rejected. They rejected only the view of those who regarded it as a created thing separate from Allah, as stated by

---

## Page 8394

Imam Ahmad:

“Allah’s speech is from Allah; it is not separate from Him.”

They also said:

“The Quran is Allah’s speech, uncreated; it originated from Him.”

Ahmad said:

“‘It originated from Him’: He is the One who spoke it. It did not originate from a created being, as those who say it is created maintain.”

Allah Most High said:

“Those to whom We have given the Scripture know that it has been sent down from your Lord in truth.”

For this reason, no one says that He created His descent, His rising over the Throne, or His coming. The same applies to His speaking to Moses and calling him: He called him and spoke to him by His will and power. Speaking is an act subsisting in His essence; it is not creation. Likewise, when a human being speaks, he performs an act of speech and brings speech into being, but within himself, not separate from him. Thus, according to the early generations and the imams, speech is an attribute of action and also an attribute of essence.

Those who say that it is created say that it is an attribute of action, and regard both the act and the speech as separate from Him.

Those who say that it is an attribute of essence say that He speaks independently of His will and power. The position of the early generations is that He speaks by His will and power, and that His speech subsists in Him. Thus, it is an attribute of

---

## Page 8395

essence and an attribute of action.

Here, however, action does not mean creation. Rather, as Imam Ahmad said:

“Making is of two kinds: making that is creation and making that is not creation.”

All of this entails that acts subsist in His essence and fall into two categories: transitive acts, such as creation, and intransitive acts, such as speaking and descending. The early generations affirm both this kind and the other.

As for making the Quran Arabic, although this is transitive in Arabic grammar, in the sense that it takes an object in the accusative, in the case of speech the act—“speaking”—is connected to its object—“speech”—and both subsist in the speaker. Thus, the object may refer to the act denoted by a verbal noun. When you say qāla qawlan hasanan (“he spoke fine words”), qawl (“saying”) may refer solely to the act of saying, or solely to the speech itself, in which case it is the object. It may also refer to both together, in which case it is both a direct object and a verbal noun.

Likewise, “Quran” is originally a verbal noun, as in qara'a qur'ānan (“he recited a recitation”), denoting the act and movement. The speech recited was then called “Quran.” Concerning the first meaning, Allah Most High said:

“Indeed, upon Us is its collection and its recitation.”

“So when We have recited it, follow its recitation.”

Concerning the second, He said:

“Indeed, this Quran”.

This has been discussed at length elsewhere, and it has been explained that recitation and reading, in

---

## Page 8396

their original sense, are verbal nouns: “talā tilāwatan (‘he recited a recitation’) and qara'a qirā'atan (‘he read a reading’), like qur'ān (‘recitation’).” However, these terms can designate the speech itself, just as “Quran” does. In that case, reading is what is read, and recitation is what is recited.

Recitation and reading may instead mean the verbal noun denoting the act. In that case, reading and recitation are not what is read and recited, but necessarily entail it. They may also mean both together. In that case, recitation is not identical to what is recited, because it includes the act; nor is it separate and distinct from what is recited, because what is recited forms part of it.

This applies when reading and what is read concern a single, specific instance, such as the Lord’s reading and what He reads, or the servant’s reading and what he reads. But if “reading” means the servant’s reading—that is, his movement—while “what is read” means the Lord’s attribute, then undoubtedly the servant’s movement is not the Lord’s attribute. Yet this is a strained distinction. Rather, the servant’s reading is what he reads, like his recited text. If his reading of the Quran means the Quran itself, then it is what he reads. If it means his movement, then it is not what he reads. If both are intended, neither statement may be made without qualification.

For this reason, among those who identify with the Sunnah, some say, “Reading is what is read”; others say, “Reading is other than what is read”; and others refrain from stating either

---

## Page 8397

of the two. Each position has an element of truth when the matter is fully understood and considered impartially. None, however, encompasses the whole truth. Rather, each is correct in one respect and may be mistaken in another.

Al-Bukhārī affirms only that the servants’ acts—their movements and voices—are created. Reading in this sense is the servant’s act, subject to commands and prohibitions. As for the speech itself, it is Allah’s speech. Al-Bukhārī did not say that the servant’s utterance is either created or uncreated, just as Ahmad prohibited both formulations. Those acts and attributes of the servants that al-Bukhārī declared created were not declared uncreated by Ahmad or any other scholar of the early generations, even if they remained silent on the matter because it was self-evident and their aim was to refute the Jahmiyyah. What Ahmad declared uncreated was Allah’s speech, not an attribute of the servants; al-Bukhārī did not declare this created.

Ahmad’s aim, however, was to refute those who considered Allah’s speech created when it is conveyed from Him, whereas al-Bukhārī’s aim was to refute those who said, “The servants’ acts and voices are uncreated.” Both aims are sound, and there is no contradiction between them. Ibn Qutaybah explained this in

---

## Page 8398

the question of utterance. But those who incline towards either extreme denounce the other side. Allah—glorified be He—knows best.

Section

As for intransitive acts, such as rising over the Throne and coming, people disagree over whether to affirm them at all. These acts involve no existing object known to them from whose existence as a created thing they could infer the act of creation. Rather, these acts are known only through revelation. Their basis, therefore, is revelation, not reason. Thus, those who deny the attributes known through revelation also deny these acts, whether they say, “The act of creation is distinct from the created thing,” or, “The act of creation is the created thing.” Those in either group who affirm the attributes known through revelation also affirm these acts.

Those who affirm the attributes known through revelation hold two views concerning these acts:

Some classify them as transitive acts by regarding them as events that arise in something else. This is the view of al-Ash‘arī, the leading scholars among his followers, and those who agreed with them, such as al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā, Ibn al-Zāghūnī, and Ibn Aqīl in many of his statements.

Al-Ash‘arī says: “Rising over the Throne is an act He performed upon the Throne, through which He became

---

## Page 8399

established over the Throne.” He says the same concerning coming and descending.

He says: “These acts are not exclusive to bodies; rather, both bodies and accidents can be described in these terms. Thus one says, ‘The fever has come,’ ‘The cold has come,’ and ‘The heat has come,’ and similar expressions.” This is also the position of al-Qādī Abū Bakr, al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā, and others.

They likewise interpreted in this way what was reported from the early generations, such as al-Awzā‘ī and others, who said concerning the descent: “Allah Himself acts above the Throne.” This was related by al-Qādī ‘Abd al-Wahhāb from al-Qādī Abū Bakr, and was also related from al-Ash‘arī and others, as mentioned in several places in his books. They regard this, however, as belonging to the attributes known through revelation. This is the position of al-Bayhaqī and a group of others, and it is the first of al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā’s two views.

Everyone who maintains that attributes subject to the Lord’s will do not subsist in Him denies that an act He wills can subsist in Him, whether intransitive or transitive. Those among them who nevertheless affirm a pre-eternal act—such as those who affirm bringing into being and hold this view—say that this pre-eternal act subsists in Him, but not by His will, just as they say concerning His pre-eternal will.

The second view is that these are, as the evidence indicates, acts that subsist in His very essence by His will

---

## Page 8400

and choice, just as they say concerning transitive acts. This is the position of the leading authorities in Sunnah, hadith, jurisprudence, and Sufism, as well as many among the various schools of speculative theology, as previously explained.

Their disagreement over the interpretation of His words rests on this distinction:

“Then He turned to the heaven,”

“Do they await anything but that Allah should come to them in canopies of clouds?”

and:

“Then He established Himself over the Throne,”

and similar passages. Those who deny these acts interpret His coming as the coming of His command or His punishment. They interpret His establishment over the Throne as power and dominion, or as exalted rank.

There are two views concerning establishment over the Throne: whether it is an attribute of action or an attribute of essence. Those who hold that it is an attribute of essence interpret it to mean that He has power over the Throne. Yet He has always possessed power and has always been exalted in rank. The weakness of this view thus becomes apparent in several respects. One is His statement:

“Then He established Himself over the Throne.”

He thus speaks of His establishment over the Throne using the conjunction “then.”

---

## Page 8401

Another is that He joined one verb to another, saying: “He created, then established Himself.” Another is that their interpretation makes no distinction between the Throne and anything else. If it is said that the Throne is the greatest of created things, this does not preclude affirming the same of other things, as in His words:

“Lord of the Mighty Throne.”

Although He mentions His lordship over the Throne because of its greatness, His lordship encompasses everything. Thus, one may say:

“Lord of the heavens and the earth and whatever is between them, and Lord of the Mighty Throne,”

and:

“Lord of all worlds,”

“Lord of Moses and Aaron.”

Establishment, however, pertains specifically to the Throne, by the agreement of the Muslims, even though He has dominion and power over everything in heaven and earth and whatever is between them. If His establishment over the Throne meant His power over it, it would be permissible to say that He established Himself over heaven and earth and whatever is between them. Various groups have used this argument, including al-Ashʿarī, who said: “The Muslims’ consensus that establishment pertains specifically to the Throne demonstrates the invalidity of this view.” Furthermore, He has had power over it ever since He created it.

Another is that they dispute the claim that the word istiwā (establishment) is used in Arabic to mean power or exalted rank. Its actual usage in the Quran, the Sunnah, and Arab speech rules out this interpretation, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

He also discussed the verse of poetry they cite as evidence:

---

## Page 8402

“Then Bishr established himself over Iraq … without a sword or blood being shed.”

He explained that, even if authentic, this verse would provide no evidence. For they did not say, “Umar established himself over Iraq” when he conquered it, nor “Uthmān established himself over Khurāsān,” nor “The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, established himself over Yemen.” Rather, this verse—if authentic—was said about Bishr ibn Marwān when he entered Iraq and established himself upon its royal throne. This expression was used just as one says, “He sat on the royal throne,” or “under royal authority.” One also says, “He took his seat as king,” intending this meaning.

Moreover, numerous Quranic verses and hadiths, together with the consensus of the early generations, indicate that Allah is above the Throne, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

As for those who say that establishment is an attribute of action, they hold two views here, corresponding to the distinction discussed earlier: Is it an act separate from Him, because “act” means the result of the act, or an act subsisting in Him that occurs through His will and power? The first is the view of Ibn Kullāb and those who followed him, such as al-Ashʿarī and others. It is also the view of al-Qādī, Ibn ʿAqīl, Ibn al-Zāghūnī, and others.

---

## Page 8403

The second is the view of the leading scholars of hadith and the Sunnah, and of many groups of speculative theologians, as discussed earlier. Thus, people have adopted six positions concerning what Allah mentions in the Quran about His establishment, coming, and similar matters.

One group says that these texts are to be understood according to their apparent meaning, and regards His coming as being of the same kind as the coming of created beings, and His descent as being of the same kind as theirs. These are those who liken Allah to His creation and equate Him with it. Some of them say: “When He descends, He leaves the Throne vacant and no longer remains above it.”

Another group says that, rather, the texts retain their apparent meaning in a manner befitting Him, as with all the other attributes with which He is described:

“There is nothing like Him”

—neither in His essence, nor in His attributes, nor in His acts. They say: “He descends in a manner befitting His majesty, and likewise comes in a manner befitting His majesty.” In their view, He descends and comes while remaining on high, above the Throne. As Hammād ibn Zayd said: “He is above the Throne and draws near to His creation however He wills.”

Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh said: “He descends without leaving the Throne vacant.” This was also transmitted from Ahmad ibn Hanbal in his letter to Musaddad. Explaining the descent as an act subsisting in His essence is the position of the scholars of hadith, and it is the position that Abū ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Barr reported from them. It is also the position of the great majority of the early followers of Ahmad, and Ibn Hāmid and others explicitly affirmed it.

---

## Page 8404

The first position—denying that matters dependent on His will subsist in Him—is that of al-Tamīmī, who agreed with Ibn Kullāb in this, and of al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā and his followers.

Two other groups say: “Rather, He neither descends nor comes,” as discussed earlier. Some interpret these expressions figuratively, while others entrust their meaning to Allah.

Two further groups suspend judgment. Some say: “We do not know what Allah intended by this,” while others go no further than reciting the Quran.

The great majority of those who identify with the Sunnah and follow the early generations reject interpretations that deny that He Himself is the One who is established and who comes. Yet many of them reject false interpretations while saying: “I do not know what Allah intended by this.” Some say: “This is among the things we have been forbidden to explain, or whose explanation is to be withheld.” Others affirm it as conveyed in the authentic hadiths and the numerous reports from the early generations—the Companions and their successors.

Abū Muhammad al-Baghawī, al-Husayn ibn Masʿūd al-Farrāʾ, known as Muhyī al-Sunnah (Reviver of the Sunnah), said in his Quran commentary:

“Then He rose towards heaven.”

Ibn ʿAbbās and most Quran commentators among the early generations said: “That is, He rose towards heaven.” And al-Farrāʾ and Ibn Kaysān

---

## Page 8405

and a group of grammarians said: “That is, He turned to creating heaven.” It was also said: “He intended to do so.” This is what Ibn al-Jawzī mentioned in his Quran commentary. He said:

“Then He rose towards heaven”—that is, He set about creating it.

Likewise, he favours the interpretation that His coming means the coming of His command, and the view of those who interpret His rising figuratively. He also mentioned this in other books and agreed with some of Ibn ʿAqīl’s views. He said: “Ibn ʿAqīl held differing views on this subject, and his opinions and scholarly judgments vary across his writings.”

In his commentary on the words, “Then He rose over the Throne,” al-Baghawī said:

“Al-Kalbī and Muqātil said: ‘He settled.’ Abū ʿUbaydah said: ‘He ascended.’ The Muʿtazilah interpreted His rising as taking dominion. As for Ahl al-Sunnah, they say: ‘Rising over the Throne is an attribute of Allah, without specifying how. One must believe in it and entrust knowledge concerning it to Allah.’

“A man asked Mālik ibn Anas about His words, ‘The Most Merciful rose over the Throne’: ‘How did He rise?’ Mālik bowed his head for a long while and began to sweat profusely. Then he said: ‘His rising is not unknown, but how is beyond comprehension. Belief in it is obligatory, and asking about it is an innovation. I can only regard you as misguided.’ Then he ordered that the man be removed, and he was taken out.”

---

## Page 8406

He said:

“It is reported from Sufyān al-Thawrī, al-Awzāʿī, al-Layth ibn Saʿd, Sufyān ibn ʿUyaynah, ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Mubārak and other scholars of the Sunnah that, concerning these verses about the ambiguous attributes, they said: ‘Let them stand as they have come, without specifying how.’”

Concerning His words,

“Are they waiting for anything but for Allah to come to them in canopies of clouds?”

he said:

“The preferable course concerning this verse and others like it is to believe in their apparent meaning, entrust knowledge concerning them to Allah, and believe that Allah is free of the characteristics of created things. This was the path followed by the leading scholars of the early generations and the scholars of the Sunnah.”

Al-Kalbī said:

“This belongs to the concealed matters that are not to be explained.”

(I say: It has also been related from him that, in explaining His words, “Then He rose,” he said: “He settled.” Thus, he explained that passage but regarded this one as belonging to the concealed matters that are not to be explained. For that passage describes Him as being above the Throne, whereas this one describes His coming in canopies of clouds.

Al-Baghawī said:

“Makhūl, al-Zuhrī, al-Awzāʿī, Mālik, ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Mubārak, Sufyān al-Thawrī, al-Layth ibn Saʿd, Ahmad and Ishāq used to say concerning this and similar passages: ‘Let them stand as they have come, without specifying how.’”

Sufyān ibn ʿUyaynah said:

“Whatever Allah has used to describe Himself in His Book, its explanation consists in reciting it and remaining silent about it. No one has the right to explain it except Allah and His Messenger.”

---

## Page 8407

And this verse is more obscure than the verse concerning rising over the Throne. Hence Abū al-Faraj inclined towards interpreting this passage figuratively, while rejecting the interpretation of rising over the Throne as gaining dominion.

He said in his commentary:

“Al-Khalīl ibn Ahmad said: ‘ʿArsh means a throne, and every royal seat is called an ʿarsh. The word ʿarsh is rarely used in the plural except out of necessity.’”

I say: Ibn Abī Hātim narrated from Abū Rawq, from al-Dahhāk, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said: “It is called an ʿarsh because of its elevation.”

I say: Its derivation supports this, as in His words, “and what they used to build,” and His words, “trellised and untrellised,” as well as Saʿd’s statement: “This man was an unbeliever among the dwellings of Mecca (al-ʿurush).” A king’s seat is higher than other seats. Thus, relative to what is below it, it is high above it; relative to what is above it, it is beneath it.

In the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“When you ask Allah, ask Him for al-Firdaws, for it is the highest and most central part of Paradise, and its roof is the Throne of the Most Merciful.”

This indicates that the Throne is the highest of all created things, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

Abū al-Faraj said:

Know that mention of the Throne was well known among the Arabs, both before Islam and after its advent. Umayyah ibn Abī al-Salt said:

> Glorify Allah, for He is worthy of glory;
> Our Lord in heaven is great.

---

## Page 8408

in the highest edifice—He who existed before mankind
and fashioned a throne above the heaven,
a spacious throne beyond the reach of human sight;
beneath it you see the angels as forms.

I say: He means that those Arabs who mentioned it without being Muslims had taken this from the People of the Book. Umayyah and others like him only took this from the People of the Book; otherwise, the polytheists had no knowledge of it.

Abū al-Faraj Ibn al-Jawzī said:

“Kaʿb said: ‘The heavens in relation to the Throne are like a lamp suspended between heaven and earth.’”

He said:

“The early generations unanimously agreed not to go beyond reciting the verse. Some, however, departed from this and said that the Throne means dominion. This abandons the literal meaning for a figurative one, while also contradicting the transmitted reports. Have they not heard His words:

‘And His Throne was upon the water’?

Would you then suppose that dominion was upon the water?”

He said:

“Some of them say that istawā (‘rose over’) means istawlā (‘gained dominion over’), citing the poet’s words:

Until Bishr established himself over Iraq,
without a sword or any blood being shed.

The poet also said:

---

## Page 8409

Rarely indeed have the two, through their merit, together
ascended the throne of kings, without falsehood.”

He said:

“This is rejected by the lexicographers.”

Ibn al-Aʿrābī said:

“The Arabs do not know istawā (‘rose over’) to mean istawlā (‘gained dominion over’). Whoever claims this has committed a grave error.”

He said:

“One only says, ‘So-and-so gained dominion over such-and-such,’ when it had been beyond his reach and control, and he subsequently gained control over it. But Allah, glorified and exalted is He, has always held dominion over all things. It is not known who composed these two lines; this is what Ibn Fāris the lexicographer stated. Even if their authenticity were established, they would offer no proof, given our explanation that gaining dominion implies not having possessed it before. We seek refuge in Allah from the heretics’ denial of His attributes and the corporealists’ likening Him to His creation.”

I say: Thus, he interpreted His words figuratively:

“Then He turned to the heaven,”

yet rejected a figurative interpretation of:

“Then He rose over the Throne.”

Regarding the expression “coming,” however, he mentioned both views, saying:

“Concerning His words, ‘that Allah should come to them in canopies,’ a number of the early generations would refrain from speaking about such matters. Al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā reported that Ahmad said: ‘What is meant is His power and His command.’”

He said:

“This is clarified in His words: ‘or your Lord’s command should come.’”

I say: This account mentioned by al-Qādī and others was transmitted by Hanbal from

---

## Page 8410

Ahmad in The Book of the Ordeal: he said this while debating them during the ordeal, when they argued against him by citing the statement, “Al-Baqarah and Āl Imrān will come.” They said, “Only something created can come.” Ahmad countered them with Allah’s words:

“And your Lord comes,”

and:

“Or your Lord should come.”

He said that “Al-Baqarah and Āl Imrān will come” means their reward, just as “And your Lord comes” means His command and His power.

Ahmad’s followers differed over what Hanbal transmitted. There is no doubt that it contradicts the statements transmitted from Ahmad through numerous independent chains, in which he prohibited figurative interpretation of this, of the descent, of rising over the Throne, and of other such actions. They held three views.

The first was that this was an error unique to Hanbal, not shared by those who recounted Ahmad’s debate, such as Sālih, Abd Allāh, al-Marwadhī, and others. They did not mention this. Hanbal does transmit certain reports alone in which a number of scholars, such as al-Khallāl and his associate, judge him to have erred.

Abū Ishāq Ibn Shāqlā said: “This is undoubtedly an error on Hanbal’s part.”

Similarly, a report was transmitted from Mālik that he interpreted “He descends to the lowest heaven” as meaning that His command descends. However, this was transmitted by his scribe Habīb, whom they unanimously considered a liar. It has also been transmitted through another route, but that chain of transmission is unknown.

The second view: a group of Ahmad’s followers said that he stated this in order to bind his opponent

---

## Page 8411

on the basis of the opponent’s own doctrine. For during the ordeal, when they argued against him by citing the saying, “Al-Baqarah and Āl Imrān will come,” he answered that it meant that the reward of Al-Baqarah and Āl Imrān would come, just as Allah’s words, “that Allah should come to them,” meant His command and His power according to their figurative interpretation—not that he himself held this view. His position was to refrain from figurative interpretation.

The third view was that they accepted this as a position reported from Ahmad. The imams’ statements may vary on such questions, but the sound, well-known position transmitted from him is the rejection of figurative interpretation. Ibn al-Zāghūnī and others mentioned both reports, noting that refraining from figurative interpretation was the well-known report followed by most of the scholars of our school. In the report allowing figurative interpretation, he explained this as deliberate intent and purpose, not as command and power, just as they interpreted, “Then He turned toward heaven.” Accordingly, if figurative interpretation is adopted, there are two ways of interpreting this.

Although Ibn al-Zāghūnī, Judge Abū Ya‘lā, and others like them hold that coming and arrival should be accepted in their apparent sense, their position on this is of the same kind as that of Ibn Kullāb and al-Ash‘arī. For he too rejects figurative interpretation of descent, arrival, and coming, and regards these as attributes known through revelation. He says: “These actions do not necessarily imply bodies; rather, things other than bodies may be described by them.” Ibn al-Zāghūnī’s discussion of this

---

## Page 8412

type of attribute and of the Lord’s rising over the Throne accords with Abū al-Hasan’s own position. This is their position on the attributes known through revelation that are mentioned in connection with these actions.

As for the Lord Himself being high above the universe, Ibn Kullāb holds that this is known through reason, as do most of those who affirm the divine attributes, as al-Khattābī, Ibn ‘Abd al-Barr, and others have mentioned.

This is also Ibn al-Zāghūnī’s position and the later of Judge Abū Ya‘lā’s two positions. Initially, the judge followed al-Ash‘arī’s view that this was an attribute known through revelation. This is also the position of Judge Abū Bakr, al-Bayhaqī, and others like them.

As for Abū al-Ma‘ālī al-Juwaynī and his followers, they differed from al-Ash‘arī and his early followers concerning the attributes known through revelation and did not affirm them. Some, however, denied them, interpreting rising over the Throne as exercising dominion over it. This was the earlier of Abū al-Ma‘ālī’s two positions. Others, such as al-Rāzī and al-Āmidī, suspended judgment, neither affirming nor denying them.

Abū al-Ma‘ālī’s later position was that figurative interpretation of the attributes known through revelation was forbidden. He stated that this was the consensus of the early generations, and that, had figurative interpretation been permissible or obligatory, they would have devoted greater attention to it than to other matters. He therefore cited their consensus as evidence that figurative interpretation is impermissible, and held that the complete pause in recitation should be made at

---

## Page 8413

His words, “And none knows its interpretation except Allah.” He mentioned this in al-Nizāmiyya fī al-Arkān al-Islāmiyya (The Nizāmiyya Treatise on the Pillars of Islam). This is the approach of most of those who identify with the Sunnah: they regard figurative interpretation as contrary to the way of the early generations.

This matter has been discussed at length elsewhere, including the ambiguity of the term ta’wīl (interpretation), the discussion of His words, “And none knows its interpretation except Allah,” and the fact that both views are correct. Those who say that none knows its ta’wīl except Allah mean the realities to which the words ultimately refer, which Allah alone knows. Those who say that people firmly grounded in knowledge know its ta’wīl mean the explanation of the Quran conveyed by the Messenger and the Companions.

The disagreement concerns only the use of the term ta’wīl for adopting the less likely meaning—that is, construing an expression in its less likely sense rather than its more likely sense, with accompanying evidence. This is a later terminological usage. It is this kind of interpretation that the early generations and the leading scholars rejected: the interpretations advanced by the people of innovation.

Likewise, Ahmad says in his Refutation of the Jahmiyya: “Those who interpreted the Quran contrary to its proper interpretation.” Ahmad discussed the Quran’s ambiguous passages and explained them all.

---

## Page 8414

And some of these explanations are agreed upon among the early generations, while others are disputed.

Abū ‘Abd Allāh, the grandfather, mentioned in his commentary something similar to what al-Baghawī mentioned, rather than what Ibn al-Jawzī mentioned. He said:

As for the coming attributed to Allah, the leading scholars of the early generations—such as Makhūl, al-Zuhrī, al-Awzā‘ī, Ibn al-Mubārak, Sufyān al-Thawrī, al-Layth ibn Sa‘d, Mālik ibn Anas, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and their followers—agree that it is to be left as it has been transmitted. The same applies to similar accounts found in the Quran or transmitted in the Sunnah, such as the hadiths concerning the descent and the like. This is the safe course and the way of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamā‘ah: they believe in their apparent meaning, entrust knowledge of them to Allah, and believe that Allah is free of the marks of createdness. The leading scholars have followed this course, generation after generation, as Allah Most High says:

“None knows its interpretation except Allah. And those firmly grounded in knowledge say, ‘We believe in it.’”

Regarding His words, “that Allah should come to them in canopies of clouds,” Ibn al-Sā’ib said: “This belongs to what is kept hidden and is not to be explained.” He also mentioned something resembling al-Khattābī’s discussion of this matter.

If it is asked, “How can someone believe in something when they do not fully comprehend its true reality?” the answer is: Just as it is possible to believe in Allah

---

## Page 8415

and His angels, His Books, His Messengers, the Last Day, Hell, and Paradise. It is well known that we do not possess exhaustive, detailed knowledge of all these things; we are only required to believe in them in general terms. Do you not see that we do not know some of the prophets and many of the angels, nor do we fully comprehend their attributes, yet this does not detract from our belief in them?

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said when describing Paradise:

“Allah Most High says: ‘I have prepared for My righteous servants what no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and no human heart has ever imagined.’”

(I say: There is no doubt that one must believe everything the Messenger has reported and affirm its truth, even if one has not understood what he said in Arabic or grasped anything of his words. This applies all the more to Arabs. Belief in general terms does not require knowing the meaning of everything he reported; there is no doubt about this. Thus, anyone who finds a verse of the Quran unclear and does not know its meaning must believe in it and entrust knowledge of it to Allah, saying, “Allah knows best.” This is agreed upon by the early and later generations. Many of the Companions would encounter a verse or an expression they did not understand and believe in it despite not understanding its meaning. But can there be anything in the Quran that no human being understands—not even the Messenger—according to those who take interpretation to mean “the meaning of the verse” and say that it is not

---

## Page 8416

known to anyone but Allah? It would follow that the Quran contains speech that neither the Messenger nor anyone in the Muslim community understands—not even Gabriel. This is the necessary consequence of maintaining that no human being understands the meanings of these verses.

This is not comparable to what was mentioned concerning the angels, the prophets, and Paradise. We have understood the words addressed to us and that they indicate the existence of bliss unknown to us. This is intelligible speech informing us that some created things are unknown to us. This is true, as in His words:

“None knows the hosts of your Lord except Him.”

And His words when they asked him about the spirit:

“You have been given only a little knowledge.”

These statements inform us that Allah has created things of which we have no knowledge, or whose kind we know without knowing their full measure, or whose attributes we know in some respects but not others. All this is true. But none of it means that neither the Messenger nor the believers can comprehend or understand the meaning of the revealed speech that we have been commanded to ponder. That is the objectionable position the scholars rejected. For Allah said:

“Indeed, We have made it an Arabic Quran so that you may understand.”

And He said:

“Do they not ponder the Quran, or are there locks upon their hearts?”

And He said:

“Have they not pondered the message?”

And He said:

“Until, when they leave your presence, they say to those who have been given knowledge, ‘What did he say just now?’ Those are the ones whose hearts Allah has sealed.”

He also drew a distinction regarding what He has not told us about, or whose attributes He has disclosed to us only in part. As for what

---

## Page 8417

He has not told us about, it does us no harm not to know it. He has made clear what He has told us: the Arabic speech that He made a guidance and a healing for people.

Al-Hasan said:

“Allah has not revealed a verse without wishing people to know what it was revealed about and what He meant by it.”

How, then, could such speech contain anything that no one understands at all? There is a difference between saying, “It is the Lord Himself who comes, in a manner befitting His majesty,” and saying, “We do not know whether it is He who comes or His command.”

Many people do not affirm either position decisively, but instead say, “Remain silent, for silence is safer.” Undoubtedly, silence is safer for someone who does not know, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day should speak good or remain silent.”

Yet this person maintains that the Messenger and the entire Muslim community were likewise unaware whether this or that was intended, and that even the Messenger did not know. Whoever says this is wrong and speaks without knowledge. It would have sufficed for him to remain silent about this, rather than assert that the Messenger and all the imams were ignorant and obliged to remain silent just as he himself is.

Moreover, this is contrary to the facts. The Prophet’s hadiths, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the statements of the early generations explaining this verse and others like it are numerous and well known. However, ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

“Speak to people about what they understand, and leave aside what they would reject. Would you like Allah and His Messenger to be disbelieved?”

Ibn Mas‘ūd said:

“There is no

---

## Page 8418

man who tells people something beyond their understanding without it becoming a source of trial for some of them.”
