If he says, “Rather, some of the early generations were certain that what was meant was His own coming,” then this is a definite affirmation that they understood its meaning and that the other view was false. They were not silent and bewildered. Undoubtedly, what He decrees and commands also comes, but He Himself comes, as He has told us about Himself, in a manner befitting His majesty.

Thus, to say, “We do not know the manner of His rising above,” is correct. But if this discourse and speech had a meaning that no one understood—neither the Messenger, nor Gabriel, nor the believers—it would not be something that could be pondered and understood. Rather, such a thing would be futile, and Allah is far above futility.

Moreover, their position necessarily entails the same regarding hadiths, such as his saying:

“Our Lord descends every night to heaven.”

Was the Messenger, then, uttering this hadith and others like it without comprehending what he was saying or understanding any meaning in it? Glory be to Allah! This is a monstrous slander, an attack on the Messenger, and an empowerment of the heretics.

If it is said that he did not understand the meaning of the very speech he brought, they will say, “Then he would be even less likely to understand other forms of rational knowledge.” The discussion concerns the attributes of the Lord. Thus, if it is said that what was revealed to him concerning

---

## Page 8419

the attributes of the Lord was understood neither by him nor by anyone else—even though it is the speech of an unlettered Arab, revealed to him—then it will be said: “He would be even less likely to understand rationally discernible meanings concerning divine matters. In that case, this subject formed no part of his message, and no knowledge of it could be obtained from him, whether through revelation or reason.”

The heretics say: “Then it must be obtained through someone else.”

If these people tell them, “This is impossible for anyone,” they reject that claim and say: “The Quran only states that no one but Allah knows the meaning of that discourse. But on what basis do you claim that divine matters cannot be known through rational proofs that mere verbal exposition and reports cannot adequately convey?”

The heretics say that the messengers addressed people through imaginative representations; the speculative theologians say through expressions requiring figurative interpretation; and these literalists say through words whose meanings were left unknown. The errors of these three groups have been discussed at length. It has been explained that the Messenger brought the utmost knowledge and clarity, such that no human being could bring anything more perfect than what he brought—may Allah bestow blessings and abundant peace upon him. The most perfect thing he brought is the Quran, and people differ enormously in their understanding of it.

Ibn al-Sāʾib’s statement, “This belongs to what is concealed and is not explained,” implies that it has an explanation which the scholars know but conceal.

---

## Page 8420

And this may be understood in two ways. Either he means that some of what the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—explained is concealed from everyone. This is outright concealment, which Allah has condemned. Such is the condition of the People of the Book. He censured those who conceal the clear proofs and guidance He explained to people, after making them clear to people in the Book. He said:

“And who is more unjust than one who conceals a testimony he has from Allah?”

Such is the condition of the People of the Book in concealing passages in their scripture: some interpret them figuratively, while others declare their meanings indeterminate. These passages contain proofs of Muhammad’s prophethood—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and other matters. For they cannot deny the wording of the Torah, the Gospel, and the other books of the prophets—which, among the People of the Book, number twenty-odd books. Rather, they distort it through false interpretation and conceal its correct meanings from their ordinary people, as Allah Most High said:

“And among them are unlettered people who know nothing of the Scripture except its recitation.”

Whoever treats the people of the Quran in the same way, instructing them to remain unlettered concerning it, knowing nothing of the Book except its recitation, has instructed them to do the very thing for which Allah condemned the People of the Book.

ʿUmar only beat Subaygh ibn ʿUsayl al-Tamīmī because, by pursuing the ambiguous passages, he sought to stir up discord and sought their interpretation. These are the people whom Allah censured in His Book, because they

---

## Page 8421

combined two things: evil intent and ignorance. They do not understand its meaning, yet seek to set parts of Allah’s Book against one another, thereby sowing confusion and doubt.

It is authentically reported from ‘Ā’ishah that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“When you see those who pursue its ambiguous passages, they are the ones Allah has named, so beware of them.”

This is what those who set scriptural texts against one another do to stir up discord—that is, doubt and uncertainty in people’s hearts. Thus, it is reported that:

“He came out to some people who were disputing about divine decree. Some were saying, ‘Did Allah not say such-and-such?’ while others were saying, ‘Did Allah not say such-and-such?’ His face looked as though pomegranate seeds had been burst upon it. Then he said, ‘Is this what you were commanded to do—to set parts of Allah’s Book against one another? Look to what you have been commanded, and do it.’”

Everyone who pursues the ambiguous passages in this manner is blameworthy. Such is the case of someone who seeks to make people doubt what they know because neither he nor they have understood what they imagine contradicts it. This is the root of discord: abandoning what is known for what is unknown, like the sophistry that generates doubts used to undermine established, certain knowledge. Such is the condition of those who corrupt people’s hearts and minds by undermining the knowledge and practice within them—the foundation of guidance. Once they make people doubt what they know, they are left bewildered.

The Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—brought clear signs attesting to

---

## Page 8422

his truthfulness. The Quran contains clear verses that form the foundation of the Book. Their meaning is known, and they are known to be true; through them, people find guidance and benefit.

Whoever pursues the ambiguous passages seeks discord and seeks their interpretation. In the first, their intention is corrupt; as for the second, they are not qualified for it. Rather, they interpret these passages in ways that distort their meaning, since they are not firmly grounded in knowledge. One firmly grounded in knowledge is someone whose understanding of the clear passages is firmly established and unwavering. He does not doubt or become uncertain about them because of ambiguous passages that appear to contradict them; rather, he believes in them. Those firmly grounded in knowledge may know the interpretation of the ambiguous passages.

As for someone who is not firmly grounded in this understanding, but doubts the clear passages whenever an ambiguous passage appears to contradict them, he considers it possible that the ambiguous passage means something that contradicts the clear one. He therefore does not know the meaning of the ambiguous passage, since he is not firmly grounded in understanding the clear passages. He seeks discord through both. Such a person is to receive a punishment that deters him, as ‘Umar did with Sabīgh. But someone whose aim is guidance and truth is not one of these people.

‘Umar himself used to ask questions, and be asked, about the subtle meanings of verses. He asked his companions about Allah’s words:

“When Allah’s help and victory come.”

They explained the apparent meaning of its wording. But when Ibn ‘Abbās explained that it informed the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—that his death was near, ‘Umar said:

“I understand nothing from it beyond what you understand.”

---

## Page 8423

And this is the verse’s inner meaning, which accords with its apparent meaning. When he was commanded to seek forgiveness upon the religion’s triumph—seeking forgiveness being enjoined at the completion of deeds, and the religion’s triumph marking the fulfilment of the purpose of his mission—they understood, together with other considerations, that this announced the approach of his death. “Above everyone endowed with knowledge is one more knowledgeable.”

A thing is inferred from what entails it. Something may have an implication, and that implication may have a further implication, and so on. Some people are more perceptive than others in recognizing these implications, inferring what is entailed from what entails it.

Others do not conceive of the implication; even if they did, they would not recognize what entails it. Instead, they say: “It may follow, or it may not; this is possible, and so is that.” Such indecision between possibilities arises from a lack of knowledge; in reality, one of the two is the case. Wherever possibilities are entertained without grounds for preferring one over another, this is due to ignorance of what is actually the case and to its evidence being hidden from the person concerned. Someone else may know both the reality and its evidence. Whoever imagines that what he does not know is also unknown to others does so out of ignorance. One should therefore deny knowledge to others only where one knows that they lack it. Above everyone endowed with knowledge is one more knowledgeable, who knows more than he does, until ultimately the matter reaches Allah Most High. This has been discussed at length elsewhere.

Then they say:

“What is transmitted from the early generations is that one should refrain from delving into

---

## Page 8424

its interpretation, believe in its apparent meaning, and refrain from explaining it, for we have been forbidden to speak about the Book of Allah on the basis of our own opinions, and Allah and His Messenger have not made its true meaning known to us.”

The response is:

Everyone is indeed commanded to remain silent about what they do not know. However, this statement implies that they did not know the verse’s meaning, explanation or interpretation. If it was not clear to them, this amounts to acknowledging their lack of knowledge of it. These are the words of someone in doubt, who does not know what is intended by the verse. Yet when certain interpretations are mentioned to them—such as interpreting it as the coming of His command and His power—they reject this on the grounds that it renders the specification meaningless. This is a denial and rejection of such interpretation.

If, despite this, they say:

“And no one knows its interpretation except Allah,”

they affirm an interpretation known only to Allah, while denying interpretation of this kind altogether.

And we say: What calls for this far-fetched interpretation? Without it, we can affirm a coming and an arrival beyond comprehension, in a manner befitting Him, just as we affirm an essence whose reality is beyond comprehension, and attributes—hearing, sight and others—that are beyond comprehension. For if it is permissible to interpret this and posit an implicit, omitted term, such as power, punishment or the like, what has prevented you from interpreting the words “You will see your Lord” in the same way?

---

## Page 8425

This argument invalidates such interpretation and takes the wording in its apparent sense, in a manner befitting Allah’s majesty.

If, alongside this, it is said that it has an interpretation known only to Allah, and “interpretation” means this same kind of interpretation, that would be a contradiction. How can someone deny this kind of interpretation while affirming an interpretation known only to Allah?

It is thus clear that the interpretation known only to Allah does not contradict understanding the passage according to what its wording indicates. Rather, it is something else that substantiates and agrees with this understanding, rather than contradicting or opposing it. As Mālik said:

“The rising is known, but its manner is unknown.”

That being so, one scholar may understand something of the verse’s meaning that accords with the Quran but is unknown to another. This would constitute an explanation of the verse and belong to the interpretation known to those firmly grounded in knowledge. For example, someone may know with absolute certainty that the verse refers to Allah’s coming, without any doubt, because of the abundant evidence available to him. At the same time, he knows that He is the Most High, the Highest, and comes in such a way that created things surround Him while He is beneath them. This contradicts His being the Most High, the Highest.

The forebear Abū ʿAbd Allāh—may Allah have mercy on him—followed the method he mentioned in his commentary. This is his usual practice, and that of others.

---

## Page 8426

He also cited Ibn al-Zāghūnī, saying: Shaykh Alī ibn Ubayd Allāh al-Zāghūnī said:

“Our imam Ahmad’s statements concerning this coming differ, with two reports regarding whether it is understood in its apparent sense or admits of figurative interpretation. According to one report, it is understood in its apparent sense: that He Himself comes.

On this basis, he says:

Figurative interpretation does not apply. Nevertheless, His own coming must be understood only in a manner befitting Him. It has been established that affirming His coming is not to be understood as affirming a departure and movement that entail vacating one place and occupying another. Such coming is characteristic of created beings, which cannot encompass places and locations because these are larger and greater than they are. Their coming to those places requires moving from what is near to what is distant. This is impossible in the case of the Creator, Most High, because nothing is greater than Him. His coming does not require movement or departure, since neither the need for this nor what necessitates it exists in His case.

We therefore affirm coming as an attribute of His, while rejecting the supposition that what necessarily applies to created beings applies to Him, since they differ in their need for such movement. Another example is His saying:

‘And your Lord comes, with the angels in row upon row.’

Another example is the well-known hadith narrated by the Companions at large, in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

‘Allah descends to the lowest heaven every night when there remains a third of

---

## Page 8427

the night—the last third—and says:

‘Who will call upon Me, that I may answer him? Who will ask of Me, that I may give him? Who will seek My forgiveness, that I may forgive him?’

We therefore affirm His descent to the lowest heaven on the authority of the hadith. We do not adopt the figurative interpretation they have mentioned, nor do we equate His descent with human descent, which involves departure and movement from above to below. Rather, we accept the transmitted account as it has come to us, reject any likening of Him to creation because there is no basis for it, and rule out figurative interpretation because it is inapplicable.”

He said:

“This report is the well-known one, and it is the position adopted by the majority of the shaykhs of our school.”

(I say:

That His coming, arrival and descent are unlike the coming, arrival and descent of created beings is self-evident and agreed upon by the scholars of the Sunnah and all people of reason. Attributes and actions correspond to the essence that possesses those attributes and performs those actions. Since His essence is distinct from all other essences and unlike them, it necessarily follows that His attributes are distinct from all other attributes and unlike them. His attributes relate to His essence as the attributes of anything relate to its essence.

There is no doubt that He is the Most High, the Most Exalted, the Magnificent. He is higher than everything and greater than everything. His descent and coming therefore cannot be such that created things encompass Him or are greater and larger than Him. That is impossible.

As for the terms “departure” and “movement,” these are ambiguous, and for this reason

---

## Page 8428

the scholars of hadith and the Sunnah have held differing views on this matter.

Uthmān ibn Saīd al-Dārimī and others objected to the Jahmiyyah’s assertion that He does not move. The Jahmiyyah cited a report stating that He does not depart, interpreting “departure” as motion. Uthmān ibn Saīd explained that, even if authentic, this report would not support their position, because it concerns the explanation of His words, “the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all.” They related from Thābit: “Everlasting and enduring, never departing from what is His due,” just as Ibn Ishāq said: “He does not depart from His station.”

I say:

Al-Kalbī himself, who transmitted this report, explains “He rose over the Throne” as “He settled,” and “Then He rose towards the heaven” as “He ascended to the heaven.”

As for “movement,” Ibn Hāmid and a group of scholars say: “He descends with motion and movement.” Others among the scholars of the Sunnah, such as al-Tamīmī, one of Ahmad’s followers, rejected this and said: “Rather, He descends without motion or movement.” A third group, including Ibn Battah and others, suspend judgment on this matter. Al-Qādī Abū Yalā mentioned these three positions in his book “Differences between the Two Transmissions and the Two Views, and the Negation of the Expression as a Whole.”

The better course in this matter is to adhere to the wording of the scriptural texts, affirming what

---

## Page 8429

Allah and His Messenger have affirmed, using the wording in which it was affirmed, and denying what Allah and His Messenger have denied, just as they denied it. This means affirming His descent, approach, and coming, while denying that He has any likeness, peer, equal, or rival. This is the reasoning al-Bukhārī and others use to deny that anything is like Him.

It is said:

He descends in a manner in which nothing is like Him. He has descended in a manner unlike the descent of created beings—a descent unique to Him—just as nothing is like Him in this or in any other attribute with which He has described Himself. He is far exalted above His descent resembling the descent, motion, movement from place to place, or departure of created beings in any respect, whether human or otherwise.

When a created being descends from above to below, it ceases to be described as being above and becomes described as being below, while something else becomes higher than it. But nothing can ever be higher than the Lord, exalted is He. Rather, He is the All-High, the Most High, and He remains the All-High, the Most High, even as He draws close to His servants, comes near them, descends wherever He wills, and comes however He wills. Throughout this, He is the All-High, the Most High, the Most Great, the Supremely Exalted: exalted in His nearness and near in His exaltedness.

Although none other than Him possesses these attributes together, this is because created beings are incapable of combining the two, just as they are incapable of being the First and the Last, the Manifest and the Hidden.

---

## Page 8430

For this reason, Abū Saʿīd al-Kharrāz was asked, “How did you come to know Allah?” He replied:

“By combining opposites.”

He meant that what would be contradictory in the case of created beings is combined in His case. Thus, He is the Creator of everything—the deeds of His servants and all other entities and actions, with whatever evil they contain—while also being just, wise, and merciful. He enables those of His servants whom He so enables to commit sins, although He has the power to prevent them, yet in this He is wise and just. He is the most knowledgeable of all who know, the wisest of judges, and the best of arbiters. He knows what is before them and what is behind them. It is therefore all the more fitting and certain that their knowledge cannot encompass what is greater than that. They asked about the spirit and were told:

“The spirit is of my Lord’s command, and you have been given only a little knowledge.”

It is related in the two Sahīh collections that, when a sparrow dipped its beak into the sea, al-Khidr said to Moses:

“My knowledge and yours have diminished Allah’s knowledge no more than this sparrow has diminished this sea.”

As for what is denied of Him and from which He is declared free, one category consists of whatever contradicts His known perfect attributes. Anything of this kind is denied of Him, as He says:

“Allah—there is no deity except Him, the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all. Neither drowsiness nor sleep overtakes Him.”

And He says:

“And put your trust in the Ever-Living, who does not die.”

Drowsiness, sleep, and death of any kind are therefore impossible for Him. It is impermissible to say of any of these, “It may apply to Him in a manner befitting Him,” because anything of this kind entails a deficiency in His perfection.

---

## Page 8431

Likewise, it is impermissible to say:

“He is below rather than above, with a lowness befitting His majesty.”

For He—glory be to Him—is the Exalted, the Most High; He is never anything but high above. Lowness is a deficiency from which He is far removed. The statement,

“You are the Inward, and nothing is nearer than You,”

implies lowness only to an ignorant person who does not understand the true nature of height and lowness, and therefore imagines that the heavens and whatever they contain may be beneath the earth, whether by night or by day. This is an error, like imagining that what lies in the eastern sky is beneath what lies in the western sky. This too is an error. Rather, the sky is always above the earth. Even though the celestial sphere is round and surrounds the earth, it is truly above the earth in every direction. This has been explained at length elsewhere.

The second category is that He is far exalted above any created thing resembling Him in any of His attributes. Accordingly, what is affirmed by the wording of the Quran and Sunnah is to be affirmed, and what is denied by their wording is to be denied. As for terms that admit more than one meaning, such as “motion,” “descent,” and “change of place,” one must say concerning them that He is far exalted above resembling created beings in any respect. He does not resemble any created being in descent, motion, change of place, departure, or anything else. As for affirming this category, as with the term “descent,” or denying it

---

## Page 8432

absolutely, as with the terms “sleep” and “death,” either approach may be followed by a group that identifies with the Sunnah.

Those who affirm these say: “We affirm motion, or motion and change of place, or motion and departure, in a manner befitting Him, just as His descent and coming befit Him.”

Those who deny them say: “Rather, this entire category must be denied.” Some of them deny this category with respect to Him in every sense, holding that no newly arising states can subsist in Him. This is the approach of the Kullābiyyah and their followers among those who profess adherence to the Sunnah and hadith.

Others do not deny what the revealed text indicates in this regard, nor do they deny this category altogether on the grounds they have mentioned—that no temporally originated events subsist in Him. For it is known from the Quranic verses, the Sunnah, and reason that He speaks by His will and power, and that He loves His believing servant when that servant follows His Messenger, along with other meanings indicated by the Quran and Sunnah. Rather, they deny whatever contradicts His attributes of perfection and deny that any created being resembles Him. These are the two things that must be denied. And Allah knows best.

Likewise, if someone says: “Allah must be declared free of the marks of temporal origination, or

---

## Page 8433

the signs of temporal origination, or anything that entails imperfection and temporal origination—the Lord is free of all such things,” this is a true, well-known statement upon which there is agreement. The issue, however, concerns what those who negate the attributes claim to be marks of temporal origination, while others dispute their claim—especially since the Quran and Sunnah contradict their position.

The Jahmiyyah say: “The subsistence of attributes in Him, or of attributes pertaining to His will, is among the marks of temporal origination.” This is false according to the early generations, the leading scholars of the Sunnah, and indeed the majority of rational people. In fact, their claim entails that everything has a temporal beginning. For every existing being has attributes subsisting in it, as well as states that arise through will and power. If this necessarily entails temporal origination, it follows that everything has a temporal beginning and that nothing in all existence is eternal. This too has been discussed at length elsewhere.

The marks of temporal origination that necessarily entail it include dependence upon another. Whatever depends upon another has a temporal beginning, coming into existence after not having existed. The Lord is entirely free of any need for anything besides Himself. Whoever thinks that He needs the Throne or the bearers of the Throne is ignorant and astray. Rather, He is self-sufficient, while everything besides Him depends upon Him in every respect. He is the One upon whom all depend, needing nothing, while everything besides Him turns to Him in need:

“All who are in the heavens and the earth ask of Him; every day He is engaged in a matter.”

---

## Page 8434

And among the marks of temporal origination are imperfections such as ignorance, blindness, deafness, and muteness. Anything possessing such qualities can only have a temporal beginning, for the Eternal, who has no beginning, is free of them. He possesses the opposites of these qualities, and the attributes of perfection are inseparable from Him. An inseparable attribute cannot cease to exist unless that which possesses it ceases to exist. His Essence is eternal, without beginning, necessarily existent in itself, and independent of everything besides itself. Nonexistence and cessation are utterly impossible for it. Its inseparable attributes therefore cannot cease to exist, nor can it possess their opposites. Only a temporally originated being can possess those opposites. They are thus marks of temporal origination, necessarily entailing that whatever possesses them has a temporal beginning. This falls within the statement:

“The Lord is free of anything that entails temporal origination or imperfection.”

An imperfection contrary to His attributes of perfection necessarily entails that whatever possesses it has a temporal beginning. Temporal origination, in turn, necessarily entails the imperfection inherent in created beings. Every created being depends upon another, comes into existence after not having existed, knows only what it has been taught, and can do only what it has been enabled to do. It is encompassed and subject to another’s power.

These imperfections, inherent in every created being, necessarily accompany temporal origination: wherever there is temporal origination, they are present. Temporal origination likewise necessarily accompanies them: wherever there is a temporally originated being, these imperfections are present. Thus, our statement is true:

“The Lord is free of anything that entails imperfection or temporal origination.”

---

## Page 8435

Temporal origination and the imperfection inherent in created beings necessarily accompany one another. The Lord is free of each on two grounds: each is intrinsically impossible for Him, and each necessarily entails the other, which is likewise intrinsically impossible for Him. Thus each, considered in two respects, is both evidence and something established by evidence, showing that the Lord is free of it and of what it implies—its necessary concomitant.

Need of another and dependence upon another necessarily entail temporal origination and the imperfection inherent in created beings. I use the qualification “inherent” to encompass all created beings. Otherwise, some imperfections characterize certain created beings but not others, and are therefore not inherent in every created being. The Lord is free of these as well. Yet if He is free of the imperfection inherent in every created being, He is all the more certainly free of imperfections peculiar to some created beings. For if a created being is held to be free of an imperfection, the Creator is all the more deserving of being declared free of it. This is the method of arguing “with greater reason,” as indicated by the Quran in several places.

In our response to “The Tadmur Questions,” entitled “Establishing the Affirmation of the Names and Attributes and Explaining the True Reconciliation of Divine Decree and Revealed Law,” we explained that, when declaring the Lord free of something, it is not permissible to rely merely on its absence from revelation and transmitted reports, saying: “Whatever is mentioned in the reports, we affirm; whatever is not mentioned, we do not affirm—rather, we deny it,” and basing our denial on the absence of a report.

---

## Page 8436

Rather, this is mistaken for two reasons:

First: the absence of a transmitted report is merely the absence of one particular proof, and the implication of a proof does not hold in reverse. Thus, if He has not informed us of something, it does not follow that it is nonexistent in reality. Allah has names by which He has named Himself and which He has reserved for Himself in His knowledge of the unseen. Just as affirmation requires evidence, so too does negation. But when no report has been transmitted concerning something and it is not known to be established, one must remain silent about it and not speak about Allah without knowledge.

Second: there are things which no transmitted report excludes from Him or declares Him free of, yet transmitted reports establish that He possesses their opposites, thereby establishing their absence from Him. The governing principle is that He is free of everything that contradicts His attributes of perfection. Both revelation and reason establish this. Whatever is not mentioned in a transmitted report, we deny if we know it to be absent; otherwise, we remain silent about it. We therefore neither affirm nor deny anything without knowledge.

Treating the negation of something—whether an attribute or anything else—as equivalent to negating the evidence for it is a method followed by some scholars of rational inquiry and transmitted reports. It is mistaken unless that evidence is a necessary concomitant of the thing in question. If the necessary concomitant is absent, that which necessarily entails it is also absent. Evidence in general, however, must imply what it establishes, whereas the converse is not required. Thus, the existence of evidence entails the existence of what it establishes, but the implication does not hold in reverse.

---

## Page 8437

Thus, there are three categories: whatever is known to be established is affirmed; whatever is known to be absent is denied; and whatever is not known to be either absent or established is left unaddressed. This is what is required. Remaining silent about something is not the same as asserting definitively that it is absent or established. Whoever makes affirmations only in the terms established by revelation, seeks clarification and further detail when other terms are used, and, if the meaning accords with what revelation affirms, affirms it using the scriptural wording, has held fast to revelation in both wording and meaning. This is the path of those who hold fast to the firmest handhold.

However, one must understand the scriptural evidence, both its chains of transmission and its texts. The authenticity of the Quran’s wording is known; one must therefore understand the ways in which it conveys meaning. As for the Sunnah, one must know what has been authentically established and what is known to be fabricated. For some who affiliated themselves with the Sunnah, revered the Sunnah and revelation, and believed that they had held fast to the Book and the Sunnah in this matter, collected hadiths concerning the divine attributes. Among these were reports known to be fabricated, reports more likely to be fabricated, reports more likely to be authentic, and reports whose status was uncertain. They made these hadiths articles of belief and compiled works on them. Some of them declare those who disagree with what these hadiths indicate to be unbelievers.

At the opposite extreme are those who reject hadith as a whole, and those who say concerning the reports in

---

## Page 8438

the two Sahīh collections and elsewhere: “These are solitary reports that do not yield certain knowledge.”

Others go further, saying: “The Quran conveys meaning through transmitted words, and evidence conveyed through transmitted words does not yield certainty.” They instead place their reliance on what they claim to be rational arguments, although these are false and unsound; some are demonstrably invalid and untrue. These people, too, may declare those who disagree with them unbelievers, just as the former group does. Both approaches are false, even if their adherents do not declare their opponents unbelievers. If someone does declare his opponent an unbeliever, he becomes one of those people of innovation who introduce an innovation and declare anyone who disagrees with them concerning it an unbeliever, as the Khawārij and others have done.

It has been explained at length elsewhere that proofs yielding certain knowledge can never contradict one another. Rational evidence yielding certain knowledge never contradicts revealed evidence yielding certain knowledge, as we have explained in the book *Averting Conflict between Reason and Revelation*.

Al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā mentioned some of these hadiths in his book *Refuting Figurative Interpretation*. These include a lengthy report from Abū Ubayda that he cited concerning the Prophet’s ascension, stating that “Muhammad saw his Lord.” Some of those who maintain that he saw his Lord with his own eyes declare those who disagree with them unbelievers because

---

## Page 8439

they believed that authentic hadiths had been transmitted on this matter, as in the case of Abū al-Hasan ʿAlī ibn Shukr. He is quick to declare anyone who disagrees with him an unbeliever over what he claims to be the Sunnah, although he may himself be mistaken, either because he relies on weak hadiths or because he relies on authentic hadiths that do not support his contention. Even where he is correct concerning the Sunnah, it is not permissible to declare everyone who disagrees with him an unbeliever. Not everyone who errs is an unbeliever, especially in subtle questions over which the Muslim community has disagreed extensively, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

Likewise, Abū ʿAlī al-Ahwāzī compiled a work on the divine attributes in which he brought together both sound and unsound material. The same applies to the material collected by ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn Mandah. Although he is among the most prolific transmitters of hadith, he narrates many weak hadiths without distinguishing the authentic from the weak. He sometimes compiles an entire chapter whose hadiths are all weak, such as those concerning eating clay and other subjects. He also narrates from Abū ʿAlī al-Ahwāzī.

Some of the unusual, fabricated reports he narrated reached Hasan ibn ʿAdī, who based false beliefs upon them and claimed that Allah can be seen directly with one’s own eyes in this world. Those of his followers who hold this view then declare anyone who disagrees with them an unbeliever. As noted above, this is the practice of the people of innovation, as exemplified by the Khawārij.

Among these is the well-known hadith of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khalīfa, who narrates from

---

## Page 8440

ʿUmar, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Abū ʿAbd Allāh Muhammad ibn ʿAbd al-Wāhid al-Maqdisī included it in his “Mukhtār” (Selected Hadiths). Some hadith scholars reject it because of inconsistencies in its transmission, as did Abū Bakr al-Ismāʿīlī, Ibn al-Jawzī and others. Most adherents of the Sunnah, however, accepted it. It contains the statement:

“His Throne—or His Footstool—encompasses the heavens and the earth, and He sits upon it, leaving not even four fingers’ breadth of it to spare—or leaving only four fingers’ breadth of it to spare—and it creaks beneath Him as a new saddle creaks beneath its rider.”

The expression “creaking” also occurs in the hadith of Jubayr ibn Mutʿim transmitted by Abū Dāwūd in his Sunan. Ibn ʿAsākir devoted a treatise to it, basing his criticism chiefly on Ibn Ishāq. Yet this hadith was transmitted by scholars of the Sunnah, such as Ahmad, Abū Dāwūd and others, and everything it contains has corroboration in another narration. The expression “creaking” also occurs in other reports.

Imam Ahmad and others transmitted Ibn Khalīfa’s hadith in an abridged form, and he mentioned that Wakīʿ had narrated it. However, many of those who transmitted it narrated it with the wording, “Only four fingers’ breadth of it remains to spare,” thus making the Throne extend four fingers’ breadth beyond Him. Al-Qādī held, as did Ibn

---

## Page 8441

al-Zāghūnī and others like them, that this wording was authentic. They therefore accepted it as transmitted and explained its meaning by saying that His rising over the Throne does not extend to that portion.

It is reported that Ibn al-‘Āyidh said: “It is the place where Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, will sit.”

Ibn Jarīr al-Tabarī transmitted the hadith in his Tafsīr, as did others, with the wording:

“He indeed sits upon it, and not even four fingers’ breadth of it remains to spare.”

This wording is negative. Even if the only difficulty in the hadith were the discrepancy between its two versions—one denying what the other affirms—it would still be impossible to state categorically that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, intended the affirmative meaning: that four fingers’ breadth of the Throne remains to spare, over which the Lord does not rise. This is a peculiar interpretation, with no corroboration whatsoever in any of the narrations. Rather, it implies that the Throne is greater and larger than the Lord. This is false and contrary to the Quran, the Sunnah and reason.

It also implies that one recognizes the Lord’s greatness only by magnifying the created Throne, while making the Throne greater than Him. Thus, the Lord would be deemed great only by comparison with a created thing that is greater than the Lord. This is an unsound meaning, contrary to what is known from the Quran, the Sunnah and reason. The Quran’s approach in this regard is to demonstrate the Lord’s greatness: He is greater than everything whose greatness one recognizes. It therefore mentions the greatness of created things and explains that the Lord is greater than they are.

---

## Page 8442

as in the other hadith recorded in the Sunan collections of Abū Dāwūd and al-Tirmidhī and elsewhere—the hadith concerning the creaking of the Throne—when the Bedouin said:

“‘We seek Allah’s intercession with you, and your intercession with Allah Most High.’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, glorified Allah until its effect could be seen on his Companions’ faces. Then he said: ‘Woe to you! Do you know what you are saying? Do you know who Allah is? Allah’s majesty is far greater than that. His Throne is above His heavens like this’—and he gestured with his hand to indicate a dome—‘and indeed it creaks under Him as a new saddle creaks under its rider.’”

He thus explained the greatness of the Throne and that it is above the heavens like a dome. He then explained how small it is before Allah’s greatness, and that it creaks under Him as a new saddle creaks under its rider. This affirms the greatness of the Throne while showing that the Lord is greater still.

Likewise, it is reported in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Are you astonished at Saʿd’s jealousy? I am more jealous than he, and Allah is more jealous than I.”

He also said:

“No one is more jealous than Allah. For that reason, He has forbidden indecencies, both open and secret.”

There are many such examples. This and other evidence indicate that the correct version of the report is the negative one: it mentions the greatness of the Throne, yet despite that greatness, the Lord has risen over all of it, with not even four fingers’ breadth remaining to spare.

This is the lower limit of the bodily measures used to measure area, just as a corresponding measure is used in weighing. Thus one says: “There is not a palm-sized patch of cloud in the sky.” People measure dimensions by the armspan and the cubit, and the smallest measure they have is

---

## Page 8443

the palm. Thus, when they wish to deny the presence of any amount, small or great, they use this measure, saying: “There is not a palm-sized patch of cloud in the sky.” This is like expressions of comprehensive negation such as:

“Indeed, Allah does not wrong even an atom’s weight,”

and:

“They do not possess even the thin membrane of a date stone,”

and similar expressions. The Messenger thus explained that none of the Throne remains to spare—not even this slight amount, four fingers’ breadth, which is the smallest measure used. This is a sound meaning, consistent with the Arabic language, with what the Book and the Sunnah indicate, and with the Messenger’s manner of explaining things; it also has supporting evidence. It can therefore be affirmed with certainty that this is what the hadith says.

Those who said, “Nothing remains to spare except four fingers’ breadth,” did not understand this meaning. They thought that he had made an exception, so they introduced an exception and were mistaken. Rather, it reinforces the negation and confirms that it is comprehensive. Otherwise, what wisdom would there be in leaving four fingers’ breadth of the Throne unoccupied? These fingers are human fingers, for that is what the expression is understood to mean. Why, then, would the Lord not have risen over this slight portion, when the Throne is small before the greatness of Allah Most High?

There is also a hadith narrated by Ibn Abī Hātim concerning His words, “Vision cannot encompass Him.” Its meaning has supporting evidence that points to this. We should therefore examine the hadith and establish the agreement between the Book and the Sunnah. Such is the matter, and Allah knows best.

---

## Page 8444

He said: Abū Zur‘ah narrated to us: Minjāb ibn al-Hārith narrated to us: Bishr ibn ‘Umārah informed us, from Abū Rawq, from ‘Atiyyah al-‘Awfī, from Abū Sa‘īd al-Khudrī, that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said concerning His words, Most High:

“Vision cannot encompass Him, but He encompasses all vision”:

“If the jinn, humankind, devils and angels, from the time they were created until they ceased to exist, were arranged in a single row, they would never encompass Allah.”

This has supporting evidence, such as the reports in the authentic collections explaining His words, Most High:

“The whole earth will be in His grasp on the Day of Resurrection, and the heavens will be rolled up in His right hand.”

Ibn ‘Abbās said: “The seven heavens, the seven earths and all who are in them are, in the hand of the Most Merciful, no more than a mustard seed in the hand of one of you.”

It is known that the Throne does not reach this degree, for it has bearers and there is space around it. Allah Most High says:

“Those who bear the Throne and those around it.”

This has been discussed at length elsewhere, in “The Question of Encompassing” and other discussions. And Allah knows best.

Section

Thus, the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained the principles that lead to the truth

---

## Page 8445

in the best possible manner, and explained most clearly the signs pointing to the Creator—glory be to Him—His most beautiful names, His sublime attributes, and His oneness, as has been discussed at length elsewhere.

As for the innovators among the speculative theologians, philosophers, and others like them, they did not establish the truth. Rather, they laid down principles that contradict it. They not only failed to find guidance themselves or direct others to the truth; they also established principles contrary to the truth. Recognizing that these principles contradicted what the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had brought, they gave them precedence over his message.

At times they say, “The Messenger brought imaginative representations”; at others, “He brought teachings requiring figurative interpretation”; and at others, “He brought teachings that leave people in ignorance.”

The philosophers and those who agree with them sometimes say: “He addressed the general public through imaginative representations. He did not intend to tell them things as they really were; rather, he told them the opposite, so that they would imagine what would benefit them.”

This is the position of those who acknowledge that he knew the truth, such as Ibn Sīnā and his like. They say: “What he accomplished through imaginative representations was the utmost that could be achieved.”

Others among them say: “He did not know the truth; rather, he imagined things and made others imagine them,” as al-Fārābī and his like maintain. They regard the philosopher as superior to the prophet and consider prophethood to be a kind of dreaming.

---

## Page 8446

As for most speculative theologians, they say:

“Rather, he intended to convey nothing but the truth, yet used expressions that do not indicate it on their own, but require figurative interpretation. This was to spur people to discover the truth through reflection and reason, and to interpret his words, thereby increasing their reward.”

The heretics follow the path of figurative interpretation and open the door to Qarmatianism. These people permit figurative interpretation among the elite.

As for those who advocate imaginative representations, they say:

“The elite already know that his intention was to provide imaginative representations for the common people, so figurative interpretation is impossible.”

Both groups follow the approach of restraining the common people from figurative interpretation. The former, however, say: “There is a figurative interpretation that the elite understand.” This is al-Ghazālī’s approach in al-Iljām. He found it objectionable to say: “They lied to serve a beneficial purpose.” Nor does he endorse interpreting actions figuratively, as the Qarmatians do, but rather accounts concerning the angels and the Last Day. A group of philosophers likewise advocates figurative interpretation in these matters. This differs from the approach of those who advocate imaginative representations.

Al-Ghazālī mentioned this position of theirs in al-Ihyā’ when discussing their excesses in figurative interpretation. He also mentioned it in several other places, as quoted from his writings in al-Sab‘īniyya and elsewhere.

---

## Page 8447

The third group consists of those who say:

“Only Allah knows the meaning of this, or it has an interpretation contrary to its apparent meaning that only Allah knows.”

These people thus regard the Messenger and everyone else as not knowing what Allah revealed. They do not permit figurative interpretation because, in their view, knowledge of the intended meaning is impossible. Nor do they consider it permissible to adopt the approach of imaginative representations, since it explicitly attributes lying to the Messenger.

Rather, they say:

“They were addressed in words they did not understand so that they might be rewarded for reciting them and believing in their wording, even without understanding their meaning.”

They regard this as purely an act of worship, following the view of the determinists, who hold that a person may be required to perform acts of worship that bring him no benefit, yet be rewarded for them. These people and the unsoundness of their position have been discussed elsewhere.

The point here is that what led them to this was their assumption that what is known through reason contradicts what the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—conveyed, or the apparent meaning of what he conveyed. This claim has been refuted at length elsewhere, where it has been shown that reason does not contradict revelation and that whatever contradicts revelation is unsound. Beyond this, it has been shown that reason agrees with what the Messenger brought, bears witness to it, and confirms its truth.

It is not merely said that reason does not oppose it; rather, reason agrees with it and confirms its truth. Those people used to say that reason disproves and contradicts it. It was first shown that reason neither disproves nor contradicts it; then, secondly, that reason confirms its truth and agrees with it.

---

## Page 8448

As for these people, it is shown that the arguments with which they oppose the Messenger are invalid and offer no valid counterargument. It is not enough merely to show that their arguments are invalid and cannot counter his message; they also contradict plain reason. They claimed that reason contradicts revelation.

Four points are therefore demonstrated: that reason does not contradict revelation; that reason agrees with it; that the rational arguments they used to oppose revelation are invalid; and that plain reason itself contradicts their position.

Moreover, it is not enough to show that reason refutes the arguments with which they opposed the Messenger. It is also shown that what they presented as proof of the Maker’s existence actually points to His nonexistence. They have advanced an argument that necessarily entails denying the Maker, even though they think they are establishing His existence through it. The point here is that the arguments by which they claimed to establish the Maker’s existence point only to denying and negating Him. It is not merely that their reasoning is invalid and fails to establish the truth; rather, it points to a falsehood that they themselves, along with all other rational people, know to be false.

For this reason, their principles were described as:

“Arranging the principles to belie the Messenger.”

They were also described as:

“Arranging the principles to oppose the Messenger and reason.”

They made these principles foundations for knowledge of the Creator, yet they are principles that contradict knowledge of Him. Knowledge of the Creator cannot, therefore, be complete unless one believes the opposite of these principles. There is a difference between a foundation and proof that necessarily lead to knowledge of the Lord, and something that contradicts and opposes knowledge of Him.

---

## Page 8449

Thus, the philosophers claim to have established the Necessary Existent. Yet they have not established His existence; rather, their arguments imply that His existence is impossible. The Jahmiyyah, the Mu'tazilah, and others like them claim to have established the eternal One who originates all that comes into being. Yet they have not established His existence; rather, their arguments imply that nothing eternal exists at all. The same applies to the Ash'ariyyah, the Karrāmiyyah, and others who claim to have established knowledge of the Creator: they have not done so; rather, their arguments imply that there is no Creator. These are the three designations they put forward: the Necessary Existent, the Eternal One, and the Maker or Creator, and similar names.

Now, it is self-evident to reason that there must exist a being whose existence is necessary in itself, who is beginningless and eternal, and who originates all that comes into being. Since this is known through our innate disposition, self-evident knowledge, and conclusive proofs, while the principles with which they opposed the Messenger contradict it, this demonstrates that those principles are invalid, both as a whole and in every detail. We have explained elsewhere that acknowledging the Maker is innate and self-evident, notwithstanding the abundance of evidence and proofs for His existence.

Here we say:

There is no doubt that we witness things coming into being, such as clouds, rain, crops, trees, and the sun, as well as the coming into being of human beings and other animals

---

## Page 8450

and the coming into being of night and day, and other such things. It is self-evident to reason that whatever comes into being must have an originator, and that an infinite regress of originated things is impossible: an originated thing has an originator, which in turn has an originator, and so on without end. This is called an infinite regress of causal agents, causes, and efficient causation. It is impossible by the consensus of rational people, as has been explained at length elsewhere, where the objections raised against it were also discussed—including the statements of al-Āmidī and al-Abharī alongside those of al-Rāzī and others. Yet this is self-evident and necessarily known to reason, and those thoughts are Satan’s whisperings. Hence, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed a person who experiences such thoughts to seek refuge in Allah from Satan and to stop pursuing them. He said:

“Satan comes to one of you and asks, ‘Who created such-and-such? Who created such-and-such?’ He replies, ‘Allah.’ Then Satan asks, ‘Who, then, created Allah?’ If any of you experiences this, let him seek refuge in Allah and stop pursuing it.”

It is known that even a single thing cannot come into being without an originator. When things that come into being multiply and form a succession, their need for an originator is all the more evident. They are all originated, so they all need an originator. That dependence can end only with an originator who needs no other, but is eternal and beginningless in Himself—glorified and exalted is He.

If it is said, “Whatever exists is either eternal or originated, and whatever is originated must depend on something eternal; therefore, something eternal must exist in either case,” this is a sound demonstration.

---

## Page 8451

Similarly, if it is said, “Either contingent or necessary,” and “contingent” is explained as meaning that which has come into being, this is an argument of the same kind. But if “contingent” is interpreted in a way that includes the eternal, as Ibn Sīnā and his followers, such as al-Rāzī, did, then this is invalid. For on this interpretation, one cannot establish at the outset the existence of a contingent being dependent on a necessary being, yet the proof cannot be completed without first establishing this. This can only be established on the basis that whatever comes into being must have an originator: its particular instances are witnessed, and its universal principles are known through reason.

As for affirming an eternal, beginningless being that is contingent, rational people unanimously agree that this is impossible. Ibn Sīnā and his followers also agreed that it is impossible, as they stated in their works on logic, following their predecessors. Yet they contradicted themselves at the outset. For both they and their predecessors say:

“What is possible, in the general and specific senses—that which can either exist or not exist—can only be originated; it cannot be necessary. Everything eternal and beginningless is necessary, in their view.”

Similarly, if it is said:

“Whatever exists is either created or uncreated, and whatever is created must depend on something existent that is uncreated; thus, the existence of something uncreated is established in either case.”

Similarly, if it is said:

“Whatever exists is either independent of anything else or dependent on something else, and that which depends on and needs something else can have its need and dependence met only by one who is independent of anything else

---

## Page 8452

and so the existence of one who is independent of anything else follows in either case.”

Similarly, if it is said:

“A living being either has life in its own right or derives its life from another. If its life comes from another, that other must all the more possess life and is therefore living in its own right. Thus, the existence of one who has life in its own right is established in either case.”

Similarly, if it is said:

“One who possesses knowledge either possesses it in his own right or has been taught by another. Whoever teaches another must all the more possess knowledge; and if he has not learned from another, he possesses knowledge in his own right. Thus, the existence of one who possesses knowledge in his own right is established under either of these two exhaustive alternatives, for no third possibility exists. Since one or the other must be the case, and under either alternative there exists one who possesses knowledge in his own right, one who has life in his own right, one who is independent in his own right, and one who is eternal and necessarily existent in his own right, it follows that such a being exists in reality and that his nonexistence is impossible in reality. This is what was to be proved.”

Similarly, if it is said:

“One who possesses power either possesses it in his own right or has been granted it by another. Whoever grants another power must all the more possess power. If his power does not come from another, it is inseparable from his very being. Thus, under every alternative, the existence of one who possesses power in his own right is established—one whose power is inseparable from his very being, whose knowledge is inseparable from his very being, and whose life is inseparable from his very being.”

---

## Page 8453

Similarly, one who possesses wisdom either possesses it in his own right or derives it from another. Whoever makes another wise must all the more possess wisdom. Thus, the existence of one who possesses wisdom in his own right follows under either alternative.

Similarly, if it is said:

“One who is merciful either possesses mercy in his own right or has been made merciful by another. Whoever makes another merciful must all the more be merciful, and his mercy is inseparable from his very being. Thus, under either alternative, the existence of one who is merciful in his own right, whose mercy is inseparable from his very being, is established.”

Similarly, if it is said:

“One who is generous and beneficent either possesses generosity and beneficence in his own right or derives them from another. Whoever makes another generous and beneficent must all the more be generous and beneficent, with these qualities inseparable from his very being.”

An authentic report relates the following about the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“He saw a woman among the captives who, whenever she saw an infant, would nurse it out of compassion. He said, ‘Do you think this woman would throw her child into the Fire?’

“They replied, ‘No, O Messenger of Allah.’

“He said, ‘Allah is more merciful to His servants than this woman is to her child.’”

He thus made clear that Allah is more merciful to His servants than even the most compassionate mother is to her child. For the One who made her merciful is more merciful than she is. This is also indicated by His words, “And your Lord is the Most Generous,” and by our saying, “Allah is greater.”

---

## Page 8454

For He, glorified be He, is the Most Merciful of those who show mercy, the best of those who forgive, the best of judges, the best of helpers, and the best of creators. He is the best Trustee, the best Protector, and the best Helper. This entails that He deserves unqualified praise for these qualities, that He suffices whoever relies upon Him, and that He graciously protects His servant and grants him mighty support. This entails that He is superior to and more perfect than everything besides Him, as is also indicated by our saying, “Allah is greater.”

Similarly, it may be said:

One who speaks, hears, and sees either possesses speech, hearing, and sight in his own right, or has been made able to hear, see, and speak by another. Whoever makes another able to speak, hear, and see must all the more possess speech, hearing, and sight himself. Otherwise, the one acted upon would be more perfect than the one acting, for these are attributes of perfection.

Likewise, it may be said:

One who is just and truthful either possesses justice and truthfulness in his own right, or has been made truthful and just by another. Whoever makes another truthful and just must all the more be truthful and just himself. All these are sound and clear lines of reasoning.

If it is objected:

This may be countered by saying: Whoever makes another unjust or untruthful must likewise be unjust and untruthful, and Ahl al-Sunnah say that He has made others so

---

## Page 8455

yet He Himself is not so—glorified be He.

The reply is: This is invalid on two grounds.

First, not everyone who causes another to possess a quality, whatever that quality may be, possesses it himself. Rather, whoever causes another to possess an attribute of perfection must all the more possess that attribute himself. As for attributes of deficiency, causing another to be deficient does not entail being deficient oneself. Someone with power can render another powerless without himself being powerless. Someone living can kill another and cause his death without himself being dead. Someone knowledgeable can render another ignorant without himself being ignorant. Someone who hears, sees, and speaks can render another blind, deaf, and mute without himself being so. Thus, causing another to be unjust and untruthful does not entail being untruthful and unjust oneself, for these are attributes of deficiency.

If it is objected that an untruthful and unjust person may sometimes compel another to be truthful and just, the reply is: He has not made him truthful and knowledgeable; he has merely commanded him to be so, and the other person has done so himself.

We did not say that everyone who commands another to do something himself possesses the quality he commands the other to have.

Second, injustice is a relative and relational matter. Thus, if someone commands another to kill a person

---

## Page 8456

and the killer kills him without knowing him to have committed any crime, the killer acts unjustly—even if the person giving the order did so only because the victim had killed his father, while the person carrying out the order did not act for that reason. Had he acted on the other’s behalf, he would not have been unjust. If, however, he had some personal motive concerning the victim and killed him unjustly, the person giving the order was nevertheless entitled to have him killed.

The same applies when someone commands another to utter something that, as spoken by the latter, is false—as when Joseph instructed the herald to say:

“O caravan, you are indeed thieves!”

Joseph, peace be upon him, meant: “You indeed stole Joseph from his father,” and in this he was truthful. The person receiving the instruction meant: “You indeed stole the measuring cup.” He thought they had stolen it, so he was not deliberately lying, even though his statement was false.

The actions of the Lord, exalted be He, cannot be measured against those of His servants. Everything He creates serves a wise purpose and a benefit, even if some of what He creates contains evil. Thus, He creates foul entities, such as impure substances and devils, for an overriding wise purpose. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that the proofs establishing the Lord’s existence are exceedingly numerous. Those who claim that reason contradicts what the Messenger has reported, and who say they have established the existence of the Necessary Existent, the Eternal, or the Maker, have not established His existence. Rather, their arguments entail denying His existence and divesting Him of His attributes. They therefore deny Him rather than affirm Him. Their arguments are invalid

---

## Page 8457

by rational standards, not sound by those standards. Knowledge of Allah does not depend on their principles. Rather, complete knowledge depends on recognizing the invalidity of their principles. Even if they call them “the foundations of knowledge and religion,” they are “the foundations of ignorance and of Satan’s religion, not the religion of the Most Merciful.” Their discourse really amounts to “setting out principles in opposition to the Messenger and reason.” As the inhabitants of the Fire said:

“If only we had listened or reasoned, we would not be among the inhabitants of the Blaze.”

Whoever opposes the Messenger therefore opposes both revelation and reason: he opposes both scriptural and rational proofs.

As for those who affirm the Necessary Existent, we have shown in several places that they have offered no proof of the Necessary Existent, and that when al-Rāzī followed Ibn Sīnā, his books contained no proof of the Necessary Existent. For they made establishing His existence dependent on establishing “the contingent,” a category in which they include the eternal. Thus, their method leaves no way to establish the Necessary Existent except by establishing an eternal contingent being. Yet this is impossible according to self-evident reason and the consensus of rational people. Their method therefore depends on a premise that clear reason shows to be false, and whose falsity rational people unanimously acknowledge. Their proof is consequently invalid. This is why their discussion of “the contingent” is utterly inconsistent. They were, however, able to argue that whatever comes into existence must have an eternal cause, and this is

---

## Page 8458

the Necessary Existent. Yet they have also affirmed something eternal that is not necessarily existent. What they affirmed concerning the eternal thus contradicts its being the Lord of all worlds, for they affirmed an eternal category comprising both the necessary and the non-necessary.

Moreover, they say that the Necessary Existent they have affirmed cannot possess any positive attribute. Such a being cannot possibly be necessary, let alone necessarily existent. This has been explained at length elsewhere, where it has been shown that they describe their supposed Necessary Existent as neither an attribute nor anything possessing attributes whatsoever. This can only be imagined in the mind; it has no reality outside it.

If “the Necessary Existent” is understood to mean the Originator of contingent beings, this is true, and the designation refers to the Essence possessing its attributes. If it is understood to mean the self-existent being that has no agent causing it, then both the Essence and the attributes are necessary. If it is understood to mean that which has neither an agent nor an originator, then the Essence is necessary, but the attributes are not necessary. If it is understood to mean that which is neither an attribute nor anything possessing attributes, this is false and has no reality. Indeed, its existence is impossible, neither contingent nor necessary. The further they go in stripping it of attributes, the more deeply they plunge into the denial of divine attributes, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

As for those who claim to have established the existence of the Eternal—the Jahmiyyah, the Muʿtazilah, and those Ashʿarīs and Karrāmīs who followed their path, arguing from the coming into existence of accidents

---

## Page 8459

and their inseparability from bodies, and from the impossibility of a beginningless succession of events, that bodies came into existence—they have not established the existence of the Maker, given the known invalidity of this argument. For they claim that the Lord cannot speak by His will or do whatever He wills. Indeed, their position ultimately implies that He cannot have always possessed power. Their arguments for this impossibility have been presented in full elsewhere, along with their own statements demonstrating the invalidity of those arguments.

As for their denial of the Creator, the implication of their position is that any being who has always spoken at will must have a beginning. It would therefore follow that the Lord has a beginning and is not eternal. Indeed, their fundamental principle implies that anything in which attributes and acts subsist must have come into existence. Every existent must possess attributes and acts, so it would follow that every existent came into existence.

For this reason, the leading proponents of this approach—the Jahmiyyah and the Muʿtazilah—explicitly denied the Lord’s attributes and denied that acts or any other matters subject to His will subsist in His Essence, since this is what their argument entails. Yet these attributes necessarily belong to Him, and denying a necessary concomitant entails denying that which it necessarily accompanies. Thus, their position ultimately amounts to negating the Lord and denying His existence.

They call attributes “accidents,” and acts and the like “events.” They therefore said, “The Lord must be held to transcend having accidents and events subsist in Him, for that would entail His being a body.”

They said:

“We have established a proof that every body came into existence. For

---

## Page 8460

a body is never free of accidents that come into existence, nor does it precede them. Whatever is inseparable from events and does not precede them must itself have come into existence.”

Yet scriptural and rational proofs have established the position of the early generations: that the Lord has always spoken whenever He wills. Their position would therefore entail that He neither preceded events nor was ever separate from them. According to their position, He must consequently have come into existence. Thus, the very principle by which they established the existence of the Eternal entails that He is not eternal and that nothing eternal exists. Likewise, the others’ principle entails that He is not necessary in Himself and that nothing necessary in itself exists. The approach by which they claim to establish the Maker’s existence contradicts the affirmation of His existence.

If they say, “Knowledge of the Maker is possible only through this approach,” the correct response is: “Rather, complete knowledge of the Maker is possible only with knowledge of the invalidity of this approach.” Hence, everyone who accepts its validity has denied some of what the Messenger reported concerning matters that necessarily belong to the Lord. Denying a necessary concomitant entails denying that which it necessarily accompanies.

Those who claim to use this as proof that bodies came into existence argue along the same lines as those others who claim to use it as proof that bodies are contingent. Both arguments are invalid.

---

## Page 8461

And the implication is that everything that exists came into existence and is contingent, and that nothing in existence is eternal or necessary in itself. Thus, their principles contradict the conclusion they seek to establish. This approach misleads rather than guides. But it is as Allah Most High says:

“And whoever turns away from the remembrance of the Most Merciful, We appoint for him a devil who becomes his companion.”

“And indeed, they turn them away from the path, while they think they are rightly guided.”

As for those who say, “We affirm the Maker and Creator,” and maintain that they follow another approach, such as inferring the Lord’s existence from attributes coming into existence—this does indeed point to Him without requiring the commitments made by the others. Al-Rāzī mentioned this approach. Al-Ashʿarī himself, however, did not use it. Rather, in al-Lumaʿ and his Epistle to al-Thaghr, he inferred from events that the things in which they inhere came into existence, as in his discussion of the sperm-drop, on the premise that a beginningless sequence of events is impossible. He then took the coming into existence of those substances—which he said he had demonstrated—as proof of the Maker’s existence. This approach is invalid, as has already been explained. The other approach, however, is sound, but they undermined it by holding that

---

## Page 8462

the things they observe coming into existence are accidents alone, as we have explained in several places.

It may then be said: These people affirm a Creator who performs no act of creation. Yet this is self-evidently impossible to reason. Thus, they have not affirmed a Creator.

Although the Karrāmiyyah say that the act of creation is distinct from the created thing, they maintain that the act of creation comes into existence without any cause necessitating its occurrence. This too is impossible. Thus, they have not affirmed a Creator.

Furthermore, both groups say: “What necessitates the selection of what comes into existence, rather than something else, is an eternal, beginningless will.”

The Karrāmiyyah say: “It is this will that determines what inheres in Him and what He creates.” According to the others, however, nothing willed inheres in Him. Rather, they say: “It is this will that determines what comes into existence.”

Both groups, and those who agree with them, say: “That will is eternal and beginningless, having always remained in one and the same state; then events came into existence without any cause whatsoever.”

They also say: “It is in its nature to select one of two equivalent alternatives over the other, and to precede what is willed from all eternity.” Thus, they ascribe to the will three invalid attributes, although sound reason plainly establishes that a will cannot be like this. Yet it is the will that brings about creation and coming into existence. If it is affirmed as they describe it, there can be neither creation nor coming into existence.

---

## Page 8463

Likewise, they have described the power they affirm in terms that make it impossible for it to be power at all. Yet power is a condition of creation. If they negate a condition of creation, creation itself is negated, and He is no longer a Creator. Thus, what they attribute to the Creator contradicts His being a Creator; it is not a necessary consequence of His being a Creator. Yet they have made it a necessary consequence rather than a contradiction.

As for the will, they ascribe to it three necessary characteristics, all of which contradict its being a will. They say that it exists without anything willed by it existing—indeed, that this has always been the case—and then what it wills comes into existence without any change in the will itself. The invalidity of this is self-evident to reason. For when an agent wills to act, what preceded was a resolve to act and an intention to do so at a future time, not a will to perform the act immediately. Rather, when he acts, there must be a will to act at that moment.

Hence the saying:

“Prior volition is resolve; concurrent volition is intention.”

It is therefore impossible for an act to occur through resolve alone, without a fresh intention arising in the agent. Thus, created things could not come into being through such a will, even supposing that events could arise without a cause. How much more so when that too is inherently impossible? Thus, the impossibility arises both from the will and from its being determined by something that is inherently impossible.

The second is their claim that the will selects one of two equivalent alternatives over the other. This is a denial of the obvious. Rather, the will can only be directed towards something whose existence the agent prefers to its nonexistence, either because he knows it to be better or because his love for it is stronger. And in his knowledge it is preferable only because

---

## Page 8464

its outcome is better. No one, then, does anything voluntarily unless he loves what he wills or loves what it leads to, such that its existence is dearer to him than its nonexistence. Its existence and nonexistence are not equal in his regard.

The third is their claim that a resolute will may fail to bring about what is willed despite the presence of power.

This too is false. Rather, whenever complete power and resolute will are present, what lies within that power must come into existence. Where this does not necessarily follow, it is only because of deficient power or the absence of complete will. As for the Lord, Exalted is He, whatever He wills comes to pass, and whatever He does not will does not come to pass. In several places, He declares that, had He willed, He would have done things that He has not done, as He says:

“Had We willed, We could have given every soul its guidance.”

“Had your Lord willed, He would have made mankind one community.”

“Had Allah willed, they would not have fought one another.”

He thus makes clear that, were He to will that, He would have the power to do it. Yet He does not do it because He has not willed it, since not willing it is more consonant with wisdom, even though He would have the power to do it were He to will it. What these people and others say about power and will has been discussed at length elsewhere.

Some of these people say that His power extends only to things distinct from Him, not to acts subsisting in Himself. This is the position of the Mu‘tazilah, the Jahmiyyah, and those among the Ash‘ariyyah and others who agree with them.

Others say that His power extends both to acts subsisting in Him and to things distinct from Him, as is reported of the Karrāmiyyah.

---

## Page 8465

The correct view, as indicated by the Quran and reason, is that His power extends to both. The Most High says:

“Yes, We are able even to restore his fingertips.”

He also says:

“Is He not able to give life to the dead?”

“Is not He who created the heavens and the earth able to create the like of them?”

“And We are certainly able to take it away.”

Such statements occur frequently in the Quran, more frequently than the other kind. What the Karrāmiyyah and Hishāmiyyah say is closer to reason and revelation than what the Jahmiyyah and those who agree with them say, although the position reported of them is mistaken insofar as they deny His power over things distinct from Him. Allah Most High has declared that He has power over all things.

It is related in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to Abū Mas‘ūd when he saw him beating his slave: “Allah has greater power over you than you have over this slave.”

And in the Quran:

“Even if We take you away, We will surely exact retribution from them.”

“Or if We show you what We have promised them, We certainly have full power over them.”

This is discussed at length elsewhere. Everything the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has reported is necessarily true in reality.
