Every attribute he has affirmed of the Lord necessarily belongs to Him. If its absence is posited, the absence of that which entails it necessarily follows. Thus, denying what the Messenger has reported entails negation of the divine attributes. Yet some of this is evident through reason—though people differ in their rational capacities—and some of it

---

## Page 8466

requires only the Messenger’s report. For whatever the Messenger has reported is true. Everything he has affirmed of the Lord necessarily belongs to Him, and whatever he has denied of Him is necessarily absent from Him. Thus, if the absence of a necessary consequence is posited, the absence of that which entails it necessarily follows.

All of this, however, is an implication of their position and demonstrates its falsity. Yet an implication of a position need not itself be a position held by its adherents. Indeed, most people make statements without accepting their implications. Thus, if someone says something that entails negation of the divine attributes, it does not follow that he believes in negating them. Rather, he may believe in affirming them without recognizing that implication.

Likewise, if the premises upon which they base their proof of the Creator are false, it does not follow that they themselves do not acknowledge the Creator, even if this is an implication of their position. If they say that He can be known only through this method, and its invalidity has become apparent, it follows that He cannot be known. Yet this implication demonstrates the falsity of their denial; it does not follow that they themselves do not acknowledge the Creator. For, as we have explained elsewhere, acknowledging the Creator, knowing Him, loving Him, and affirming His oneness are innate: they may be firmly established in a person’s heart while he thinks they are not there.

For this reason, the great majority of these people already acknowledged and recognized the Creator before pursuing this method of rational inquiry, whether it was sound or unsound. And this

---

## Page 8467

is something they know from their own experience. It is therefore clear that not following this method does not entail a lack of knowledge. Many of them have acknowledged this, as we have explained elsewhere.

Some of them say that the method of rational inquiry they follow has increased their insight and knowledge. Ibn Hazm and others say this. He followed the argument based on accidents.

Many people say that this method has brought them nothing but doubt and uncertainty. These people have a sounder innate disposition, for such methods are unsound.

Others say: “I gained nothing from it—neither knowledge nor doubt.” This is because it yielded them no knowledge, nor did they accept it; thus neither its soundness nor its unsoundness became clear to them. Some people do not understand what they mean by it. Most of their followers do not understand it either; rather, they follow them out of unquestioning acceptance and a favorable opinion of them.

Section

It should also be understood that we do not say that a thing can be known only by affirming all its necessary consequences. No rational person says this. Indeed, most things may be known even though many

---

## Page 8468

of their necessary consequences remain unknown. Muslims may know that the Lord has power over all things and does whatever He wills, while remaining unaware of many necessary consequences of His power and will. Yet those who adhere to the right path do not deny these consequences simply because they do not know them, for denying them is an error. Not knowing them all, however, is inevitable for everyone. Glory be to Him whose knowledge encompasses all things and who has numbered everything precisely. As for all besides Him:

“They encompass nothing of His knowledge except what He wills.”

And He, glorified be He,

“knows what is before them and what is behind them, but they cannot encompass Him in knowledge.”

The point, however, is to explain that those who oppose the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—even in a single word must have a corresponding measure of error in what they say. It is also to explain that the rational and revealed proofs transmitted from all the prophets agree with what the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—brought, and contradict the claims of the people of innovation who oppose the Book and the Sunnah.

When they say that reason conflicts with revelation, they err on five fundamental points:

First: sound reason does not contradict revelation.

Second: it agrees with revelation.

Third: what they claim to be a rational argument opposing revelation is not sound.

Fourth: the purported rational argument they put forward against revelation is itself contrary to sound reason.

Fifth: the arguments by which they seek to establish the foundations, such as knowledge of the Creator and His attributes, do not establish them; rather, they contradict their establishment.

---

## Page 8469

Section

This is because what the Messenger brought is from Allah’s knowledge. Whatever he reported from Allah was declared by Allah Himself. He, glorified be He, speaks with knowledge; it is impossible for Him to declare anything contrary to His knowledge. Whatever the Messenger commanded comes from Allah’s judgment, and Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise. Allah Most High says:

“But Allah bears witness to what He has sent down to you: He sent it down with His knowledge. The angels also bear witness, and sufficient is Allah as a witness.”

Allah Most High also says:

“Or do they say, ‘He has fabricated it’? Say, ‘Then bring ten fabricated surahs like it, and call upon whomever you can besides Allah, if you are truthful.’”

“But if they do not respond to you, then know that it was sent down with Allah’s knowledge and that there is no deity except Him. Will you then submit?”

Regarding His words, “He sent it down with His knowledge,” al-Zajjāj said: “He sent it down containing His knowledge.” Abū Sulaymān al-Dimashqī said: “He sent it down from His knowledge.” Others have given the same explanation.

This meaning is transmitted from the early generations, as Ibn Abī Hātim narrated from ‘Atā’ ibn al-Sā’ib, who said: “Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān taught me to recite the Quran. Whenever he taught one of us to recite the Quran, he would say, ‘You have received Allah’s knowledge, so no one today is better than you except by virtue of his deeds.’ Then he would recite:

‘He sent it down with His knowledge. The angels also bear witness, and sufficient is Allah as a witness.’”

Likewise, regarding the words of Allah Most High, “Then know that it was sent down with Allah’s knowledge,” they said: “He sent it down containing His knowledge.”

---

## Page 8470

I say:

The preposition bā’ can indicate accompaniment, as when you say, “He came with his masters and children.” Thus, He sent it down containing and bearing His knowledge. Whatever reports it contains are given with Allah’s knowledge, and whatever commands it contains are issued with Allah’s knowledge. This is unlike speech sent down from someone other than Allah. Such speech may be false and unjust, like Musaylimah’s Quran. Or it may be true, yet contain only the knowledge of the created being who uttered it. It indicates Allah Most High’s knowledge only by implication: namely, that Allah knows whatever is true.

The Quran, however, contains Allah’s knowledge directly. He sent it down with His knowledge, not anyone else’s; nor is it speech devoid of knowledge. Since He sent it down with His knowledge, it follows that it is truth from Allah, and that the Messenger is indeed a messenger from Allah, who has made His knowledge clear within it.

Al-Zajjāj said:

A “witness” is one who makes clear what he testifies to. Allah makes that clear and also knows it to be true.

(I say:

Regarding His words, “But Allah bears witness,” His testimony consists in His clarification and disclosure, His demonstration and declaration. The clear signs through which He has demonstrated the Messenger’s truthfulness attest to it. Among them is the Quran, which is testimony in words. It is itself a sign and a miracle attesting to his truthfulness, just as the other signs do. All these signs are testimony from Allah, just like testimony in words, and may be even more compelling. This is why He mentioned this in Sūrat Hūd when He challenged them to produce its like, saying:

---

## Page 8471

“Then bring ten fabricated surahs like it, and call upon whomever you can besides Allah, if you are truthful.”

“But if they do not respond to you, then know that it was sent down with Allah’s knowledge and that there is no god but Him. Will you then submit?”

Their inability to produce anything comparable demonstrates, all the more, the inability of others to do so. It thus becomes clear that all creation is incapable of producing anything comparable to it, and that it is a clear sign attesting to the Messenger’s mission and to Allah’s oneness.

Likewise, His words, “But Allah bears witness to what He has sent down to you,” follow the passage beginning, “We have revealed to you,” and ending, “so that people would have no argument against Allah after the messengers.” It has been reported that some of the disbelievers said, “We do not testify that Muhammad is a messenger,” whereupon Allah Most High said, “But Allah bears witness to what He has sent down to you.”

A better explanation, however, is that when He said, “so that people would have no argument against Allah after the messengers,” He denied that creation had any argument against the Creator. He then stated: Yet Allah’s proof against creation stands established through His testimony to the Messenger’s mission. He bears witness to what He has sent down to you; He sent it down with His knowledge. Thus, creation has no argument against Allah; rather, His is the conclusive proof. It is He who has guided His servants through what He sent down.

In light of the foregoing, His words, “He sent it down with His knowledge,” mean that it contains His knowledge of what has been, what will be, and what He has disclosed. This, too, is evidence that it is true. For if he reports matters of the unseen that none but Allah knows, this indicates that Allah informed him of them.

---

## Page 8472

as in His words:

“Knower of the unseen, He discloses His unseen to no one except a messenger whom He approves,” to the end of the verse.

It has also been said: He sent it down knowing both it and you. Ibn Jarīr al-Tabarī said concerning the verse in Sūrat al-Nisā’: “He sent it down to you knowing that you are His chosen one from among His creation.”

Al-Zajjāj mentioned two interpretations concerning the verse in Sūrat Hūd. The first is: “He sent it down knowing that He was sending it down and knowing that it is the truth from Him.” The second is: “He sent it down containing the unseen matters He disclosed in it, indicating what will happen and what has already happened.”

(I say: This latter interpretation is the one explained above. The first, however, is of the same kind as Ibn Jarīr’s explanation. For He knows it and the one to whom He sent it down; He knows that it is true and that its recipient is worthy of that for which Allah chose him. This would then resemble His words:

“And We certainly chose them, knowingly, above all other peoples,”

and the words of the one who said:

“I was only given it because of knowledge”—

meaning: because Allah knows that I deserve it.

(I say: This interpretation is included in the meaning of the first explanation. For when speech is sent down with the Lord’s knowledge, this entails that everything it contains comes from His knowledge, including its statements concerning itself and the Messenger. This is the correct interpretation, held by the majority; some of them mentioned no other.

---

## Page 8473

The first interpretation, although correct in meaning, is only part of this explanation. As for taking the second to be what the verse means, that is mistaken, because the fact that the Lord—glorified is He—knows something does not indicate that it is either praiseworthy or blameworthy. He knows all things. No one would say that He sent it down without knowing it. Yet someone might suppose that He sent it down without His knowledge—meaning that it does not contain His knowledge—and that it was sent down by Satan, as the Most High says:

“Shall I inform you upon whom the devils descend? They descend upon every sinful liar.”

He Himself sends the devils forth and causes them to descend, but the speech they bring is not sent down from Him, nor is it sent down with Allah’s knowledge. Rather, it is sent down bearing the lies and other things the devils utter. This is why, whenever He—glorified is He—mentions the Quran being sent down, He specifies that it is sent down from Him, as in His words:

“The revelation of the Book is from Allah.”

“Those to whom We have given the Scripture know that it has been sent down from your Lord in truth.”

“Say: The Holy Spirit has brought it down from your Lord in truth.”

This is among the evidence used by Imam Ahmad and other leading scholars of the Sunnah to establish that the Quran is Allah’s speech, not something created that He brought into being in a locus other than Himself. Had that been the case, it would have been sent down from that locus, not from Allah. He also stated that it was sent down with Allah’s knowledge, that it is from Allah’s knowledge, and that Allah’s knowledge is uncreated.

---

## Page 8474

And Ahmad said:

“Allah’s speech is from Allah; it is not a part of Him.”

For this reason, the early generations said:

“The Quran is Allah’s speech, sent down and uncreated. From Him it began, and to Him it returns.”

They said, “From Him it began”—it did not begin from anyone other than Him, as the Jahmiyyah claim. They say that it began from the locus in which it was created. This has been discussed at length elsewhere. The point is that if it contains His knowledge, then it is true, whereas the discourse used to oppose it contradicts Allah’s knowledge and is therefore false. It is like the association of partners with Allah, concerning which Allah Most High says:

“They worship besides Allah that which neither harms nor benefits them, and say, ‘These are our intercessors with Allah.’ Say, ‘Are you informing Allah of something He does not know in the heavens or on earth?’ Glory be to Him! Exalted is He above what they associate with Him.”

Section

The principle I have stated—that the fundamentals of religion must be referred back to the Book and the Sunnah—makes the truth clear through both scriptural and rational proofs. As I have explained, the Book sets out the rational proofs through which truths concerning Allah are known, and explains what establishes the Messenger’s truthfulness in everything he says. It also makes clear that the expression “reason and transmitted revelation” has come to be used ambiguously. For everyone who

---

## Page 8475

puts forward something based on his own opinion calls it “rational arguments,” while another exposes his errors, likewise claims to rely on reason, and presents other arguments that are also mistaken, as has been explained at length elsewhere. This is comparable to someone who argues from transmitted revelation using weak or fabricated hadiths, or authentic texts that do not establish what he seeks to prove.

Many speculative theologians treat the Quran and hadiths as evidence solely on the basis of their being reports. Yet it is well known that such reports yield knowledge only once the truthfulness of the one conveying them has been established. They therefore find themselves compelled to make rational knowledge the foundation, as do Abū al-Ma‘ālī, Abū Hāmid, al-Rāzī, and others.

The leading speculative theologians acknowledge that the Quran sets out rational proofs, as al-Ash‘arī and others mention, along with ‘Abd al-Jabbār ibn Ahmad and other Mu‘tazilites. Yet they sometimes present arguments that they identify as the Quran’s proofs when they are not actually the same. Al-Ash‘arī did this in al-Luma‘ and elsewhere, where he argued from the creation of the human being and cited Allah’s words:

“Have you considered the semen you emit? Is it you who create it, or are We the Creator?”

However, he assumes that the sperm-drop contains enduring substances, and that its transition through

---

## Page 8476

accidental states indicates that it came into existence. He thus argues that the substances of the sperm-drop came into existence. This, however, is neither the method of the Quran nor that of most rational people. Rather, they know that the sperm-drop comes into existence after not having existed, through transformation from human blood, and that it is then transformed into a lump of flesh. They know that Allah creates this second substance from the first material through transformation and causes the first material to cease to exist. Its individual substances do not remain perpetually unchanged, as explained earlier.

Those who examine the Quran fall into three ranks: some disregard its rational proofs; some acknowledge them but misunderstand them; and some understand them correctly. Likewise, they fall into three groups regarding what it conveys through reports: some say that it does not indicate the revealed attributes; some cite it as evidence for what it does not indicate; and some cite it as evidence for what it does indicate.

Al-Ash‘arī and those like him occupy an intermediate position between the early generations and the Jahmiyyah. They took sound statements from the former, and from the latter rational principles that they thought sound, though they were unsound. Some people were drawn to him because of his adherence to the early generations, while others were drawn to him because of his innovated Jahmī tendencies, such as Abū al-Ma‘ālī and his followers. Others followed their approach, such as the leading scholars among their adherents, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

For the point here is that taking the Quran as the guide to be followed in the foundations of religion

---

## Page 8477

and its branches is the religion of Islam. This is the way of the Companions, those who followed them in righteousness, and the leading scholars of the Muslims. They never accepted anyone’s opposing the Quran with a rational argument or opinion to which he gave precedence over it. Rather, if someone encountered a difficulty, he would ask until the correct understanding became clear to him. For this reason, Imam Ahmad wrote Refutation of the Heretics and the Jahmiyyah concerning Their Doubts about Unclear Passages of the Quran and Their Misinterpretation of Them. Likewise, the four imams and others referred questions concerning Allah’s oneness and attributes to the Quran and the Messenger, not to anyone’s opinion, reasoning, or analogy.

Al-Awzāʿī said:
“When the Successors were still numerous, we used to say: Allah is above His Throne, and we believe in those attributes of His that are reported in the Sunnah.”

Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal said:
“Allah is to be described only as He has described Himself and as His Messenger has described Him. One must not go beyond the Quran and hadith.”

Al-Shāfiʿī said in the introduction to al-Risālah (The Epistle):
“Praise be to Allah, who is as He has described Himself and above whatever descriptions His creatures give of Him.”

Mālik said:
“Istiwā (rising above) is known, its manner is unknown, and belief in it is obligatory

---

## Page 8478

and asking about it is an innovation.” He disapproved of the newly introduced speculative theology.

It is reported from him and from Abū Yūsuf:
“Whoever seeks religion through speculative theology falls into heresy.”

Al-Shāfiʿī said:
“My judgment concerning the practitioners of speculative theology is that they should be beaten with palm branches and sandals, paraded through the marketplaces, and it should be proclaimed: ‘This is the recompense of those who abandon the Book and the Sunnah and turn to speculative theology.’”

He also said:
“I have discovered things among the practitioners of speculative theology that I had never imagined. For a servant to be afflicted with every sin except associating partners with Allah is better for him than to be afflicted with speculative theology.”

His statements and those of others have been explained at length in several places. It has been clarified that what they meant by speculative theology was the theology of the Jahmiyyah, through which they denied the divine attributes and claimed to establish that the universe began to exist—namely, the argument from accidents.

Ahmad also said:
“The scholars of speculative theology are heretics. No one who has clothed himself in speculative theology has ever prospered.”

The statements of ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Abī Salamah al-Mājishūn on this subject are extensive.

The followers of Abū Hanīfah related, through Abū Yūsuf, that Abū Hanīfah said:
“No one should say anything about Allah on the basis of his own opinion. Rather, he should describe Him as He has described Himself.”

Abū Hanīfah also said:
“Two guests came to us from Khurāsān, both astray: the Jahmiyyah and those who liken Allah to His creation.”

---

## Page 8479

Abū ʿIsmah reported:
“I asked Abū Hanīfah, ‘Who belongs to the community?’ He replied, ‘Whoever gives precedence to Abū Bakr and ʿUmar, loves ʿAlī and ʿUthmān, does not prohibit nabīdh (a drink made by steeping ingredients in water) prepared in earthenware jars, declares no one an unbeliever on account of a sin, accepts wiping over leather socks, believes that divine decree—both its good and its evil—is from Allah, and says nothing concerning Allah.’”

Khālid ibn Sabīh narrated that Abū Hanīfah said:
“Adherence to the community consists of seven things: giving precedence to Abū Bakr and ʿUmar, loving ʿUthmān and ʿAlī, offering the funeral prayer for anyone among the people of the qiblah who dies having committed a sin, and saying nothing concerning Allah.”

I say:
His statement in these two reports, “saying nothing concerning Allah,” is explained in the report transmitted by Abū Yūsuf: “One should not say anything concerning Allah on the basis of one’s own opinion; rather, one should describe Him as He has described Himself.” Thus, the imams condemn anyone who speaks about the Lord’s attributes on any basis other than what the Messenger conveyed.

How much more, then, does this apply to those who claim that the Book and the Sunnah do not yield knowledge, and give precedence to their own opinions over them, despite those opinions being unsound in many respects!

Hishām narrated from Muhammad, from Abū Hanīfah and Abū Yūsuf—and this was also Muhammad’s own position—that they said:
“The Sunnah upon which the community’s practice rests is that no one among the people of the qiblah should be declared an unbeliever and excluded from Islam on account of a sin; that a person should not doubt his religion, saying, ‘I do not know whether I am a believer or an unbeliever’; that he should not espouse the doctrine of the Qadariyyah; and that he should not rise up

---

## Page 8480

against the Muslims with the sword; that he should give precedence to those Companions of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—who are to be given precedence, and acknowledge the superiority of those accorded it.”

They related that Abū Yūsuf said:
“The way of the people of the community, as we understand it, and the practice we found among the body of jurists who had not adopted religious innovations or wayward doctrines, is that one should neither revile any of the Companions of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—nor speak of their faults, nor recount the disputes that arose among them, thereby turning people’s hearts against them. One should have no doubt that they are believers. One should not declare any person among the people of the qiblah who professes Islam and believes in the Quran an unbeliever, nor exclude him from faith because of a sin he may have committed. One should neither espouse the doctrine of the Qadariyyah nor engage in religious disputation, for such disputation is among the gravest innovations. This is the position of the people of the Sunnah and the community.

“No one should ask concerning this, ‘How?’ or ‘Why?’ Someone who asks about it should receive no response other than being told not to ask; if he persists, one should refrain from sitting or walking with him. No one among the people of the Sunnah and the community should associate with any follower of wayward doctrines to the point of becoming his companion and confidant, for fear of being led astray, or of leading someone else astray through that association.”

He said:
“Religious disputation is an innovation, and the refutations that followers of wayward doctrines direct against one another are a newly introduced innovation. Had there been any merit in this, the Companions of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and their followers would have preceded others in it, for they were better able to undertake it and had greater insight into it. And He said:

---

## Page 8481

Allah Most High:

‘If they dispute with you, say, “I have submitted myself to Allah, as have those who follow me.”’

He did not command him to engage in disputation. Had He willed, He would have sent down arguments and told him, ‘Say such and such.’”

Abū Yūsuf said:
“Disregard the views of those given to disputation and the followers of doctrinal innovations among the Murji’ah, Rāfidah, Zaydiyyah, Mushabbihah, Shī‘ah, Khawārij, Qadariyyah, Mu‘tazilah, and Jahmiyyah.”

They said:
“It was also related that Muhammad said, ‘Abū Bakr and ‘Umar are superior to ‘Alī.’”

I say: What Abū Yūsuf stated concerning disputation resembles the statements of many leading scholars of the Sunnah, including Imam Ahmad and others. This subject calls for fuller discussion and detailed clarification, for which this is not the place. For this reason, Bishr ibn al-Walīd, a companion of Abū Yūsuf, loved Ahmad and was inclined toward him. Indeed, Abū Yūsuf was more inclined toward hadith than others were. And Allah knows best and is Most Wise.

---

## Page 8482

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

The short surahs at the end of the Quran are interrelated. Sūrat Iqra’ (“Recite”) was the first revelation of the Quran. Hence it opens with the command to recite, closes with the command to prostrate, and speaks in between of prayer. Recitation is prayer’s finest utterance and its first after the opening takbīr; prostration is its finest action and its last before the closing salutation.

Thus, after he was commanded to recite, Sūrat al-Muddaththir (“The Cloaked One”) was revealed to him so that he would convey the message. He was told:

“Arise and warn.”

Through the first he became a prophet, and through the second he became a messenger. Hence he was addressed as “the cloaked one”: someone seeking warmth against the chill of terror and alarm caused by the magnitude of what had suddenly overwhelmed him. He had returned to Khadījah with the muscles between his neck and shoulders trembling, saying, “Cover me! Cover me!” It was thus as though he was being forbidden to seek warmth and commanded to rise and warn. Similarly, in Sūrat al-Muzzammil (“The Enwrapped One”), he was addressed as someone wrapped up for sleep when he was commanded to rise for prayer.

Having commanded recitation in this surah, Allah mentioned in the next the revelation of the Quran on the Night of Decree, and the descent of the angels and the Spirit. In Sūrat al-Ma‘ārij (“The Ways of Ascent”), He mentioned the ascent of the angels and the Spirit, and in Sūrat al-Naba’ (“The Tidings”), the standing of the angels and the Spirit. He thus mentioned ascent, descent, and standing; then

---

## Page 8483

in the following surah, He mentioned its recitation to those being warned, saying:

“Reciting purified scriptures”
“containing upright writings.”

These three surahs thus form a connected treatment of the Quran: the command to recite it, the account of its revelation, and the Messenger’s recitation of it to those being warned. Then Sūrat al-Zalzalah (“The Earthquake”), al-‘Ādiyāt (“The Chargers”), al-Qāri‘ah (“The Striking Calamity”), and al-Takāthur (“Rivalry in Worldly Increase”) speak of the Last Day and its rewards and punishments. Both the Quran and the Last Day have been identified as “the great news.”

Then Sūrat al-‘Asr (“Time”), al-Humazah (“The Slanderer”), al-Fīl (“The Elephant”), Li-Īlāf (“For the Familiar Security”), Ara’ayt (“Have You Seen?”), al-Kawthar (“Abundance”), al-Kāfirūn (“The Disbelievers”), al-Nasr (“Victory”), and Tabbat (“May They Perish”) speak of deeds, both good and evil, although each surah has its own distinctive focus.

As for Sūrat al-Ikhlās (“Sincerity”) and the two surahs of refuge, al-Ikhlās contains praise of Allah, while the two surahs of refuge contain the servant’s supplication to his Lord to grant him refuge. Praise is coupled with supplication, just as Allah brought them together in the Mother of the Quran, which is divided between the Lord and the servant: half is praise of the Lord, and half is supplication on the servant’s part.

The connection here is clear. Belief in the Messenger begins with belief in the message he brought, namely the Quran. Then comes belief in its purpose and ultimate end: the bliss and punishment to which matters ultimately lead—that is, recompense. Then comes knowledge of the path to that end and the means of reaching it, namely deeds: good deeds, so that one may perform them, and evil deeds, so that one may avoid them.

---

## Page 8484

Then He concluded the Quran with the essence of faith: remembrance of Allah and supplication to Him, upon which the Mother of the Quran is likewise founded. For speech constitutes the inner essence of the human being, and speech is of two kinds: statements and non-declarative utterances.

The finest, most beneficial, and most binding statements are those about Allah, as in the first half of al-Fātihah and in Sūrat al-Ikhlās. The finest, most beneficial, and most binding non-declarative utterances—that is, requests—are those addressed to Allah, as in the second half of al-Fātihah and in the two surahs of refuge.

---

## Page 8485

Sūrat al-Bayyinah (The Clear Proof)

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

Concerning His saying, exalted is He:

“Those who disbelieved among the People of the Scripture and the polytheists would not desist until the clear proof came to them.”

This is a surah of immense significance, and reports have been transmitted concerning its merits. It is established in an authentic report that Allah commanded His Prophet to recite it to Ubayy ibn Ka'b. In the two Sahīh collections, Anas ibn Mālik reported that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said to Ubayy:

“Allah has commanded me to recite the Quran to you.”

He asked, “Did Allah name me to you?”

He replied, “Allah named you to me.”

Thereupon Ubayy began to weep.

Another version reads:

“Allah has commanded me to recite to you: ‘Those who disbelieved…’”

He asked, “Did He name me to you?”

He replied, “Yes.” So he wept.

In a version recorded by al-Bukhārī:

“And I was mentioned before the Lord of all worlds?”

He replied, “Yes.” Then his eyes overflowed with tears.

Qatādah said: “I was informed

---

## Page 8486

that he recited to him: ‘Those who disbelieved among the People of the Scripture…’” Singling out this surah for recitation to Ubayy implies that it possesses distinctive qualities that warranted this choice.

His words, “to recite to you,” mean a recitation intended to convey, make heard, and teach—not a recitation undertaken to receive instruction and correction, as a student recites to a teacher. Some have mistakenly understood it in this latter sense and regarded it as an expression of humility. Abū Hāmid cited it as evidence of a learner’s humility, but this interpretation has no basis. It was to Gabriel that he would recite in this manner, reviewing the Quran with him every year, for Gabriel was the one who brought the Quran down to him. As for other people, they learned it from him. How, then, could he recite to any of them to have his recitation corrected, or recite as a student does?

Rather, his recitation to Ubayy ibn Ka'b was like his recitation of the Quran to humans and jinn. He did indeed recite the Quran to the jinn. Whenever he went out to the people, he would call them to Islam and recite the Quran to them. He would also recite it to people both during prayer and outside it.

Allah, exalted is He, says:

“So what is the matter with them that they do not believe, and when the Quran is recited to them, they do not prostrate?”

And He, exalted is He, says:

“When the verses of the Most Merciful were recited to them, they fell down

---

## Page 8487

prostrate and weeping.” And He, exalted is He, says:

“Allah certainly bestowed a great favor upon the believers when He sent among them a Messenger from among themselves, reciting His verses to them.”

He mentions the same elsewhere. Thus, the Messenger recites Allah’s verses to the believers. The Prophet was commanded to single out Ubayy ibn Ka'b for recitation because of Ubayy’s merit and his particular mastery of the Quran. As is established in the authentic hadith collections, 'Umar said: “Ubayy is our best Quran reciter, and 'Alī our most skilled judge.”

It is also related in an authentic hadith that he said to Ibn Mas'ūd: “Recite the Quran to me.”

He replied: “Shall I recite it to you when it was revealed to you?”

He said: “I love to hear it from someone else.”

Ibn Mas'ūd’s recitation to him on this occasion was therefore intended to let him hear it, not to receive correction or instruction.

Regarding the meaning of Allah’s statement that neither of these two groups would break away, several Quran commentators have mentioned three interpretations. Does it mean that they would not abandon disbelief? Or does it mean that they did not reject Muhammad until he was sent, and thus had not broken away from Muhammad and belief in his prophethood until he was sent? Or does it mean that they would not be left alone until a messenger was sent to them?

---

## Page 8488

Among those who mentioned this was Abū al-Faraj ibn al-Jawzī. He said:

“‘Those who disbelieved among the People of the Book’—meaning the Jews and Christians—and the polytheists, who worship idols, ‘would not break away,’ meaning separate or desist. One says, ‘I detached something, and it became detached,’ meaning that it separated. The meaning is that they would not abandon their disbelief and polytheism until the clear proof came to them. The wording is in the future tense, but its meaning is past. The clear proof is the Messenger, Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who made clear to them their misguidance and ignorance. This describes Allah’s favor upon those of the two groups who believed, since He delivered them through him.”

Al-Baghawī’s wording is similar. He said:

“They would not desist from their disbelief and polytheism. The linguists say that ‘breaking away’ means separating or departing. One says, ‘I detached something, and it became detached,’ meaning that it separated. ‘Until the clear proof comes to them’ is worded in the future tense, but its meaning is past: until the clear proof—the manifest evidence—came to them. This means Muhammad, who brought them the Quran, made clear to them their misguidance and ignorance, and called them to faith. Thus Allah delivered them through him from ignorance and misguidance.”

He mentioned no other interpretation.

Abū al-Faraj said:

“Some commentators held that the verse means that they did not disagree that Allah would send them a prophet until He sent him; then they became divided. Others said: They were not free of Allah’s proofs until the clear proof was established against them.”

---

## Page 8489

He said: “The correct interpretation is the first.”

Abū Muhammad ibn ‘Atiyya mentioned all three interpretations, but he explained and supported the third without attributing it to anyone else. He said:

“His words, ‘breaking away,’ mean separating and parting. One says that something ‘broke away’ from something else when it became separated from it.”

He said:

“The expression mā infakka (‘has not ceased’), which belongs to the class of auxiliary verbs associated with kāna (‘was’), has no place in this verse. Thus, it is clear here that the description signifies separation.”

He said:

“People differed over what they were separating from. Mujāhid and others said: ‘They would not abandon disbelief and misguidance until the clear proof came to them.’ The future tense in ‘comes to them’ is used in place of the past because the force and magnitude of the Sharia had not yet manifested themselves.

“Al-Farrā’ and others said: ‘They would not abandon their recognition of Muhammad’s prophethood, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and their conviction concerning him until the clear proof came to them; then they became divided.’”

He said:

“Some grammarians understood the preceding negative construction with munfakkīn (‘breaking away’) as belonging to the same class as kāna. The implied predicate is reportedly supplied as ‘knowing the matter of Muhammad,’ or something similar.”

He said:

“There is a third interpretation of the verse, remarkable in its meaning: that the intended meaning is that these people were not free of Allah’s command, His power, and His watch over them, until

---

## Page 8490

He sent them a messenger to warn them, through whom the proof would be established against them and His favor perfected for those who believed. It is as though He said: ‘They were not [to](1) be left to themselves.’”

He said:

“There are parallels to this meaning in Allah’s Book.”

Al-Tha‘labī mentioned three interpretations. However, he attributed the third to someone who understood the intended meaning to be their destruction through the establishment of proof against them, and interpreted “breaking away” as meaning “perishing.”

He said:

“They were not ‘breaking away,’ meaning desisting from their disbelief and their association of partners with Allah. The linguists said it means ‘ceasing.’ The Arabs say, ‘So-and-so has not ceased doing such-and-such,’ meaning that he continues to do it. The basic meaning of fakk is ‘opening,’ as in unsealing a document or opening an anklet.

“‘Until the clear proof came to them’ means the manifest proof: Muhammad, who brought them the Quran, exposed their misguidance and ignorance, and called them to faith.”

He said:

“Ibn Kaysān said: ‘Its meaning is that these disbelievers did not abandon the description of Muhammad in their scripture until he was sent. When he was sent, they became divided concerning him.’”

He also said:

“The scholars said that the opening of the surah, up to His words, ‘containing upright scriptures,’ concerns those among the People of the Book and the polytheists who believed. ‘Nor did they become divided’ concerns those among the People of the Book who did not believe after the proof had been established against them.”

---

## Page 8491

He said:

“Some leading linguists said: ‘His words “breaking away” mean “perishing.” This derives from the expression, “A woman’s salā has come apart during childbirth,” meaning that it separates and does not join together again, so she perishes. The verse means that they would not perish in their state of denial until proof had been established against them through the sending of the Messenger and the revelation of the Book.’”

Al-Baghawī mentioned this interpretation and the first, then said: “The first is more correct.”

(I say: The second interpretation, which he related from Ibn Kaysān, is that of al-Farrā’. Al-Mahdawī presented it before the first, saying:

“‘Breaking away’ derives from the expression ‘one thing breaks away from another,’ meaning that it separates from it. The meaning is that they would not become divided except when the Messenger came to them, because they abandoned the accounts and descriptions of him that they possessed and disbelieved after the clear proofs.”

He said:

“On this interpretation, ‘breaking away’ does not require a predicate. This is supported by His words:

‘Nor did those who were given the Scripture become divided until after the clear proof had come to them.’”

He said:

“Mujāhid said: ‘The meaning is that they would not desist from their ways.’ It is also related from Mujāhid: ‘They would not believe until the clear proof came to them.’”

He said:

“Al-Farrā’ said: ‘They did not cease mentioning the references to the Prophet that they possessed until he appeared. When he appeared, they became divided and disagreed.’”

---

## Page 8492

I say:

This is the interpretation he presented first. However, al-Farrā’ and Ibn Kaysān understood “breaking away” as their abandoning and ceasing to mention him, the reports concerning him, and the glad tidings of his coming. In other words, they did not abandon or forsake what they knew of the reports concerning him until he appeared; then they broke away.

The other account says:

“They would not ‘break away’—that is, become divided—until the Messenger came, because they abandoned the reports concerning him that they possessed.”

This is the meaning of what Abū al-Faraj related:

“They did not disagree that Allah would send them a prophet until he was sent; then they became divided.”

Thus, “breaking away” means either their separating from one another or their abandoning the knowledge and reports they possessed concerning him. This interpretation is weak and is certainly not what is intended by this verse. Allah did not refer to the People of the Scripture as a whole; rather, He mentioned the disbelievers among the polytheists and the People of the Scripture. It is well known that the polytheists did not know him, mention him, or find him described in their scriptures as the People of the Scripture did. Nor had they been united upon a single religion before his mission, only to become divided when he came.

It is therefore impossible to say:

“The polytheists had not abandoned their knowledge of Muhammad, their mention of him, or their belief in him, nor had they disagreed or become divided over this, until he was sent.”

This meaning is false with regard to the polytheists. Nor does it hold true of the People of the Scripture, for Allah mentioned only the disbelievers among them, saying:

“It was not the case that those who disbelieved among the People of the Scripture

---

## Page 8493

and the polytheists.” It is well known that those who recognized his prophethood, acknowledged him, and spoke of him before he was sent were not all disbelievers. Rather, faith predominated among them.

This is clarified by the fact that, when Allah mentions the division of those who were given the Scripture after the clear evidence had come to them, He speaks of them generally, saying:

“And those who were given the Scripture did not become divided until after the clear evidence had come to them.”

He does not say:

“The disbelievers among the People of the Scripture were united upon the truth until the clear evidence came to them.”

Furthermore, this use of the expression “breaking away” is unknown; no attested example of it is known in the language. Describing division and disagreement as “breaking away” is not a recognized usage.

Moreover, He supplies no predicate for “breaking away,” as in the expressions “They had not ceased mentioning Muhammad,” “They continued to believe in him,” and the like. When this verb functions as one of the auxiliaries that behave like kāna (“to be”), one does not say mā kuntu munfakkan (“I had not ceased”); rather, one says mā infakaktu af‘alu kadhā (“I have not ceased doing such-and-such”). Thus, the verb immediately follows the negative particle mā.

Furthermore, nothing in the wording indicates that this breaking away relates specifically to the matter of Muhammad.

Moreover, this meaning is mentioned in His words:

“And those who were given

---

## Page 8494

the Scripture did not become divided until after the clear evidence had come to them.” Had this been the intended meaning here, it would have been mere repetition.

The first interpretation is better known among the exegetes. Some, such as al-Baghawī and others, mention another interpretation. It is known from Mujāhid and al-Rabī‘ ibn Anas, as in the well-known commentary transmitted by Ibn Abī Najīh from Mujāhid. Concerning “breaking away,” he said: “Hypocrites; they would not believe until the truth became clear to them.” Al-Rabī‘ ibn Anas said: “They persisted in doubt and uncertainty until the clear evidence and the messengers came to them.”

This interpretation entails praising and commending them after the coming of the clear evidence. Those who held it therefore had to say: “This concerns those of the two groups who believed, explaining Allah’s favor upon them.” They understood His words:

“And those who were given the Scripture did not become divided”

as referring to those among them who did not believe in Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This too is weak.

For the People of the Scripture became divided and disagreed before Muhammad was sent to them, as Allah states in several places. The Most High says:

“We certainly gave the Children of Israel the Scripture, judgment, and prophethood, provided them with good things, and favored them above all peoples.”

“And We gave them clear proofs concerning the matter, and they did not differ until

---

## Page 8495

after knowledge had come to them, out of mutual rivalry. Your Lord will judge between them on the Day of Resurrection concerning that over which they differed.”

He also says:

“Then We set you upon a clear way concerning the religion, so follow it, and do not follow the desires of those who do not know.”

The Most High says:

“Mankind was one community. Then Allah sent the prophets as bearers of good news and warnings, and sent down with them the Scripture in truth to judge between people concerning that over which they differed.”

Then He says:

“None differed over it except those who had been given it, after clear proofs had come to them, out of mutual rivalry. Then Allah, by His permission, guided those who believed to the truth concerning which they had differed. Allah guides whom He wills to a straight path.”

He thus states that Allah, by His permission, guided the believers to the truth concerning which people had differed. This disagreement therefore existed before the community of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, came into being.

The Most High says:

“The Sabbath was imposed only upon those who differed concerning it. Your Lord will surely judge between them on the Day of Resurrection concerning that over which they differed.”

The Most High also says:

“We certainly settled the Children of Israel in a good dwelling place and provided them with good things. They did not differ until knowledge had come to them. Your Lord will judge between them on the Day of Resurrection concerning that over which they differed.”

Then the Most High says:

“If you are in doubt about what We have sent down to you, ask those who have been reading the Scripture before you. The truth has certainly come to you from your Lord, so never be among those who doubt.”

The Most High says:

“By Allah, We certainly sent messengers to nations before you, but they were enticed by

---

## Page 8496

Satan, who made their deeds seem fair to them. He is their ally today, and theirs will be a painful punishment.”

“We have sent down the Scripture to you only so that you may make clear to them that over which they differed, and as guidance and mercy for people who believe.”

The Most High thus states that He sent messengers to nations before Muhammad, that Satan made their deeds seem fair to them and was their ally when Muhammad was sent, and that He sent down the Scripture to them so that Muhammad might make clear to them that over which they differed.

The Most High says:

“Indeed, this Quran recounts to the Children of Israel most of that over which they differ. And it is certainly guidance and mercy for the believers.”

He says to the community of Muhammad:

“Do not be like those who became divided and differed after clear proofs had come to them. For them there will be a tremendous punishment.”

This makes clear that they became divided and differed after clear proofs had come to them, before Muhammad, and Allah has forbidden his community to be like them.

The Most High also says:

“From those who said, ‘We are Christians,’ We took their covenant, but they forgot a portion of what they had been reminded of. So We stirred up enmity and hatred among them until the Day of Resurrection.”

He says of the Jews:

“We cast enmity and hatred among them until the Day of Resurrection.”

He also says:

“We scattered them throughout the earth as communities. Some of them were righteous, and some were otherwise.”

Hadiths have been transmitted through various chains in the Sunan collections and the Musnad, reporting that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The Jews divided into seventy-one sects, and this community will divide into seventy-three sects.”

Although some, such as Ibn Hazm, judge these hadiths to be weak, most scholars have accepted them and affirmed their truth.

---

## Page 8497

In the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“Leave me alone so long as I leave you alone. Those before you perished only because of their excessive questioning and their disagreements with their prophets. When I forbid you something, avoid it; and when I command you to do something, do as much of it as you can.”

It is also reported in the two Sahīh collections that he said:

“We are the last, but will be the foremost on the Day of Resurrection, although they were given the Scripture before us and we were given it after them. This, then, is their day concerning which they differed, and Allah guided us to it. In this, the people follow us: tomorrow is for the Jews, and the day after tomorrow for the Christians.”

It is known through mass transmission that the People of the Scripture differed and became divided before Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent. Indeed, the Jews split into sects before the Messiah came; then, when the Messiah came, they differed concerning him. The Christians subsequently developed further differences. How, then, can it be said that His words,

“Those who were given the Scripture did not become divided until after the clear proof had come to them,”

refer to those among them who did not believe in Muhammad?

Furthermore, those who disbelieved in Muhammad are disbelievers, and they are the ones mentioned in His words:

“Those who disbelieved among the People of the Scripture and the polytheists would not desist until the clear proof came to them.”

---

## Page 8498

the clear proof.” They had become divided and disagreed over what the prophets brought before Muhammad, and those among them who disbelieved had done so before Muhammad was sent.

Among them were also those who did not disbelieve, but believed in the prophets, as Allah Most High said:

“Among the people of Moses is a community who guide by the truth and establish justice thereby.”

“We dispersed them throughout the earth into communities; among them are the righteous, and among them are those less so.”

Allah Most High also said:

“They are not all alike. Among the People of the Scripture is an upright community who recite Allah’s verses during the hours of the night, prostrating themselves.”

“They believe in Allah and the Last Day, enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong, and hasten to do good deeds. They are among the righteous.”

Allah Most High also said:

“Had they upheld the Torah, the Gospel, and what was revealed to them from their Lord, they would have received provision from above them and from beneath their feet. Among them is a community who follow a moderate course, but evil indeed is what many of them do.”

In Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere, ʿIyād ibn Himār reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah looked upon the people of the earth and detested them, Arabs and non-Arabs alike, except for a remnant among the People of the Scripture. My Lord said to me, ‘Rise among Quraysh and warn them.’ I said, ‘My Lord, they would then smash my head until they left it like a loaf of bread.’ He said, ‘I shall test you and test others through you, and send down to you a Book that water cannot wash away, which you will recite asleep and awake. Send forth an army, and We shall send twice their number; and with those who obey you, fight those who disobey you.’”

The hadith is longer than this.

---

## Page 8499

Our purpose here is to discuss the verse, so we say:

The third view is the soundest, both in wording and in meaning. As regards the wording, its significance and what it conveys, this expression is used when a person frees himself from something that binds him—his will, that is—and is imposed upon him. One says, “He broke free of it,” as with a captive or a slave held under the constraint of slavery or captivity. One says, “I released the captive, and he became free,” and “I freed a slave.” Allah Most High said:

“And what can make you know what the steep path is? It is the freeing of a slave.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in the authentic hadith narrated by al-Bukhārī:

“Visit the sick, feed the hungry, and free the captive.”

It is also reported in the Sahīh that when ʿAlī was asked what was in the document, he said, “It contains blood money, the release of captives, and the ruling that a Muslim is not to be killed for a disbeliever.”

Thus, releasing someone means separating him from whoever subjugates him and exercises control over him against his will, and parting the two.

One also says, “So-and-so will not leave so-and-so alone until he gets him involved in such-and-such,” and “The one in charge will not let this man go until he does such-and-such.” Such expressions describe someone who persistently holds another in his grip and gains control over him, whether through power and coercion or through making things attractive and appealing and employing means by which the other becomes obedient to him.

---

## Page 8500

And of someone held under another’s control, one says, “He cannot break free of this,” just as a captive or slave cannot break free of whoever holds him in subjection. Thus, His words:

“Those who disbelieved among the People of the Book and the polytheists were not to break free”

mean that they were not to be left to their own devices, doing whatever they desired without restraint, as someone who has broken free is no longer restrained. He did not say “mafkūkīn” (released), but rather “munfakkīn” (breaking free). This wording is better, for it negates an action on their part. Had He said “released,” the implied meaning would have been that they were not to be left unrestrained and alone, thus negating an action on the part of someone else.

The point is that they were not to be left without commands or prohibitions, with no messengers sent to them, doing whatever they wished in pursuit of their desires. The meaning is that Allah does not leave them alone or abandon them. He does not release them until He sends them a messenger. This resembles His words:

“Does man think that he will be left without purpose?”

That is, without being commanded or forbidden. Does he imagine that this will happen? It will never happen; rather, he must be given commands and prohibitions. Similar to this are His words, Most High:

“Indeed, We have made it an Arabic Quran so that you may understand. And indeed, it is in the Mother of the Book with Us, exalted and full of wisdom. Shall We then turn the Reminder away from you, disregarding you, because you are a transgressing people?”

This is a rhetorical question expressing rejection: Are We, because of your transgression, to cease sending down the Reminder and refrain from sending messengers? And who resents their being sent?

---

## Page 8501

For the former denies their existence, while the latter entails hatred of them and aversion to what they brought. Allah Most High says:

“That is because they hated what Allah revealed, so He rendered their deeds worthless.”

He also related the words of the believer from Pharaoh’s household:

“Joseph had already come to you before with clear proofs, yet you remained in doubt about what he brought you. Then, when he died, you said, ‘Allah will never send a messenger after him.’ Thus does Allah lead astray whoever is a transgressor, full of doubt.”

As for anyone who denies the messengers after they have been sent, his disbelief is evident. But someone may suppose that Allah will send him no messenger and that he will be left without purpose, neglected, neither commanded nor forbidden. This too is something Allah has condemned, when messengers must necessarily be sent and scriptures revealed, just as deeds must necessarily be recompensed with reward and punishment, and the Resurrection must take place. Thus He, glorified be He, rebukes those who suppose that this will not happen. Allah Most High says:

“We did not create the heaven and the earth and all that is between them without purpose. That is the assumption of those who disbelieve. So woe to those who disbelieve because of the Fire!”

“Or should We treat those who believe and do righteous deeds like those who spread corruption on earth? Or should We treat the righteous like the wicked?”

Allah Most High also says:

“Did you then think that We created you without purpose and that you would not be returned to Us?”

Allah Most High also says:

“We did not create the heavens and the earth and all that is between them except in truth. The Hour is certainly coming, so pardon graciously.”

“Indeed, your Lord is the Creator, the All-Knowing.”

He also says:

“Allah created the heavens and the earth in truth, so that every soul may be recompensed for what it has earned, and they will not be wronged.”

---

## Page 8502

And He says of those endowed with understanding:

“Those who remember Allah while standing, sitting, and lying on their sides, and reflect on the creation of the heavens and the earth: ‘Our Lord, You did not create this without purpose. Glory be to You! Protect us from the punishment of the Fire.’”

These and similar passages in the Quran show that commands and prohibitions, reward and punishment, and the Resurrection are inevitable. They rebuke anyone who supposes or imagines that these things will not occur. This entails that they must necessarily occur and that their not occurring is impossible.

This is agreed upon by those among the adherents of the religions who believe the messengers, whether Muslims or others, on the basis of accepting the report as true. Allah has foretold this, and His report is true. What He has foretold must therefore occur; it is necessary by virtue of His promise and His report. For if He knows that something will happen and has declared that it will happen, it must happen. Nothing can possibly be contrary to what He has known, declared, written, and decreed. Moreover, He has willed this: whatever He wills happens, and whatever He does not will does not happen. Everything He wills must occur.

Whether, however, it may be said that His will renders this necessary is disputed.

Likewise, it is also disputed whether it may be said that this has become necessary because He has made it incumbent upon Himself, or because His wisdom requires it. Whatever He has sworn to do must occur. An oath encompasses the meaning of a declaration

---

## Page 8503

and the sense of urging and demanding.

However, people disagree over whether the latter applies to Allah, as in His words:

“I will surely fill Hell with you and all those among them who follow you.”

And His words:

“And when your Lord declared that He would surely send against them, until the Day of Resurrection, those who would subject them to grievous torment.”

Those who say that His wisdom, His judgment, or His will necessitates this maintain that it may be known through reason. They say that reason may establish that messengers must necessarily be sent, and that this is necessary according to His judgment and wisdom. This is the position of many groups, or most of them.

Others say that none of this can be known except through revelation. This is the position of the Jahmiyyah and the Ashʿariyyah, whereas the former is the position of the Muʿtazilah, the Karrāmiyyah, and the Hanafīs, or most of them. Among the followers of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad, some hold this view. The majority of jurists, however, together with the early generations, affirm divine wisdom and that His acts have underlying reasons. Only those among them who agree with the Jahmī determinists deny this, such as al-Ashʿarī and those who agree with him.

Likewise, the majority of them affirm that acts possess attributes by virtue of which they are good or evil and reprehensible. They do not regard their goodness and badness as a preference for one of two alternatives without any determining ground beyond sheer will, as the Jahmiyyah and those who agree with them maintain.

---

## Page 8504

This is the position of the imams and the majority, just as they affirm divine decree and believe in it: Allah is the Creator of all things; whatever He wills comes to pass, and whatever He does not will does not come to pass. They accept neither the position of those among the Muʿtazilah and others who deny divine decree, nor that of the Jahmī determinists and others who deny the Lord’s wisdom.

Thus, they accept neither the position of the Qadariyyah who deny divine decree nor that of the determinist Qadariyyah, whose position entails denying commands and prohibitions, promises and threats, and recompense through reward and punishment—especially those among them who state this openly or say:

“For anyone who witnesses divine decree, commands and prohibitions, promises and threats cease to apply.”

The deniers of divine decree believed, in general, in what the messengers brought. They regarded as obligatory what Allah made obligatory and as forbidden what Allah forbade. They believed in Paradise and Hell and strove to follow the messengers. However, they erred in denying divine decree and supposing that affirming it contradicts commands and prohibitions, promises and threats. They also supposed that their belief that Allah is just and truthful could not be complete unless they denied divine decree and the release from the Fire of those guilty of major sins. They imagined that Allah had declared that He would never bring out of the Fire anyone whose sin merits punishment, nor ever show him mercy. Thus, they did not allow for the possibility that someone might be punished for his sin and then shown mercy. Rather, in their view, anyone whose sin merits punishment would never be shown mercy. Although they did not deliberately intend to deny the messengers, this position of theirs entails

---

## Page 8505

contradicting the reports from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—which hadith scholars recognize as mass-transmitted, concerning sinners being brought out of the Fire and intercessors interceding for them. It also entails making people despair of Allah’s mercy, while denying the all-encompassing scope of Allah’s creation, will, and power: they claimed that some events lie beyond His power and are neither willed nor created by Him.

In this respect, they resembled the Magians, so much so that it was said:

“The Qadariyyah are the Magians of this community.”

Those at the opposite extreme withheld judgment concerning Allah’s statements altogether, even rejecting both kinds of general statement. Thus, they did not derive from His statements knowledge of the promises and threats He conveyed. They would not affirm with certainty the salvation of those whom Allah knows to have believed, done righteous deeds, and been among the most obedient to Him, if any one of them had committed a single minor sin. Nor would they affirm with certainty the punishment of those whom Allah knows to be the most wicked and evil members of the Muslim community. Rather, despite Allah’s knowledge of both groups, they considered it possible that He might punish people who had performed great good deeds for a minor sin with a punishment He inflicts on no one else in the Muslim community, and that He might admit the wicked members of the Muslim community into Paradise alongside the earliest forerunners in faith. A detailed discussion of both groups belongs elsewhere.

---

## Page 8506

The point here is that this surah indicates what other passages of the Quran also indicate: Allah sends messengers to people to command and forbid them, sending them as bearers of glad tidings and warnings. As He, the Exalted, says:

“We send the messengers only as bearers of glad tidings and warnings.”

They warn those who do evil of the punishments for their deeds, and give those who believe and do righteous deeds glad tidings of everlasting bliss and “that they will have a good reward,” “in which they will remain forever.”

Thus, His words,

“Those who disbelieved among the People of the Book and the polytheists were not to be left alone until the clear evidence came to them,”

explain that Allah would not leave the disbelievers as they were, in their disbelief. Rather, He does not leave them alone without first sending them the Messenger as a bearer of glad tidings and warnings,

“so that He may repay those who do evil for what they have done and reward those who do good with the best.”

This is further clarified by the fact that hattā (“until”) is a particle marking an endpoint, and what follows that endpoint differs from what precedes it, as in His words:

“until the white thread of dawn becomes distinct to you from the black thread,”

and His words,

“until they become pure,”

and His words,

“until she marries a husband other than him,”

and similar passages. If the intended meaning were that they would not desist from disbelief and believe until the truth became clear to them, it would follow that, after the clear evidence came, they all desisted and believed, since the wording encompasses them all.

---

## Page 8507

Likewise, if the intended meaning were that they had agreed on affirming the Messenger’s truthfulness until he was sent, it would follow that they had all known of him before he was sent to them, and that they all became divided and disagreed after he was sent. Both implications are false. Many of them are unlettered and know the Scripture only through wishful notions; they were unaware of what the scriptures contained concerning his being sent and other matters. When he was sent, a great many of them believed in him; they did not all turn away from faith in him.

Thus, the verse does not contain blanket praise of them, as supposed by those who understood it to mean that they had not desisted from disbelief or come to faith until the truth became clear to them. Nor does it contain blanket condemnation of them, as supposed by those who understood that, when the Messenger came to them, they became divided and disagreed after having agreed on affirming his truthfulness. Rather, it praises those among them who believed in the Messenger, condemns those who did not believe, and declares that the Messenger would inevitably be sent to them, whereupon some would believe in him and others would disbelieve. Allah, the Exalted, says:

“We have favored some of those messengers over others. Among them were those to whom Allah spoke, and He raised some in rank. We gave Jesus, son of Mary, clear proofs and strengthened him with the Holy Spirit. Had Allah willed, those who came after them would not have fought one another after the clear proofs had come to them. But they differed: some believed and others disbelieved. Had Allah willed, they would not have fought one another, but Allah does whatever He wills.”

---

## Page 8508

Moreover, He must test those who believe in the messengers, thereby distinguishing the truthful from the liars. As Allah, the Exalted, says:

“Do people think they will be left to say, ‘We believe,’ without being tested?”

“We certainly tested those before them, so Allah will surely know those who are truthful, and He will surely know the liars.”

Then He says:

“Or do those who commit evil deeds think they can escape Us? How badly they judge!”

When messengers are sent to people, each person falls into one of two categories: either someone who outwardly believes in them and must therefore be tested until the truthful can be distinguished from the liar; or someone who commits evil deeds and does not believe. Such a person cannot escape Allah; rather, He—glorified and exalted—is sure to seize him.

Thus, people fall into three categories in their response to the messengers: a believer inwardly and outwardly; a disbeliever who openly professes disbelief; and a hypocrite who outwardly professes faith while inwardly concealing disbelief. This division arose once the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, emigrated to Medina. At the beginning of Sūrat al-Baqarah, Allah, the Exalted, revealed four verses describing the believers, two verses describing the disbelievers, and ten-odd verses describing the hypocrites. But when he was in Mecca and the believers were oppressed, no one had any need

---

## Page 8509

to resort to hypocrisy. Rather, some believers concealed their faith from many people. Others uttered words of disbelief under compulsion while their hearts remained firm in faith. Such a person is a believer both inwardly and outwardly. Although he outwardly professes disbelief before certain people under compulsion, or conceals his faith from them, he professes faith in private and among those he trusts, and fulfils whatever duties he can. Whatever he is unable to fulfil is waived for him.

For this reason, scholars, including Ahmad ibn Hanbal, said that hypocrisy was not possible for them; hypocrisy arose only in Medina. There were, however, people in Mecca whose hearts were diseased, as He says in the Meccan surah:

“And so that those given the Scripture and the believers may have no doubt, and so that those in whose hearts is disease and the disbelievers may say, ‘What does Allah intend by this as an example?’”

He—glorified is He—has stated that He would not leave those who profess faith without distinguishing the wicked from the good and testing them. As He, the Exalted, says:

“Allah would not leave the believers in the state you are in until He distinguishes the wicked from the good.”

He also says:

“Or do you think that you will be left alone before Allah has made evident those among you who strive and take no confidants other than Allah, His Messenger, and the believers? And Allah is fully aware of what you do.”

And He, the Exalted, says:

“Or do you think that you will enter Paradise without experiencing the like of what befell those who passed away before you? They were afflicted with poverty and hardship and were so shaken that the messenger and those who believed with him said, ‘When will Allah’s help come?’ Indeed, Allah’s help is near.”

And there are other passages to the same effect.

---

## Page 8510

Likewise, He would not leave those who disbelieved without sending them the Messenger with clear signs. This is the meaning of His words:

“Those who disbelieved among the People of the Scripture and the polytheists were not to be left alone until the clear evidence comes to them.”

When the clear evidence comes to them, some believe and others disbelieve.

If it is said that, beyond this, the verse also conveys the other meaning—that they would not find guidance, know the truth, and believe until the clear evidence came to them, since they have no way of knowing the truth except through a messenger who comes from Allah; or that they would not desist and heed admonition, even if they knew the truth, until someone came to them from Allah to remind them—then this meaning does not contradict the former.

This is unlike the interpretation of those who say: “The polytheists and the People of the Scripture had not abandoned their recognition of Muhammad or ceased mentioning him, nor were they divided concerning him. Rather, they were agreed upon believing in him until the clear evidence came to them; then they abandoned belief in him and became divided.” This is certainly not the intended meaning.

Among the things that clarify this is His wording, “until the clear evidence comes to them.” He did not say, “until it came to them.” Because those interpreters did not understand the meaning of the verse, they thought that the past tense was called for here, and that the intended meaning was: they did not depart from their previous state, whether disbelief or faith, until the clear evidence came to them. Thus, the wording “until the clear evidence comes to them” puzzled them. And it was said:

---

## Page 8511

some of them: “Not all of it has yet come to them.” According to the correct meaning, however, the imperfect tense is appropriate here, as in Allah’s words:

“Allah would not leave the believers in the state you are in until He distinguishes the evil from the good.”

The intended meaning is that they were not to be released and left alone until the clear evidence comes to them. He, glorified be He, said: “Those who disbelieved were not…” Although the particle lam gives the imperfect verb a past meaning, this applies when the verb stands alone. Thus, lam ya’ti and lam yadhhab mean mā atā (“he did not come”) and mā dhahaba (“he did not go”). But when one says, “He would not do this,” or, as Allah says:

“Allah would not forgive them, nor would He guide them to a way,”

the intended meaning is an enduring action, without restriction to a particular time. Saying, “So-and-so would not come until so-and-so went to him,” differs from saying, “So-and-so had not come until so-and-so went to him.” Likewise, saying, “So-and-so would not do this until such-and-such happens,” conveys a similar meaning, unlike saying, “So-and-so had not done it until so-and-so came.” Thus, the imperfect verb is negated, with an active participle as its predicate, denoting continuity.

The intended meaning is that they were not to be left alone, in the present or the future, until the clear evidence comes to them. Had the wording here been “until the clear evidence came to them,” it would not have been appropriate.

---

## Page 8512

Likewise, if He had meant giving up disbelief and embracing faith, the wording would have been, “until the clear evidence comes to them”—meaning that they would not know the truth until a prophet comes to make it known to them, or would not heed admonition and act upon it until someone comes to admonish and remind them. This is therefore not a place for the past tense, unlike saying, “They remained disbelievers until he came to them.”

The verse thus states that clear evidence must be established and that they cannot be released without it. Its purpose is not merely to report that they were not released and then were released at some point in the past. It is as though He had said, “They would not be released until the clear evidence comes to them.” Here, however, He used the plural active participle, munfakkīn (“released”).

Having explained that messengers must be sent to those who disbelieved among the polytheists and the People of the Book, so that the proof might thereby be established against them, He, glorified be He, then explained that the People of the Book who believed in the messengers became divided only after the clear evidence had come to them and the proof had been established against them. Allah’s clear signs and His proof were thus established against both groups. He punished neither group until the clear evidence had come to them and the proof had been established against them, as in the account of Moses and those to whom he was sent. Allah did not leave Pharaoh and his people alone without sending Moses to them, nor did He punish them until after establishing the proof against them. Then, once the Children of Israel had believed in the Scriptures and the messengers, they became divided and fell into disagreement only

---

## Page 8513

after the clear evidence had come to them. Thus, they had no excuse for this. For this reason, Muhammad’s community was forbidden to imitate them:

“And do not be like those who became divided and differed after the clear proofs had come to them.”

The people to whom Muhammad was sent are likewise. Whoever disbelieved would not be released until the clear evidence came to him. Those among the nations who believed in Muhammad and then became divided and differed did so only after the clear evidence had come to them. All of them were commanded

“only to worship Allah, devoting their religion sincerely to Him, turning away from falsehood, to establish prayer and give zakah. That is the upright religion.”

The verse praises the Lord and describes His wisdom, justice, and proof: He does not leave them without sending them a messenger. As He said to the People of the Book:

“Our Messenger has come to you, making things clear to you after an interval between messengers, lest you say, ‘No bearer of good tidings or warner has come to us.’ Now a bearer of good tidings and a warner has come to you”—to the end of the verse.

It does not praise them for persisting in disbelief until the Messenger comes. At most, this means that they are not punished for it until the Messenger comes, not that they are praised for it until he comes. No rational person would say such a thing, nor has anyone said it—especially since the proof had already been established against the People of the Book through prophets before him.

---

## Page 8514

A parallel wording occurs in His saying:

“And they carry your loads to a land you could not reach except with great hardship.”

This does not mean that you could not reach it in the past; rather, this is their constant condition. Thus, His words, “Those who disbelieved would not be released until it comes to them,” imply that this is always their condition.

The surah mentions the different classes of creation and what Allah has commanded all His servants. It explains that this is indispensable: messengers must be sent, scriptures revealed, and the blessed—the people of Paradise—and the wretched—the people of the Fire—made known. His words:

“Those who disbelieved among the People of the Book and the polytheists would not be released until the clear evidence comes to them—a messenger from Allah, reciting purified scriptures,”

form a statement explaining that the Messenger was sent to them all. His words:

“Those who were given the Scripture did not become divided until after the clear evidence had come to them,”

establish the proof against the followers of revealed laws, condemn their divisions and disagreements, and explain that these arose after the clear evidence had come to them.

These two statements are paralleled by His saying:
