“Mankind was one community; then Allah sent the prophets as bearers of good tidings and warners, and sent down with them the Scripture in truth to judge between people concerning that over which they differed.”

Then He said:

“None differed concerning it except those who were given it, after

---

## Page 8515

clear proofs had come to them, out of mutual animosity. Then Allah, by His permission, guided those who believed to the truth concerning which they differed.”

Likewise, Allah Most High says:

“He has ordained for you the religion He enjoined upon Noah, and which We have revealed to you, and which We enjoined upon Abraham, Moses, and Jesus: ‘Establish the religion and do not become divided in it.’ What you call them to is difficult for the polytheists to accept. Allah chooses for Himself whom He wills and guides to Himself whoever turns to Him.”

Then He said:

“They did not become divided until after knowledge had come to them, out of mutual animosity. Had it not been for a prior decree from your Lord granting them respite until an appointed time, judgment would have been passed between them. Indeed, those who inherited the Scripture after them are in troubling doubt about it.”

And His saying:

“We certainly gave Moses the Scripture, but differences arose concerning it. Had it not been for a prior decree from your Lord, judgment would have been passed between them. Indeed, they are in troubling doubt about it,”

in Surah Hūd and Surah ‘Ayn Sīn Qāf.

Then He mentioned what all were commanded to do, saying:

“They were commanded only to worship Allah, devoting their religion sincerely to Him and inclining to the truth, to establish prayer and give zakah. That is the upright religion.”

Then He mentioned the final outcome of those who disbelieved among the People of the Book and the polytheists, and the final outcome of those who believed and did righteous deeds.

---

## Page 8516

Section

Regarding His saying:

“Those who were given the Scripture did not become divided until after the clear proof had come to them.”

A group of commentators said: This refers to their division concerning Muhammad after they had been united in believing in him. Some of these commentators understood their division to mean that some believed while others disbelieved.

Al-Baghawī said: Then He mentioned those among the People of the Book who did not believe, saying:

“Those who were given the Scripture did not become divided until after the clear proof had come to them.”

That is, the clear statement in their scriptures that he was a prophet sent by Allah. The commentators said: The People of the Book remained united in affirming Muhammad’s truthfulness until Allah sent him. Once he was sent, they became divided concerning him and disagreed: some believed in him, while others disbelieved in him.

A group of commentators gave a similar explanation of His saying:

“We certainly settled the Children of Israel in a good dwelling place and provided them with good things. They did not differ until knowledge had come to them.”

Abū al-Faraj said: Ibn ‘Abbās said: They did not differ concerning Muhammad; they continued to affirm his truthfulness until knowledge came to them—meaning the Quran.

It is also reported from him that “until knowledge came to them” means Muhammad. According to this interpretation, “knowledge” here stands for what is known. This is explained by the fact that when he came to them

---

## Page 8517

they differed over accepting his truthfulness, and most of them disbelieved in him out of transgression and envy, after having been united in affirming his truthfulness—out of transgression and envy. Some commentators held that all those who became divided were disbelievers.

Ibn ‘Atiyyah said:

Then the Exalted censured those among the People of the Book, from the Children of Israel, who did not believe: they did not become divided concerning Muhammad until after they had seen the clear signs. Previously, they had agreed on his prophethood and his description. But when he came from among the Arabs, they envied him.

Al-Tha‘labī likewise said:

Those who were given the Scripture did not become divided concerning Muhammad and reject him until after the clear proof had come to them—the clear statement in their scriptures that he was a prophet sent by Allah. The scholars said: From the beginning of this surah up to His saying,

“containing upright writings,”

the passage concerns those among the People of the Book and the polytheists who believed. His saying,

“They did not become divided,”

concerns those among the People of the Book who did not believe after the proof had been established against them. Abū al-Faraj said likewise.

He said:

“Those who were given the Scripture did not become divided”

—meaning those who did not believe—

“until after the clear proof had come to them.”

There are three interpretations of the clear proof. The first is that it refers to Muhammad, meaning that they remained united in believing in him until he was sent. This was the view of the majority;

---

## Page 8518

and the second is the Quran, according to Abū al-‘Āliyah. The third is the explanation of his prophethood found in their scriptures, as mentioned by al-Māwardī.

(I say: This is the interpretation that most commentators affirmed decisively; al-Tha‘labī, al-Baghawī and others mentioned no other interpretation. Abū al-‘Āliyah, however, said only “the Scripture”; he did not say “the Quran.” This is how Ibn Abī Hātim narrated it through the well-known chain from al-Rabī‘ ibn Anas:

“Until after the clear proof had come to them.”

He said: Abū al-‘Āliyah said, “The Scripture.” What Abū al-‘Āliyah meant was Scripture in general, which includes the earlier Scripture, as Allah says in two places in the Quran:

“We certainly gave Moses the Scripture, but differences arose concerning it.”

And the Exalted says:

“Then Allah sent the prophets as bearers of good tidings and warners, and sent down with them the Scripture in truth to judge between people concerning that over which they differed.”

Then He says:

“None differed over it except those who were given it, after clear proofs had come to them, out of mutual transgression. Then Allah, by His permission, guided those who believed to the truth concerning which they differed.”

This interpretation is well known from Abū al-‘Āliyah, who narrated it from Ubayy ibn Ka‘b. Ibn Abī Hātim and others narrated it from al-Rabī‘, from Abū al-‘Āliyah, from Ubayy ibn Ka‘b, who used to recite it:

“Mankind was one community, then Allah sent

---

## Page 8519

the prophets as bearers of good tidings and warners.” He explained that Allah sent the messengers and revealed the scriptures only when disagreement arose.

“And sent down with them the Scripture in truth.”

He said: He revealed the Scripture when disagreement arose.

“None differed over it except those who were given it.”

This means the Children of Israel, who were given the Scripture and knowledge.

“After clear proofs had come to them, out of mutual transgression.”

He says: They transgressed out of rivalry for this world, seeking its dominion, splendor, and adornment, vying over who among them would wield authority and command awe among the people. Thus they transgressed against one another and struck one another’s necks.

“Then Allah, by His permission, guided those who believed to the truth concerning which they differed.”

He says: Allah guided them when disagreement arose: they remained steadfast upon what the messengers had brought before the disagreement. They maintained sincere devotion to Allah alone, worshipping Him without associating any partner with Him, establishing prayer, and giving zakat. They adhered to the original way that had prevailed before the disagreement and kept apart from the disagreement. Thus they would be witnesses over mankind on the Day of Resurrection, testifying against the people of Noah, the people of Hūd, the people of Sālih, the people of Shu‘ayb, and Pharaoh’s people that their messengers had conveyed the message to them and that they had rejected their messengers.

I say:

The Quran speaks of two kinds of disagreement. In the first, all those who disagree are condemned, as in His words:

“Indeed, those who differ concerning the Scripture are in extreme dissension.”

And His words:

“They will continue to differ, except those upon whom your Lord has mercy.”

In the second, He praises the believers and condemns the disbelievers, as in His words:

“Had Allah willed, those who came after them would not have fought one another after clear proofs had come to them. But they differed: some believed and some disbelieved. And had it been the will of

---

## Page 8520

Allah, they would not have fought one another, but Allah does whatever He wills.” And His words:

“These are two adversaries who have disputed concerning their Lord. For those who disbelieve, garments of fire will be cut out.”

Through to His words:

“Indeed, Allah will admit those who believe and do righteous deeds.”

And His words:

“Indeed, those who believe, the Jews, the Sabians, the Christians, the Magians, and those who associate partners with Allah—Allah will judge between them on the Day of Resurrection. Indeed, Allah is Witness over all things.”

Since this is so, the division and disagreement among the People of the Scripture that He condemned involved condemnation of them all. He forbade imitating them, saying:

“Do not be like those who became divided and differed after clear proofs had come to them.”

And He said:

“None differed over it except those who were given it, after clear proofs had come to them, out of mutual transgression.”

This occurs when one group believes in some truth but rejects the truth held by another, and adds falsehood to the truth, as the Jews and Christians differed concerning the Messiah and other matters.

We therefore say:

If someone says that the People of the Scripture did not become divided over Muhammad until after he was sent, meaning that some believed while others disbelieved—as some have said—then those condemned here are the disbelievers, not the believers. Thus, not everyone involved in the disagreement is condemned. Rather, condemnation falls upon those who knew that he was a messenger but, when he came, disbelieved in him out of envy or transgression, as the Most High said:

“And when there came to them a Book from Allah confirming what was with them, although previously they had

---

## Page 8521

sought victory over those who disbelieved—when there came to them what they recognized, they disbelieved in it. So Allah’s curse is upon the disbelievers.”

If, however, their division concerning him is taken to mean that they all disbelieved in him but differed in what they said about him, that is not the case. The Quran has made clear in several places that they became divided and disagreed before Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent. Thus, their disagreement and division concerning Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, form part of their wider division and disagreement. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 8522

Surah al-Takāthur

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section: “Surah al-Takāthur”

The words used in this surah,

“Until you visit the graves,”

draw attention to the fact that a visitor must leave the place he visits. This therefore points to the resurrection.

Then He says:

“No! You will soon know.”
“Then no! You will soon know.”

This foretells what they will know in the future. Hence, it is reported from Alī that this refers to punishment in the grave. Then He says:

“No! If only you knew with the knowledge of certainty!”

This refers to their knowing in the present, with the consequent left unstated. That is: the reality would be beyond description; you would know something momentous, and it would turn your attention away from what now distracts you. For distraction through rivalry for more arises only from heedlessness and a lack of certainty.

As He says:

“They denied Our signs and were heedless of them.”

It is also like the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“If you knew what I know, you would laugh little and weep much.”

The consequent of “if” is frequently omitted in the Quran to emphasize its magnitude and gravity, for it is too momentous

---

## Page 8523

to be described or conceived merely by hearing words, for one who reports something is not like one who witnesses it firsthand. Hence He follows this with an oath affirming that they will see it: this is the certainty of sight, which surpasses a report that yields the knowledge of certainty. He says:

“You will surely see Hellfire.”

“Then you will surely see it with the certainty of sight.”

This statement refers to the future and is the response to an unstated oath, while the consequent of “if” is omitted, as explained above. This is one of the two interpretations.

According to the other, it is connected to “if.” But an objection is raised: the consequent of “if” must be in the past tense, so the wording would be: “You would have seen Hellfire.” This is like the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“If you remained in the state you are in when you are with me, the angels would shake hands with you in your streets and on your beds.”

If it were in the past tense, it would not take an emphatic ending. Rather, one says: “If he were to come, I would come.” The answer to this objection is that it is the response to an unstated oath, standing in place of the consequent of “if.” This is like His saying:

“And if you obey them, you are surely polytheists.”

There are parallels to this in the Quran and in Arabic speech. When a statement contains both an oath and a condition, each requiring a response, the response is given to whichever comes first. Here, that is the oath, which is the intended emphasis.

According to this interpretation, the meaning would be: “By Allah, if you knew with the knowledge of certainty, you would surely see Hellfire with your hearts.”

The first interpretation, however, is the well-known one. Some Quran commentators mention no other, and it is the interpretation they transmitted from their predecessors. What demonstrates its soundness and establishes that it is correct is that His words,

“Then you will surely see it,”

and,

“Then you will surely be questioned,”

are conjoined to what precedes them and therefore fall within its scope. Thus, if the first statement were dependent on the condition, the statements conjoined to it would be

---

## Page 8524

likewise conditional. But this is untenable, because seeing it with the certainty of sight and being questioned about worldly blessings are not conditional upon knowing of it in this world with the knowledge of certainty.

Moreover, interpreting an unqualified reference to seeing as seeing with the heart is not customary Arabic usage. Furthermore, the condition would then be identical to its consequent: the meaning would be, “If you knew with the knowledge of certainty, you would see with your hearts.” Yet that is itself knowledge, so the meaning would be, “If you knew, you would know,” which conveys nothing. Even if beholding with the heart were intended to mean something beyond mere knowledge, it is already known that someone who knows a thing can bring himself to behold it with his heart. Moreover, even if this meaning conveyed something, it would not warrant an oath, for it is of little consequence.

Furthermore, in His words:

“If you knew with the knowledge of certainty,”

He does not specify the object of knowledge such that knowing it would necessarily entail knowing of Hellfire. If a particular object of knowledge is intended, nothing in the conditional clause indicates it so as to establish the connection. If the broader object of knowledge is intended—namely, what comes after death—then that entails knowledge of Hellfire and other things. This, however, is open to question.

For someone may ask: In His words:

“You will soon know,”

and:

“Then, no indeed! You will soon know,”

He does not specify

---

## Page 8525

what is to be known, but leaves it unspecified. Yet everyone will come to know something they did not previously know.

The answer is that the context conveys warning and threat, since He begins by saying:

“Competition for more has distracted you.”

Moreover, such language has conventionally come to be used chiefly to convey a threat, or else a promise. Since the knowledge in question is delimited by the wording of the context and by conventional usage, His words:

“If you knew”

refer to that same knowledge. He foretold that it would occur in the future, then made its occurrence in the present the condition, specifying that the knowledge constituting this condition must be knowledge of certainty. For they may have knowledge of what comes after death, but not knowledge that amounts to certainty.

---

## Page 8526

Surah al-Humazah

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

His words:

“Woe to every slanderer and backbiter.”

This means someone who maliciously attacks others and finds fault with them, as He says:

“A slanderer, going about spreading malicious gossip.”

And He says:

“Among them are those who criticize you concerning the distribution of charities.”

And He says:

“Those who criticize the believers who give charity voluntarily.”

Hamz (malicious criticism) is more severe, because hamz means a forceful thrust. From this comes the name of the letter hamzah, which is a sharp catch in the throat. Another instance is His saying:

“And say: ‘My Lord, I seek refuge in You from the incitements of the devils.’”

Another is the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“I seek refuge in Allah from the accursed Satan, from his goading, his blowing, and his spitting.”

He also said: “His goading is al-mūtah,” meaning a seizure. Thus hamz resembles taʿn (stabbing) in both expression and meaning, whereas lamz is akin to condemnation and fault-finding. He condemned only those who engage frequently in hamz [and lamz, for humazah and lumazah denote someone who does this frequently, whereas (humzah) and (lumzah)

---

## Page 8527

denote someone to whom this is done. The same distinction occurs in analogous pairs such as duhkah (someone laughed at) and duhakah (someone who laughs often), and lu‘bah (someone played with) and lu‘abah (someone who plays often).] (*)

His words:

“Who amasses wealth and repeatedly counts it.”

He describes him as attacking people and finding fault with them, and as amassing wealth and repeatedly counting it. This parallels His words in Surah al-Hadīd:

“Allah does not love anyone who is conceited and boastful.”

“Those who are miserly.”

There is a passage with a similar meaning in Surah al-Nisā’. The slanderer and backbiter resembles the conceited boaster, while the one who amasses wealth and keeps counting it resembles the miser.

Another parallel to these two traits is His saying:

“A slanderer, going about spreading malicious gossip.”

“Withholding good, a transgressor, sinful.”

“Harsh and, moreover, ignoble.”

He describes him as arrogant and miserly. Likewise, He says:

“But as for the one who is miserly and considers himself self-sufficient.”

These are five passages. Such conduct springs from the love of status and wealth: love of status drives a person to belittle others through slander, backbiting, boasting and conceit, while love of wealth drives him to miserliness. His opposite is someone who gives rather than being miserly, and fears Allah rather than slandering or backbiting.

Furthermore, the giver benefits people, while the person who fears Allah does not harm them: he thus brings benefit without causing harm. By contrast, the conceited, boastful miser deprives them of good through his miserliness and inflicts harm upon them through his boasting. He therefore harms them without benefiting them. The same applies to “the slanderer who amasses wealth.” Another example is Qārūn, who amassed wealth and belonged to the people of Moses, yet oppressed them.

Whoever reflects upon the Quran will find that its passages explain one another. For, as Ibn ‘Abbās said in the report transmitted by al-Wālibī, it encompasses categories and parables. This explains the description: “mutually consistent and oft-repeated.”

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 140):
> The vowel marks were not supplied. The intended distinction is as follows:
> Al-humaza and al-lumaza—with a fatha (a) on the mīm—denote someone who frequently disparages and finds fault with others.
> Al-humza and al-lumza—with a sukūn (no vowel) on the mīm—denote someone whom others disparage and find fault with. This is comparable to:
> Al-duhaka: someone who laughs at others; al-duhka: someone who is laughed at.
> Al-lu‘aba: someone who plays frequently; al-lu‘ba: someone or something that is played with; and so forth.

---

## Page 8528

For this reason, the Book of Allah is comprehensive, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“I have been given concise, comprehensive speech.”

Allah Most High says:

“A Book, mutually consistent and oft-repeated.”

Its mutual consistency concerns its parallels, while its repetition concerns its categories, for the dual form can signify repetition in general. This interpretation has been given to His words:

“Look again, twice over.”

Likewise, Hudhayfah said, “Between the two prostrations, we used to say: ‘My Lord, forgive me; my Lord, forgive me.’” Similarly, one says, “I did this time after time.” Thus, doubling an expression can signify repetition, for number denotes what exceeds one, beginning with two. Likewise, “I folded the garment” can refer to folding it just twice or to folding it any number of times.

The whole Quran is mutually consistent: its parts confirm one another and are not discordant. Rather, every report and command in it accords with its reports, because both share a single purpose, and because the reality to which existing things ultimately refer is one. Since the realities sought and those that exist have a single source—Allah, glorified be He—truthful discourse about them, whether reporting or commanding, is mutually consistent. It is not discordant and contradictory, as is found in the speech of most people. Even their great authors say something and then contradict it.

The whole Quran is also oft-repeated, because all the different categories are fully covered within it. Allah says:

“And of everything We created pairs.”

Mentioning both members of a pair constitutes such repetition. Describing realities as they are, so that a thing receives the same judgment as its counterpart—a judgment concerning the single meaning they share, whether expressed as a statement or a directive—is mutually consistent discourse. Thus, it is mutually consistent and oft-repeated.

---

## Page 8529

In the realm of meanings, this corresponds to the multiple senses and parallel usages of words. Any two things—whether concrete entities, accidents, or otherwise—are either alike or unlike. Those that are alike are counterparts, and bringing them together constitutes composition. When they are expressed by the same word, they constitute parallel usages. If one is unlike the other, it differs from it, whether or not the two are opposites.

One may also say that either a genus encompasses them both or it does not. If no genus encompasses them, they are remote from one another, with no correspondence between them. If a genus encompasses them, they constitute its divisions, and bringing them together constitutes classification. A single word’s signifying different meanings is called its “multiple senses.”

Comprehensive discourse fully covers the different divisions and the corresponding counterparts, bringing together what is alike and distinguishing what is different, so that its coverage remains complete. Otherwise, mentioning only one of two divisions or counterparts does not achieve completeness: the discourse is neither all-encompassing nor comprehensive. Yet this is what most people do in their speech.

Realities themselves include both what differs and what accords. Two differing realities agree in one respect and differ in another. When discourse encompasses the different divisions and the corresponding counterparts, it is comprehensive. In view of these distinctions, rational, logical syllogisms take three forms: categorical syllogisms, conjunctive conditional syllogisms, and disjunctive conditional syllogisms. The first concerns similar realities subsumed under a comprehensive proposition. The second concerns differing realities that are not mutually opposed, but sometimes entail one another and sometimes do not.

---

## Page 8530

The third concerns opposed realities that are mutually incompatible, whether in existence or nonexistence—in which case they are contradictories—or in existence alone, which is broader than contradiction, or in nonexistence alone, which is narrower than contradiction.

Thus, categorical syllogisms concern two or more similar realities, while disjunctive conditional syllogisms concern two or more opposed realities. Such reasoning is called division, testing, the posing of alternatives, and expository reasoning. Conjunctive conditional syllogisms concern two differing realities that are not opposites; this is called mutual entailment.

---

## Page 8531

Surah al-Kawthar

Shaykh al-Islām Abū al-ʿAbbās Ahmad ibn ʿAbd al-Halīm ibn ʿAbd al-Salām ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

“Surah al-Kawthar”

How magnificent this surah is, and how abundant its benefits despite its brevity! Its true meaning is understood from its ending. Allah, glorified and exalted is He, cuts off whoever hates His Messenger from every good. He cuts off his remembrance, his family, and his wealth, so that he loses these in the Hereafter. He cuts his life off from good, so that he neither benefits from it nor stores up righteous deeds during it for his return to Allah. He cuts his heart off from good, so that it cannot comprehend goodness, nor does He make it fit to know Him, love Him, and believe in His messengers. He cuts off his deeds, so that He does not employ him in any act of obedience. He cuts him off from supporters, so that he finds neither supporter nor helper.

He cuts him off from all acts of devotion and righteous deeds, so that he neither savours them nor finds any sweetness in them. Even if he performs them outwardly, his heart wanders away from them. This is the recompense of anyone who hates some of what the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—brought and rejects it on account of his own desires, someone he follows, his shaykh, his ruler, or his superior. Such is the person who hates the verses and hadiths concerning Allah’s attributes and interprets them in a way other than that intended by Allah

---

## Page 8532

and His Messenger, or construes them in accordance with his own school and that of his group, or wishes that the verses concerning Allah’s attributes had never been revealed and that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—had never uttered the hadiths concerning His attributes. One of the clearest signs of his hatred and aversion toward them is that, when he hears Ahl al-Sunnah citing them as evidence for the truth they convey, he recoils, turns away, and draws back because of the hatred and aversion toward them in his heart. Who could hate the Messenger more than such a person? Likewise, those given to musical listening, who dance to singing, poetry, tambourines, and flutes, find the Quran tedious and burdensome when they hear it recited in their gatherings. What hatred could be greater than this? Apply the same reasoning to the other groups in this regard.

The same applies to anyone who gives precedence to people’s words and learning over the Quran and Sunnah. Were he not hateful toward what the Messenger brought, he would not do so. Some even forget the Quran after having memorized it, occupying themselves instead with the words of so-and-so and so-and-so. But those who hate and reject it most are those who disbelieve in it, deny it, and describe it as “tales of the ancients” and “magic handed down.” Such a person is cut off most severely and grievously. Everyone who hates it has a share of this severance in proportion to his hatred. Because these people hated and opposed it, Allah recompensed them by making all good hostile to them and cutting them off from it. He favoured His Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—with the opposite: He granted him al-Kawthar, which is part of the abundant good Allah bestowed upon him in this world

---

## Page 8533

and in the Hereafter. Among what He granted him in this world were guidance, victory, support, joy of eye and soul, an uplifted heart, and such bliss in remembering and loving Him that no worldly pleasure could possibly compare with it. In the Hereafter, He granted him al-Wasīlah and the Praised Station, and made him and his community the first for whom the gate of Paradise would be opened. He also granted him the Banner of Praise and the great Cistern at the gathering on the Day of Resurrection, among other gifts. He made all believers his children, and he is a father to them. This is the very opposite of the condition of the one cut off, who hates him and hates what he brought.

His words, “Indeed, the one who hates you,” mean the one who detests you. The one cut off is someone whose line of descent is severed, who brings forth neither good nor righteous deeds: no good or righteous deed proceeds from him.

Abū Bakr ibn ʿAyyāsh was told, “There are people in the mosque who sit, and others gather around them.” He replied, “Whoever sits to receive people will have people gather around him. But the people of the Sunnah die while their memory lives on, whereas the people of innovation die and their memory dies with them.”

This is because the people of the Sunnah revived what the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—brought, and thus had a share in His words:

“And We have raised high your renown.”

The people of innovation hated what the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—brought, and thus had a share in His words:

“Indeed, the one who hates you is the one cut off.”

So beware, beware, O man, of hating anything that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—brought, or rejecting it because of your own desires, or in defence of your school, or

---

## Page 8534

your teacher, or because of your preoccupation with desires or worldly affairs. For the only obedience to anyone that Allah has made obligatory is obedience to His Messenger and adherence to what he brought. Thus, if a servant were to oppose all creation while following the Messenger, Allah would not question him about opposing anyone. For whoever obeys or is obeyed is only to be obeyed in subordination to the Messenger; if he commanded something contrary to the Messenger’s command, he would not be obeyed. Understand this, then: listen, obey, and follow. Do not introduce innovations, lest you become cut off and your deeds be rejected. Indeed, there is no good in any deed cut off from following the Messenger, nor in the one who performs it. And Allah knows best.

As for His words, exalted is He:

“Indeed, We have granted you al-Kawthar.”

This verse indicates an abundant gift from a mighty, self-sufficient Giver of boundless bounty. It also indicates that He, exalted is He, His angels, and His host are with him.

He began the verse with inna (“indeed”), which conveys emphasis and affirms the truth of the statement. The verb appears in the past tense, indicating certainty and that this is an established reality. This is not negated by its indication that the granting of al-Kawthar had already been ordained in the original decree, when the destinies of all creatures were determined fifty thousand years before He created them.

He omitted the noun qualified by al-kawthar (“abundant”), making the expression more emphatically general by leaving it unspecified, and used the adjective instead. That is, He, glorified and exalted is He, said:

“Indeed, We have granted you al-Kawthar.”

He thus described it as abundant. The well-known al-Kawthar is a river in Paradise, as explicitly stated in authentic hadiths. Ibn ʿAbbās said that al-Kawthar is part of the abundant good that Allah granted him. And if the lowest-ranking of the inhabitants of

---

## Page 8535

Paradise has the equivalent of ten times this world, what, then, must we think of what Allah has prepared there for the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him? Al-Kawthar is thus a sign and an indication of the manifold blessings Allah has prepared for him, their continuity and increase, and his lofty and exalted rank. That river, al-Kawthar, is the greatest of the rivers of Paradise, with the finest, freshest, and sweetest water, and is the highest of them. This is because He used the definite article, which indicates the perfection and completeness of what is named.

This is like saying, “Zayd is the learned man; Zayd is the brave man,” meaning that no one is more learned or braver than he is. Likewise, His words:

“Indeed, We have granted you al-Kawthar.”

These indicate that He has granted him all goodness, complete and abundant. If any member of his community receives a share of it, that share comes through the blessing of following him and taking him as an example. Moreover, he, peace and blessings be upon him, receives a reward equal to that person’s, without diminishing the follower’s reward in any way. This points to Allah Most High granting him, in Paradise, the equivalent of the rewards of his entire community, without diminishing their rewards, for he is the means of their guidance and salvation.

The servant should therefore—indeed, must—follow him, take him as an example, comply with his commands, and perform abundant righteous deeds: fasting, prayer, charity, and purification, so that the Messenger may receive a reward equal to his. For when the servant commits forbidden acts, the Messenger misses out on a reward equal to that for the good the servant has neglected. If the servant commits what is forbidden while abandoning what is commanded, his burden of sin grows heavier and his salvation becomes more difficult, because he has both committed the forbidden and abandoned the commanded. But if he performs what is commanded while committing what is forbidden, he will be among those for whom intercession is made

---

## Page 8536

by the Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, since he receives a reward equal to that person’s for fulfilling what was commanded. To Allah all creation will return, and with Him rests their reckoning. He knows their condition best—that is, the circumstances of His servants. His intercession is for those of his community who commit major sins. Whoever does good does so only because Allah enables him, while the wrongdoer has neither a defence nor an excuse.

The point is that al-Kawthar is a river in Paradise, and it forms part of the abundant good Allah has granted His Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, in this world and the Hereafter. This is in addition to the reward Allah grants him equal to the rewards of his community until the Day of Resurrection. Thus, whenever anyone reads, acquires knowledge, performs a righteous deed, teaches another, gives charity, performs Hajj, undertakes jihad, guards the frontiers, repents, remains patient, places his trust in Allah, or attains any spiritual station of the heart—such as reverent awe, fear, knowledge of Allah, or anything else—the Messenger receives a reward equal to his, without diminishing that person’s reward in any way. And Allah knows best.

As for His words:

“So pray to your Lord and sacrifice.”

Allah commanded him to combine these two great acts of worship: prayer and sacrifice. Both express nearness to Allah, humility, dependence upon Him, thinking well of Him, firm certainty, and the heart’s peaceful trust in Allah, His assurance, His command, His bounty, and His recompense. This is the opposite of the condition of those marked by arrogance and aversion, who consider themselves independent of Allah: those who bring no need before their Lord in prayer to ask Him to fulfil, and who do not offer sacrifices to Him out of fear of poverty, unwillingness to help the poor and give to them, and ill thoughts of their Lord. This is why Allah joined the two together in His words, Exalted is He:

“Say,

---

## Page 8537

my prayer, my sacrifice, my living and my dying are for Allah, Lord of all worlds.” Sacrifice here means slaughtering an animal seeking His pleasure.

The point is that prayer and sacrifice are the greatest acts by which one draws near to Allah. He introduces the command to perform them with the particle fa (“so”), indicating causation: performing these acts—prayer and sacrifice—is a means of giving thanks for the al-Kawthar and abundant good that Allah has granted him. These two acts of worship are the greatest expressions of the recipient’s gratitude to his Benefactor and worship of Him. Indeed, prayer is the pinnacle of worship and the ultimate goal.

It is as though He were saying:

“Indeed, We have granted you al-Kawthar”—

abundant good—and bestowed this blessing upon you so that you may perform these two acts of worship for Us in gratitude for Our favor upon you. They are also the reason for Our bestowing this favor upon you, so perform them for Us. Prayer and sacrifice are thus preceded by favor and followed by further favor.

The greatest act of worship involving wealth is sacrifice, and the greatest bodily act of worship is prayer. What comes together for the servant in prayer does not come together for him in any other act of worship, as those with living hearts and lofty aspirations know. Likewise, what comes together when he offers sacrifice—giving Allah precedence, thinking well of Him, firm certainty, and trust in what is in Allah’s hands—is truly remarkable when accompanied by faith and sincerity.

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, obeyed his Lord’s command: he prayed frequently to his Lord and offered many sacrifices. During the Farewell Pilgrimage, he slaughtered sixty-three camels with his own hands, and he would offer sacrifices on the Eids and at other times.

In His words:

“Indeed, We have granted you al-Kawthar.”

“So pray to your Lord and sacrifice.”

there is an indication

---

## Page 8538

that you should not grieve over anything of this world, as mentioned at the end of Surahs Tā Hā and al-Hijr, and elsewhere. These verses also indicate that you should pay no heed to people or to what you suffer at their hands; rather, pray to your Lord and sacrifice. They also allude to the condition of the hater who is cut off, whose prayer and sacrifice are devoted to other than Allah.

His words,

“Indeed, it is the one who hates you who is cut off,”

contain several forms of emphasis:

First: beginning the sentence with inna (“indeed”).

Second: using the separating pronoun, which reinforces the statement and conveys exclusivity.

Third: using the elative form for the predicate rather than a passive participle.

Fourth: making it definite with the definite article, indicating that this quality applies to him in its entirety and that he deserves this description more than anyone else. A similar example of emphasis occurs in His words:

“Do not fear; indeed, it is you who will have the upper hand.”

Among the surah’s subtle lessons is the shift in grammatical person in His words:

“So pray to your Lord and sacrifice.”

This indicates that your Lord deserves this worship and that it is fitting for you to worship Him and offer sacrifice to Him. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 8539

Surah al-Kāfirūn (The Disbelievers)

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

In the surah “Say, O disbelievers,” scholars have offered several explanations for the repetition of the disavowal on both sides, where Allah says:

“I do not worship what you worship,
nor are you worshippers of what I worship.”

Then He says:

“Nor am I a worshipper of what you have worshipped,
nor are you worshippers of what I worship.”

Among these explanations are two well-known views mentioned by many commentators: is the statement repeated for emphasis, or to deny such worship both now and in the future?

Abū al-Faraj said:

“There are two views concerning this repetition. The first is that it emphasizes the point and puts an end to their hopes of winning him over. This was al-Farrā’s view.

“We have treated this fully in Surah al-Rahmān. Ibn Qutaybah said: ‘The repetition in Surah al-Rahmān is for emphasis.’ He said: ‘It is the Arabs’ practice to repeat for emphasis and to ensure understanding, just as they employ brevity for ease

---

## Page 8540

and conciseness. For it is better for a learner or an orator to employ varied forms of expression than to confine himself to a single form throughout.

Someone may say, “By Allah, I will not do it; again, by Allah, I will not do it,” when he wishes to emphasize his refusal and extinguish any hope that he will do it. Similarly, when seeking brevity, he may say, “By Allah, I will do it,” leaving the negative “not” implicit. To a hurried messenger, one says, “Hurry, hurry!” and to an archer, “Shoot, shoot!”

A poet said:
“How many blessings you once enjoyed—how many, how many!”

Another said:
“Did you ask the hosts of Kindah,
on the day they fled, ‘Where? Where?’”

Sometimes they wish to emphasize a descriptive word but find it awkward to repeat the very same word, so they change one of its letters.’

Ibn Qutaybah said: ‘When Allah enumerated His blessings in this surah, reminded His servants of His favors, and drew their attention to His power, He placed each repeated statement between two blessings, so that they would understand those blessings and acknowledge them. It is like saying to someone: “Did I not give you lodging when you were an outcast? Do you deny this? Did I not take you on pilgrimage when you had never performed it before? Do you deny this?”’”

I say: Ibn Qutaybah said: “The repetition of the words in ‘Say, O disbelievers’

---

## Page 8541

corresponds to repeated periods of time. This was because they said, ‘If you would like us to follow your religion for a year, then follow our religion for a year.’ So this surah was revealed.”

I say: The repetition of wording that he mentioned is indeed found in the speech of Arabs and non-Arabs alike, for all nations emphasize requests or statements by repeating their words. An example is the report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“By Allah, I will certainly campaign against Quraysh! Again, by Allah, I will certainly campaign against Quraysh! Again, by Allah, I will certainly campaign against Quraysh!”

He then said, “If Allah wills,” but he did not campaign against them.

It is also reported that, during the expedition to Tabūk, Hudhayfah was leading his mount while ‘Ammār was driving it from behind. A group of over ten men set out and ascended the mountain pass on their mounts, their faces veiled. They had intended to assassinate the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He said to Hudhayfah, “Lead on! Lead on!” and to ‘Ammār, “Drive on! Drive on!”

There are many more examples of this, but nothing of this kind occurs in the Quran. The Quran has a unique character: no human speech resembles it, whether that of a prophet or anyone else, even though it was revealed in the Arabic language. No created being can produce a surah, or even part of a surah, like it. Thus, nowhere in the Quran is the very same wording repeated immediately after its first occurrence. Rather, in

---

## Page 8542

Surah al-Rahmān, He repeats that address after each verse, rather than in uninterrupted succession. This manner of expression is loftier than the first. Likewise, the Quran’s narratives contain no repetition of the kind some have supposed. Nor does “Say, O disbelievers” contain any repeated wording except His saying:

“Nor are you worshippers of what I worship.”

Even here, a sentence separates the two occurrences.

They have compared what occurs in Surah al-Rahmān to the words of someone addressing a person whom he has treated well and showered with favours, but who denies them and shows ingratitude:

“Were you not poor, and did I not enrich you? Do you deny this? Were you not naked, and did I not clothe you? Do you deny this? Were you not unknown, and did I not make you known?”

And so forth. This is a closer comparison than consecutive repetition, as in the repeated oath.

Likewise, some say that one expression may be joined to another merely because the wording differs, as in the verse:

“He found her words a lie and a falsehood.”

There is nothing of this kind in the Quran. Allah never includes an additional word in it without an additional meaning, even if that meaning includes emphasis. Whatever additional wording occurs in such statements as His saying:

“So it was by mercy from Allah that you were gentle with them,”

and His saying:

“Before long, they will surely become regretful,”

and His saying:

“How little you take heed!”

conveys more meaning than would be conveyed without it. Thus, additional wording conveys additional meaning, and the strength of the wording corresponds to the strength of the meaning. The dammah (u-vowel) is stronger

---

## Page 8543

than the kasrah (i-vowel), and the kasrah is stronger than the fathah (a-vowel). Thus, the dammah is assigned to the stronger meaning, as in kurh and karh. Kurh denotes something disliked, as in His saying:

“Fighting has been prescribed for you, though it is hateful to you.”

Karh is the verbal noun, as in His saying:

“Willingly or unwillingly.”

Something that is itself disliked is stronger in meaning than the mere dislike felt by the one who dislikes it. The same applies to dhibh and dhabh. Dhibh denotes what is slaughtered, as in His saying:

“And We ransomed him with a mighty sacrifice.”

Dhabh denotes the act of slaughtering. A dhibh is something slaughtered—a body subjected to slaughter—and is thus more complete than the act alone.

Abū al-Faraj said:

“The second interpretation is that the meaning is: ‘I do not worship what you worship’ in my present state; ‘nor are you,’ in your present state, ‘worshippers of what I worship’; and ‘nor will I worship what you have worshipped’ in the future. The same applies to ‘you.’ Thus, he denies this of them both in the present and in the future. This concerns particular people whom Allah informed him would not believe, as we have related from Muqātil. On this interpretation, therefore, there is no repetition.”

He said: “This is the view of Tha‘lab and al-Zajjāj.”

I say: A number of scholars have mentioned both interpretations, but some have attributed the first interpretation to most scholars concerned with the meanings of the Quran.

They said—the wording is al-Baghawī’s:

“The verse means: I do not worship what you worship at present, nor will I worship what you have worshipped in the future

---

## Page 8544

nor will you worship what I worship in the future. This addresses those whom Allah knew beforehand would not believe.”

He said: “Most scholars concerned with the meanings of the Quran said:

‘It was revealed in the language of the Arabs, following their customary modes of speech. Among their practices is repetition for emphasis and clarification, just as they also use abridgment for ease and brevity.’”

I say:

Some commentators mentioned only the second interpretation, including al-Mahdawī and Ibn ‘Atiyyah.

Ibn ‘Atiyyah said:

“Since His words, ‘I do not worship,’ could mean ‘at present,’ leaving the future open as to what he might worship, clarification came in His words:

‘Nor will I worship what you have worshipped,’

meaning: never, as long as I live.

Then the second occurrence of His words:

‘Nor will you worship what I worship,’

declared decisively that they would never believe, as people whose unseen future had been disclosed, just as Noah was told:

‘None of your people will believe except those who have already believed.’

Note, however, that this addresses particular individuals, whereas that declaration encompassed the people of Noah collectively.”

He said:

“This, then, is the meaning of the reiteration in the surah, and it is masterfully eloquent. It is not mere repetition; rather, it conveys what I have mentioned, together with forceful expression, emphasis, further clarification of the matter, and disavowal of them.”

I say:

This interpretation is better than the preceding one insofar as its proponents explain a meaning

---

## Page 8545

beyond mere repetition. Yet it is deficient in another respect: they take this to be an address to particular individuals, thereby restricting the surah’s meaning. This is an error.

For His words:

“Say, ‘O disbelievers!’”

address every disbeliever. The Prophet used to recite this surah in Medina after those particular individuals had died, instruct others to recite it, and say, “It is a disavowal of polytheism.” Had it addressed only those particular individuals, or those among them whom he knew would die as disbelievers, he would not have addressed with it anyone whose ultimate fate he did not know. Moreover, even if it is true that he addressed only those particular individuals, he did not know at that time that they would die in disbelief. The claim that he addressed only particular individuals with this surah was not made by anyone whose authority can be relied upon.

Muqātil ibn Sulaymān did, however, say that it was revealed concerning Abū Jahl and the mockers, and that none of those concerning whom it was revealed ever believed. Reports transmitted by Muqātil alone cannot be relied upon, by agreement of the hadith scholars, just like those transmitted by al-Kalbī. This is why scholars of transmitted reports who compiled Quranic commentaries—such as Muhammad ibn Jarīr, ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Abī Hātim, and Abū Bakr ibn al-Mundhir—do not cite anything from either of them, let alone scholars such as Ahmad ibn Hanbal and Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh. Others have related this concerning Quraysh generally, as reported by ‘Abd ibn Humayd

---

## Page 8546

from Wahb ibn Munabbih, who said:

Quraysh said to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “If it would please you, we could follow your religion for a year, and you could follow ours for a year.” Then the surah beginning “Say, ‘O disbelievers!’” was revealed in full.

It is also reported from Ibn ‘Abbās that Quraysh said, “O Muhammad, if you were to touch our gods, we would worship your God.” Then the surah was revealed.

Qatādah said, “Allah commanded him to address the disbelievers, so he addressed them with His words, ‘O you.’” Ibn Abī Hātim also narrated this from Wahb ibn Munabbih, who began, “The disbelievers of Quraysh said,” and then related the report.

‘Ikrimah said, “Through this surah, Allah dissociated him from the worshippers of all idols and the religion of all disbelievers.” Qatādah said, “Allah commanded His Prophet to disavow the polytheists, and he disavowed them.”

Qatādah narrated from Zurārah ibn Awfā that it used to be called “al-Muqashqishah (the healer).” The expression qashqasha fulān is used when someone has recovered from an illness; thus, this surah frees those who hold to it from polytheism. This is how the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, described it in the well-known hadith recorded in al-Musnad and by al-Tirmidhī, transmitted by Isrā’īl from Abū Ishāq, from Farwah ibn Nawfal, from his father, who reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to him:

“What brings you here?”

He replied, “I have come, O Messenger of Allah, so that you may teach me something to say when I go to sleep.”

He said, “When you lie down to sleep, recite ‘Say, O disbelievers!’ Then go to sleep with

---

## Page 8547

its final words on your lips, for it is a disavowal of polytheism.”

Several narrators transmitted it from Abū Ishāq, who sometimes related it with a connected chain and sometimes with a link omitted. Zuhayr and Isrā’īl transmitted it from him with a connected chain. Shu‘bah also transmitted it from him but did not mention “from his father.” Instead, he said, “From Abū Ishāq, from a man, from Farwah ibn Nawfal,” without saying, “from his father.”

Al-Tirmidhī said, “Zuhayr’s narration is more likely to be correct and more authentic than Shu‘bah’s narration.” He added, “This hadith has also been transmitted through another chain: ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Nawfal narrated it from his father, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Nawfal is the brother of Farwah ibn Nawfal.”

I say: Ismā‘īl ibn Abī Khālid also transmitted it from Abū Ishāq, who said:

“A man from Ashja‘ came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, teach me some words to say when I go to sleep.’

“He replied, ‘You are a foster father to us. Recite “Say, O disbelievers!” when you go to sleep, for it is a disavowal of polytheism.’”

Thus, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed one of the Muslims to recite this surah and told him that it is a disavowal of polytheism. If its address were directed only to those who die as polytheists, it would be a disavowal of their religion alone, not of the polytheism practised by someone who later embraces Islam. Yet it is clear that its purpose is to disavow every form of polytheism, whether in belief or practice.

---

## Page 8548

And His words:

“For you is your religion, and for me is my religion.”

This addresses every disbeliever, even if he later embraces Islam. His religion before Islam was his own, and the believers disavow it, even if Allah forgives him for it when he repents of it. As He said to His Prophet:

“But if they disobey you, say, ‘I am free of what you do.’”

For he disavows the sins of his Companions, even if they repent of them. This is like His words:

“And if they deny you, say, ‘For me are my deeds, and for you are your deeds. You are free of responsibility for what I do, and I am free of responsibility for what you do.’”

Ibn Abī Hātim related: My father narrated to us: Muhammad ibn Mūsā al-Jurashī narrated to us: Abū Khalaf ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Īsā narrated to us: Dāwūd ibn Abī Hind narrated to us, from ‘Ikrimah, from Ibn ‘Abbās:

“Quraysh offered the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, enough wealth to make him the richest man among them, to marry him to whichever women he wished, and to follow at his heels—that is, to make him their leader. They said, ‘We offer you all this, O Muhammad, if you stop reviling our gods and do not speak ill of them. If you will not, we offer you one proposal that will benefit both you and us.’

“He asked, ‘What is it?’

“They said, ‘Worship our gods, al-Lāt and al-‘Uzzā, for a year, and we will worship your God for a year.’

“He said, ‘Let me wait until I see what comes to me from my Lord.’

“Then revelation came to him from Allah, from the Preserved Tablet: ‘Say, O disbelievers!’ to the end of the surah. Allah also revealed to him:

“‘Say, “Is it other than Allah that you command me to worship, O ignorant ones?”’

“‘It has certainly been revealed to you and to those before you: “If you associate partners with Allah, your deeds will surely come to nothing, and you will certainly be among the losers.”’

“‘Rather, worship Allah and be among the grateful.’”

---

## Page 8549

And His words:

“Say, ‘Is it other than Allah that you command me to worship, O ignorant ones?’”

address everyone who worships other than Allah, even if it has been decreed that he will later repent. Likewise, every believer addresses these words to anyone who worships other than Allah.

As for his words in this hadith, “Until I see what comes to me from my Lord,” someone may say this intending to ward off wrongdoers in the best manner, so that his argument will be that the one whom he is bound to obey has forbidden it. He therefore postpones his answer until he seeks permission, even though he knows that their proposal is entirely out of the question.

A man may receive a proposal for his daughter’s hand and say, “Until I consult her mother,” intending thereby not to marry her to the suitor and knowing that her mother will not advise him to do so. Likewise, a deputy may say, “Until I consult the ruler.” Such an answer implies neither hesitation on his part nor any consideration that Allah might permit him to do that.

A number of men from Quraysh had commanded him and his Companions to worship other than Allah, fought them, and shown them intense hostility over this. Yet they later repented, embraced Islam, and recited this surah.

Some transmitters identify individuals different from those identified by others. Some mention Abū Jahl and a group; others mention ‘Utbah ibn Rabī‘ah and a group; while others

---

## Page 8550

mention al-Walīd ibn Mughīrah and a group.

Some say that they proposed worshipping Allah with him for a year, on condition that he worship their gods with them for a year. Others say that they asked him to touch their idols reverently. Still others say that they proposed sharing in one another’s worship, as Ibn Abī Hātim and others narrated from Ibn Ishāq, who said: Sa‘īd ibn Mīnā, the client of Abū al-Bakhtarī, related to me:

“Al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah, al-‘Ās ibn Wā’il, al-Aswad ibn al-Muttalib, and Umayyah ibn Khalaf met the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said: ‘Come, let us worship what you worship, and you worship what we worship, so that you and we may share in all our religious affairs. If what you have brought is better than what we have, we will have shared in it with you and received our portion of it. And if what we have is better than what you have, you will have shared in our religion and received your portion of it.’ Then Allah revealed the surah.”

This is also related from ‘Ubayd ibn ‘Umayr, whose account identifies ‘Utbah and Umayyah as those who spoke to him. These reports thus agree on one meaning: they asked him to adopt something of their religion, while they would adopt something of his. If all these reports are authentic, then different proposals were made to him on different occasions, with one group proposing one thing and another group proposing another. In any case, the address encompasses all polytheists, those who have gone before and those who will come until the Day of Resurrection.

---

## Page 8551

Allah commanded him to disavow everything worshipped besides Him. This is the religion of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, and he was sent with that same religion. Allah Most High said:

“And when Abraham said to his father and his people, ‘I am free of what you worship, except the One who created me, for He will guide me.’ And he made this an enduring word among his descendants.”

Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, also said:

“O my people, I am free of whatever you associate with Allah. I have turned my face towards the One who created the heavens and the earth, inclining solely to Him, and I am not one of the polytheists.”

Allah also said:

“You have an excellent example in Abraham and those with him, when they said to their people, ‘We are free of you and whatever you worship besides Allah. We reject you, and enmity and hatred have arisen between us and you forever, until you believe in Allah alone.’”

He said to His Prophet:

“If they deny you, say, ‘To me belong my deeds, and to you belong yours. You are innocent of what I do, and I am innocent of what you do.’”

Thus Allah commanded him to disavow the deeds of everyone who denied him. This disavowal encompasses both the polytheists and the People of the Book. Al-Mahdawī mentioned this view alongside two others. He said:

“The definite article refers to a known group, although it denotes a class insofar as the word is an adjective, for its lām addresses those whom Allah knew beforehand would die as disbelievers. Thus, this is a particular reference expressed in general terms. The passages repeated in this surah involve no repetition in either meaning or wording, except in

---

## Page 8552

one instance, where the repetition is in wording but not in meaning. Rather, ‘I do not worship what you worship’ refers to the present; ‘Nor do you worship what I worship’ refers to the present; ‘Nor am I a worshipper of what you have worshipped’ refers to the future; and ‘Nor do you worship what I worship’ refers to the future.”

He said: “Thus, both the wording and the meaning differ between His words ‘I do not worship’ and the subsequent ‘Nor am I.’ But ‘Nor do you worship what I worship’ is repeated in wording, not in meaning.”

He said: “It has also been said that the first occurrence means, ‘Nor do you worship what I have worshipped,’ while the second means, ‘Nor do you worship what I worship.’ The wording ‘I have worshipped’ was avoided to indicate that what he worshipped in the past is what he will worship in the future. One tense may stand in place of the other, and this occurs most often in statements made by Allah Most High.

“It is also possible that mā together with its verb forms a verbal noun. It has been said that the meaning of these verses, expressed in full, is: ‘Say: O disbelievers, I do not worship the idols that you worship. Nor do you worship the One whom I worship, because you associate partners with Him and take idols alongside Him. If you claim that you worship Him, you are lying, for you worship Him while associating partners with Him. Thus, I do not worship as you have worshipped’—that is, in the manner of your worship. In the second occurrence, therefore, it forms a verbal noun. Likewise, in ‘Nor do you worship what I worship,’ the second occurrence also forms a verbal noun, meaning: ‘Nor do you worship in the manner of my worship, which is worshipping Allah alone.’”

---

## Page 8553

I say:

The third view is equivalent in meaning to the second, but assigns two meanings to His words, “Nor do you worship what I worship”: one occurrence means “what I have worshipped,” and the other means “what I worship.” This makes them correspond to His words addressed to them: “I do not worship what you worship” and “Nor am I a worshipper of what you have worshipped.” Thus, just as he disavowed worshipping, in the present and future, what they worshipped in the past and worship in the present, he likewise declared that they did not worship what he worships in the present and future. However, the wording referring to them was expressed in the past tense.

Those who hold this view said: “The expression ‘what I have worshipped’ was not used in his case, to indicate that what he worshipped in the past is what he worships in the future.”

I say:

The proponents of this view sought correspondence, as explained above. But if “what you have worshipped” refers to the past, as does “what I worship” in one of its two occurrences, then, according to their explanation, the full meaning would be: “Nor will I worship in the future what you worshipped in the past.” He would thus have denied that he would worship in the future what they worshipped in the past, without denying that he would worship what they worship in the future.

Likewise, if “Nor do you worship what I worship” means what was worshipped in the past, then whether their worship is understood as present or future, it only negates worship of what they worshipped in the past. This detracts from the meaning of the verse. How could he disavow worshipping in the future only what they worshipped in the past? And likewise in their case?

---

## Page 8554

If it is argued, “In the future they may worship Allah by abandoning disbelief, whereas he does not, either now or in the future, worship what they have worshipped,” the response is: On this interpretation, they cannot be told, “Nor will you worship in the future what I worshipped in the past.” Rather, if they abandon disbelief, they may in the future worship his Lord, whom he worshipped in the past.

If it is argued, “Their view is the second view: I do not worship now what you worship now, nor will I worship in the future what you worship in the future,” the response is: The wording of the verse is:

“Nor am I a worshipper of what you have worshipped,”

not, “Nor am I a worshipper of what you worship.”

Thus, if His words “what you have worshipped” refer to the past, as these proponents intended, the meaning becomes unsound. If they refer to the future, this invalidates their contention that the imperfect verb carries a past meaning in His words:

“Nor do you worship what I worship.”

For the past-tense verb here then carries the meaning of the imperfect. If the imperfect verb corresponds to it, it remains an imperfect verb and is not shifted into the past. The result is therefore the opposite of what they intended.

The fourth view he mentioned is that of those who treat mā as a particle introducing a verbal noun in the second statement, but not in the other. Here too, nothing in the wording indicates any distinction between them. Treating it as introducing a verbal noun in all the statements would be closer to the correct interpretation. Yet the meaning conveyed by mā when it introduces a verbal noun is already conveyed by His use of mā (“what”). For He did not say, “Nor do you worship whom I worship”; rather, He said, “what I worship.”

---

## Page 8555

The word mā (“what”) indicates a quality, unlike man (“who”), which denotes the individual. Thus, He says:

“Marry such women as are pleasing to you,”

meaning those who are pleasing; and:

“By the sky and Him who built it,”

meaning its Builder. A similar example is His saying:

“When he said to his sons, ‘What will you worship after me?’ They said, ‘We will worship your God and the God of your fathers.’”

He did not say, “Whom will you worship after me?” This is exactly parallel to His saying:

“Nor do you worship what I worship.”

The meaning, then, is: I do not worship the object of your worship, nor do you worship the object of mine.

His statement, “Nor do you worship what I worship,” also applies to their associating partners with Allah, for that is not worship of Allah. Allah accepts only deeds done sincerely for His sake. When they associate partners with Him, they are not worshipping Him, even if they supplicate to Him and pray to Him.

Moreover, they have not worshipped what he worships: the One described as the exclusive object of his worship. Rather, this encompasses worshipping Him alone, and it encompasses the Lord of whom he has spoken, with His names and attributes. Whoever denies anything he has reported about Him has not, in every respect, worshipped what he worships.

Furthermore, religious laws may differ in their prescribed acts of worship, so that the One worshipped is the same even though the manner of worship differs. Such people are not to be disavowed. Thus, everyone who worships Allah

---

## Page 8556

with sincere devotion to Him is a Muslim at all times. Yet his worship of Him must conform to what He has prescribed.

Had he said, “I do not worship in your manner, nor do you worship in mine,” one might suppose that this includes disavowal of every act of worship whose outward form differs from his own. Rather, the disavowal concerns the object of worship and the worship of it.

Section

Having clarified this, we say:

The Quran is a revelation from One who is All-Wise, Praiseworthy. It is a Book whose verses have been perfected and then explained in detail. If a human being had some knowledge or wisdom to convey, or a sermon, poem, or written work, and carefully refined its wording, introducing variations like these, it would be understood that he intended some wise purpose thereby, and that he had not varied the wording pointlessly while keeping the meaning the same. How much more, then, must this apply to the speech of the Lord of all worlds, the wisest of judges—especially when He has said concerning it:

“Say, ‘If mankind and the jinn gathered together to produce the like of this Quran, they could not produce its like, even if they helped one another.’”

We therefore say:

In the language, the imperfect verb denotes ongoing time other than the past, thus encompassing both the present and the future. As Sībawayh said: “And they formed it to express what has elapsed of

---

## Page 8557

time, for what is ongoing and has not ceased, and for what has yet to come”—meaning the past tense, the imperfect, and the imperative. The imperfect was thus assigned to time that is ongoing and has not ceased, and it may encompass both the present and the future.

The words “I do not worship” therefore negate his worship of their object of worship in both the present and the future. The words “what you worship” encompass what they worship in the present and the future. Both verbs are in the imperfect.

In the second statement, he says of himself:

“Nor am I a worshipper of what you worshipped.”

He does not say, “I do not worship,” but rather, “Nor am I a worshipper.” Nor does he say, “what you worship,” but rather, “what you worshipped.” Thus, the wording describing his action and theirs differs from that in the first statement. The negation in this second statement is more comprehensive than that in the first, for he says, “Nor am I a worshipper of what you worshipped,” using the past tense. This encompasses what they worshipped in the past, because the polytheists worship various gods. What they worship at one time is not necessarily what they worship at another, just as each group has an object of worship different from that of another group.

Thus, the words “I do not worship what you worship” constitute a disavowal of everything they worshipped in times

---

## Page 8558

past, just as he initially disavowed what they worship in the present and future. Together, the two statements therefore express disavowal of everything that polytheists and disbelievers worship at any time—past, present, or future.

His first statement:

“I do not worship what you worship”

does not encompass all of this. In His words:

“Nor am I a worshipper,”

the active participle functions as a verb and is not in a genitive construction; it therefore also encompasses the present and future.

It is, however, a nominal sentence, and negation by means of what follows the verb carries an additional meaning. Thus, you say, “I do not do this,” and “I am not one to do it.” Saying, “He is not one ever to do this,” is more emphatic than saying, “He never does it.” The former denies that such an act could proceed from the person himself. By contrast, saying, “He does not do this,” does not rule out his being capable of it or its being permissible for him, nor does it indicate that it is unsuitable or unbecoming for him. This differs from saying, “He is not one to do it,” or, more emphatically, “He is certainly not one to do it,” as in His words:

“Those who have been favored would not hand over their provision to those whom their right hands possess.”

And His words:

“I cannot come to your aid, nor can you come to mine.”

And His words:

“Allah is not unaware of what you do.”

“Nor can you guide the blind.”

“Nor can you make those in the graves hear.”

“They cannot harm anyone through it except by Allah’s permission.”

Nor may it be said that the nominal sentence abandons permanence, and that negating this does not entail negating

---

## Page 8559

its occasional occurrence. For this sentence conveys negation like a verbal sentence, since its participle functions as a verb. Yet it concerns a characteristic of the person himself, and thus denies that such an act could occur on his part, declaring him above it and denying any disposition in him to accept it. The first negates the act in the past and future; the second negates his acceptance of it in the past, as well as the present and future. Thus, His words:

“Nor am I a worshipper of what you have worshipped”

mean: I myself cannot accept, nor is it fitting for me, to worship anything you have ever worshipped, even if you worshipped it only in the past. Whatever object of worship you have worshipped at any time, I will not accept worshipping it at any time whatsoever.

This encompasses their worship in the past and future, and expresses the strength of his disavowal, refusal, and rejection of such worship at all times, in a way that the first sentence does not. That sentence negates the act at times other than the past, whereas this one negates the possibility of his worshipping—and his acceptance of worshipping—anything they have worshipped, even if only during some period in the past.

The meaning is: Whatever you have worshipped, even during some period in the past, I can never worship, nor is it ever permissible for me to worship it. However, he negates only what might proceed from him in the present and future, because the point is his own disavowal in the present and future. Every Muslim is commanded to recite this surah, even if he associated partners with Allah before reciting it.

---

## Page 8560

Thus, in the present and future he disavows whatever the polytheists worship at any time. He denies that he may worship their object of worship and makes clear that such a thing cannot occur, is unfitting, and is impermissible. He thus denies both its religious permissibility and its possible occurrence.

Such language is used only of reprehensible actions, as when someone is invited to commit an injustice or an indecency and says:

“Would I do such a thing? I am not one who would ever do that!”

This is more emphatic than saying, “I will never do it.” It is like His words:

“You will not follow their direction of prayer, nor will they follow one another’s direction of prayer.”

This conveys a refusal to perform the act out of hatred and aversion to it, unlike saying, “I will not do it.” A person may refrain from something for another purpose while still loving it. Thus, when he says, “I am not a worshipper of what you have worshipped,” this expresses hatred, aversion, and loathing for their object of worship and for their worship of it. This is disavowal.

Hence, disavowal is used as the opposite of allegiance: one says, “Give your allegiance to so-and-so and disavow so-and-so.” As Allah Most High says:

“When they said to their people, ‘We disavow you and whatever you worship besides Allah’”—to the end of the verse.

As for His words concerning the disbelievers:

“Nor are you worshippers of what I worship,”

this addresses disbelievers as a class, even if they later embrace Islam. It addresses them so long as they remain disbelievers. Once they embrace Islam, it no longer applies to them, for they are then believers, not disbelievers.

---

## Page 8561

If they are hypocrites, they are inwardly disbelievers, so the address applies to them.

This is like saying:

“Say: O you who wage war, dispute, fight, and show hostility!”

The address applies to them so long as they retain these characteristics. So long as a disbeliever remains a disbeliever, he does not worship Allah; rather, he worships Satan, whether he openly professes this or not, as in the case of the Jews. For the Jews do not worship Allah; rather, they worship Satan, because worship of Allah can only be through what He has prescribed and commanded. Although they claim to worship Him, those altered and forbidden practices are things He detests, hates, and forbids; they are therefore not worship.

Thus, anyone who disbelieves in Muhammad does not worship what Muhammad worships so long as he remains a disbeliever. The present-tense verb encompasses what continues without interruption. Hence, so long as he remains a disbeliever, he does not worship the One whom Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—worships, whether in the present or the future. He did not say of them:

“Nor do you worship what I worship.”

Rather, He used a nominal sentence to make clear that your very souls, wicked and disbelieving as they are, are devoid of worship of the God of Muhammad. They cannot worship Him so long as they remain disbelieving, for they can be His worshippers only by worshipping Him

---

## Page 8562

alone, in accordance with what He commanded through Muhammad. Whoever disbelieves in Muhammad can never have his deeds count as worship of Allah. The declaration that they are devoid of the worship of Allah uses the same wording: a nominal sentence indicating that their very selves are devoid of worship of Allah, rather than merely negating the act. He did not need to say of them:

“Nor are you worshippers of what I have worshipped,”

as he said of himself:

“Nor am I a worshipper of what you have worshipped,”

for two reasons.

The first is that every believer is commanded to recite this surah, and some believers formerly worshipped something other than Allah. Had such a person said, “Nor are you worshippers of what I have worshipped,” they could have replied, “On the contrary, we worship what you used to worship when you were a polytheist.” This would not apply if he said, “Nor are you worshippers of what I worship at this time.”

Nor did he say, “what I am a worshipper of,” since the speaker himself may not worship Him at all times. An individual may worship something other than Allah in the future; someone who does not worship what that individual worships in the future would therefore not be blameworthy. By contrast, when a believer addresses others with this surah, he worships none but Allah at the time he recites it. He is thus saying to the disbelievers, “Nor are you worshippers of what I worship now.”

The negation concerning the disbelievers is repeated in both sentences so that each corresponds closely to its counterpart. Thus, when he said:

“I do not worship what you worship,”

negating the act, he then said:

“Nor are you worshippers of what I worship.”

---

## Page 8563

Then, when he strengthened the negation by denying the permissibility of such worship and declaring himself free of it, expressing his aversion to it and its ugliness, and denying that he would worship anything they had worshipped, even for a time, he said:

“Nor are you worshippers of what I worship.”

Rather, you have no part in worshipping what I worship. My disavowal—my complete disavowal and remoteness from what you worship, and my complete nearness to Allah through worshipping Him alone, without partner—does not mean that you have any share in this worship. Rather, in this state too, you do not worship what I worship: neither in the first state nor in the second.

Had he confined his declaration that they had no part in worshipping Allah to the first sentence, it would not have excluded them from that worship in this second state. Thus, he declared their disassociation from the One he worshipped both when making the first, specific disavowal and when making the second, comprehensive and decisive disavowal. Their condition, however, did not differ between the two states: in both, they did not worship what he worshipped. There was therefore no benefit in changing the wording concerning them. The wording was changed only concerning him and the believers because the two meanings differ.
