A person’s certainty, sincerity, affirmation of Allah’s oneness, disavowal of polytheism and its adherents, and hatred of what they worship and of their worship grow stronger, raising his rank in this respect. In that state, he says to the disbelievers:

“You do not worship what I worship,”

whether or not their disbelief and hatred of him have increased.

---

## Page 8564

The purpose of the surah, then, is for the believer to disavow them and inform them that they are disassociated from him. His disavowal of them is a performative declaration that he brings into effect, just as one does by uttering the two testimonies of faith. This can increase or decrease, grow stronger or weaker. As for them, he is reporting their disassociation from him in this state, not bringing about anything in them that was not already there.

The believer’s words concerning their condition are therefore a statement of fact, and a statement of fact corresponds to what it describes. Thus, the wording of his statement about them does not change, since whenever he worships Allah, they do not worship what he worships. This declarative wording corresponds to their condition at all times, whether their disbelief increases or decreases. The believer may not bring about an increase in their disbelief, for that is forbidden. Rather, he is commanded to call them to faith. Nor may he understate their actual condition when describing them. His account of their condition therefore neither adds to nor subtracts from what they actually are. Accordingly, the declarative wording remains unchanged in the two cases: the same wording is used in both.

As for the believer himself, he is commanded to cultivate greater sincerity towards Allah alone, worship Him alone, and renounce every object of worship besides Him—to cultivate his worship of Allah and his disavowal of every such object and its worshippers.

His words:

“I do not worship what you worship,”

although declarative in form, have a performative meaning, like other performative expressions, such as:

“I bear witness that there is no god but Allah,”

and:

“Indeed, I am free of what you worship, except the One who created me,”

and:

“Indeed, I am free of whatever you associate with Allah.”

All these utterances have a performative meaning, which the believer brings into effect within

---

## Page 8565

himself by increasing his disavowal of polytheism. This surah is “the Healer,” which heals polytheism as a patient is healed of illness. For polytheism and disbelief are the gravest diseases of the heart. Thus, the believer is commanded to make a declaration that brings about in his heart a degree of disavowal of polytheism that was not there before. Whenever he repeats it, his disavowal of polytheism increases, and his heart is further healed of its disease—even if the disbelievers addressed only increase in disbelief through what is stated about them.

Declarative sentences correspond to what they describe, whereas a performative utterance brings into being something that did not previously exist. Thus, it is said:

“Say, ‘O disbelievers, I do not worship what you worship.’”

That is: I refuse this and abandon it. Then He says:

“Nor will I be a worshipper of what you have worshipped.”

That is: I disavow this, keep myself above it, and purify my soul of it. For nothing defiles the soul more than polytheism, and the greatest purification and cleansing of the soul is to purify and cleanse it of polytheism.

I am never a worshipper of anything you have worshipped at any time. Yet you do not worship what I worship; rather, you are disassociated from what I worship. I disavow what you worship, am commanded to disavow it, seek to increase that disavowal, and strive to do so. I report that you are disassociated from what I worship, either because you command this, or because you worship Him—but I do not report that, for it would be a lie—or because you strive in your disavowal and carry it to great lengths. Your states differ in this respect.

---

## Page 8566

And I may not say anything that would undo your disassociation, nor do I speak falsely about you. For you lessen that disassociation when I declare my disavowal. Indeed, such disavowal invites and prompts anyone with understanding to examine the reason for it, especially in the case of the Messenger, who was first addressed with the words:

“Say.”

Let anyone with understanding consider why I disavow polytheism and what you follow; why, in doing so, I choose to incur your enmity, patiently endure your abuse, and bear these tremendous hardships. Previously, you held me in the highest esteem, acknowledged my trustworthiness, called me “the Trustworthy,” and preferred me over others. My lineage among you is the noblest, and you know the understanding, knowledge, noble character, good intentions, and pursuit of justice and kindness that Allah has bestowed upon me. You also know that I wish none of you ill and have no desire to harm anyone. Is my choice to disavow what you worship and openly revile and denounce those objects of worship purposeless, with no compelling reason behind it? Reflect on this.

Thus, the surah invites and encourages the disbelievers to seek and recognize the truth, while also expressing complete disavowal of them. Its many noble meanings would take a long time to describe.

His words:

“Say, ‘O disbelievers’”

include every disbeliever. Thus, he does not worship what any of the disbelievers worship—neither the Arab polytheists nor any other polytheists

---

## Page 8567

nor the disbelievers among the People of the Book—neither Jews nor Christians—nor any other category of disbelievers. For He said:

“I do not worship what you worship.”

He used the word mā (“what”), rather than saying “whom you worship.” As explained earlier, mā indicates an attribute. As for the explanation given by al-Mahdawī and others—that He said “what I worship” rather than “whom I worship” to parallel “Nor am I a worshipper of what you have worshipped,” where idols are meant—it is exceedingly weak. It distorts linguistic usage, restricts the Quran’s deliberately general scope, and removes the very meaning upon which this disavowal rests. For in Arabic, mā is used either for entities without knowledge or for the attributes of those endowed with knowledge, as in His words:

“Marry those who please you,”

“and Him who fashioned it,”

and “and Him who created the male and the female.”

Likewise, among the transmitted expressions of glorification to be said upon hearing thunder is: “Glory be to Him whom it glorifies.” There are many similar examples. Thus, His words,

“Nor are you worshippers of what I worship,”

conform to ordinary Arabic usage.

Furthermore, His words,

“I do not worship what you worship,”

address disbelievers without restriction. Thus, he does not worship the angels or anything else worshipped besides Allah, even when those worshipped possess knowledge and reason and are therefore referred to using man (“who”). Restricting this disavowal of polytheism to the polytheism of the Arab idolaters is a grave error. Rather, it is a disavowal of every form of polytheism. For the Lord to be characterized by the lack of knowledge characteristic of idols is something that cannot

---

## Page 8568

be attributed to Him, nor is such parallel wording valid here. Rather, the purpose is to mention attributes and identify the One worshipped by the Messenger and the believers, so that he may disavow what they worship and dissociate them from the One he worships.

When the Jews say, “We intend to worship Allah,” they are speaking falsely, whether or not they realize it. Likewise, the Christians say, “We worship Allah alone and associate no partners with Him,” yet they are speaking falsely. For if they intended to worship Him, they would worship Him as He commanded—according to His revealed law, not what has been abrogated and altered.

Moreover, the Lord whom they claim they intend to worship is, in their view, a Lord who neither revealed the Gospel nor the Quran, and who sent neither the Messiah nor Muhammad. Indeed, some of them regard Him as poor, others as miserly, others as powerless, and others as unable to change what He has legislated. In the view of all of them, He has supported liars who fabricate falsehoods against Him: men who claim to be His messengers but who, in their view, are not His messengers at all, but lying sorcerers. He has supported them and granted them victory, and has granted their followers victory over His believing allies—for they consider themselves alone, to the exclusion of everyone else, to be His allies.

Thus, the Lord they worship is forever granting victory to His enemies. They worship this Lord, whereas the Messenger and the believers do not worship this object of Jewish worship. He is exalted above the attributes the Jews ascribe to the one they worship

---

## Page 8569

in respect of His being their object of worship; He is exalted above this attribution. He is not, therefore, the object of Jewish worship. Rather, within their natural dispositions are attributes that are not His, which Satan has made appealing to them. They intend to worship the one possessing those attributes, but that is none other than Satan. Thus, the Messenger and the believers do not worship anything the Jews worship, even if they worship the same one. This illustrates the value of the distinction we have mentioned.

Accordingly, His words:

“To you your religion, and to me mine”

address all disbelievers, as the verse itself indicates.

This also reveals the error of those who say that it addresses the polytheists and Christians, but not the Jews, as in Ibn Zayd’s statement concerning:

“To you your religion, and to me mine.”

He said: “It addresses the polytheists and Christians. The Jews worship none but Allah and associate no partners with Him, except that they disbelieve in some of the prophets and what they brought from Allah. They also disbelieve in the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and what he brought, and they killed groups of prophets unjustly and in transgression.”

He said: “Except for the group who made this claim when Nebuchadnezzar came forth; it was said: those who called Ezra ‘the son of Allah,’ yet did not worship him. They did not do as the Christians did, who said, ‘The Messiah is the son of Allah,’ and worshipped him.”

His statement that the Jews do not associate partners with Allah as the Arabs and Christians did is correct. Nevertheless, they do not worship Allah. Rather, they are too arrogant to worship Him and worship Satan; they do not worship Allah. Whoever says that the Jews

---

## Page 8570

worship Allah has made a grievous error. Everyone who worships Allah is among the blessed people of Paradise and among Allah’s righteous servants. The Most High said:

“Did I not enjoin upon you, O children of Adam, not to worship Satan—for he is surely your clear enemy—but to worship Me? This is a straight path.”

The two Sahīh collections report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal when he sent him to Yemen:

“You are going to a people of the Scripture, so let the first thing you call them to be the testimony that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah.”

In another version:

“Call them to worship Allah. When they come to know Allah, inform them…”

Thus, after He sent Muhammad and his message became known and was conveyed, none but Allah is to be worshipped. Hence, the scholars unanimously agree that their deeds are nullified. Had they worshipped Allah, their deeds would not have been nullified, for Allah wrongs no one.

Before Muhammad was sent, only those who worshipped Allah as He had commanded were worshipping Him. As for someone who abandons worshipping Him as He has commanded and follows his own desires, he does not worship Allah; rather, he worships Satan and false gods.

Allah has stated that the Jews worshipped false gods, that He cursed them and became angry with them, and that He made some of them apes, swine, and worshippers of false gods. The term “false god” is a generic designation encompassing Satan, idols, and soothsayers

---

## Page 8571

and dirhams and dinars, among other things. The Most High said:

“Have you not seen those who were given a portion of the Scripture, who believe in superstition and false gods?”

He also said:

“A group of those who were given the Scripture cast the Book of Allah behind their backs as though they did not know, and followed what the devils recited during Solomon’s reign. Solomon did not disbelieve”—and the rest of the verse.

They are more hostile to the believers than the Christians are, their disbelief is graver, and they have incurred Allah’s anger. Hence it has been said that they are below the Christians in the Fire. If the Jews do not worship the Messiah, they have nevertheless fabricated lies against him and his mother that are graver than the Christians’ disbelief. For this reason, Allah has placed the Christians above them until the Day of Resurrection.

The Christians are polytheists who worship Allah while associating partners with Him. As for the Jews, they do not worship Allah; rather, they abandon His worship and disdain it. Whenever a messenger brought them something contrary to their desires, they grew arrogant: some they denied, and others they killed. Rather, they follow their desires and worship Satan. Thus, the Prophet and the believers do not worship what the Jews worship. Although they describe Allah with some attributes that befit Him, they also ascribe to Him things far beneath His perfection. Their hearts contain no worship devoted to Him alone, for that is found only in those who worship Him as He has commanded.

In this surah, He did not say:

“O polytheists,”

such that it could be said that it

---

## Page 8572

addressed only those who associate partners with Allah. Rather, He said:

“O disbelievers.”

Thus, it encompasses every disbeliever, whether one who openly associates partners with Allah or one who denies Allah what is due to Him and arrogantly refuses to worship Him. Such denial is worse than associating partners with Him, and everyone guilty of it must also associate partners with Him. Despite their association of partners with Allah, the Christians perform many acts of worship, whereas the Jews are among the nations least given to worship and furthest removed from worshipping Allah alone. They may, however, know things that the Christians do not, but without worship or putting their knowledge into practice. Thus, they have incurred Allah’s anger, while the others have gone astray. Allah has declared His Messenger and the believers free of both groups.

Among this community, too, are people who know things that the Jews and Christians do not, yet fail to act upon their knowledge. They therefore bear a resemblance to them, as Sufyān ibn ʿUyaynah said:

“Those of our scholars who become corrupt resemble the Jews, and those of our worshippers who become corrupt resemble the Christians.”

Indeed, Abū Hurayrah said:

“How closely tonight resembles last night! You are the people most like the Children of Israel.”

Indeed, an authentic hadith states:

“You will surely follow the ways of those before you, span by span and cubit by cubit, so much so that if they entered a lizard’s burrow, you would enter it too.”

They asked, “The Jews and the Christians?”

He replied, “Who else?”

In another narration: “The Persians and the Romans?”

He replied, “Who else among mankind but those?”

He also said:

“The Jews split into seventy-one sects, and so too did

---

## Page 8573

the Christians, into seventy-two sects. This community will split into seventy-three sects, all of them in the Fire except one.”

This has been discussed at length elsewhere, where the condition of the saved sect has been explained: those who follow what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions followed.

What further clarifies the preceding discussion is that mā (“what”) in His words:

“I do not worship what you worship,
nor are you worshippers of what I worship”

refers to that which is worshipped. It is, however, an unrestricted expression encompassing one or many, masculine or feminine. It therefore encompasses everything they worship.

That which is worshipped is a god. Thus, it is as though He said: “I do not worship your god, nor do you worship my God.” This is like what Allah relates in the account of Jacob. The Most High says:

“Or were you witnesses when death approached Jacob, when he said to his sons, ‘What will you worship after me?’ They said, ‘We will worship your God and the God of your forefathers, Abraham, Ishmael and Isaac—one God—and to Him we submit.’”

The terms “god” and “object of worship” imply a relationship to the worshipper. He says:

“The God of your forefathers, Abraham, Ishmael and Isaac.”

He is the One whom these men—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon them—worship and take as their God. Only those who follow their religion worship Him, as Joseph said:

“Indeed, I have abandoned the religion of a people who do not believe in Allah and who disbelieve in the Hereafter.”

“And I have followed the religion of my forefathers, Abraham

---

## Page 8574

, Isaac and Jacob. It is not for us to associate anything with Allah. That is Allah’s grace upon us and upon mankind,” through to His words:

“That is the upright religion, but most people do not know.”

It is thus clear that the religion of his forefathers is the worship of Allah, and it is the religion of Abraham. The Most High has said:

“Who would turn away from the religion of Abraham except one who makes a fool of himself?”

Through to His words:

“So do not die except as Muslims.”

Since this is so, the Jews and Christians do not follow the religion of Abraham. If they do not follow his religion, they do not worship the God of Abraham, for whoever worships the God of Abraham follows his religion. The Most High says:

“They say, ‘Become Jews or Christians, and you will be guided.’ Say, ‘Rather, we follow the religion of Abraham, upright in faith, and he was not one of the polytheists.’”

Through to His words:

“And He is the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing.”

His words, “Say, ‘Rather, we follow the religion of Abraham,’” make clear that what the Jews and Christians follow is incompatible with the religion of Abraham. After Muhammad’s commissioning as a prophet, this is beyond doubt, for he was sent with the religion of Abraham. Both communities had departed from it through the alterations they had made. The Most High says:

“Indeed, those with the strongest claim to Abraham are those who followed him, this Prophet, and those who believe.”

He also says:

“Say, ‘Indeed, my Lord has guided me to a straight path, an upright religion—the religion of Abraham,’”

and the remainder of the verse. He also says:

“Then We revealed to you: ‘Follow the religion of Abraham, upright in faith.’”

His words, “Who would turn away from the religion of Abraham except one who makes a fool of himself?” make clear

---

## Page 8575

that everyone who turns away from it has made a fool of himself. There are two views concerning the grammar and meaning of this expression. The first is that of al-Farrāʾ and other Kufan grammarians, preferred by Ibn Qutaybah and others; it also expresses the meaning of the explanations given by most of the early generations. According to this view, it is the self that has become foolish. The verb safiha (“to be foolish”) is intransitive and takes no direct object. The meaning, however, is “except someone who is foolish.” The verb is therefore attributed to the person, while “self” is placed in the accusative as a specifying complement, not as an indefinite noun. This resembles His words:

“and my head has become aflame with grey hair.”

The Kufan grammarians recognized both constructions. Al-Farrāʾ said: “The word ‘self’ is placed in the accusative by analogy with an explanatory complement, as in diqtu bi-l-amri dharʿan (‘I was overwhelmed by the matter’), meaning dāqa dharʿī bihi (‘My capacity was insufficient for it’). His words, ‘and my head has become aflame with grey hair,’ are similar: they mean that grey hair has spread like fire through the head.”

He said: “Other examples include alima fulānun raʾsahu (‘So-and-so suffered pain in his head’), wajiʿa batnahu (‘He suffered pain in his stomach’), and rashada amrahu (‘He was rightly guided in his affairs’).”

The underlying constructions were safihat nafsu Zaydin (“Zayd’s self became foolish”) and rashada amruhu (“His affairs took a right course”). When the verb was reassigned to Zayd, the following noun became an accusative of specification. These are examples that al-Farrāʾ knew from Arab speech.

Similar expressions are “So-and-so was deficient in judgment” and “He grew insolent in his livelihood.” His words, “which grew insolent in its livelihood,” are of the same kind: that is, the livelihood itself grew insolent.

This is the meaning of Yamān ibn Rabāb’s statement, “His judgment and his self became foolish.” It is also the meaning of Ibn al-Sāʾib’s statement, “He went astray through his own self,” and of the statement of

---

## Page 8576

Abū Rawq: “His judgment fell short regarding his own self.” The Basran grammarians did not recognize this usage. Some of them therefore interpreted it as “He was ignorant of himself,” as Ibn Kaysān and al-Zajjāj did. He explained: “For whoever worships anyone other than Allah is ignorant of himself, because he does not know his Creator.”

Their interpretation, however, is weak. Even if its meaning is granted to be correct, the word He used is safiha; and safuha is an intransitive verb, not a transitive one, whereas jahila (“to be ignorant of”) is transitive. The Arabs never use saffahtu kadhā to mean “I was ignorant of such-and-such.” Rather, they say safuha, with the vowel u, and its verbal noun safāhah, meaning “he became foolish”; and safiha, with the vowel i, meaning “he displayed foolishness.” They make a similar distinction between faqiha (“he understood”) and faquha (“he became learned”).

Some have also recorded the expression safihat al-shurba, used when one drinks excessively. This accords with the usage reported by al-Farrāʾ: that is, his drinking became foolish, so he “was foolish in his drinking” when he exceeded the proper limit.

Al-Akhfash and Yūnus said: “It is in the accusative because a preposition has been omitted”; that is, safiha fī nafsihi (“he was foolish concerning himself”). Their explanation, “because a preposition has been omitted,” does not establish a general rule on which analogies may be based. Rather, prepositions are sometimes omitted in constructions attested in Arab speech, allowing the verb to govern the noun directly—although this can be extended by analogy in certain constructions.

Safiha, however, is not such a case. One does not say safihtu amra Allāhi (“I was foolish concerning Allah’s command”) or safihtu dīna al-Islāmi (“I was foolish concerning the religion of Islam”) to mean “I was ignorant of it,” on the understanding that an omitted fī (“concerning”) is implied. Rather, only what pertains specifically to the person is described as foolish and placed in the accusative as a specifying complement.

---

## Page 8577

such as his own self or his drinking, and the like. The point is that everyone who turns away from the religion of Abraham is foolish.

Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: “The Jews and Christians turned away from the religion of Abraham and introduced Judaism and Christianity, which are not from Allah, abandoning Abraham’s religion.”

Likewise, Qatādah said: “They altered the religion of the prophets and followed what had been abrogated.” As for Moses, the Messiah, and their followers, they adhere to the religion of Abraham, following him as their leader. This is the meaning of His words:

“Indeed, the people closest to Abraham are those who followed him, this Prophet, and those who believe.”

This encompasses those who followed him both before and after Muhammad was sent as a messenger.

It has also been said that it is general in scope. Al-Hasan al-Basrī said: “Every believer is an ally of Abraham, whether among those who have gone before or those who remain.”

Al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas said: “They are the believers who affirmed Allah’s prophet and followed him. Muhammad and the believers with him were the people closest to Abraham.” This and other evidence make clear that the Jews and Christians do not worship Allah and do not follow the religion of Abraham.

If it is said: A polytheist worships Allah and others besides Him, as evidenced by the words of Allah’s intimate friend:

“Have you considered what you have been worshipping—you and your forefathers? They are enemies to me, except the Lord of all worlds.”

He thus excepted Him from what they worshipped, indicating that they did worship Allah. Likewise, his words:

“Indeed, I am free of what you worship, except the One who created me,”

and he made an exception for Him

---

## Page 8578

also. In the Musnad and elsewhere is the hadith of Husayn al-Khuzā‘ī, whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, asked: “Husayn, how many gods do you worship today?”

He replied: “Seven gods: six on earth and one in heaven.”

He asked: “Which of them do you turn to in hope and fear?”

He replied: “The One in heaven.”

The reply is: This is what the polytheists say, just as the Jews and Christians say, “We worship Allah.” They imagine that worshipping Him while associating partners with Him constitutes worship, but their claim is false.

As for the words of Allah’s intimate friend, there are two interpretations. One group said that this is an exception referring to something outside the class mentioned. ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Zayd said: “They used to worship Allah alongside their gods.” According to this interpretation, the expression is qualified, for he said, “what you worship.” He thus called it worship when the intended meaning was understood, but it is not worship that Allah recognizes as such. For, as He, Most High, said:

“I have less need of partners than anyone. Whoever performs a deed in which he associates another with Me, I disavow it, and it belongs entirely to the one he associated with Me.”

This is like His words, Most High:

“Most of them do not believe in Allah without associating others with Him.”

He called it belief with a qualification. Otherwise, a polytheist who sets up another god alongside Allah is not included within the meaning of faith when the term is used without qualification. He also said:

“They believe in jibt and tāghūt.”

And:

“Give them tidings of a painful punishment.”

These expressions are qualified. Used without qualification, faith means faith in Allah, and glad tidings mean tidings of good.

---

## Page 8579

His words, “Nor do you worship what I worship,” negate worship in the unqualified sense; they do not negate what may be called worship with a qualification. When a polytheist worships Allah and worships another besides Him, it may be said that he worships Allah and another, or that he worships Him while associating partners with Him. It is not said, without qualification, that he worships. The denier who worships nothing is worse than he is. Worship in its unqualified, proper form is what is accepted, whereas the polytheist’s worship is not accepted.

This is further clarified by His words:

“Or were you witnesses when death approached Jacob?”

And the rest of the verse. In it, they said, “We worship your God and the God of your fathers,” then said, “one God.” According to the more evident of the two grammatical interpretations, the latter phrase stands in apposition to the former. An indefinite noun may stand in apposition to a definite one, as in His words:

“We shall surely seize him by the forelock—a lying, sinful forelock.”

The forelock is thus mentioned in definite form and then described. Likewise, they said, “We worship your God,” identifying Him with a definite expression, then said, “one God,” describing Him.

An appositional construction sometimes has the force of repeating the governing element, as in His words:

“The arrogant chiefs among his people said to those who were deemed weak—to those among them who believed.”

The implied wording is therefore: “We worship your God; we worship one God, and to Him we submit.” They thus combined two affirmations: that they worship his God, and that they worship only one God. Whoever worships two gods does not worship his God and the God of his fathers. Only someone who worships one God worships his God. If someone who worshipped Allah while also worshipping another besides Him were considered a worshipper of Him, then worship of Him would be of two kinds: worship involving partners and worship devoted exclusively to Him. If that were so, it would not be

---

## Page 8580

possible for His words, “one God,” to stand in apposition. For this is whole-for-whole apposition, not part-for-whole apposition. It is thus clear that his God and the God of his fathers can only be one God.

The second grammatical interpretation is that His words, “one God,” are in the accusative as a circumstantial qualifier. Yet this qualifier denotes an inseparable state, for He can only be one God. This is like His words, “It is the truth, confirming”—for it can only confirm. Other examples include “the religion of Abraham, inclining to truth” and “and they kill the prophets without right.” Whoever worships another alongside Him has not worshipped Him as one God, and whoever associates partners with Him has not worshipped Him. He can only be one God. Thus, if someone does not worship Him in His inseparable state, there is no other state in which he could worship Him; he has therefore not worshipped Him.

If it is said, “The polytheist sets up other gods alongside Him, so he worships Him in a state in which He is not the only God,” the answer is: This is a mistake arising from the fact that the word “god” can mean either the One who rightfully possesses divinity or something that people have taken as a god, even though it is not a god in reality. Rather, these are names that they and their fathers have given them. Those things are not gods in themselves; they are gods only in the minds of their worshippers.

Their divinity is therefore something the polytheists have imagined and established in their own minds, without its corresponding to external reality. It is like regarding someone who is not learned as learned, someone who is not alive as alive, or someone who is neither truthful nor upright as truthful and upright. Such a person is told: “In your view, this person is truthful, upright and learned.” These are beliefs that do not correspond to reality, and statements that are false and unfitting.

---

## Page 8581

For this reason, He, glorified be He, classes this as fabrication and falsehood, as the Companions of the Cave said:

“These people of ours have taken gods besides Him. Why do they not bring clear proof for them? Who, then, does greater wrong than one who fabricates a lie against Allah?”

Abraham, Allah’s close friend, said:

“You worship only idols besides Allah, and you invent falsehood.”

And Allah said:

“What do those who invoke partners besides Allah follow? They follow nothing but conjecture, and they do nothing but guess.”

That is: what do those who associate partners with Allah follow? They follow only conjecture and guesswork—that is, estimation. This is the correct interpretation: the word mā (“what”) is interrogative. It has also been said that it is a negative particle, and some, such as Abū al-Faraj, mention no other interpretation. This, however, is a weak interpretation, as has been explained elsewhere.

Hud said:

“Worship Allah; you have no god other than Him. You are nothing but fabricators.”

If the divinity of anything besides Allah is an invention that exists in thought and speech but has no existence in external reality—a matter of falsehood and false belief that does not correspond to reality—then the love, fear, and hope that its worshippers direct towards it follow from that false belief. This is like someone who believes a person to be truthful, accepts what he says, and bases many actions on his reports. When that person’s dishonesty becomes clear, the unsoundness of those actions becomes apparent. Such is the case with the followers of Musaylima and al-Aswad, and others associated with zawāyā and falsehoods, and with what they prescribe for their followers without Allah’s permission. This is unlike a truthful person and truthfulness.

---

## Page 8582

For this reason, the declaration of Allah’s oneness is

“like a good tree, whose root is firmly fixed and whose branches reach into the sky.”

Concerning the declaration of polytheism, He said:

“Like a foul tree, uprooted from the surface of the earth, having no stability.”

It has neither a firm foundation nor a firmly established branch, for it is false, like the words and deeds of liars. Indeed, it is the greatest lie and fabrication, coupled with love for it. Associating partners with Allah is the greatest injustice. Ibn Mas‘ūd said: “I asked, ‘O Messenger of Allah, which sin is greatest?’ He replied, ‘To set up a rival to Allah when it is He who created you.’”

Their devotion to these objects, their worship, veneration, love and invocation of them, their belief that they are gods, and their assertions that they are gods—all this exists, just as belief in liars exists. But these objects do not actually possess divinity, just as Musaylima did not possess prophethood.

There are thus two conditions to consider: that of the worshipper and that of the object of worship. All worshippers have worship and devotion in their hearts towards whatever they worship. As for the objects of worship, the Most Merciful possesses divinity, whereas anything besides Him possesses none. Rather, it is dead, unable to bring its worshippers either harm or benefit.

“Say, ‘If there were gods alongside Him, as they say, they would have sought a way to the Lord of the Throne.’”

According to the sounder of the two interpretations, this means “a way” of drawing near to Him through worshipping and remembering Him. This is why He said thereafter:

“The seven heavens and the earth and all who are in them glorify Him, and there is not a thing

---

## Page 8583

but glorifies Him with praise.” Thus, He states that all created beings glorify Him with praise. This has been discussed at length elsewhere.

In His words, “We worship your God,” followed by “one God,” if the latter phrase is understood to be in the accusative as a circumstantial qualifier, it must qualify either the subject—the worshipper—or the object—the One worshipped.

The first interpretation is: we worship Him while sincerely devoting ourselves to Him, worshipping none but Him. The second is: we worship Him in the state inseparable from Him, namely, that He is one God. Thus, we worship Him sincerely, acknowledging that He alone is God, to the exclusion of all others.

If the intended meaning is the second, a polytheist cannot be worshipping Him, because he does not worship Him in this state, and He—glory be to Him—has no other state in which we might worship Him. If the intended meaning is the first, however, we could conceivably worship Him in another state, taking other gods alongside Him in our own minds.

Yet His words “one God” indicate that the circumstantial qualifier describes the One worshipped. This differs from saying, “We worship Him, devoting our religion sincerely to Him,” where the circumstantial qualifier describes the subject. This construction therefore occurs frequently in the Quran, as in His words:

“So worship Allah, being sincere to Him in religion.”

And His words:

“Say, ‘It is Allah I worship, being sincere to Him in my religion.’”

Here, the circumstantial qualifier describes the subject

---

## Page 8584

for the worshipper may at one time be sincerely devoted and at another associate partners with Allah. The Lord Most High, however, is never anything but one God. Although the circumstantial qualifier describes the object, it also qualifies the subject.

For they said that they worship Him in this state. It necessarily follows that their worship of Him takes place in no other state. He also made clear that, in His words, “We worship your God and the God of your fathers” … “one God,” the circumstantial qualifier relates to both subject and object—to the worshipper and the One worshipped. For the grammatical element governing this qualifier is worship, which is the worshipper’s act, while what Arabic grammar calls the object is the One worshipped.

Similarly, regarding the clause “and we submit to Him,” it has been said that “and” is a coordinating conjunction, and also that it introduces a circumstantial clause, meaning: we worship Him in this state. They said: “It qualifies either the subject of ‘we worship’ or its object, since the pronominal suffix in ‘to Him’ refers back to the latter.”

This presentation of alternatives is mistaken, for it qualifies both together. If they worship Him while submitting to Him, then they are submitting both while they are worshippers and while He is worshipped. This circumstance does not accompany only their being worshippers to the exclusion of His being worshipped, or vice versa. Here, the adverbial and circumstantial expression is an utterance, not an isolated word. This is why they were confused about it: a single word cannot grammatically qualify both subject and object.

Thus, if you say, “I struck Zayd sitting,” the sitting qualifies either the subject or the object. But if you say, “I struck him while the people

---

## Page 8585

were sitting,” this circumstance does not qualify one of the two to the exclusion of the other. Rather, it accompanies the act of striking to which it relates, as though one had said: “I struck him at the time when the people were sitting.”

It is thus an adverbial qualification of the action involving both subject and object. This differs from saying: “I struck him while I was sitting,” or “while he was sitting,” where the meaning changes accordingly.

The verse contains the words “one God.” This unquestionably qualifies the One worshipped. It necessarily follows that they worshipped Him only while He was one God, and this is inseparable from Him.

If it is said, “What is meant is: while He is the only One worshipped, with no other object of worship taken alongside Him. This circumstance is not inseparable from Him; rather, it describes the worshippers, not Him,” the reply is:

This contains no praise of Him and does not describe Him as deserving worship; it merely describes them. Moreover, His words “one God” are like His words, “Your God is one God.” He is, in Himself, one God, even if the polytheists set up gods alongside Him through fabrication and love. The intended meaning must therefore be what this designation signifies.

Had they intended that other meaning, they would have said: “We worship Him with sincere devotion to Him alone.” They have already expressed this meaning in the second clause, namely, “and we submit to Him,” especially if this is understood as a circumstantial clause—that is: we worship Him as one God while submitting to Him.

---

## Page 8586

And their submission to Him entails sincere devotion to Him, their humility and their submission to His rulings, unlike non-Muslims. Thus He commanded the believers to say:

“We believe in Allah and what has been revealed to us, and what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob and the Tribes, and what Moses and Jesus were given, and what the prophets were given by their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them, and we submit to Him.”

Then He said:

“The colouring of Allah! And whose colouring is better than Allah’s? And we worship Him alone.”

“Say: Do you dispute with us concerning Allah, when He is our Lord and your Lord? We have our deeds, and you have your deeds, and we are sincerely devoted to Him.”

These verses contain profound meanings, which this is not the place to explore fully.

Section

This disagreement concerning His words, “Say: O disbelievers”—whether they address disbelievers as a whole, as most scholars maintain, or those known to die as disbelievers, as some maintain—relates to what is meant by the terms “disbeliever” and “believer.”

---

## Page 8587

One group says that this applies only to those who come to the Resurrection with faith. In their view, the designation “believer” applies only to someone who dies as a believer. If someone believes and then apostatizes, they do not regard his earlier belief as faith. This is the position adopted by al-Ashʿarī, a group of Ahmad’s followers, and others. Likewise, they say that a disbeliever is someone who dies as a disbeliever.

They say that Allah’s love and hatred, pleasure and displeasure, friendship and enmity depend solely on a person’s state at death. Thus, Allah loves those whom He knows will die as believers, is pleased with them, and befriends them with an eternal love and an eternal friendship.

They say that ʿUmar was a friend of Allah even while he was a disbeliever. This position is known to have been held by Ibn Kullāb and those who followed him, such as al-Ashʿarī and others.

Most groups disagree with him on this. They say that a person may be an enemy of Allah and then become His friend, and that Allah may hate him and then love him. This is the position of the jurists and the general public. It is also the position of the Muʿtazilah, the Karrāmiyyah, all the Hanafīs, and the early Mālikīs, Shāfiʿīs, and Hanbalīs. The Quran supports this, as in His words:

“Say, ‘If you love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you.’”

“And if you are grateful, He is pleased with that for you.”

And His words: “Indeed, those who believed

---

## Page 8588

then disbelieved, then believed, then disbelieved.” Thus, He described them as disbelieving after believing and believing after disbelieving. He also stated that those who disbelieved are disbelievers, and that if they desist, their past sins will be forgiven. He said:

“So when they angered Us, We took vengeance upon them.”

And He said:

“That is because they followed what angered Allah and disliked what pleased Him, so He rendered their deeds worthless.”

In the two Sahīh collections, in the hadith of intercession, the prophets say:

“My Lord has become angry as He has never been before and will never be again.”

The pilgrims’ supplication at al-Multazam, reported from Ibn Abbās and others, includes:

“If You have been pleased with me, then increase Your pleasure with me; otherwise, be pleased with me from now on.”

Some omitted “be pleased with me,” so some jurists thought that min in “fa-min al-ān” (“then from now”) derived from al-mann (“bestowing favor”). This is a textual misreading. It is simply the preposition min (“from”), as the complete wording makes clear: “Otherwise, be pleased with me from now on.” This shows that His pleasure increases and that He becomes pleased at a particular time. There is abundant evidence for this, and it has been discussed at length elsewhere.

Section

Parallel to the interpretation of “Say, ‘O disbelievers’” are the two interpretations of His words:

“Indeed, those who disbelieve—it is all the same to them whether you warn them or do not warn them; they will not believe.”

People hold two views concerning this verse.

---

## Page 8589

The first is that it applies specifically to those who die as disbelievers. This is reported from Muqātil, who gave the same interpretation of His words, “Say, ‘O disbelievers.’” It is also reported from al-Dahhāk. Both said: “It was revealed concerning the Arab polytheists who never embraced Islam, such as Abū Jahl, Abū Tālib, and Abū Lahab.” Al-Dahhāk also said: “It was revealed concerning Abū Jahl and five members of his household.”

Some exegetes, such as al-Thaʿlabī, al-Baghawī, and Ibn al-Jawzī, mentioned no other interpretation. Al-Baghawī said: “This verse concerns people for whom the decree of wretchedness had already been established in Allah’s foreknowledge.”

Ibn al-Jawzī said:
“Our shaykh, Alī ibn Ubayd Allāh, said: ‘Although this verse is expressed in general terms, its intended scope is specific. It declares that the disbelievers will not believe when warned, yet many disbelievers have believed upon being warned. Were it understood in its apparent general sense, Allah’s statement would contradict the reality it describes. It must therefore be understood as referring to a specific group.’”

The second view is that the verse retains its apparent meaning: warning and the absence of warning are alike for the disbeliever so long as he remains a disbeliever. Warning neither benefits nor affects him. The same has been said of the signs: they do not necessarily bring about faith. Allah brings the two together in His words:

“Neither signs nor warnings benefit a people who do not believe.”

---

## Page 8590

The signs—in the horizons, on earth, and in the Quran—provide evidence that leads to knowledge, whereas warning evokes fear. The signs are for someone who acts upon the truth once he recognizes it; such a person benefits from wisdom. Warning is for someone who recognizes the truth but has desires that hold him back. He is therefore warned of punishment, the fear of which prompts him to resist his desires. This is the person who needs good admonition. Another person refuses to accept the truth and therefore needs reasoned debate, which must be conducted in the best manner.

Allah Most High has said:

“Even if We sent down the angels to them, and the dead spoke to them, and We gathered everything before their very eyes, they would not believe unless Allah willed.”

He also said:

“You are only a warner to those who fear it.”

“You can only warn those who follow the Reminder and fear the Most Merciful unseen.”

The meaning, then, is that as long as the disbeliever remains a disbeliever, he does not accept the truth, whether he is warned or not, and he will not believe as long as he remains in that state. This is because barriers over his heart, hearing, and sight prevent understanding and acceptance. The same applies to someone overpowered by his desires. He, glorified be He, did not say, “They will not believe.” That was said concerning those for whom wretchedness had already been decreed, or against whom the decree had become binding, as in His words:

“Indeed, those against whom your Lord’s decree has become binding will not believe, even if every sign comes to them, until they see the painful punishment.”

He thus makes clear that these people will not believe until their faith no longer benefits them, at the time of

---

## Page 8591

seeing the painful punishment, as with Pharaoh’s faith, mentioned earlier. Moses had prayed against him, saying:

“Our Lord, destroy their wealth and harden their hearts, so that they will not believe until they see the painful punishment.”

“He said, ‘The prayer of you both has been answered.’”

But when He, glorified be He, speaks of disbelievers without qualification, this is like His words, “Even if We sent down the angels to them,” and the rest of the verse. He thus makes clear that they may believe if He wills. The verse in al-Baqarah is unqualified and general. At the beginning of the surah, He mentions four verses describing the believers, two describing the disbelievers, and ten-odd verses describing the hypocrites. He explains the condition of the disbeliever who persists in his disbelief: warning does not benefit him because of the barriers over his heart, hearing, and sight.

He did not say, “Allah will not guide any of these people so that he listens and accepts.” Rather, while he is a disbeliever, the verse does not apply to him.

This is analogous to saying of a disbeliever with belligerent status: “No covenant of protection may be concluded with him, nor can he ever be among the inhabitants of the abode of Islam, so long as he retains his belligerent status.” Disbelievers remain in this condition as long as they remain disbelievers. They have impediments that prevent them from believing, just as hypocrites have impediments that prevent them as long as they remain in that condition, even if they are warned. This is like His words:

“The example of those who disbelieve is like that of one who shouts at what hears nothing but calls and cries. Deaf, dumb, and blind, so they do not understand.”

This, then, is the likeness of every disbeliever as long as he remains a disbeliever.

---

## Page 8592

And this does not preclude their being able to hear once the covering over their hearts, hearing, and sight is removed. Their failure to hear is due to the quality from which their designation is derived—namely, disbelief. As long as this remains their condition, they remain as described. Yet their condition can change, as He says:

“Unless Allah wills.”

And this is what actually happens.

Such statements teach a person not to suppose that his calling, warning, and explanation will necessarily bring about guidance, even if he is the most accomplished of people. Although the caller may be righteous, sincere in his counsel, and pure in his intentions, the person he calls may not respond—not because of any deficiency in the call, but because of corruption in its recipient. This is because achieving the desired result depends both on the agent’s action and on the recipient’s receptiveness. A sharp sword, for example, has an effect only if the material it strikes is susceptible to it; it does not cut stone, iron, and the like. Blowing has an effect when there is something receptive to it; it has no effect on ashes.

Calling, teaching, giving guidance, and everything of this kind involve an agent—the speaker who conveys knowledge, guidance, and warning—and a recipient, who is the listener. If the listener is receptive, the warning, instruction, and guidance take full effect. If he is not receptive, one says: “I taught him, but he did not learn; I guided him, but he did not follow the guidance; I addressed him, but he did not listen attentively,” and so forth.

---

## Page 8593

His statement in the Quran,

“Guidance for the God-fearing,”

is of this kind. Only those who accept guidance are guided—namely, the God-fearing—not everyone. This does not mean that they were God-fearing before being guided; they may, in fact, have been disbelievers. Yet only those who are God-fearing are guided by it. Whoever fears Allah is guided by the Quran. Knowledge and warning come only through what the Quran commands.

Likewise, His statement,

“To warn whoever is alive,”

refers to warning that takes full effect, for the living person accepts it. Hence He says:

“And that the word may be fulfilled against the disbelievers.”

They did not accept the warning. Similar is His statement:

“You are only a warner to those who fear it.”

The converse is His statement:

“He leads none astray through it except the transgressors.”

That is, everyone who goes astray through it is a transgressor. This censures whoever goes astray through it, since he is a transgressor; it does not mean that he was a transgressor beforehand. Hence Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqās interpreted this verse as referring to the Khawārij and called them “transgressors” because they went astray through the Quran. Whoever goes astray through the Quran is therefore a transgressor.

His statement,

“Indeed, those who disbelieve,”

belongs to this same category. The meaning is: For anyone whose heart He has sealed and over whose hearing and sight He has placed a covering, it makes no difference whether you warn him or not: he will not believe—that is, as long as he remains in that condition.

---

## Page 8594

But this condition may cease. In the description of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, it says:

“Indeed, We have sent you as a witness, a bearer of glad tidings, and a warner, and as a safeguard for the unlettered. You are My servant and My Messenger. I have named you ‘the one who relies upon Allah.’ You are neither harsh nor hard-hearted, nor loud in the marketplaces. He does not repay evil with evil, but pardons and forgives. I shall not take his soul until, through him, I have set the crooked faith straight and opened blind eyes, deaf ears, and closed hearts.”

He has also said:

“So that you may warn a people whose forefathers were not warned, and who are therefore heedless. The word has indeed been fulfilled against most of them, so they will not believe.”

This indicates that some of them will believe. He then said:

“Indeed, We have placed shackles around their necks,”

continuing to His words:

“You can only warn those who follow the Reminder and fear the Most Merciful unseen.”

This is warning that takes full effect: warning that the person warned accepts and benefits from. His words,

“It makes no difference to them whether you warn them or not,”

refer to the act of warning itself. Similarly, one might say of someone who is dull-witted or whose mind is preoccupied with worldly concerns and desires: “It makes no difference whether you teach him or not: he does not learn or accept guidance.” Of an intelligent person whose mind is free of distractions, one says: “Only someone like this learns.”

Yet someone who is preoccupied may become free of distractions. A mind may become sound after being corrupt, and become corrupt after being sound, depending on the corruption or soundness of the heart.

Most of the explanations transmitted from the early generations accord with this view, as recorded by Ibn Ishāq and related by Ibn Abī Hātim and others. Ibn Ishāq said: “I was told by Muhammad ibn Abī

---

## Page 8595

Muhammad, from ‘Ikrima or Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, from Ibn ‘Abbās:

‘Indeed, those who disbelieve’ means those who disbelieve in what has been sent down to you, even if they say, ‘We believe in what came to us before you.’ ‘It makes no difference to them whether you warn them or not; they will not believe’ means that they have disbelieved in the mention of you in their own scriptures and denied the covenant taken from them. Thus, they have disbelieved both in what has come to you and in what they possess of the revelation brought to them by others before you. How, then, will they heed any warning or admonition from you?”

It is therefore clear that they do not heed the warning because they disbelieve in what they possess and in the truth that has come to them. Yet it is well known that many of them subsequently repented and believed.

It is also reported from al-Rabī‘ ibn Anas, from Abū al-‘Āliya, who said: “Two verses concern the leaders of the confederates: ‘Indeed, those who disbelieve—it makes no difference to them whether you warn them or not; they will not believe.’”

He said: “They are those whom Allah mentions in this verse: ‘Have you not seen those who exchanged Allah’s favor for disbelief and led their people into the abode of ruin?’”

(I say: He identified them as the leaders of the confederates because they led their followers astray and brought them into the abode of ruin. Yet most of the leaders of the confederates at the Battle of the Trench embraced Islam and became good Muslims, including ‘Ikrima ibn Abī Jahl, Safwān ibn Umayya, Suhayl ibn ‘Amr, and Abū Sufyān. Some of these embraced Islam in the year of the Conquest; they were those granted amnesty. Others embraced Islam before that. The other confederate group was Ghatafān, and they too embraced Islam.

---

## Page 8596

And the verse must include the disbelievers among the People of the Book, as Ibn Ishāq said, for the surah was revealed in Medina, even though it also includes the polytheists. It thus encompasses every disbeliever. Muqātil and al-Dahhāk restrict its scope to certain Arab polytheists.

Ibn al-Sā’ib says that it was revealed specifically concerning the Jews, including Huyayy ibn Akhtab. Likewise, Ibn Ishāq reports from Ibn ‘Abbās that it concerns the Jews.

Abū al-‘Āliya says that it was revealed concerning the leaders of the confederates. Yet the verse encompasses all these people and others, just as the verses concerning the believers and hypocrites were occasioned by the believers and hypocrites present when they were revealed, but encompass both them and all other believers and hypocrites until the Hour is established.

The point is that His words,

“It makes no difference to them whether you warn them or not; they will not believe,”

are like His words,

“You cannot make the dead hear, nor can you make the deaf hear the call when they turn their backs and retreat.”

“Nor can you guide the blind out of their error.”

And His words,

“Can you make the deaf hear, even though they do not understand?”

“And among them are those who look at you. But can you guide the blind, even though they do not see?”

All this makes clear that merely calling them, conveying the message to them, and being eager for their guidance does not bring that guidance about. It comes about only if Allah wills to guide them and opens their hearts to Islam, as the Most High says: “Even if you are eager for their guidance, Allah

---

## Page 8597

does not guide those He leaves astray.” This offers consolation to His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The verse makes clear to him that, even if conveying the message does not lead them to guidance, it brings other immense benefits. It also makes clear that true guidance is Allah’s guidance:

“Whoever Allah guides is truly guided, and whoever He leaves astray—you will never find for him a protecting ally to guide him.”

He also said to him:

“You cannot guide whom you love, but Allah guides whom He wills.”

This affirms Allah’s oneness and the purpose of the prophetic mission. He, glorified be He, spoke of those who will not believe, saying:

“Indeed, those against whom your Lord’s word has become binding will not believe, even if every sign comes to them.”

And He said:

“So that you may warn a people whose forefathers were not warned, and who are therefore heedless.”

Then He said:

“The word has indeed become binding against most of them, so they will not believe.”

Thus, in this verse, as in the other, He singles out “those against whom your Lord’s word has become binding.” These are those against whom the word has become binding—that is, what Allah, glorified be He, has said, written and decreed has become binding against them. He thus identifies the determining cause as the prior decree, which is His word.

A statement may be merely a declaration of what will happen, or it may entail other things, like an oath that entails urging or restraining. He has mentioned in several places that an oath had already been made, as in His words:

“Had We willed, We would have given every soul its guidance, but My word has become binding.”

And there are other passages of this kind.

---

## Page 8598

Thus, this is an account of what He has said, or what He has said and written. This is the decree, which entails that He has determined what He will do, known it, and written it down. The scriptural texts abundantly attest that Allah determined the destinies of His creatures fifty thousand years before creating the heavens and the earth. His decree encompasses His knowledge of what will occur and His will that what He has decreed—and knows He will create—should come into existence.

A statement may be a declaration, or it may carry the force of a demand, urging or restraining through an oath, or through what He has prescribed for Himself, as in His words:

“Your Lord has prescribed mercy for Himself.”

And His words:

“It was incumbent upon Us to help the believers.”

And His words:

“O My servants, I have forbidden injustice for Myself and made it forbidden among you, so do not wrong one another.”

As for His words:

“But the word of punishment has become binding against the disbelievers,”

this applies specifically to the disbelievers. It is the threat of punishment that entails recompense for deeds, as when He, exalted be He, said to Iblīs:

“I will surely fill Hell with you and all those among them who follow you.”

And His words:

“Had it not been for a prior word from your Lord and an appointed term, it would have been inevitable.”

That is, their punishment has an appointed time: either the Day of Resurrection, a time in this world such as the day of Badr, or immediately after death. All three interpretations have been mentioned concerning this verse. Thus, were it not for a prior word from your Lord postponing it until an appointed time, the punishment would have been inevitable—that is, inescapable for them. For the grounds warranting it are fully present: their disbelief.

---

## Page 8599

But when He speaks of the disbelievers without qualification, He does not mean only those among them who will never believe. The wording does not indicate that at all. Moreover, such an interpretation serves no purpose, since those people are unknown: they are merely a group against whom the word has become binding, indistinguishable from others. Rather, the Prophet is commanded to warn everyone, including those who will believe and those who will not. Using general wording while intending those people alone neither clarifies the particular group intended, nor states the reason why they will never believe, nor connects the judgment to the general meaning. Allah’s speech, exalted be He, is above such shortcomings.

The obstacles He mentions are present in everyone who fails to heed the warning—whether a disbeliever, a hypocrite, a sinner, or otherwise—because of some cause that entails such failure. These obstacles thus prevent the warning from being heeded. Yet these obstacles may disappear, for they are not inseparable from every disbeliever. If, however, the obstacle is the prior word that has become binding against them, it may never disappear, as He says:

“Indeed, those against whom your Lord’s word has become binding will not believe, even if every sign should come to them, until they see the painful punishment.”

He may mention both of these.

---

## Page 8600

But when He mentions only the obstacles within them, without mentioning the prior decree, their removal may be hoped for and is possible—unless He also mentions something that precludes a change in their condition and their attaining guidance.

Section

“Say, ‘O disbelievers, I do not worship what you worship.’”

The address here uses mā (“what”), not man (“who”). One explanation offered is that He says, “I do not worship what you worship,” rather than “I do not worship whom you worship,” because man is used for beings endowed with knowledge, whereas idols have no knowledge.

This explanation is very weak. What the polytheists worship includes both beings endowed with knowledge—such as angels, prophets, jinn, and human beings—and things without knowledge. When the two occur together, the form used for beings endowed with knowledge takes precedence, as in His words:

“Among them are those that move on their bellies, those that walk on two legs, and those that walk on four.”

Thus, when He describes them in terms appropriate to beings endowed with knowledge, He refers to them in terms of servitude to Him, as in His words:

“Indeed, those whom you call upon besides Allah are servants like you. So call upon them, and let them answer you, if you are truthful. Do they have feet with which they walk, or hands with which they grasp?”

And so the verse continues. He thus refers to them using the masculine plural pronoun, which is used for beings endowed with knowledge.

---

## Page 8601

As for things without knowledge, their plural takes feminine agreement, as when you say: “The wealth—I gathered it,” and “The stones—I threw them.” Thus, mā (“what”) is used for things without knowledge and for the attributes of beings endowed with knowledge. Hence it can denote an entire class, since a class encompasses its members by virtue of their attributes. As He says:

“Marry such women as are pleasing to you.”

That is, those who are pleasing—the pleasing among women. Since He intended to speak of those characterized as pleasing, focusing on this attribute rather than merely on the individuals themselves, He used mā. Had He used man (“who”), the focus would have been merely on the individuals, with the attribute serving to identify them rather than being intended in its own right, even if it ceased to apply. This is like saying: “Someone who knows came to me,” “Someone who was in the mosque yesterday came to me,” or “Someone who did such-and-such came to me,” and similar expressions. The intent is to speak of the individual, while the relative clause serves to identify them, even if that attribute no longer applies.

Another example is His saying:

“By the heaven and the One who built it,”
“by the earth and the One who spread it out,”
“and by a soul and the One who proportioned it.”

According to the correct view, mā here is a relative pronoun, and the meaning is: by its Builder, its Spreader, and its Fashioner. But when He says:

“Successful indeed is the one who purifies it,”
“and failed indeed is the one who corrupts it,”

He uses man, because the intent is to speak of the individual’s success, even though their acts of purification or corruption have already passed in this world. The oath in those earlier verses is therefore by the One bearing the attribute: He swears precisely by this One, while the attribute

---

## Page 8602

is inseparable from Him. For the heaven cannot be built except by its Builder, nor the earth spread out except by its Spreader, nor the soul proportioned except by its Fashioner. As for the person who purifies or corrupts his soul, his work has ended in this world, and his success or failure in the Hereafter does not require that work to continue. Similar to this is His saying:

“And by the One who created the male and the female.”

For this reason, mā (“what”) is used to ask about the attributes of beings endowed with knowledge, as in His saying:

“What is the Lord of all worlds?”

Likewise, in one respect, it is used to ask in His saying:

“What do you worship?”

As for His saying:

“If you ask them who created the heavens and the earth, they will surely say, ‘Allah,’”

the question concerns the identity of the Creator, to distinguish Him from the gods that are worshipped. For those being questioned already acknowledged the attribute of being the Creator; the question sought only to identify and distinguish Him, and to establish the proof against them that He alone deserves worship.

Pharaoh, however, denied the very One described and named, so he asked his question using mā (“what”). He was not acknowledging His existence and seeking to identify Him. Thus Moses answered this question by saying:

“The Lord of the heavens and the earth,”

and:

“Your Lord and the Lord of your forefathers.”

Thus, he too answered by stating an attribute. In the other passage, however, He says:

“If you ask them who created them, they will surely say, ‘Allah.’”

The answer was therefore the name that distinguishes the One named from all others. The same applies to His saying:

“Say, ‘To whom belong the earth and all who are in it?’”

—and so on to the end of these verses.

---

## Page 8603

Thus, His words:

“I do not worship what you worship, nor are you worshippers of what I worship”

entail dissociation from everything described as an object of their worship. For whatever a disbeliever worships must be disavowed, since no disbeliever’s object of worship can be the God whom the believer worships. If He were his object of worship, he would be a believer, not a disbeliever. This entails several things.

First: it necessarily entails disavowing the very beings whom they worship besides Allah.

Second: when they worship Allah and others, their object of worship is that combination. He does not worship that combination; he worships Allah alone. He worships Him with exclusive devotion, not by associating others with Him in worship. This clarifies the distinction between this passage and the words of Abraham, Allah’s close friend:

“Indeed, I am free of whatever you worship, except the One who created me,”

and his words:

“Have you considered what you have been worshipping—you and your forefathers? They are enemies to me, except the Lord of all worlds.”

The distinction may be explained as follows: here, worship of the combination is negated, but that does not negate worship of the One who is Allah. Abraham, however, disavowed the whole, which entails disavowing each individual member; hence he made an exception.

Alternatively, one may say: Abraham disavowed all the objects of worship of all those people, so the Lord of all worlds had to be excepted. Thus, since an exception had already been made at the beginning of the passage, in His words:

---

## Page 8604

“You have an excellent example in Abraham and those with him, when they said to their people, ‘We disavow you and whatever you worship besides Allah’”,

there was no need for another exception. As for this surah, it expresses dissociation from worshipping what they worship, not from the objects of their worship themselves. He disavows them, their worship, and what they worship.

For all of that is false, as authentically reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that Allah says:

“I have no need whatsoever of partners. Whoever performs a deed in which he associates another with Me, I disavow it, and it belongs entirely to the one he associated with Me.”

Thus, the worship of one who associates partners with Allah is entirely invalid. It cannot be said that Allah’s share of it is valid while the remainder is invalid. The objects of their worship, however, are different: Allah is the true God, while all others are false gods. Hence, when Abraham, Allah’s close friend, disavowed the objects of worship, he needed to except the Lord of all worlds. Here, however, he dissociates himself from worshipping what they worship. Since what is negated is worship, he dissociates himself from worshipping the whole combination of those whom the disbelievers worship.

Third: if the negation concerns something insofar as it is their object of worship, rather than that thing itself, then he does not worship anything insofar as it is their object of worship. For insofar as it is their object of worship, they associate partners with Allah in worshipping it; worshipping it in that manner must therefore be disavowed. Had the wording been “whom you worship”, one would have said, “except the Lord of all worlds”, because the negation applies to

---

## Page 8605

the object of worship itself. His not worshipping what they worship does not mean that he disavows it and is hostile to it, such that an exception would be needed. Rather, he refrains from worshipping what they worship.

This becomes clear from the fourth point, namely the statement:

“Nor are you worshippers of what I worship.”

This denies that they worship the One he worships. Thus, when they worship Allah while associating partners with Him, they are not worshipping the One he worships. Likewise, when he worships Him, sincerely devoting his religion to Him alone, he is not worshipping the object of their worship.

The fifth point:

If they were to identify Allah with something that is not Allah, intending to worship Allah while believing that this is Allah—as with those who worshipped the calf, those who worshipped the Messiah, those who worship the Dajjāl, those who worship whatever they worship of their worldly interests and desires, and those of this community who engage in such worship—then, in their own minds, they would be worshipping only Allah. Yet this object of their worship is not Allah. Thus, when he says:

“I do not worship what you worship,”

he disavows these objects of worship, even though the worshippers intend to worship Allah.

The sixth point:

If they attribute to Allah things of which He is entirely free, such as a consort, a child or a partner, or describe Him as poor, miserly or anything of the kind, and worship Him as thus described, then he disavows their object of worship, for this is not Allah

---

## Page 8606

as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do you not see how Allah deflects the insults of Quraysh from me? They insult Mudhammam (the blameworthy), whereas I am Muhammad (the praised).”

Although they intended him personally, because they described him as blameworthy, their insults fell upon someone blameworthy, whereas he is Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Likewise, that object of worship is not Allah. The believers therefore disavow what these people worship.

The seventh point:

Anyone who does not believe in the attributes with which the Messenger described his Lord has, in reality, not worshipped the One whom the Messenger worshipped, in that respect. Apply this reasoning to similar cases. These meanings should therefore be carefully considered, distilled and refined. And Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 8607

Surah Tabbat

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

“Surah Tabbat”

This surah was revealed concerning this man and his wife, who belonged to the two noblest clans of Quraysh. He was ‘Alī’s paternal uncle, and she was Mu‘āwiyah’s paternal aunt. These two clans—Banū Umayyah and Banū Hāshim—were the ones between whom the caliphate passed within the Muslim community. Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, however, belonged to two tribes more distantly related to him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. A degree of concord prevailed during their rule that was not achieved after them.

Among those who disbelieved in him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, none is condemned by name in the Quran except this man and his wife. This shows that lineage carries no weight; rather, a person of noble standing deserves greater condemnation for failing to fulfil his duty. As Allah Most High says:

“O wives of the Prophet! Whoever among you commits a blatant indecency will have her punishment doubled”—to the end of the verse.

Al-Nahhās said:

“May the hands of Abū Lahab perish”

is a supplication for his ruin. In ‘Abd Allāh’s reading, it says: “And he has indeed perished.” His words,

“and what he earned,”

mean his children. For His words:

---

## Page 8608

“and what he earned”

includes his children, as in the hadith: “His children are part of his earnings.” This has been cited as evidence that it is permissible to eat from one’s child’s wealth.

Then He declares:

“He will burn in a Fire.”

He thus foretells the loss of good and the onset of evil. The term al-siliyy denotes both entering the Fire and being burned.

As for His words:

“the carrier of firewood,”

if this is a metaphor for tale-bearing—because it kindles evil—then it is firewood for hearts. Yet it may be said: her sin is graver, and carrying tales is not described in terms of a rope around the neck. If, however, this describes her condition in the Hereafter, just as He describes her husband—he burns while she carries firewood to pile upon him, just as she helped him in his disbelief—then this is an instance of partners being gathered together. It contains a lesson for any two people who cooperate in sin, or in any particular sin or act of aggression.

The Quran would thus have covered all four possible configurations of a married couple: Abraham and his wife; this man and his wife; Pharaoh and his wife; or Noah and his wife, and Lot.

It is also tenable to interpret carrying firewood as tale-bearing in terms of carrying fuel in the Hereafter, as in the saying: “Whoever has two tongues,” and so on. And Allah knows best.
